18The trace asymptotic and the spectral test

18.1The zoom bound and the logarithmic zoom bound

The trace sections below compare scales along the selected run through the reference inverse coupling \(\hat{\beta}_j\). Two elementary facts about the cap on the chart zoom are used there: the cap has an exponential bound of rate zero (Subsection 18.2), and the logarithm of the zoom factor is at most \((\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48}\) at the frame level \(j\) (the scale parameter paragraph of the stress transfer producer, Subsection 18.17). This section defines the cap and proves both facts. They use only the definition of the scale parameter; no analytic estimate enters.

The scale parameter. The selected run has rates

\(\displaystyle \hat{\beta}_j = \bar{\beta} + \kappa \log (\textsf{a}_j^{- 1}), \qquad j \in \mathbb{N},\)

where \(\textsf{a}_j > 0\) is the field scale at level \(j\) and \(\bar{\beta}, \kappa\) are fixed constants with \(\kappa > 0\). We use only two properties of these numbers:

\(\displaystyle \hat{\beta}_j > 1 \quad \text{for every } j, \qquad \hat{\beta}_j \longrightarrow \infty \quad (j \to \infty) . \) (18.1)

Put

\(\displaystyle L_j = \log \hat{\beta}_j > 0, \qquad u_j = \log L_j = \log \log \hat{\beta}_j .\)

By (18.1), \(L_j \to \infty\), hence \(u_j \to \infty\). The scale parameter monitor of Subsection 14.19 is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{M}_j = \exp (u_j^2) \geqslant 1.\)

For a real exponent \(c\) the magnified monitor is \(\mathcal{M}_j^{\hspace{0.17em} c} = \exp (c \hspace{0.17em} u_j^2)\). Since \(\mathcal{M}_j \geqslant 1\), the map \(c \mapsto \mathcal{M}_j^{\hspace{0.17em} c}\) is nondecreasing on \(\mathbb{R}\).

Definition 18.1. (Zoom bound) The zoom bound at level \(j\) is the magnified monitor at the exponent \(u_j\):

\(\displaystyle \text{zoomCap} (j) =\mathcal{M}_j^{\hspace{0.17em} u_j} = \exp (u_j^3) = \exp ((\log \log \hat{\beta}_j)^3) .\)

Tickets. A sequence \((f_j)_{j \in \mathbb{N}}\) of real numbers has a ticket of rate \(\rho \in \mathbb{R}\) if for every \(\rho' > \rho\) there is \(j_0\) such that

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant f_j \leqslant \exp (\rho' (\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48}) \qquad \text{for all } j \geqslant j_0 .\)

For a nonnegative sequence this is the notion used in Subsection 18.2.

Lemma 18.2. (Exponential rate zero for the zoom bound)

  1. For every \(j\) with \(\log \hat{\beta}_j \geqslant 1\),

    \(\displaystyle 1 \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j) \leqslant \exp ((\log \hat{\beta}_j)^3) .\)

  2. For every integer \(p \geqslant 0\) the sequence \(j \mapsto \text{zoomCap} (j)^p\) has an exponential bound of rate \(0\). In particular \(\text{zoomCap}\) itself and its fifth power have exponential bounds of rate \(0\).

Proof. (1) Let \(L_j = \log \hat{\beta}_j \geqslant 1\). Then \(u_j = \log L_j \geqslant 0\), and the inequality \(\log x \leqslant x - 1 < x\) for \(x > 0\) gives \(u_j < L_j\). Hence \(0 \leqslant u_j^3 \leqslant L_j^3\), and applying the increasing function \(\exp\) gives \(1 \leqslant \exp (u_j^3) \leqslant \exp (L_j^3)\).

(2) Fix \(p \geqslant 0\) and \(\rho' > 0\). Since \(L_j \to \infty\), there is \(j_0\) such that for all \(j \geqslant j_0\)

\(\displaystyle L_j \geqslant 1 \qquad \text{and} \qquad L_j^{45} \geqslant \frac{p}{\rho'} .\)

For such \(j\), part (1) gives

\(\displaystyle 1 \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j)^p = \exp \left( p \hspace{0.17em} u_j^3 \right) \leqslant \exp \left( p \hspace{0.17em} L_j^3 \right) \leqslant \exp (\rho' L_j^{45} L_j^3) = \exp (\rho' (\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48}) .\)

In particular \(0 \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j)^p\) for \(j \geqslant j_0\). As \(\rho' > 0\) was arbitrary, \(\text{zoomCap}^p\) has an exponential bound of rate \(0\).\(\Box\)

The chart receipt. Let \(j_i\) be the frame level of the \(i\)th selected chart and \(\textsf{b}_i > 0\) its zoom factor, so that the chart radius is \(r_i = \textsf{a}_{j_i} \textsf{b}_i\). The selected chart is produced at an exact clock gap (Subsection 17.2): its zoom factor is a magnified monitor \(\textsf{b}_i =\mathcal{M}_{j_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} c_i}\) whose base arc \(c_i\) satisfies \(5 < c_i\) and is at most the permitted angular bound \(\log \log \hat{\beta}_{j_i} = u_{j_i}\). Because \(c \mapsto \mathcal{M}_j^{\hspace{0.17em} c}\) is nondecreasing and \(\mathcal{M}_j \geqslant 1\), these two inequalities give

\(\displaystyle 1 \leqslant \mathcal{M}_{j_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} 5} \leqslant \textsf{b}_i \leqslant \mathcal{M}_{j_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} u_{j_i}} = \text{zoomCap} (j_i) \qquad \text{for every } i. \) (18.2)

This is the receipt \(1 \leqslant \textsf{b}_i \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j_i)\) used in Subsection 18.14 and the bound \(0 < \textsf{b}_i \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j_i)\) used in Subsection 18.2. The lemma below assumes only (18.2) on a tail, not the exponent form of \(\textsf{b}_i\).

Lemma 18.3. (Logarithmic zoom bound) Let \((j_i)\) be a sequence of levels with \(j_i \to \infty\) and let \(\textsf{b}_i > 0\) satisfy \(1 \leqslant \textsf{b}_i \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j_i)\) for all sufficiently large \(i\). Then there is \(i_0\) such that for all \(i \geqslant i_0\)

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant \log \textsf{b}_i \leqslant (\log \log \hat{\beta}_{j_i})^3 \leqslant (\log \hat{\beta}_{j_i})^3 \leqslant (\log \hat{\beta}_{j_i})^{48} . \) (18.3)

Consequently

\(\displaystyle \frac{\log \textsf{b}_i}{\hat{\beta}_{j_i}} \longrightarrow 0.\)

Proof. Since \(j_i \to \infty\) and \(\hat{\beta}_j \to \infty\), we have \(\hat{\beta}_{j_i} \to \infty\); choose \(i_0\) such that for \(i \geqslant i_0\) both \(\log \hat{\beta}_{j_i} \geqslant 1\) and \(1 \leqslant \textsf{b}_i \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j_i)\). Fix \(i \geqslant i_0\) and write \(L = \log \hat{\beta}_{j_i} \geqslant 1\) and \(u = \log L\). Taking logarithms in \(1 \leqslant \textsf{b}_i \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j_i) = \exp (u^3)\) gives \(0 \leqslant \log \textsf{b}_i \leqslant u^3\). The proof of Lemma 18.2(1) gives \(0 \leqslant u < L\), so \(u^3 \leqslant L^3\); and \(L^3 \leqslant L^{48}\) because \(L \geqslant 1\). This proves (18.3).

For the limit, put \(t_i = \hat{\beta}_{j_i} \to \infty\). By (18.3), \(0 \leqslant \log \textsf{b}_i / t_i \leqslant (\log t_i)^{48} / t_i\) for \(i \geqslant i_0\). With \(s = \log t\), \((\log t)^{48} / t = s^{48} e^{- s} \to 0\) as \(t \to \infty\), since the exponential dominates every power. The squeeze gives the claim.\(\Box\)

In the stress transfer clock inputs (18.172) the third clause, \(0 \leqslant \log \textsf{b}_i \leqslant (\log \hat{\beta}_{j_i})^{48}\) eventually, is therefore a consequence of the chart receipt (18.2) and the scale parameter (18.1), with the stronger exponent \(3\) in place of \(48\). The other two clauses of (18.172), the ratio limits \(\zeta (j) / \hat{\beta}_j \to 1\) and \(\zeta (j) / \zeta (\text{summit} (j)) \to 1\), are not addressed here.

18.2A priced mission forces the weighted response to vanish

This section records the quantitative argument behind the weighted field-image estimate used by the trace receiver. It exposes the local bound, scale, and detector inputs. It does not derive the mission's local bound bound from the finite-volume estimates.

Let \(S\) be the selected interacting table, and let \(\mathcal{U}_i^{(d)} : E_d \to S_d\) be its scale chart from the reference grade space \(E_d\) into the selected grade space \(S_d\). Here \(E_d\) is the grade-\(d\) field space of the reference (standard) table; it carries a complex-antilinear real-structure involution and an action \(R (s)\) of the signed permutations \(s\) of the four axes. Let \(\mathcal{V}\) be the finite-dimensional real vector space of grade-five response arrays \(y = (y_{\alpha})_{\alpha \in \text{Axis}}\), \(y_{\alpha} \in E_5\), which are real and signed-equivariant: every \(y_{\alpha}\) is fixed by the real-structure involution, and

\(\displaystyle R (s) y_{\alpha} = \text{sign} (s, \alpha) \hspace{0.17em} y_{s \alpha} \qquad \text{for every signed permutation } s \text{ and every axis } \alpha,\)

where \(\text{sign} (s, \alpha)\) is the sign that \(s\) attaches to the axis \(\alpha\). Both conditions are part of the definition; signed equivariance alone defines a larger space. Let \(\mathcal{B}\) be the real vector space of arrays of raw grade-five labels defined by the same two conditions, with the conjugation and the signed-permutation action of the raw labels in place of those of \(E_5\). The normalized response map is a real-linear map \(s_i : \mathcal{B} \to \mathcal{V}\). Write \(j_i\) for the selected frame level and \(k_i\) for its associated scale index, the summit index of \(j_i\); in particular \(k_i > j_i\), and \(j_i \to \infty\) along the selected sequence. Two distinct positive scales are used:

\(\displaystyle z_i = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}, \qquad \zeta_i = \zeta (k_i),\)

where \(\zeta (k) > 0\) is the arch scale at level \(k\). The first, \(z_i\), appears in the polynomial weight and rate estimate; the second, \(\zeta_i\), is the Arch scale in the medium and cancels from the response identity below. Let \(a_i\) be the field dilation at level \(j_i\), \(b_i\) the frame scale, \(m_i\) the frame cap, and \(r_i\) the physical dilation. The selected scale relations are

\(\displaystyle r_i = a_i b_i, \qquad 0 < b_i \leqslant m_i, \qquad m_i = \text{zoomCap} (j_i) .\)

Let \(v_i \in \mathcal{B}\) be the selected medium and \(x_i \in \mathcal{B}\) its core field. The three identities (18.4)–(18.6) below are used only for all sufficiently large \(i\). They are hypotheses of Lemma 18.4 and are not proved in this section; the first two are required only on such a tail, and the third requires \(k_i\) to be at least the grade-five reference level, which holds for large \(i\) because \(k_i > j_i \to \infty\). The core representation is

\(\displaystyle v_i (\alpha) = \zeta_i^{- 1} \mathcal{E}_{k_i} (x_i (\alpha)) \qquad (\alpha \in \text{Axis}), \) (18.4)

where \(\mathcal{E}_k\) is the core-to-strip map at scale index \(k\). The response normalization is, for every \(v \in \mathcal{B}\) and every axis \(\alpha\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{U}_i^{(5)} ((s_i (v))_{\alpha}) =\mathcal{V}_i (v_{\alpha}), \) (18.5)

where \(\mathcal{V}_i = \text{Vault} (S, Z, 5, k_i, c_i)\) is the grade-five strip evaluation at scale index \(k_i\) with crown normalization \(c_i\). For a raw grade-five label \(x\) which is odd under central signed inversion, meaning \(F (\text{archV}) x = - x\) (the night condition), the exact scaling identity is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{V}_i (\mathcal{E}_{k_i} (x)) = c_i P_5 x, \qquad c_i = \zeta_i (a_i b_i)^5, \) (18.6)

where \(P_5 = S. \text{project}_5\) maps raw grade-five labels into \(S_5\). These are exact identities on the stated tail, assumed as hypotheses; they are not consequences of the estimate below. Every entry \(y_{\alpha}\) of an array \(y \in \mathcal{B}\) satisfies the night condition: the central inversion fixes each axis and attaches the sign \(- 1\) to it, so signed equivariance reads \(F (\text{archV}) y_{\alpha} = - y_{\alpha}\). In particular (18.6) applies to \(x = x_i (\alpha)\).

The tested configuration consists of two points. With \(e_0\) the unit vector of axis \(0\), put

\(\displaystyle \zeta_i^{\text{pt}} = \left( b_i^{- 1} (\lceil b_i \rceil, 1, 1, 1), \hspace{0.17em} 0 \right), \qquad \zeta_{\infty}^{\text{pt}} = (e_0, 0) .\)

The scale input is \(b_i \to \infty\); hence \(\zeta_i^{\text{pt}} \to \zeta_{\infty}^{\text{pt}}\), the axial configuration. By \(r_i = a_i b_i\), the dilated configuration \(r_i \zeta_i^{\text{pt}} = (a_i (\lceil b_i \rceil, 1, 1, 1), 0)\) consists of two distinct points of the lattice of spacing \(a_i\), the kind of configuration at which the finite-volume estimate is formulated. For a reference grade-four field \(w \in E_4\) and \(Y \in \mathcal{V}\), let \(\lambda_{i, w} (Y)\) be the two-point function of the selected table with entries \(\mathcal{U}_i^{(5)} (Y_0)\) and \(\mathcal{U}_i^{(4)} w\), at the shrinking configuration \(r_i \zeta_i^{\text{pt}}\); only the axis-\(0\) entry \(Y_0\) of the array enters. Let \(\lambda_w (Y)\) be the two-point function of the reference table with entries \(Y_0\) and \(w\) at \(\zeta_{\infty}^{\text{pt}}\). Denote the selected-table two-point function at \(r_i \zeta_i^{\text{pt}}\) on explicitly supplied grade-five and grade-four entries by \(\mathfrak{p}_{i, w}\); it is complex-linear in the grade-five entry. The local bound input is the bound

\(\displaystyle | \mathfrak{p}_{i, w} (a_i^5 P_5 x_i (0), \mathcal{U}_i^{(4)} w) | \leqslant \Theta_i, \quad \text{eventually in } i \text{ for each fixed } w, \) (18.7)

where

\(\displaystyle \Theta_i = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( - \frac{100}{10^4} (\log \hat{\beta}_{j_i})^{48} \right)\)

is the positive local bound allowance at the frame level \(j_i\). The bound (18.7) follows from Proposition 18.27, using the actual localizer, measured root estimates and constructed Crown of Subsection 18.24. The finite-volume three-term pairing estimate there uses the same paid labels as the mission, before the fixed-level ultrafilter passage. In particular no new label is chosen for each spectator test.

The detector properties can now be verified for these same reference fields and selected charts.

  1. Separation at the axial configuration. The reference identification (18.161) preserves the real structure and signed motions, so it identifies \(\mathcal{V}\) with the reference vector table \(\mathcal{V}_5\) of Subsection 16.1. Take the two grade-four fields represented by the quadratic curvature probes \((01, 01)\) and \((12, 12)\) there, and call them \(w_1, w_2\). The definitions of the functionals give \(\lambda_{w_1} = \phi_1\) and \(\lambda_{w_2} = \phi_2\): in both cases the output-index-\(0\) entry is inserted at \(e_0\) and the quadratic probe at the origin. The isomorphism \(\Phi = (\phi_1, \phi_2)\) proved after (16.11) therefore implies that \(\lambda_{w_1} (y) = \lambda_{w_2} (y) = 0\) forces \(y = 0\). The covariance normalization is unchanged under the reference identification. Thus the full family \(\{\lambda_w : w \in E_4 \}\) separates \(\mathcal{V}\).

  2. Convergence of fixed tested pairings. Fix \(u \in E_5\), \(w \in E_4\), and \(\xi_i \to (e_0, 0)\). A small closed ball about \((e_0, 0)\) is a compact set of distinct-point configurations, and contains \(\xi_i\) eventually. Apply the order-zero case of (18.167) on this compact set to the two fixed labels. Its uniform error tends to zero. The reference Wick correlation is smooth there, hence its value at \(\xi_i\) tends to its value at \((e_0, 0)\). Their sum gives the required limit for the selected correlation at \(r_i \xi_i\). This uses exactly the completed maps \(\mathcal{U}_i^{(5)}, \mathcal{U}_i^{(4)}\) and radii of the chart family; coordinate ties at the limiting configuration cause no problem because its two points remain distinct.

The finite detector is deduced from them as follows. The maps \(y \mapsto \lambda_w (y)\), \(w \in E_4\), are real-linear functionals on the finite-dimensional space \(\mathcal{V}\) with zero common kernel by (1), so finitely many of them already have zero common kernel. Hence there are fixed reference fields \(w_1, \ldots, w_m \in E_4\) such that the limiting pairings define an injective real-linear map

\(\displaystyle \Lambda (y) = (\lambda_{w_r} (y))_{r = 1}^m : \mathcal{V} \longrightarrow \mathbb{C}^m .\)

By (2), applied with \(\xi_i = \zeta_i^{\text{pt}}\), \(u = y_0\) and \(w = w_r\), the scale-dependent real-linear maps \(\Lambda_i (y) = (\lambda_{i, w_r} (y))_{r = 1}^m\) converge to \(\Lambda\) pointwise on \(\mathcal{V}\), and therefore in operator norm, since \(\mathcal{V}\) is finite-dimensional. If \(\mathcal{V} \neq \{0\}\), injectivity gives a positive minimum \(2 c\) of \(\| \Lambda y\|\) on its unit sphere, and \(\| \Lambda_i - \Lambda \| \leqslant c\) for large \(i\). Thus some \(c > 0\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \| \Lambda_i y\| \geqslant c \|y\| \qquad \text{for all sufficiently large } i \text{ and every } y \in \mathcal{V}. \) (18.8)

For \(\mathcal{V}= \{0\}\) the desired conclusion is immediate. The separation and compact comparison just verified are what permit this finite-dimensional lower bound.

The scalar rate input is

\(\displaystyle z_i^p m_i^5 \Theta_i \longrightarrow 0 \qquad \text{for each fixed integer } p \geqslant 0. \) (18.9)

It follows by multiplying three rate tickets for sequences indexed by the frame level \(j\) and then substituting \(j = j_i \to \infty\). The first follows from the summit-clock ratio in (18.172): eventually \(\hat{\beta}_{\text{summit} (j)} \leqslant 2 \hat{\beta}_j\), and \(\log (2 \hat{\beta}_j) / (\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48} \to 0\). The proof of that clock ratio uses only the selected scale schedule, not the small-means estimate being proved here. The second ticket is Lemma 18.2, and the third is immediate from the definition of \(\Theta_i\). Here a nonnegative sequence \(f_j\) has a ticket of rate \(\rho\) if, for every \(\rho' > \rho\), \(f_j \leqslant \exp (\rho' (\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48})\) for all sufficiently large \(j\). The three tickets are: the scale \(\hat{\beta}\) at the summit index of \(j\), which for \(j = j_i\) is \(z_i\) itself, has a zero-rate ticket (it is eventually at most twice \(\hat{\beta}_j\)); the cap \(\text{zoomCap} (j)\) of Definition 18.1 has a zero-rate ticket, hence so does its fifth power (Lemma 18.2); and \(\exp (- 100 \delta (\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48})\), which is \(\Theta_i\) for \(j = j_i\), has rate \(- 100 \delta\), with the fixed constant \(\delta = 10^{- 4}\) of the definition of \(\Theta_i\). Ticket products add rates, so for fixed \(p\) the product has negative rate \(- 100 \delta < 0\) and tends to zero, because \(\hat{\beta}_j \to \infty\).

Lemma 18.4. (Weighted field decay from the priced mission) Assume that, for all sufficiently large \(i\), the scale relations \(r_i = a_i b_i\) and \(0 < b_i \leqslant m_i\), the medium representation (18.4), the response identity (18.5) and the scaling identity (18.6) for \(x = x_i (0)\) hold. Assume the estimate (18.7) for each of the finitely many detector fields \(w_1, \ldots, w_m\), the detector bound (18.8), and the rate (18.9). Then, for each fixed integer \(p \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle z_i^p s_i (v_i) \longrightarrow 0 \quad \text{in } \mathcal{V}.\)

Proof. Set \(Y_i = z_i^p s_i (v_i) \in \mathcal{V}\), and restrict to \(i\) so large that all the assumed identities and the finitely many estimates hold. By the medium representation, response normalization, and night scaling, together with the real linearity of \(s_i\), \(\mathcal{U}_i^{(5)}\) and \(\mathcal{V}_i\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{U}_i^{(5)} ((Y_i)_0) = z_i^p \zeta_i^{- 1} c_i P_5 x_i (0) = z_i^p (a_i b_i)^5 P_5 x_i (0) .\)

The Arch scale \(\zeta_i\) cancels; it is distinct from the polynomial weight \(z_i\). Linearity in the grade-five entry gives, for each detector field \(w_r\),

\(\displaystyle \lambda_{i, w_r} (Y_i) = z_i^p b_i^5 \mathfrak{p}_{i, w_r} (a_i^5 P_5 x_i (0), \mathcal{U}_i^{(4)} w_r) .\)

The local estimate and \(b_i \leqslant m_i\) imply

\(\displaystyle \| \Lambda_i Y_i \| \leqslant \sqrt{m} \hspace{0.17em} z_i^p m_i^5 \Theta_i \longrightarrow 0.\)

The detector lower bound gives \(\|Y_i \| \leqslant c^{- 1} \| \Lambda_i Y_i \| \to 0\). This proves the desired limit in the full finite-dimensional response space.\(\Box\)

The lemma is applied to the selected medium \(v_i\). The proof identifies the mechanism: the powered local bound controls finitely many separating coordinates, and the negative rate forces each coordinate to zero. The uniform local bound receipt, mission construction, and detector convergence still depend on the selected finite-volume funding and chart construction.

18.3The finite-volume Crown buffer

We record the buffer argument used to pass from a local Crown approximation to the selected two-point observable. It is useful to separate its three ingredients: a rooted product estimate for the good approximation, a finite-volume estimate for the exceptional set, and the exact product identification across the coarse-to-fine map.

Throughout, one selected family is fixed, and every scale, coupling, effective representation, tiling and extension map below is the one attached to that family's selected history; no second history is chosen. Fix a scale index \(i > 0\), a finite-volume index \(N\), and a period \(p\) with \(i < k = \text{summit} (i) \leqslant N\). Put \(k_- = k - 1\). Write

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} T & = \text{tiles} (i, k, N, p), & F_i (q) & = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{q}{10^4} \hspace{0.17em} G_i \right),\\ G_i & = \text{logScale} (\hat{\beta}_i), & \Pi_k & = \text{prism} (\hat{\beta}_k),\\ \beta & = \beta_{N, p}, & \beta_k & = \beta_{N, p} (k), \qquad A_j = C.S.a (j) . \end{array}\)

Here \(T\) is the tiling of the volume-\(N\) lattice by the cells of scale \(i\). For a tiling \(T\) with cell set \(I\), its rooted functional is \(\textsf{R}_T^{\beta} (h) = |\mathbb{E}_{\beta} [\prod_{q \in I} T. \text{copy} (q, h)] |^{1 / |I|}\); the root is normalized by the number \(|I|\) of cells.

Three conditions are used repeatedly. A complex function \(h\) of the lattice configuration is interior to a set \(K\) of sites if it is gauge invariant, measurable and bounded, and depends only on the restriction of the configuration to \(K\). The window of radius \(l\) is the image of the centred box of radius \(l\) in the scale-\(k\) lattice. The scale \(i\) has room for the radius \(l\) at scale \(k\) if

\(\displaystyle 2 (A_k l + \text{tail} (k)) < A_i, \qquad \text{tail} (k) = 30 A_k (J_k + 5),\)

with strict inequality, where \(J_k\) is the stage index of the clock at scale \(k\). If a scale-\(k\) field is interior to the window of radius \(l\) and the scale \(i\) has room for \(l\), then its extension \(\mathcal{B}_{N, k}\) to volume \(N\), defined below, is interior to the base chamber of \(T\). Finally, the seam condition for the Wilson measure at coupling \(\beta\) is the conjunction of reflection positivity across every lattice cut, the tiled-product inequality

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E}_{\beta} \left[ \prod_{q \in I} T. \text{copy} (q, h_q) \right] \right| \leqslant \prod_{q \in I} \textsf{R}_T^{\beta} (h_q)\)

for every tiling \(T\) and all fields \(h_q\) interior to its base chamber, and subadditivity of \(\textsf{R}_T^{\beta}\) on such fields. For \(\beta \geqslant 0\), reflection invariance of the Wilson law and Proposition 3.3 give positivity at each translated seam. Proposition 4.2 then gives the tiled-product inequality with exactly these strict chambers and oriented copies. Only that inequality is used below; subadditivity is not an additional premise of the buffer argument. Write \(\mathcal{B}_{N, k}\) for the local extension from scale \(k\) to volume \(N\). For distinct coarse sites \(a, b\) at scale \(i\), let \(\alpha_{i, k} (a)\) and \(\alpha_{i, k} (b)\) denote their scale-\(k\) anchors, and let \(\alpha_{i, N} (a), \alpha_{i, N} (b)\) denote their volume-\(N\) anchors. For scale-\(k\) fields \(X, Y\), define the complex pairing

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \mathcal{Q} (X, Y) & =\mathbb{E}_{\beta} \hspace{-0.17em} [((\mathcal{B}_{N, k} X) \circ \text{translate}_{\alpha_{i, N} (a)}) ((\mathcal{B}_{N, k} Y) \circ \text{translate}_{\alpha_{i, N} (b)})],\\ X_a & = X \circ \text{pull}_{\alpha_{i, k} (a)}, \qquad Y_b = Y \circ \text{pull}_{\alpha_{i, k} (b)} . \end{array}\)

The exact product identity used below is \(\mathcal{Q} (X, Y) =\mathbb{E}_{\beta} [\mathcal{B}_{N, k} (X_a Y_b)]\) when \(a \ne b\), both fields are interior to a common window of some radius \(u\), and the scale \(i\) has room for \(u\). The scale receipts give, eventually in \(i\),

\(\displaystyle e^{- 2 \Pi_k} \leqslant F_i (- 1, 200, 000), \qquad 3 \leqslant F_i (1) . \) (18.10)

For the first inequality, use \(i \leqslant k\), monotonicity of the gate, and the selected estimates

\(\displaystyle 100, 000 \hspace{0.17em} G_k \hspace{0.17em} \text{Lyra} (k) \leqslant \Pi_k / 32, \qquad G_i \leqslant G_k .\)

Since \(\text{Lyra} (k) \geqslant 1\) eventually, this gives \(120 G_i \leqslant 2 \Pi_k\), which is the exponent inequality in (18.10). The second inequality follows from \(G_i \to \infty\).

Suppose \(f\) is the field to be tested and a Crown witness provides a field \(w\), a support \(P\), and a window radius \(l\) such that both fields are interior to that window, the scale has room for \(l\), and

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} |P| & \leqslant F_i (1000), & \sup_U |f (U) - w (U) | & \leqslant F_i (500, 000), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.11)}\\ |f (U) - w (U) | & \leqslant F_i (- 500, 000) & & \text{if $U$ is healthy for $P$ at threshold } \vartheta_k / 16, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.12)}\\ \textsf{R}_T^{\beta} (\mathcal{B}_{N, k} w) & \leqslant F_i (- 1, 200, 000) . & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.13)} \end{array}\)

Let \(g\) be the second field in the desired probe, with \(\sup_U |g (U) | \leqslant F_i (150, 000)\). Let \(l_4\) be the fixed grade-four test radius (the grade-four stencil radius plus four); assume that \(g\) is interior to the window of radius \(l_4\) and that the scale \(i\) has room for \(l_4\) at scale \(k\). Both \(l\) and \(l_4\) then satisfy the room condition, so \(f\), \(w\), \(f - w\) and \(g\) are all interior to the window of radius \(\max \{l, l_4 \}\), for which the scale has room; this is the common window used in the product identity, and it makes the extensions of \(w\) and \(g\) interior to the base chamber of \(T\). Let \(a \ne b\) be distinct coarse sites. Define the pulled-back factors

\(\displaystyle f_a (U) = f (\text{pull}_{\alpha_{i, k} (a)} \hspace{0.17em} U), \quad w_a (U) = w (\text{pull}_{\alpha_{i, k} (a)} \hspace{0.17em} U), \quad g_b (U) = g (\text{pull}_{\alpha_{i, k} (b)} \hspace{0.17em} U), \quad D_a = f_a - w_a .\)

Here \(\vartheta_k\) is the clock threshold at scale \(k\); the pull maps use the scale-\(k\) anchors defined above.

We make explicit the finite-volume input used for the correction. Let \(\rho_k\) be the nonnegative density of the effective scale-\(k\) representation of the volume-\(N\) Wilson measure, and put \(Z = \int \rho_k = \text{Partition} (N, p)\). We use the normalized transfer identity

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\beta_{N, p}} [\mathcal{B}_{N, k} h] = Z^{- 1} \int \rho_k (U) h (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k (U) . \) (18.14)

Let \(I_x (U)\) indicate that the local flag at site \(x\) exceeds threshold \(b\). The one-site estimate is an incoming local-action receipt:

\(\displaystyle \int \rho_k (U) I_x (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k (U) \leqslant Z \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \left( \frac{A_{k_-}}{A_k} \right)^4 \hspace{-0.17em} \text{vacuumBound} (k) - \beta_k \frac{\text{clip}_k}{5} b^2 \right) . \) (18.15)

It applies when \(2 b \leqslant \vartheta_k\) and the scale has room for the radius-eight indicator. Given this receipt, obtain the corresponding estimate for a support \(P'\) by splitting configurations into those healthy for all sites of \(P'\) and the complement. On the healthy set, use the bound \(U_0\) for the field. On the complement, there is at least one \(x \in P'\) with \(I_x = 1\), so the pointwise absolute integrand is at most \(V_0 \rho_k \sum_{x \in P'} I_x\). Integration, the triangle inequality, and (18.15) give

\(\displaystyle |\mathbb{E}_{\beta_{N, p}} [\mathcal{B}_{N, k} h] | \leqslant U_0 + V_0 |P' | \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \left( \frac{A_{k_-}}{A_k} \right)^4 \hspace{-0.17em} \text{vacuumBound} (k) - \beta_k \frac{\text{clip}_k}{5} b^2 \right) . \) (18.16)

The exponent in (18.16) is eventually at most \(- 2 \Pi_k\) by the scale estimate

\(\displaystyle \left( \frac{A_{k_-}}{A_k} \right)^4 \hspace{-0.17em} \text{vacuumBound} (k) - \beta_k \frac{\text{clip}_k}{5} b^2 \leqslant - 2 \Pi_k, \) (18.17)

for \(b = \vartheta_k / 16\). This is the exact threshold used in the witness bound. Here is the scalar calculation behind that estimate. Put \(x = \hat{\beta}_k\), \(y = \text{Lyra} (k)\), \(q = \Pi_k\), \(C = \text{clip}_k\), and \(m = \text{monitor} (k)\). These satisfy \(x, y, C > 0\) and \(q \geqslant 0\). The selected scale receipts give

\(\displaystyle \left( \frac{A_{k_-}}{A_k} \right)^4 \text{vacuumBound} (k) \leqslant y^6, \quad 10 \cdot 16^2 y (y^6 + 2 q) \leqslant m^2, \quad yC \geqslant 1, \quad x \vartheta_k^2 = m^2, \quad \beta_k \geqslant x / 2.\)

Since \(b = \vartheta_k / 16\), the second and fourth inequalities imply \(y^6 + 2 q \leqslant m^2 / (2560 y)\). The third gives \(1 / y \leqslant C\), so

\(\displaystyle y^6 + 2 q \leqslant \frac{Cm^2}{2560} = \frac{x}{2} \frac{C}{5} \left( \frac{\vartheta_k}{16} \right)^2 \leqslant \beta_k \frac{C}{5} b^2 .\)

Subtracting from the first bound yields the exponent in (18.17). Thus the exceptional-set estimate is obtained from the one-site flag bound and the displayed scale receipts, not from locality by itself.

Lemma 18.5. (Good Crown term) Assume the Crown witness (18.13), the stated bound, interiority and room hypotheses for \(g\), the seam condition for the Wilson measure at coupling \(\beta\), and \(a \ne b\). Then

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{Q}(w, g) | \leqslant F_i (- 1, 050, 000) .\)

Proof. The extensions \(\mathcal{B}_{N, k} w\) and \(\mathcal{B}_{N, k} g\) are interior to the base chamber of \(T\), by the window and room hypotheses. The translated form of the tiled-product inequality, for two fields placed at the two distinct cells \(a \ne b\), gives

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{Q}(w, g) | \leqslant \textsf{R}_T^{\beta} (\mathcal{B}_{N, k} w) \hspace{0.17em} \textsf{R}_T^{\beta} (\mathcal{B}_{N, k} g) .\)

Here is the reduction to Proposition 4.2. In each occupied cell, undo the coordinate reflections prescribed by the parity of that cell, and conjugate the home field if that parity is odd. The resulting home field is still bounded, measurable, gauge invariant and chamber-interior. Copying it to the occupied cell cancels those reflections and conjugations, leaving the desired translated factor. In the product over all cells, changing a home field by one of these reflections permutes the cell indices; an odd parity can additionally conjugate the full product. Thus its absolute expectation, and hence its rooted functional, is unchanged. Apply the planted inequality to these two home fields in the distinct cells \(a, b\) (equivalently put the unit field in every other cell). This proves the displayed estimate. The Crown bound controls the first factor by \(F_i (- 1, 200, 000)\). For the second, the rooted sup estimate says that a field bounded pointwise by \(R \geqslant 0\) has root at most \(R\); hence it is at most \(F_i (150, 000)\). Multiplying and using \(F_i (s) F_i (t) = F_i (s + t)\) gives the claim.\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.6. (Exceptional-set buffer) Assume the Crown witness (18.13), the stated bound, interiority and room hypotheses for \(g\), \(a \ne b\), the transfer identity (18.14), and, for the given \(i\), the scale inequalities (18.10) and (18.17) together with the one-site estimate (18.15) at \(b = \vartheta_k / 16\). Then the correction term satisfies

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{Q}(f - w, g) | \leqslant F_i (- 350, 000) + F_i (- 549, 000) .\)

Proof. By the exact product identity, applied to \(f - w\) and \(g\) in the common window of radius \(\max \{l, l_4 \}\), the correction is the finite-volume Wilson expectation of the extension of the local field \(D_a g_b\), which is measurable and bounded. This field has envelope obtained by multiplying the two local envelopes. Its global bound is \(F_i (500, 000) F_i (150, 000) = F_i (650, 000)\); on the healthy set for the translated support, the bound improves to \(F_i (- 500, 000) F_i (150, 000) = F_i (- 350, 000)\). Translation preserves the number of support sites, so the support cardinality is at most \(F_i (1000)\). Apply (18.16) with \(U_0 = F_i (- 350, 000)\), \(V_0 = F_i (650, 000)\), \(P'\) the translate of \(P\), and \(b = \vartheta_k / 16\). By (18.17), this bounds \(|\mathbb{E}_{\beta} [\mathcal{B}_{N, k} (D_a g_b)] |\) by

\(\displaystyle F_i (- 350, 000) + F_i (650, 000) F_i (1000) e^{- 2 \Pi_k} .\)

Apply (18.10) to the last factor and combine the exponents: \(650, 000 + 1000 - 1, 200, 000 = - 549, 000\). Thus

\(\displaystyle F_i (- 350, 000) + F_i (- 549, 000) . \) (18.18)

\(\Box\)

Proposition 18.7. (Crown buffer for the two-point probe) There is \(i_0\) such that the following holds for every \(i \geqslant i_0\), every volume index \(N \geqslant k = \text{summit} (i)\) and every period \(p\). Let \(f\) be a scale-\(k\) field with a Crown witness (18.13), let \(g\) be a scale-\(k\) field with \(\sup_U |g (U) | \leqslant F_i (150, 000)\) which is interior to the window of radius \(l_4\), and assume that the scale \(i\) has room for \(l_4\) at scale \(k\). Assume the seam condition, the transfer identity (18.14) and the one-site estimate (18.15) for the selected history. Then, for all distinct coarse sites \(a \ne b\) at scale \(i\),

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{Q}(f, g) | \leqslant F_i (- 30, 000) .\)

The threshold \(i_0\) depends only on the selected scale receipts (18.10) and (18.17); it does not depend on \(N\), \(p\), the fields, the witness, or the sites.

Proof. Choose \(i_0\) so that (18.10) holds for \(i \geqslant i_0\) and (18.17) holds at \(k = \text{summit} (i)\) for \(i \geqslant i_0\); this is possible because \(\text{summit} (i) > i \to \infty\). Set \(D_a = f_a - w_a\). The extension \(\mathcal{B}_{N, k}\) is linear and the expectation is linear, and all fields involved are measurable and bounded, so

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Q} (f, g) =\mathcal{Q} (w, g) +\mathcal{Q} (f - w, g) .\)

The first term is bounded by Lemma 18.5 and the second by (18.18). Each exponent is at most \(- 31, 000\), so the sum is at most \(3 F_i (- 31, 000)\). Since eventually \(3 \leqslant F_i (1)\), this is at most

\(\displaystyle F_i (- 31, 000) F_i (1) = F_i (- 30, 999) \leqslant F_i (- 30, 000) .\)

\(\Box\)

The proof uses the stated Crown witness and the finite-volume buffer estimate. For the grade-five field used in the probe assembly, the witness is constructed in the actual Crown assembly of Subsection 18.24. Its paired-lens estimate and healthy-region identity give, after the same dilation and signed average, precisely the three bounds in (18.13). The proposition is stated for any field with such a witness; it does not assert a witness for an arbitrary field.

18.4The finite bound recurrence

We give the scale induction that turns the paid direction and the local one-step response estimate into the endpoint inequality used in Subsection 18.11. The one-step response estimate is derived below from the finite-volume local insertion, local uniqueness, and block-size bounds. The remaining scale inputs are stated as hypotheses of Proposition 18.9.

Fix a reference degree \(d\), a selected scale history \(H\), and integers \(m \leqslant k\). Write

\(\displaystyle \Gamma_j = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{S_k - S_j}{8} \right), \qquad \kappa_j = \sqrt{\text{monitor} (j)}, \qquad I_j = \frac{1}{\Gamma_j \kappa_j} \quad (m \leqslant j \leqslant k) .\)

Here \(S_j\) is the scale coordinate of the history. For \(m \leqslant j < k\), let \(L_j\) be the block side length at step \(j\), let

\(\displaystyle P_j = C (J (j + 1)), \qquad b_j = \frac{1}{10 K (J (j + 1))}, \qquad \rho_j = \text{rate} (L_j), \qquad s_j = \frac{b_j}{\kappa_{j + 1}},\)

where \(J (j)\) is the stage at rung \(j\), \(C\) is the selected coefficient-cost profile, \(K\) is the fixed positive worth profile, and \(\rho_j > 0\) is the block remainder-cost factor. For each rung \(j\), write \(c_j \geqslant 1\) for the selected column allowance of the history at that rung. It is a real parameter of the selected stage data, not the cardinality of a coefficient index set; \(c_k\) is its value at the terminal rung, matching the notation of Subsection 18.11. Put \(p_j = L_j^{1 / 8}\), and define

\(\displaystyle H_j = \Gamma_j^{- 1} + \frac{P_j L_j^d + \rho_j}{\Gamma_{j + 1}}, \qquad \varepsilon_j = \frac{I_{j + 1}}{10 P_j H_j} . \) (18.19)

All quantities in the denominators are positive.

The scale and budget choices give, at every step,

\(\displaystyle \kappa_{j + 1} \leqslant 2 \kappa_j, \qquad 1 \leqslant P_j, \qquad 4 P_j \leqslant p_j, \qquad 2 P_j b_j \leqslant \frac{1}{3}, \qquad \Gamma_j = \Gamma_{j + 1} p_j . \) (18.20)

Consequently

\(\displaystyle P_j I_j \leqslant \frac{1}{2} I_{j + 1} . \) (18.21)

Indeed, the ratio of the left side to \(I_{j + 1}\) is \(P_j \kappa_{j + 1} / (p_j \kappa_j)\), which is at most \(2 P_j / p_j \leqslant 1 / 2\).

For a coefficient \(x\) at rung \(j\), write \(x^{\circ}\) for its projection to nonconstant coordinates, and let \(\mathcal{L}_j (x)\) be the coefficient produced by the local block insertion. The diagonal weight map \(\mathcal{V}_{L_j}\) multiplies a grade-\(r\) coordinate by \(L_j^{d - r}\). Thus \(\|\mathcal{V}_{L_j} (x^{\circ})\| \leqslant L_j^d \|x^{\circ} \|\). The one-step local response estimate, for a paid comparison with adjacent coefficients \(x_j, x_{j + 1}\) and nonnegative remainder-cost bounds \(r_j, r_{j + 1}\), is

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} \|x_j -\mathcal{L}_j (x_{j + 1})\| & \leqslant s_j (\|x_{j + 1}^{\circ} \|+ r_{j + 1})\\ & \quad + \varepsilon_j (r_j + P_j \|\mathcal{V}_{L_j} (x_{j + 1}^{\circ})\|+ \rho_j r_{j + 1}) . \end{aligned} \ \) (18.22)

Here \(\|x\|\) is the coefficient \(\ell^1\)-norm, the sum of the absolute values of all coordinates including the unit coordinate, and \(x^{\circ}\) deletes exactly the unit coordinate. In the selected construction, under its standing scale hypotheses (in particular the stage at rung \(j\) is at least \(d + 3\)), the estimate holds for all sufficiently large period indices \(n\), with a threshold depending on \(j\) and on the constant \(\varepsilon_j\), and then uniformly in the volume index \(N > j\) and in the pair of adjacent fields, provided both fields belong to the effective representations of the same selected history at the rungs \(j\) and \(j + 1\) for these \(N\) and \(n\), have the required order-zero format and remainder bounds at the normalizations \(\sqrt{\text{forcedAllowance}_j}\) and \(\sqrt{\text{forcedAllowance}_{j + 1}}\) with costs \(r_j, r_{j + 1}\), satisfy the exact density transfer (the rung-\(j\) density is \(L_j^d\) times the pushed rung-\((j + 1)\) density), and have marked sites related by the scale map. The separate local insertion estimate is also

\(\displaystyle \|x_{j + 1} \| \leqslant P_j \|\mathcal{L}_j (x_{j + 1})\| . \) (18.23)

We explain the finite-volume comparison producing the second line of (18.22). Let \(\mathcal{Q}_d\) be the finite family of coefficient-recovery probes, with probe bounds \(\pi (\tau)\) for \(\tau \in \mathcal{Q}_d\). Put

\(\displaystyle M_d = 1 + \sum_{q = 0}^d 2^q (q + 1)^q .\)

Here is the derivative comparison paid by this constant. Let \(h\) be the difference of two \(C^d\) functions near the identity, and write \(\tilde{h}\) for \(h\) in the exponential link chart at the identity. For a fixed direction \(v\), the curve with links \(\exp (tv_e)\) is the flow of the corresponding left-invariant direction. Thus the repeated left-direction derivative of order \(q\) is \(D^q \tilde{h} (0) [v, \ldots, v]\). This assertion uses a single repeated direction; mixed left-invariant derivatives need not commute. Suppose its absolute value is at most \(c\) for every unit direction, for each \(q \leqslant d\). The symmetric \(q\)-linear derivative \(A_q = D^q \tilde{h} (0)\) satisfies, for \(q \geqslant 1\), the real polarization identity

\(\displaystyle A_q (v_1, \ldots, v_q) = \frac{1}{2^q q!} \sum_{\epsilon \in \{- 1, 1\}^q} \left( \prod_{j = 1}^q \epsilon_j \right) A_q \left( \sum_j \epsilon_j v_j, \ldots, \sum_j \epsilon_j v_j \right) .\)

For \(\|v_j \| \leqslant 1\), homogeneity and the triangle inequality bound the right side by \(cq^q / q! \leqslant c \hspace{0.17em} 2^q (q + 1)^q\). The order-zero bound is \(|h (1) | \leqslant c\). Each of these constants is at most \(M_d\). The probe bound \(\pi (\tau)\) includes the absolute chain-rule coefficients and the products of the derivatives of its logarithmic-link test curve. Applying the chain rule to \(\tilde{h}\) therefore bounds the difference of the two probe values by \(\pi (\tau) M_d c\). This proves the asserted comparison without identifying mixed chart derivatives with mixed left-invariant derivatives. Let \(T_d\) be the finite coefficient reconstruction constant for this probe family. Thus for any coefficient difference \(z\),

\(\displaystyle \|z\| \leqslant T_d \sum_{\tau \in \mathcal{Q}_d} |R_{\tau} (z) | .\)

A probe of order at most \(d\) annihilates a regular source term whose support is nonwrapping and whose format has the required vanishing of its first \(d\) local variations. Let \(B_0\) be the support radius and \(\sigma > 0\) the support exponent of the local representation, and define

\(\displaystyle M_{B_0} (X, W) = e^{\sigma \text{cover}_{B_0} (X)} W.\)

For each of the two coefficients, the regular part has a countable presentation by regular terms with origin in their supports and with total \(M_{B_0}\)-mass bounded by its remainder budget. Each term has a common healthy chart. If its purchased sup-bound is \(u_a\), then on this chart every ordered directional derivative of orders \(q = 0, \ldots, d\) has norm at most \(u_a\), after the local variation bounds have been factored out. The chart is large enough for each recovery probe and its local stencil to fit, and the admissible local presentation has the required order-\(d\) vanishing on each nonwrapping term. These are the finite recovery and regularity hypotheses used below; a value bound alone would not imply the probe estimate. Their signed union is still absolutely summable, even when some supports overlap.

Let \(\mathcal{I}_0\) be the indices of nonwrapping terms, and take a finite subset \(D \subset \mathcal{I}_0\). The probe applied to the presented coefficient equals the corresponding derivative of its local presentation; this is the coefficient-readout identity. On the common quiet chart, the forced contributions vanish, and the prescribed masks replace the local masks. Thus each density is the same nonzero exponential action factor times its presented coefficient plus its regular series. Equality of the two densities lets us cancel this common factor and gives the signed difference of the presented coefficients as the sum of all signed regular terms: writing \(g\) for the local presentation of the coefficient difference and \(f_a\) for the signed regular terms,

\(\displaystyle g (U) = \sum_a f_a (U) \) (18.24)

for every configuration \(U\) in the quiet set \(\mathcal{D}\), the set of configurations all of whose plaquette variables are strictly below the threshold. The set \(\mathcal{D}\) is open and contains the identity configuration, every support is healthy on it, and the series converges absolutely there because \(|f_a | \leqslant u_a\) and \(\sum_a u_a < \infty\).

The probe estimate needs the ordered derivatives of \(g\), so the identity (18.24) must be differentiated. We claim that for every \(U \in \mathcal{D}\) and every ordered list \((A_1, \ldots, A_q)\) of normalized directions with \(q\) at most the order of the budget,

\(\displaystyle \partial_{A_1} \cdots \partial_{A_q} g (U) = \sum_a \partial_{A_1} \cdots \partial_{A_q} f_a (U), \) (18.25)

where the series converges absolutely. The proof is by induction on \(q\); the case \(q = 0\) is (18.24). For the induction step, let \(W (t)\) be the increment curve through \(U\) in the direction \(A_1\). Since \(\mathcal{D}\) is open, \(W (t) \in \mathcal{D}\) for \(|t| < r\) with some \(r > 0\). On this interval the functions \(v_a (t) = \partial_{A_2} \cdots \partial_{A_q} f_a (W (t))\) are differentiable by regularity of the terms on healthy configurations, their derivatives are \(\partial_{A_1} \cdots \partial_{A_q} f_a (W (t))\), and these are bounded by \(u_a\) uniformly in \(t\), with \(\sum_a u_a < \infty\). The series \(\sum_a v_a (0)\) converges. The theorem on termwise differentiation of a series whose derivatives are dominated by a summable sequence on an interval therefore shows that \(\sum_a v_a\) is differentiable at \(0\) with derivative \(\sum_a v_a' (0)\). By the induction hypothesis, applied at every point \(W (t)\), \(|t| < r\), the sum \(\sum_a v_a (t)\) equals \(\partial_{A_2} \cdots \partial_{A_q} g (W (t))\); differentiating at \(t = 0\) gives (18.25).

Now fix the finite set \(D\) and let \(G_D = \sum_{a \in D} f_a\). The finite sum \(G_D\) has zero probe: every term has the vanishing property just stated, and a finite sum may be differentiated termwise. By (18.25) at the identity configuration, every ordered derivative of \(g - G_D\) of order at most \(d\) along normalized directions is the sum of the corresponding derivatives of the omitted nonwrapping terms and of the wrapping terms, so it is bounded by \(\sum_{a \in \mathcal{I}_0 \setminus D} u_a + \sum_{a \not\in \mathcal{I}_0} u_a\). The polarization estimate stated with the definition of \(M_d\) then bounds the difference between the probe of the full presented field and the zero probe of the truncation \(G_D\). This estimate is uniform in finite \(D\), because the local section has order-\(d\) regularity and the source terms have summable sup-bound mass. If the probe has order \(r\), the chain rule expands its local link variation into ordered partitions of \(\{1, \ldots, r\}\). Let \(\lambda_{\tau, q}\) be the bound of a local logarithmic variation of order \(q\); the finite sum of the products \(\prod_{A \in \mathcal{P}} \lambda_{\tau, |A|}\) over these partitions, with the zeroth-order term included, is \(\pi (\tau)\). Supremum bounds on the omitted terms and the triangle inequality therefore give

\(\displaystyle |R_{\tau} (P.x - Q.x) | \leqslant \pi (\tau) M_d \left( \sum_{a \in \mathcal{I}_0 \setminus D} u_a + \sum_{a \not\in \mathcal{I}_0} u_a \right),\)

where \(u_a\) is the sup-bound of the corresponding signed regular term. The remainder certificates give \(\sum_a M_{B_0} (X_a, u_a) \leqslant a + b\), so \(\sum_a u_a\) is finite as well. For the wrapping terms, the elementary support inequality

\(\displaystyle 10 (h (4 B_0 + 2) + 2 B_0) < n, \quad 0 \in X, \quad X \text{ wrapping}, \quad W \geqslant 0 \quad \Longrightarrow \quad W \leqslant e^{- \sigma h} M_{B_0} (X, W)\)

follows from the lower bound on the cover radius of a wrapping support. Absolute summability of \(\sum_a u_a\) justifies the tail limit and permits the finite sets \(D\) to increase through all of \(\mathcal{I}_0\). The nonwrapping sup-bound tail tends to zero, and the wrapping sup-bound sum is at most \(e^{- \sigma h} (a + b)\); taking this limit gives the probe estimate

\(\displaystyle |R_{\tau} (P.x - Q.x) | \leqslant \pi (\tau) M_d e^{- \sigma h} (a + b) .\)

This is the truncation-and-tail argument. The probe is applied termwise only to the finite truncations, where the vanishing property is available; the infinite collection of omitted and wrapping terms enters only through the derivative identity (18.25) and the resulting sup-bound.

The coefficient recovery inequality is

\(\displaystyle \|z\| \leqslant T_d \sum_{\tau \in \mathcal{Q}_d} |R_{\tau} (z) | .\)

It follows from finite-dimensionality: the probes jointly separate coefficients, so their maximum is a norm on the coefficient space; compactness of its unit sphere gives a finite comparison constant, and a maximum is bounded by the sum. Summing the probe estimate over the finite family gives

\(\displaystyle \|P.x - Q.x\| \leqslant \mathfrak{C}_d e^{- \sigma h} (a + b), \qquad \mathfrak{C}_d = T_d M_d \sum_{\tau \in \mathcal{Q}_d} \pi (\tau) . \) (18.26)

Take the torus side length \(n\) so that \(10 (h (4 B_0 + 2) + 2 B_0) < n\). Then every probe fits in the region of nonwrapping supports. This proves the required local uniqueness estimate from coefficient recovery, local vanishing, the exponential support tail, and absolute summation.

For the insertion itself, first decompose the next-level density into its primary term, its countable regular terms, and its countable forced terms. The primary term has only finitely many coefficient coordinates. The regular series is absolutely summable by its sup-bound mass budget. The forced series is absolutely summable after division by the positive factor \(y = \sqrt{\text{forcedAllowance}_{j + 1}}\), using the forced part of the same remainder certificate. Write the regular mass sum as \(a\) and the forced mass sum as \(yb\), where \(a, b \geqslant 0\) and \(a + b \leqslant r_{j + 1}\) by the remainder certificate. For a regular term of volume mass \(m_a\), the paid atom costs are \(s_j m_a\) and \(\rho_j m_a\); for a forced term of mass \(m_a\), they are \(s_j m_a / y\) and \(\rho_j m_a / y\). Thus the combined regular and forced costs are bounded by \(s_j r_{j + 1}\) and \(\rho_j r_{j + 1}\), respectively. The primary coefficient atom contributes the finite coordinate costs \(s_j \|x_{j + 1}^{\circ} \|\) and \(P_j \|\mathcal{V}_{L_j} (x_{j + 1}^{\circ})\|\). Summing first over the finite coefficient basis, then over the countable regular and forced lists, is legitimate because their total costs and coefficient norms are summable. The interchange of the countable sums with the integral needs a bound for the factors multiplying the atoms in the density, and this is not a consequence of compactness of the link variables alone. Two properties of the fine effective representation are used; both are part of its representation certificate and are inputs here. First, its effective density is measurable and bounded. Second, its vacuum activities \(K_j\), \(j \in \mathbb{N}\), have a summable budget: there are \(a_j \geqslant 0\) with \(\sum_j a_j < \infty\) and \(|K_j (U) | \leqslant a_j\) for all \(U\). The multiplier that dresses the \(i\)-th forced insertion is the sum, over the finite admissible index sets \(b \ni i\), of \(\prod_{j \in b \setminus \{i\}} K_j (U)\). Erasing the index \(i\) is injective on such sets, so every dressed multiplier is bounded, uniformly in \(i\) and \(U\), by

\(\displaystyle C_{\text{gas}} = \sum_{b \subset \mathbb{N} \text{finite}} \prod_{j \in b} a_j \leqslant \exp \left( \sum_j a_j \right) < \infty .\)

Hence a summable majorant \(\beta_i\) for the forced insertions gives the summable majorant \(C_{\text{gas}} \beta_i\) for their dressed versions, and the same absolute summability justifies rearranging the sum over marked index sets into the sum of dressed insertions. The absolute integrand of the countable atomic sum is therefore bounded by the bound of the effective density times a summable majorant. This is an integrable bound on the normalized finite-volume Haar space, so Fubini preserves the density identity. Thus the countable atomic sum has the pushed density. Denote its actual coefficient by \(\tilde{x}_j\). The coordinate leads sum to \(\mathcal{L}_j (x_{j + 1})\), and the paid insertion estimate gives \(\| \tilde{x}_j -\mathcal{L}_j (x_{j + 1})\| \leqslant s_j (\|x_{j + 1}^{\circ} \|+ r_{j + 1})\). Its coarse remainder budget is at most \(P_j \|\mathcal{V}_{L_j} (x_{j + 1}^{\circ}) \|+ \rho_j r_{j + 1}\). This is the content of the countable summation argument: dominatedness and absolute summability justify both the density identity and the interchange of summation with integration.

Compare this intermediate coefficient with the actual coefficient at rung \(j\) using the local uniqueness estimate (18.26). Their local densities agree by the paid comparison's density identity and the insertion's pushed-density identity. Choose \(h\) so that \(\mathfrak{C}_d e^{- \sigma h} \leqslant \varepsilon_j\), then choose the torus side long enough for the displayed support inequality. The triangle inequality gives exactly (18.22), uniformly over the allowed fields.

For (18.23), coefficient coordinates have grades \(r = 0, 1, \ldots, d\), so each weight \(L_j^{d - r} \geqslant 1\) when \(L_j \geqslant 1\). Consequently the weighted coordinate norm dominates the original norm. The legal block cap then controls that weighted norm by \(P_j\) times the norm after insertion. This is the size estimate for the admissible leading map; it is not merely the inequality \(L_j \geqslant 1\).

Lemma 18.8. (One-step propagation) Suppose the adjacent data satisfy the local hypotheses above and, for some \(t \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle r_j + \|x_j \| \leqslant \frac{t}{\Gamma_j}, \qquad r_{j + 1} + \|x_{j + 1} \| \leqslant \frac{t}{\Gamma_{j + 1}}, \qquad \|x_j \| \leqslant tI_j .\)

Then

\(\displaystyle \|x_{j + 1} \| \leqslant tI_{j + 1} .\)

Proof. The projection to nonconstant coordinates is contractive, so the paid-cone bound gives \(\|x_{j + 1}^{\circ} \| + r_{j + 1} \leqslant \|x_{j + 1} \| + r_{j + 1} \leqslant t / \Gamma_{j + 1}\). It also gives

\(\displaystyle r_j + P_j \|\mathcal{V}_{L_j} (x_{j + 1}^{\circ}) \|+ \rho_j r_{j + 1} \leqslant t \left( \Gamma_j^{- 1} + \frac{P_j L_j^d + \rho_j}{\Gamma_{j + 1}} \right) = tH_j .\)

Apply (18.22) and then (18.23). The triangle inequality yields

\(\displaystyle \|x_{j + 1} \| \leqslant P_j \left( \|x_j \|+ s_j \frac{t}{\Gamma_{j + 1}} + \varepsilon_j tH_j \right) .\)

Using \(\|x_j \| \leqslant tI_j\), \(s_j = b_j / \kappa_{j + 1}\), and the definition of \(I_{j + 1}\), the right side is

\(\displaystyle tI_{j + 1} \left( \frac{P_j I_j}{I_{j + 1}} + P_j b_j + \frac{1}{10} \right) \leqslant tI_{j + 1} \left( \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{6} + \frac{1}{10} \right) = \frac{23}{30} tI_{j + 1} \leqslant tI_{j + 1} .\)

Here \(P_j b_j \leqslant 1 / 6\) follows from the fourth inequality in (18.20). This proves the claim, including the degenerate case \(t = 0\).\(\Box\)

Proposition 18.9. (Finite endpoint estimate) Assume the selected history satisfies the scale inequalities (18.20) for every \(m \leqslant j < k\). Let a paid comparison have coefficient vectors \(x_j\), remainder costs \(r_j\), and marked sites at the spatial origin for \(m \leqslant j \leqslant k\). Assume its left coefficient is zero, \(x_m = 0\), and that its adjacent pairs satisfy the local estimates (18.22) and (18.23). Assume also the paid direction conditions of Subsection 15.2, the room and bank inequalities (15.6), and the terminal spread bound (15.7) with endpoint budget \(w \geqslant 0\) and with the column allowances \(c_j\). Then, for all period indices and volume indices for which the local one-step estimates hold at every rung \(m \leqslant j < k\),

\(\displaystyle \kappa_k \|x_k \| \leqslant 4 (1 + c_k) w. \) (18.27)

If, as in the selected construction, the one-step estimates hold for all period indices \(n \geqslant n_j\), uniformly in the volume index \(N \geqslant k\) and in the comparison data, then the endpoint estimate holds for all \(n \geqslant n_0 = \max (\{0\} \cup \{n_j : m \leqslant j < k\})\) (the set of rungs is empty when \(m = k\), where the estimate is \(x_k = x_m = 0\)), uniformly in \(N \geqslant k\), in the paid comparison and in \(w \geqslant 0\).

Proof. We first verify that the comparison satisfies the discounted paid-cone bound used in the recurrence. In the canonical cone estimate, choose at rung \(j\) the worth coefficient \(K_j = K (J (j + 1))\), the column allowance equal to the selected column allowance \(c_j\) of the history, and the bank comparison coefficient \(M_j = K (J (j + 1) + 1)\). The scale-slow condition gives \(K_j \leqslant K (J (j) + 1)\), so the room inequality yields \(64 K_j^2 \leqslant c_j\). The bank condition is exactly \(100000 M_j^6 (1 + c_{j + 1})^2 \leqslant p_j\), and monotonicity gives \(M_j \geqslant K_j \geqslant 1\). The paid direction in that cone is the adjacent coefficient comparison with diagonal weights \(L_j^{d - r}\) and loss \(p_j^{- 3}\); this is the direction hypothesis assumed here. The zero left coefficient and terminal spread are precisely its boundary conditions. Therefore equation (15.8) gives

\(\displaystyle \Gamma_j (r_j +\|x_j \|) \leqslant 4 (1 + c_k) w \qquad (m \leqslant j \leqslant k) . \) (18.28)

Put \(t = 4 (1 + c_k) w\). Since \(x_m = 0\), we have \(\|x_m \| = 0 \leqslant tI_m\). Suppose inductively that \(\|x_j \| \leqslant tI_j\). The paid-cone bound gives \(r_j + \|x_j \| \leqslant t / \Gamma_j\) and \(r_{j + 1} + \|x_{j + 1} \| \leqslant t / \Gamma_{j + 1}\). In particular the local remainder allowances satisfy the two bounds in Lemma 18.8. That lemma gives \(\|x_{j + 1} \| \leqslant tI_{j + 1}\). Finite induction reaches \(j = k\), where \(\Gamma_k = 1\), so \(I_k = 1 / \kappa_k\). Multiplying by \(\kappa_k\) gives (18.27). The finite intersection of the period thresholds for \(j = m, \ldots, k - 1\) is still an eventual set. Each adjacent estimate is uniform over the volume index and the specified comparison data, so one threshold works for all of them.\(\Box\)

Thus the endpoint estimate combines the already proved paid-cone argument of Subsection 15.2 with the one-step quantitative bound recurrence proved here. The only new induction is the finite propagation from the zero left coefficient to the terminal rung.

18.5Choosing the local paid budgets

The one-step insertion uses two kinds of local input: a finite list of coefficient-recovery probes, and positive budgets for the basis, regular, and forced atoms. The first choice is elementary once the probe list is finite. The eventual threshold can then be made monotone in the scale and uniform over all admissible adjacent levels. This section proves those two selection steps and then matches their budgets to the actual basis, regular and forced insertions. Those estimates supply the one-step recurrence of Subsection 18.4.

Fix a finite set \(\mathcal{T}\) of probes and nonnegative constants \(O, H, W, G_d, a, c\), where \(O\) is the motion-average norm factor, \(H\) the local readout factor, \(W\) the test factor, \(G_d\) the polarization factor, \(a\) the block weight, and \(c\) the tail-decay factor. For each probe \(\tau\), let \(\pi_{\tau} \geqslant 0\) be its variation bound and define its local coefficient

\(\displaystyle q_{\tau} = 4 aG_d \pi_{\tau} + 2 a.\)

Given a target bound \(y > 0\), the required atom budget inequalities are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} q_{\tau} v & \leqslant u & & (\tau \in \mathcal{T}), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.29)} \\ O (Wu + 2 Hav) & \leqslant y, & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.30)} \\ 4 OHav & \leqslant y, & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.31)} \\ O (Wv + 2 Havc) & \leqslant y. & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.32)} \end{array}\)

The two outputs \(u, v\) are the probe and summability budgets. In the Wilson application, at coefficient grade \(d\) and block side \(L\), the constants are specialized as follows: \(\mathcal{T}\) is the finite coefficient-recovery probe family \(\mathcal{Q}_d\) of Subsection 18.4 and \(W = T_d\) its reconstruction constant; \(G_d\) is the polarization constant \(M_d\) and \(\pi_{\tau} = \pi (\tau) \geqslant 1\) the probe bound defined there; \(O\) is the orbit-average cost and \(H\) the readout bound of the grade-\(d\) coefficient frame; \(a = L^d\); and \(c\) is the credibility of the fine profile, that is, its uniform mass bound for a normalized frame-column insertion, fixed by its support exponent, order and power. This is the constant called \(h\) in Subsection 18.8. With these values \(q_{\tau}\) bounds the local variation coefficient, while the other three inequalities pay respectively for regular terms, large-support terms, and the primary atom. The same number \(v\) is later used as the summability budget in all of the local atom estimates of one transition; the budgets are not chosen separately for each estimate.

Lemma 18.10. (Positive local budget choice) For every finite probe family and every \(y > 0\), there are \(u, v > 0\) satisfying (18.29)–(18.32).

Proof. Set

\(\displaystyle K = \sum_{\tau \in \mathcal{T}} q_{\tau}, \qquad B = \max \{1, O [W (1 + K) + 2 Ha], 4 OHa, O [W + 2 Hac]\} .\)

These are finite nonnegative numbers. Choose

\(\displaystyle v = \frac{y}{2 B}, \qquad u = (1 + K) v.\)

Both are strictly positive. Since \(q_{\tau} \leqslant K \leqslant 1 + K\), \(q_{\tau} v \leqslant u\). The left side of the regular-term inequality is \(vO [W (1 + K) + 2 Ha] \leqslant vB = y / 2\). The large-support and primary left sides are respectively \(4 OHav \leqslant Bv = y / 2\) and \(vO [W + 2 Hac] \leqslant Bv = y / 2\). Each is therefore at most \(y\), as required.\(\Box\)

We next record the uniform threshold selection used to turn fixed-parameter estimates into a single scale profile. Here \(j\) is a stage index of the selected stage data, with profile orders \(\text{order}_i\), powers \(\text{power}_j\) and first stage \(i_0\). At a stage \(j\), the admissible pairs of an adjacent stage \(i\) and a coefficient grade \(d\) form the set

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{I}_j = \{ (i, d) : i \leqslant j \leqslant i + 1, i_0 \leqslant i, d + 1 \leqslant \text{order}_i, d \leqslant \text{power}_j, d + 2 \leqslant j \} .\)

The adjacent stage is \(j\) itself or its immediate predecessor, so there are at most two, and \(d + 2 \leqslant j\) confines the grades to the finite set \(\{0, \ldots, j - 1\}\). Thus \(\mathcal{I}_j\) is finite, and only finitely many eventual requirements occur at each fixed \(j\).

Lemma 18.11. (Monotone threshold envelope) Let \(b_j \in \mathbb{R}\) be any prescribed lower threshold. For each \(j\), let \(S_j\) be a finite set and, for each \(s \in S_j\), let \(E_{j, s} \in \mathbb{R}\) have the property that a desired conclusion \(\mathcal{P}_{j, s} (x)\) holds whenever \(x \geqslant E_{j, s}\). Then there is a nondecreasing sequence \(B_j\) such that \(B_j \geqslant b_j\) and, for every \(s \in S_j\), \(\mathcal{P}_{j, s} (x)\) holds whenever \(x \geqslant B_j\).

Proof. Define

\(\displaystyle e_j = \max (\{b_j \} \cup \{E_{j, s} : s \in S_j \}), \qquad B_j = \max_{0 \leqslant r \leqslant j} e_r .\)

The maxima exist because the sets are finite and nonempty after adjoining \(b_j\). The prefix maximum \(B_j\) is nondecreasing and dominates \(b_j\). If \(s \in S_j\), then \(E_{j, s} \leqslant e_j \leqslant B_j\), so every \(x \geqslant B_j\) satisfies the stipulated conclusion.\(\Box\)

Apply this lemma at each stage \(j\) with \(S_j =\mathcal{I}_j\). Let a positive target bound \(y_{j, i}\) be prescribed for every pair of stages. For each fixed \((i, d) \in \mathcal{I}_j\), the atom-budget lemma, with \(y = y_{j, i}\), first supplies positive budgets \(u, v\); for these fixed budgets, the finite-volume local estimates then supply an eventual threshold \(E_{j, (i, d)}\) in the scale variable \(x\) for the corresponding primary, regular, and forced atom conclusions. These local estimates require, in addition, that the smallness parameter \(\varepsilon\) attached to the transition \((j, i)\) is at most \(1 / 4\). Taking the finite maximum and then the prefix maximum gives one monotone threshold profile that works simultaneously for every adjacent transition and every permitted coefficient grade. This is the uniformization step needed before the scale induction. The atom estimates to which this uniformization applies are established and matched below.

18.5.1The three atom estimates used in the recurrence

We verify the estimates for the selected transition, with the same budget choices \(u, v\) as above. Put \(\rho = L^{- 2 / 5}\) and \(\chi = \sqrt{H (x)}\). The selected response package is Theorem 18.17, with its probe and cloud bounds proved in Subsection 18.8.2. In particular, every cloud of fine mass \(m\) has charge at most \(vm / \chi\) on a common tail. The two real/imaginary channel charges may be equal; they are nevertheless counted twice.

Regular atoms.
For a regular source of mass \(m\), use the actual small- and large-support constructions in Subsection 18.10.1. In the small case the local probe charge is at most \(q_{\tau} vm / \chi \leqslant um / \chi\) for every recovery probe. Coefficient recovery costs \(W\), and the two cloud readouts cost at most \(2 Havm / \chi\). Including the motion factor \(O\) gives
\(\displaystyle \frac{m}{\chi} O (Wu + 2 Hav) \leqslant \frac{ym}{\chi} .\)

In the large case each of the two chart terms and each of the two cloud terms is at most \(vm / \chi\). Hence the total is \(4 OHavm / \chi \leqslant ym / \chi\). Proposition 18.22 gives the associated presentations, their exact pushed density, and remainder cost at most \(\rho m\) with the prescribed coarse forced normalization. Its derivative and vanishing-jet assumptions hold for the regular atoms of the effective-representation certificate; they are not inferred from a pointwise bound. Thus the cost pair is \((ym / \chi, \rho m)\).

Basis atoms.
Let \(e_{\alpha}\) be a nonconstant normalized basis column, and insert its actual local section with the fine threshold. The cloud mass in its primary correction is bounded by the fine-profile credibility \(c\). The primary expansion and coefficient extraction are those of Subsection 10.8. Choose the response package at bound \(v\). For a recovery probe \(p\), compare the actual principal row first to its Gaussian reference, then to its cropped deterministic prediction, then to its full polynomial forecast. The first two comparisons, in the two real/imaginary channels, have ordered derivative error at most \(2 (e|u (x) | + \delta_x)\): the insertion bound supplies \(e |u (x) |\) in each channel, and the cap bound supplies \(\delta_x\) for the fluctuation-to-prediction comparison. Polarization and the derivative bounds of the probe curve cost \(\pi (p) \Lambda\); multiplication by the physical factor costs \(L^d\). The cropped-to-full comparison costs the additional \(\delta_x\) by (18.61). Thus the actual principal probe error is bounded by
\(\displaystyle \pi (p) \Lambda L^d \hspace{0.17em} 2 (e|u (x) | + \delta_x) + \delta_x \leqslant C_x \leqslant v / \chi,\)

exactly the charged expression of Theorem 18.17. The Gaussian-to-prediction comparison is the normalized derivative comparison of Subsection 10.8; the cap pays its Gaussian moment before any probe or column is chosen. Its full predicted coefficient is the leading map of that same section, with both the amplitude-degree and spatial-grade factors retained. The other rows have summed readout charge at most \(Ha (\mathcal{M}+\mathcal{M}) \leqslant 2 Havc / \chi\) by the cloud bound. The coefficient-extraction identity is linear; the finite recovery map costs \(W\), and its motionwise norm costs \(O\). The error from the prescribed leading coefficient is therefore at most

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{\chi} O (Wv + 2 Havc) \leqslant \frac{y}{\chi} .\)

The graded principal remainder and the local splitting are proved in Subsection 10.8: for a column of spatial grade \(g_{\alpha}\) they cost at most \(PL^{d - g_{\alpha}}\), with the fixed frame ledger constant \(P\). The splitting preserves the exact density by (7.61); it subtracts precisely the extracted local section and puts the residual in the regular and forced lists. It also preserves order-zero format, since the residual has zero identity value and zero nonwrapping test jets. Covariance of extraction under each lattice motion gives presentations of the motion of one coefficient, rather than unrelated coefficients for different motions. The unit column has its exact constant transport and no error. By finite linearity, a coefficient \(z\) consequently has error at most \((y / \chi) \|z^{\circ} \|_1\) and remainder at most \(P \|\mathcal{V}_L z^{\circ} \|_1\), where \((\mathcal{V}_L z)_{\alpha} = L^{d - g_{\alpha}} z_{\alpha}\).

Forced atoms.
Let a forced source have fine mass \(m \geqslant 0\), and set \(g = \sqrt{\text{forcedAllowance}_{\text{fine}}} \in (0, 1]\). We use the localized expansion with its marked profile and address-background bounds from Subsection 7.12, with the small bounds funded as in Subsection 9.2. These are the hypotheses of the projection calculation for the actual transition. For a forced insertion the principal cluster is zero: its flag is incompatible with the healthy principal term. In the projection calculation of Subsection 7.12 we may therefore set its principal bound \(V\) to zero. The two remaining bounds and the hit-width condition are paid in Subsection 9.2. With the notation there, \(e_0 = (\rho / H_d) m\), so the motionwise coefficient cost and the normalized coarse remainder cost are both at most \(H_d e_0 = \rho m\). The exact density and order-zero format are preserved by that same splitting calculation. The flagged profile identity there is required; equality of scalar densities alone would not justify it.

Lemma 18.15 gives \(\rho g \leqslant y / \chi\) eventually. Consequently

\(\displaystyle \rho m \leqslant \frac{y}{\chi} \frac{m}{g}, \qquad \rho m \leqslant \rho \frac{m}{g} .\)

These are the required forced cost pair, including \(m = 0\). The first inequality uses the fine allowance in \(g\); the remainder on the left already uses the coarse forced normalization supplied by the projection. No identification of those two allowances is made.

All statements concern the actual two effective representations of one transition. The finite basis sum and the absolutely summable regular and forced lists are combined in the density-summation argument of Subsection 18.4; the bounded-density and summable-activity estimates there justify the interchange with integration. At a fixed stage and grade there are only finitely many probe, basis, channel and budget requirements. Their thresholds can therefore be included in the finite maximum above, before the period, volume or source is chosen. This is the promised source of the eventual local estimates, not an additional choice of an atom-dependent threshold.

18.6Rate conversion and the local scale budget

This section records the quantitative selection after the Fourier-tail and jet comparison in Subsection 18.9. It proves the finite-rate summation used to select the probe estimate and the scalar domination used to choose the local chart budget. The strip, growth, weighted-vanishing, chart, and support receipts themselves remain the inputs identified in that section.

Fix the block side \(L\). Write

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} H (x) = \text{fineMonitor} (x), \quad \chi (x) = \sqrt{H (x)}, \quad \beta (x) = \beta_F (x),\\ R (x) = \text{radiusD} (x), \quad \rho (x) = \text{reach} (x), \quad b (x) = \text{radiusB} (x) . \end{array}\)

For fixed \(L\), these are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} \beta (x) = x + c_L, \quad b (x) = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil,\\ R (x) = b (x) (4 b (x) + 2) + 2 b (x), \quad \rho (x) = 45 (1 + b (x)),\\ \text{depthW}_L (R) = L (R + 2), \end{array}\)

where \(c_L\) is fixed. Here \(H (x) = \exp ((\log \log \beta (x))^2)\), so \(H \geqslant 1\), \(H \to \infty\), and \(\chi\) is the monitor factor in the priced conclusion. For a function \(f\), write \(f \in \mathcal{R}_a\) if for all \(v > a\) one has eventually \(|f (x) | \leqslant \beta (x)^v\); the rate is thus defined by strict powers. Finite sums preserve a common rate, products add rates, and \(\chi \in \mathcal{R}_0\).

Lemma 18.12. (The exponential tail pays the finite probe) Let \(d \in \mathbb{N}\), \(\sigma > 0\), and let \(K, M\) and the priced local derivative bound \(\Phi\) be fixed nonnegative constants. Let \(\delta_k (x)\) and \(C_1 (x)\) be the quantities of (18.72)–(18.73), with \(b = \rho (x)\) and \(m = R (x)\). Set

\(\displaystyle E (x) = \sum_{k = 0}^d \delta_k (x), \qquad Z_d (x) = \sum_{j = 0}^d \# \text{Part}^{\text{ord}} (j) \hspace{0.17em} 2^j E (x) C_1 (x)^j \hspace{0.17em} \Phi . \) (18.33)

Then \(Z_d \in \mathcal{R}_{- 1}\), and consequently

\(\displaystyle \chi (x) Z_d (x) \longrightarrow 0. \) (18.34)

In particular, for every \(y > 0\), eventually \(Z_d (x) \leqslant y / \chi (x)\). If the local receipts of Proposition 18.21 hold and \(E (x) \leqslant C_1 (x)\), every derivative order \(j \leqslant d\) of the selected probe is bounded by \(Z_d (x) w\), where \(w\) is the derivative bound of the observable appearing in (18.68) and the bound factor \(\Phi\) has already been included in \(Z_d\).

Proof. By the polynomial geometry receipts, \(R\) and the lift-size polynomial \((1 + 4 L (R + 2))^{dk}\) have rate zero. The width cost and all other factors in \(\delta_k\), apart from \(\exp (- \sigma \rho (x) / 2)\), are fixed constants or rate-zero polynomial factors. The reach cutoff has every negative rate: for each \(a > 0\), eventually \(\exp (- \sigma \rho (x) / 2) \leqslant \beta^{- a}\). Thus each \(\delta_k\), and their finite sum \(E\), have rate \(- 1\) (the stronger arbitrary negative rate is more than needed). The finite sum in \(C_1\) has rate zero. By the product and finite-sum rules, \(Z_d \in \mathcal{R}_{- 1}\).

Since \(\chi \in \mathcal{R}_0\), their product has rate \(- 1\). The strict-power definition then implies convergence to zero: choose any exponent \(v\) strictly between \(- 1\) and \(0\); eventually \(| \chi Z_d | \leqslant \beta^v\), and \(\beta \to \infty\). This proves (18.34) and the stated eventual estimate. The final assertion follows by applying Proposition 18.21 at each order and bounding its finite ordered-partition sum by (18.33).\(\Box\)

Actual finite-rate receipts.
In the construction the polynomial direction-growth constants are
\(\displaystyle h_k (x) = 4 KM \hspace{0.17em} C_{dk} (\sigma) (1 + 4 L (R (x) + 2))^{dk}, \qquad C_1 (x) = 1 + \sum_{k = 0}^d h_k (x) .\)

The error at order \(k\) is

\(\displaystyle \delta_k (x) = 8 KMe^{- \sigma \rho (x) / 2} (1 + 4 R (x))^{dk} C_{dk} (\sigma / 2) .\)

These formulas contain a factor four from the four edge components and an additional factor two from the two input arrays. The coefficient \(E (x)\) is required to be at most \(C_1 (x)\) before applying the finite multilinear comparison. This is a separate eventual estimate; it follows because \(E \in \mathcal{R}_{- 1}\) and \(C_1 \geqslant 1\).

Proposition 18.13. (The selected local budget) Fix natural numbers \(n, p, r\) and a fixed positive integer block side \(L\). Let

\(\displaystyle C.C \geqslant 1, \quad C.M \geqslant 1, \quad D.Q \geqslant 1, \quad D.K \geqslant 1, \quad D.Z \geqslant 1, \quad D. \varepsilon > 0\)

be fixed real constants, the scalar entries of the chart data \(C\) and of the local record \(D\); the dotted notation records which datum each constant belongs to. These ranges are the only meaning of “admissible” below, and no other property of \(C\) and \(D\) is used except the nonnegative linear clock introduced below. Put

\(\displaystyle P_n = \sum_{j = 0}^n \# \text{Part}^{\text{ord}} (j), \qquad \text{comparisonConst} (n, Q) = 2 Q (1 + P_n),\)

and let \(\mathcal{C}_L\) be the finite index set \(\text{Site}_L \times \text{Axis} \times I_G\), where \(\text{Site}_L\) is the set of sites of the periodic lattice of side \(L\) and \(I_G\) is the finite set of colour coordinates, of cardinality \(\dim \mathfrak{g}\). For \(x\) large, define the covering and response quantities

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} P_{\ast} (x) & = (2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{depthW}_L (R (x)) + 1)^4,\\ B_{\ast} (x) & = (r + 48) (1 + b (x)) (1 + P_{\ast} (x)),\\ A_{\ast} (x) & = \text{boost}_{C, D, L} \hspace{0.17em} B_{\ast} (x)^{\hspace{0.17em} 8 + 2 p + 2 pn} + \frac{16}{D. \varepsilon},\\ S_{\ast} (x) & = \text{comparisonConst} (n, D.Q) (2 C.C (1 + R (x))^p)^2,\\ U_{\ast} (x) & = (A_{\ast} (x) + S_{\ast} (x)) \hspace{0.17em} \text{fineCut} (x) + \frac{2 A_{\ast} (x) + 2}{H (x)},\\ K_{\ast} (x) & = 1 + \text{guests}_{G, D.K} (n + 1, L) B_{\ast} (x)^{\hspace{0.17em} 4 (1 + 4 (n + 1))} . \end{array}\)

These abbreviations use the coefficients

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} s_L & = 1 + 16 \hspace{0.17em} \#\mathcal{C}_L,\\ b_0 & = (\text{comparisonConst} (n, D.Q) + C.M) (2 C.C)^2,\\ \text{boost}_{C, D, L} & = b_0 + (b_0 + 1) L^3 + 2 ns_L^2 (D.Z + 1) D.Z,\\ \text{guests}_{G, D.K} (q, L) & = 2 s_L^{1 + 4 q} \text{inflate}_{4 q} (D.K^{- 1} / 8),\\ \text{meter} (h, D.K, m) & = 2 (1 + h)^{m + 1} \text{inflate}_m (D.K^{- 1} / 8) . \end{aligned} \ \) (18.35)

Here \(h\) is the number of heads, the subscript of \(\text{guests}\) records its dependence on the gauge group (through \(I_G\)) and on the scalar \(D.K\), and \(\text{inflate}_m (v) = (1 + m / v)^m\) is the Gaussian moment inflation factor. The scalar cost terms below are nonnegative by the stated ranges of the constants. The clock is a fixed function of the form \(\text{scaleRate} (t) = c_{\text{clock}} t\) with \(c_{\text{clock}} = \text{scaleRate} (1) \geqslant 0\). Let \(\beta \geqslant \beta (x) / 2\). Suppose the support and budget satisfy

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} B. \text{threshold} = \text{fineCut} (x), \quad 0 \leqslant P. \text{threshold} \leqslant 2 B. \text{threshold},\\ B.B \leqslant 2 b (x), \quad P. \text{power} = p, \quad \text{coverNumber} (B.B, X) \leqslant P_{\ast} (x) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.36)} \end{array}\)

Put \(F_0 = B. \text{threshold}\), \(u = \beta^{- 1 / 2}\), \(v_{\rho} = C.C \hspace{0.17em} P. \text{threshold} (1 + \rho (x))^p\), \(v_R = C.C \hspace{0.17em} P. \text{threshold} (1 + R (x))^p\), \(h = \text{heads} (B, X)\), and \(\text{cup} = D.Z (1 + \text{scope} (B, X, r, x))^{pn}\). Define the displayed scalar cost by

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} F (x) = & (\text{comparisonConst} (n, D.Q) + C.M) v_{\rho}^2 (2 + \text{breadth} (B, X))^{pn} / F_0\\ & + \frac{\text{comparisonConst} (n, D.Q) v_{\rho}^2 + u}{F_0} L^3\\ & + n (2 F_0) h^2 (\text{cup} + 1) \text{cup}\\ & + \frac{16}{D. \varepsilon} (F_0 + u / F_0)\\ & + \frac{\text{comparisonConst} (n, D.Q) v_R^2 + u}{F_0} . \end{aligned} \ \) (18.37)

This is the response-plus-paint cost used by the local chart construction. It satisfies, eventually in \(x\),

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant F (x) \leqslant U_{\ast} (x), \qquad \text{meter} (\text{heads} (B, X), D.K, 4 (n + 1)) \leqslant K_{\ast} (x), \) (18.38)

and

\(\displaystyle \chi (x) U_{\ast} (x) K_{\ast} (x) \longrightarrow 0. \) (18.39)

Consequently, for every \(y > 0\), eventually and uniformly in all data satisfying (18.36),

\(\displaystyle F (x) \leqslant 1, \qquad \text{scaleRate} (t) F (x) \text{meter} (\text{heads} (B, X), D.K, 4 (n + 1)) \leqslant \frac{y}{\chi (x)} t \quad (t \geqslant 0) . \) (18.40)

This is the scalar estimate consumed by the local regular-source branch.

Proof. We give the domination and limiting arguments separately. First, the cover formula gives

\(\displaystyle 1 + \rho (x) \leqslant B_{\ast} (x), \quad 2 + \text{breadth} (B, X) \leqslant B_{\ast} (x), \quad 1 + \text{scope} (B, X, r, x) \leqslant B_{\ast} (x) . \) (18.41)

Write \(a = b (x)\) and \(q = \text{coverNumber} (B.B, X)\). The definitions give

\(\displaystyle \text{breadth} (B, X) = q (4 B.B + 2) + 2 B.B + 8 \leqslant q (8 a + 2) + 4 a + 8, \quad \text{scope} = ra + \text{breadth} + 1.\)

With \(S_X = (r + 48) (1 + a) (1 + q)\), direct expansion, using \(a, q, r \geqslant 0\), gives \(1 + 45 (1 + a) \leqslant S_X\), \(2 + q (8 a + 2) + 4 a + 8 \leqslant S_X\), and \(ra + q (8 a + 2) + 4 a + 10 \leqslant S_X\). These are (18.41) with \(S_X\) in place of \(B_{\ast}\). Since \(q \leqslant P_{\ast}\) and the bulk formula is increasing in its cover-count argument, \(S_X \leqslant B_{\ast}\); hence (18.41) follows.

Put \(F_0 = B. \text{threshold}\), \(u = gStep (\beta) = \beta^{- 1 / 2}\), and \(S_X = \text{bulk} (\text{coverNumber} (B.B, X), r, x)\). Since \(F_0 = \beta (x)^{- 1 / 2} H (x)\), (14) yields

\(\displaystyle \frac{u}{F_0} = \frac{1}{H (x)} \sqrt{\frac{\beta (x)}{\beta}} \leqslant \frac{\sqrt{2}}{H (x)} \leqslant \frac{2}{H (x)} . \) (18.42)
