The cover inequalities (18.41) and \(P. \text{threshold} \leqslant 2 F_0\) give

\(\displaystyle \text{loom} (P, \rho (x), C.C) \leqslant 2 C.CF_0 S_X^p . \) (18.43)

Put \(t = F_0\) and \(v = \text{loom} (P, \rho (x), C.C)\). The loom is \(C.C \hspace{0.17em} P. \text{threshold} (1 + \rho (x))^p\); (18) and \(P. \text{threshold} \leqslant 2 t\) give \(v \leqslant 2 C.CtS_X^p\). The segment term is \((\text{comparisonConst} + C.M) v^2 \text{heft} / t\), where \(\text{heft} = (2 + \text{breadth})^{pn} \leqslant S_X^{pn}\). Thus, with \(b_0 = (\text{comparisonConst} + C.M) (2 C.C)^2\),

\(\displaystyle \text{segment} \leqslant b_0 tS_X^{2 p + pn} \leqslant b_0 \zeta (B, u) S_X^{8 + 2 p + 2 pn} .\)

The tranche term is \(((\text{comparisonConst} \hspace{0.17em} v^2 + u) / t) L^3\). Its first numerator part is bounded by \(b_0 tS_X^{2 p}\); its second is \(u / t\). Since \(t \leqslant \zeta (B, u) = t + u / t\), \(S_X \geqslant 1\), and \(2 p \leqslant 8 + 2 p + 2 pn\), this gives \(\text{tranche} \leqslant (b_0 + 1) L^3 \zeta (B, u) S_X^{8 + 2 p + 2 pn}\).

For the scratch term, its exact factors are \(n (2 t) \text{heads}^2 (\text{cup} + 1) \text{cup}\), where \(\text{cup} = D.Z (1 + \text{scope})^{pn}\). The head count is \((2 \text{scope} + 1)^4 \#\mathcal{C}_L\). From (18.41), \(1 + \text{heads} \leqslant s_L S_X^4\), with \(s_L = 1 + 16\#\mathcal{C}_L\). Also \(\text{cup} \leqslant D.ZS_X^{pn}\) and \(\text{cup} + 1 \leqslant (D.Z + 1) S_X^{pn}\). Replacing \(t\) by \(\zeta (B, u)\) therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \text{scratch} \leqslant 2 ns_L^2 (D.Z + 1) D.Z \hspace{0.17em} \zeta (B, u) S_X^{8 + 2 pn} .\)

Adding these three bounds gives the coefficient \(\text{boost} = b_0 + (b_0 + 1) L^3 + 2 ns_L^2 (D.Z + 1) D.Z\). Since \(\text{opal} = \text{rust} + (16 / D. \varepsilon) \zeta\), and \(S_X \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \text{opal} \leqslant A_{\ast} (x) (F_0 + u / F_0) . \) (18.44)

The paint term is evaluated with the loom at \(R (x)\), not at the reach. Its direction is \(v_R = C.C \hspace{0.17em} P. \text{threshold} (1 + R (x))^p \leqslant 2 C.CF_0 (1 + R (x))^p\). Squaring this inequality and dividing by \(F_0 > 0\) gives \(\text{comparisonConst} (n, D.Q) v_R^2 / F_0 \leqslant S_{\ast} (x) F_0\). The remaining paint term is \(u / F_0\). Thus the paint contribution is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \text{paintW} \leqslant S_{\ast} (x) F_0 + u / F_0 . \) (18.45)

Equations (18.42)–(18.45) give \(F \leqslant U_{\ast}\).

The head-count moment estimate follows by inserting \(1 + \text{heads} \leqslant s_L S_X^4\) into the explicit moment formula (18.35): for \(m = 4 (n + 1)\),

\(\displaystyle \text{meter} (\text{heads} (B, X), D.K, 4 (n + 1)) \leqslant \text{guests}_{G, D.K} (n + 1, L) S_X^{4 (1 + 4 (n + 1))} \leqslant K_{\ast} (x) . \) (18.46)

For the limit, \(B_{\ast}, A_{\ast}, S_{\ast}, K_{\ast}\) are fixed polynomials in \(1 + \text{radiusB} (x)\), hence have rate zero and are subpower relative to \(H\). Indeed \(\text{radiusB} (x) \leqslant 1 + (\log x)^2\), while \(\beta (x) = x + O (1)\) for fixed \(L\), and \(\log H = (\log \log \beta)^2\); the logarithm of any such fixed polynomial is \(O (\log \log \beta) = o (\log H)\). Thus for each \(\eta > 0\), eventually each nonnegative polynomial factor \(Q\) here satisfies \(Q \leqslant H^{\eta}\). The first term in \(\chi U_{\ast} K_{\ast}\) has rate \(- 1 / 2\), since \(\text{fineCut} = H / \sqrt{\beta}\) and all other factors have rate zero. For the second term choose the subpower bounds \(A_{\ast} + 1 \leqslant 2 H^{1 / 8}\), \(K_{\ast} \leqslant H^{1 / 8}\); then

\(\displaystyle \chi \hspace{0.17em} \frac{(2 A_{\ast} + 2) K_{\ast}}{H} \leqslant 4 H^{1 / 2 + 1 / 8 + 1 / 8 - 1} = 4 H^{- 1 / 4} \longrightarrow 0.\)

This proves (18.39). Also \(H \geqslant 1\), \(K_{\ast} \geqslant 1\), and \(F \leqslant U_{\ast}\), so \(F \leqslant \chi U_{\ast} K_{\ast}\); (18.39) makes this at most one eventually. Finally \(\text{scaleRate} (t) = t \text{scaleRate} (1)\) for \(t \geqslant 0\). Choose \(d_{\ast} = 1 + \text{scaleRate} (1)\). From (18.39), eventually \(d_{\ast} \chi U_{\ast} K_{\ast} \leqslant y\), and (18.38) proves the second inequality in (18.40).\(\Box\)

The bound is conditional only on the selected local chart and support inequalities (18.36); the algebra of the support-cover, rust, and Gaussian-moment estimates is expanded above. It does not produce the separate strip, field-growth, weighted vanishing, same-history, or healthy-chart receipts required to enter this local branch.

18.7Three local scalar estimates

This section proves three scalar bound conversions used by the selected trace construction. The arguments are independent of the construction of the local response and chart records that consume these bounds.

Fix a block side \(L\), and retain \(\beta (x)\), \(H (x)\), and \(\chi (x) = H (x)^{1 / 2}\) from Subsection 18.6. For a fixed bound \(a > 0\), write

\(\displaystyle \textsf{s}_a (x) = a / \chi (x) .\)

A negative strict-power rate has the following elementary consequence: if \(f \in \mathcal{R}_{\alpha}\) with \(\alpha < 0\), then \(\chi (x) f (x) \to 0\). Indeed \(\chi \in \mathcal{R}_0\), so the product has rate \(\alpha\); choose \(v \in (\alpha, 0)\), and use \(\beta (x)^v \to 0\).

Lemma 18.14. (Cloud bound) Let \(Q\) be a target with rate exponent \(q < 0\). Suppose its poster coefficient \(p_Q (x)\) has strict-power rate \(q / 4\), and define the cloud response for a cloud \(Z\) on a representation \(R\) by

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{M}_Q (Z ; x) = m_R (Z) \exp (- \epsilon_Q N_R (Z)) p_Q (x), \qquad m_R (Z) \geqslant 0, \quad N_R (Z) \geqslant 0, \quad \epsilon_Q > 0.\)

Then for every \(a > 0\), eventually, uniformly over all clouds,

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{M}_Q (Z ; x) | \leqslant \textsf{s}_a (x) m_R (Z) . \) (18.47)

Proof. Since \(q < 0\), choose \(v\) with \(q / 4 < v < 0\). The rate hypothesis implies \(|p_Q (x) | \leqslant a / \chi (x)\) eventually: equivalently \(\chi |p_Q | \to 0\), and then make it at most \(a\). The attenuation obeys \(0 < \exp (- \epsilon_Q N_R (Z)) \leqslant 1\), independently of the cloud. Multiply these inequalities by the nonnegative mass \(m_R (Z)\).\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.15. (Forced gas bound) Let \(g (x) = \exp (- H (x)^{1 / 5} / 2)\), and let \(\text{DoorsRate} (L) = L^{- 2 / 5} > 0\) be the block remainder-cost factor. For every \(a > 0\), eventually

\(\displaystyle \text{DoorsRate} (L) g (x) \leqslant \textsf{s}_a (x) . \) (18.48)

Proof. Set \(t = \log \log \beta (x)\), so \(\log H = t^2\) and \(\log \beta = e^t\). Then

\(\displaystyle \log (\chi (x) g (x)) = \tfrac{1}{2} t^2 - \tfrac{1}{2} e^{t^2 / 5} \longrightarrow - \infty,\)

because \(e^{t^2 / 5}\) grows faster than \(t^2\). Thus \(\chi g \to 0\); multiplying by the fixed positive \(\text{DoorsRate} (L)\) and making the product at most \(a\) proves (18.48).\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.16. (Tuition bound) For a target with goal \(\beta (x)^q\), define its tuition at fixed integers \(s, n \geqslant 0\) by

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{T}_Q (x ; s, n) = A (x)^{sn} \hspace{0.17em} P_{1 / 2} (sn) \hspace{0.17em} \beta (x)^{- q} \exp (- b (x) / 2), \) (18.49)

where \(A\) and the polynomial bound \(P_{1 / 2} (sn)\) have rate zero and \(b (x) = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil\). Explicitly,

\(\displaystyle A (x) = 4 (2 b (x) + 1), \qquad P_{1 / 2} (k) = 2^k \hspace{0.17em} k! \hspace{0.17em} e^{1 / 2},\)

so that \(A\) is a fixed polynomial in \(b (x)\) and \(P_{1 / 2} (sn)\) is a constant; both have rate zero. Then \(\mathcal{T}_Q (\cdot ; s, n) \in \mathcal{R}_z\) for every real \(z\). In particular, for every \(a > 0\), eventually

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{T}_Q (x ; s, n) \leqslant \textsf{s}_a (x) . \) (18.50)

Proof. The first two factors in (18.49) have rate zero, and \(\beta^{- q}\) has rate \(- q\). The last factor has every strict-power rate: for every fixed \(c > 0\), \(b (x) / 2 \geqslant c \log \beta (x)\) eventually, since \(b (x) \asymp (\log x)^2\) while \(\log \beta (x) \asymp \log x\). Thus \(e^{- b / 2} \leqslant \beta^{- c}\) eventually. Choose \(c > \max (0, - q - z)\); the product rate \(- q - c\) is then less than \(z\), so the strict-power definition gives the claimed rate. Taking \(z = - 1\) and using the negative-rate consequence above proves (18.50).\(\Box\)

The three bounds enter the selected local record with two different bounds. For one transition with block side \(L\) and one value of \(x\), the cloud bound (18.47) and the tuition bound (18.50) are used with the same bound \(a = z\) as the response and chart estimates of that record, while the gas bound (18.48) is used with the separate bound \(a = y\); both \(y, z > 0\) are fixed before the eventual threshold in \(x\) is taken. The three lemmas produce only these scalar entries of the record, not its response and chart entries.

18.8Construction of the finite local response package

We give the quantitative construction behind the local response package used by the later trace estimates. The output contains its probe error, chart error, bound, and cloud allowance; these are built together because the final cloud billing uses the same bound budget as the probe estimate.

Fix the scale data of the trace construction and write

\(\displaystyle \chi (x) = \sqrt{H (x)}, \qquad e = \text{title} (P_1), \quad a = \text{title} (P_0), \quad h = \text{credibility} (P_1), \quad \nu = \text{DoorsRate} (L) .\)

Here \(H\) and \(\chi\) are the monitor and its square root from Subsection 18.6, whose functions \(\beta (x)\), \(b (x)\), \(R (x)\) and \(\rho (x)\) are also used below; we write \(b (\beta (x))\) for the radius function evaluated at \(\beta (x)\),

\(\displaystyle \text{fieldCut} (x) = x^{- 1 / 2} \exp ((\log \log x)^2), \qquad \text{fineCut} (x) = \text{fieldCut} (\beta (x)) = \beta (x)^{- 1 / 2} H (x) .\)

Further, \(P_0\) and \(P_1\) are the two stage profiles of the transition, and \(L\) is the block side; \(e, a, h, \nu\) are positive, \(e, a \geqslant 1\), and \(\nu = L^{- 2 / 5}\). Let \(\mathscr{F}\) be the grade-\(d\) coefficient frame and \(F\) its finite family of coefficient-recovery probes; \(F\) is distinct from the finite basis of the frame, whose indices are denoted by \(\alpha\) below. The bounds \(\mathcal{B}_{\text{test}}\), \(\mathcal{B}_{\text{read}}\), \(\mathcal{B}_{\text{err}}\) and \(\text{orbitCost}\) below are constants of the frame \(\mathscr{F}\) (the second and third also of the selected holonomy map), not of the probe family. For \(p \in F\), let \(w_p = \pi (p) \geqslant 1\) be its probe bound, and write \(\Lambda = M_d\) for the polarization constant in the probe estimate, both as in Subsection 18.4. Put

\(\displaystyle Z =\mathcal{B}_{\text{test}} (\mathscr{F}), \qquad T =\mathcal{B}_{\text{read}} (\mathscr{F}), \qquad c = \text{orbitCost} (\mathscr{F}) \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{B}_{\text{err}} (\mathscr{F}) \hspace{0.17em} \text{corn} (d) \hspace{0.17em} a.\)

The coefficient-recovery and readout constants \(Z, T\) are chosen at least one: the defining finite-dimensional inequalities remain valid when their constants are enlarged to one. The error bound is the test bound times one plus a finite sum of nonnegative chain-rule bounds; the orbit cost is one plus a finite sum of coefficient sizes; and \(\text{corn} (d)\) is one plus a finite sum of nonnegative comparison constants. All three are at least one. Together with \(a \geqslant 1\) this gives \(c \geqslant 1\), in particular \(c > 0\). These are elementary properties of the chosen frame constants, not additional analytic inputs. For a positive target bound \(\zeta\), define

\(\displaystyle \kappa = \min \left\{ \frac{\nu}{2 cZ}, \zeta \right\} > 0, \qquad g_p (s) = w_p \Lambda L^d \hspace{0.17em} 2 (es + s) + s. \) (18.51)

Finiteness of the probe family \(F\) is essential here: each \(g_p\) is continuous, \(g_p (0) = 0\), and therefore there is one \(b > 0\) such that \(g_p (s) \leqslant \kappa\) for all \(p \in F\) and \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant b\). Set \(Q = b / 2\).

Theorem 18.17. (Finite-probe and cloud billing) Let \(d \in \mathbb{N}\), and assume \(e, a, h, \nu, c, Z, T, \zeta > 0\), \(e, a, Z, T \geqslant 1\), and a finite probe family \(F\) with weights \(w_p \geqslant 0\) and normalization \(\Lambda \geqslant 0\). Suppose the selected response satisfies \(u \in \mathcal{R}_{- 1 / 2}\) and \(\chi \in \mathcal{R}_0\), with \(\chi (x) \geqslant 1\) eventually. For each probe, the charged scalar is the continuous function (18.51). Set

\(\displaystyle \kappa = \min \left\{ \frac{\nu}{2 cZ}, \zeta \right\}, \qquad \delta_x = Q / \chi (x), \qquad C_x = \kappa / \chi (x) .\)

Assume the following selected-scale receipts hold on one common eventual tail in \(x\); the tail is fixed before the probe, the frame index and all the other data listed in each receipt are chosen, and each receipt holds uniformly in those data. For the threshold \(\theta = \text{fieldCut} (x)\) and the fine threshold \(\Theta = \text{fineCut} (x)\),

\(\displaystyle \tfrac{1}{2} \leqslant \frac{\Theta}{\theta} \leqslant 2. \) (18.52)

The accompanying spatial comparison is \(b (\beta (x)) \leqslant 2 b (x)\) on this same tail. Indeed, that section gives \(\beta (x) = x + c_L\) for a fixed \(c_L\) and \(b (x) = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil\). Thus \((\log (x + c_L))^2 / (\log x)^2 \to 1\), while rounding changes either quantity by at most one. The ratio \(b (\beta (x)) / b (x)\) tends to one, and is eventually at most two. Both comparisons are used when applying the local budget estimate: the fine threshold is within a factor two of the coarse threshold, and the fine budget radius fits the allowed coarse radius. The probe receipt is as follows. Let \(p \in F\) have order \(r_p\) and probe functional \(R_p\) on coefficients, and let \(\alpha\) be a basis index of the coefficient frame, with basis coefficient \(\textsf{e}_{\alpha}\). Consider any step whose block count is \(L\) and whose first-order row is literally the row of the selected kernel with block side \(L\), with coarse side larger than \(2 \rho (x)\), and any two thresholds \(w, y \in (0, 1]\) within a factor two of each other. Let \(U_{\alpha}\) be the coarse-lattice function obtained from the local presentation of \(\textsf{e}_{\alpha}\) at the origin with threshold \(w\) by cropping the coarse configuration to radius \(\rho (x)\) about the parent of the origin and routing it through the first-order row of the step; let \(V_p\) be the logarithmic-link test curve of \(p\) with amplitude \(y\); and let \(\mathcal{L}\) be the leading coefficient map determined by the selected kernel and the two thresholds. The probe-to-run difference is

\(\displaystyle L^d \left. \frac{d^{r_p}}{ds^{r_p}} U_{\alpha} (V_p (s)) \right|_{s = 0} - R_p \left( \mathcal{L} \hspace{0.17em} \textsf{e}_{\alpha} \right),\)

and the receipt is that its absolute value is at most \(Q / \chi (x)\) for every \(p \in F\), every \(\alpha\), and all such steps and thresholds. The cap receipt is: for every support \(X\) and every pair of budgets \((P, B)\) such that \(P\) has threshold \(\theta\) and the power of \(P_0\), \(B\) has threshold \(\Theta\) and radius \(b (\beta (x))\), and \(\text{coverNumber} (B.B, X) \leqslant 1\), and for every \(\beta \geqslant \beta (x) / 2\), the scale cap \(O_x\) (the response cost of (18.37) without its last, paint, line) and the local moment \(M_x\) (the head-count moment of order \(4 (n + 1)\) there) obey

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant O_x \leqslant 1, \qquad \text{scaleRate} (e) \hspace{0.17em} O_x \hspace{0.17em} M_x \leqslant \frac{Q}{\chi (x)} . \) (18.53)

For every cloud \(Z_0\) of mass \(m_0 \leqslant h\), put \(\mathcal{M}_x (Z_0) = \text{marvel}_x (Z_0) \geqslant 0\). Its cloud receipt is

\(\displaystyle L^d \hspace{0.17em} 2\mathcal{M}_x (Z_0) \leqslant Sm_0, \qquad S = \frac{\nu}{2 cTh} . \) (18.54)

Then for all sufficiently large \(x\), with the same uniformity in the data of each receipt:

  1. the two selected thresholds satisfy the factor-two comparison;

  2. the response amplitude \(s_x = |u (x) |\) obeys \(s_x \leqslant \delta_x\) and \(s_x \leqslant \zeta / \chi (x)\);

  3. the scale cap is at most one, and its clock-weighted moment is at most \(\delta_x\);

  4. every selected probe has approximation error at most \(\delta_x\);

  5. for each probe \(p\), the explicit charge \(w_p \Lambda L^d \hspace{0.17em} 2 (es_x + \delta_x) + \delta_x\) is at most \(C_x\);

  6. for every cloud \(Z_0\) of mass at most \(h\), the combined charge

    \(\displaystyle c \left[ ZC_x + TL^d \hspace{0.17em} 2\mathcal{M}_x (Z_0) \right] \leqslant \nu .\)

In particular \(C_x \leqslant \zeta / \chi (x)\).

Proof. The product rate gives \(\chi u \in \mathcal{R}_{- 1 / 2}\). Choose \(q \in (- 1 / 2, 0)\); the strict-power definition implies \(\chi (x) |u (x) | \leqslant \beta (x)^q \to 0\). Thus, eventually,

\(\displaystyle |u (x) | \leqslant Q / \chi (x), \qquad |u (x) | \leqslant \zeta / \chi (x), \qquad \chi (x) \geqslant 1. \) (18.55)

The first two give the asserted response amplitude bound and the desired estimate for the amplitude. The first selected geometry estimate gives the factor-two threshold comparison.

The selected scale-cap estimate is called at bound \(Q / e\). Its estimate is linear in the allowed scalar multiplier \(t\); evaluating it at \(t = e\) gives exactly the second inequality in (18.53), while its companion bound gives \(O_x \leqslant 1\). The probe approximation estimate is called at tolerance \(Q\), which gives the bound stated above directly.

For a probe \(p\), the amplitude bound (18.55) gives

\(\displaystyle 2 (e|u (x) | + \delta_x) \leqslant \frac{2 (eQ + Q)}{\chi (x)} .\) (18.56)

Consequently its charged expression is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \frac{w_p \Lambda L^d \hspace{0.17em} 2 (eQ + Q) + Q}{\chi (x)} = \frac{g_p (Q)}{\chi (x)} \leqslant \frac{\kappa}{\chi (x)} = C_x .\)

This is the simultaneous finite-probe choice made above; it is not a pointwise choice depending on the probe.

Finally, let a cloud have mass \(m_0 \leqslant h\). The cloud estimate (18.54) gives

\(\displaystyle TL^d (2\mathcal{M}_x (Z_0)) \leqslant TSh = \frac{\nu}{2 c} .\)

The probe part costs at most \(ZC_x \leqslant Z \kappa / \chi (x) \leqslant Z \kappa\). By the definition of \(\kappa\), \(cZ \kappa \leqslant \nu / 2\). Adding the cloud and probe charges therefore gives at most \(\nu\). This proves all six clauses.\(\Box\)

18.8.1Matching the finite-volume response estimate

We now identify the local insertion receipt in the actual response construction. Fix a leaf insertion of weight \(\tau\), and hold its masked ingredient fixed while varying the local field. The two functions compared are the actual-sector and Gaussian-reference integrals

\(\displaystyle \Phi_6 (y) = \int R_6 (y, z) \chi_6 (y, z) h (z) \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0} (z), \qquad \Phi_9 (y) = \int R_9 (y, z) \chi_9 (y, z) h (z) \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0} (z), \) (18.57)

where \(h\) is the fixed measurable masked insertion and \(|h| \leqslant \tau\). The density comparison in Subsection 9.3 proves, under its displayed finite-dimensional covariance, mean, cutoff, and chart hypotheses,

\(\displaystyle \| \Phi_6 - \Phi_9 \|_{J^n_0} \leqslant \tau P_n (N, Q, S) (\delta + 2 Ne^{- v + M / 2}) . \) (18.58)

The primitive mean and covariance estimates supplying \(\delta\) are proved in Subsection 9.4. The fixed insertion is not differentiated. The local chart directions are the same ordered variations used in the response test, with \(j \leqslant n\) and their prescribed directional and canopy bounds.

In the exact construction, the fixed leaf is held together with its regular masked ingredient \(L'\). For each fixed grade and block, the bounds \(N (x), Q (x), S (x), \delta (x)\) and the cutoff-tail bounds are common to every admissible period, forest and tag choice, every leaf whose cover is at most \(B\), and every normalized direction list allowed in the comparison. They are independent of the leaf weight \(\tau\). Thus the one finite-probe choice and selected response \(u (x)\) work uniformly over all these inputs. The local read set, forest, tags, insertion mask, and chart are fixed during each individual differentiation. The two local observables are

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{O}_{\text{act}} (y) = \Phi_6 (y), \qquad \mathcal{O}_{\text{ref}} (y) = \Phi_9 (y) \) (18.59)

and these identities follow by writing each side with its own normalized Gaussian density and using the protected-support agreement on the insertion's read set. Thus the response difference is exactly \(\Phi_6 - \Phi_9\), not a comparison of different insertions or different Gaussian spaces. The local finite bound cutoff makes the integrands vanish outside a common compact coordinate ball; the cutoffs are one on the protected smaller ball. Differentiation under the integral is justified with the bounded measurable \(h\) fixed, by the uniform derivative bounds on that compact ball and dominated convergence.

The selected rate budgets choose the scalar envelope

\(\displaystyle B_{\ast} (x) = P_n (N (x), Q (x), S (x)) (\delta (x) + 2 N (x) e^{- v (x) + M (x) / 2}) .\)

The covariance discrepancy has strict-power rate \(- 1 / 2\), the mean discrepancy has rate \(- 1\) before conversion to Gaussian coordinates, and the good-coordinate tail has every negative rate. The fixed polynomial factor has rate zero. Therefore \(B_{\ast} \in \mathcal{R}_{- 1 / 2}\). The response construction packages the concrete derivative, cutoff, and tail budgets into one scalar envelope. The component bounds are assembled from the finite product and sum rules for the displayed rates. With this envelope, for every admissible leaf and every ordered list of at most \(n\) normalized chart directions, the derivative of the actual-minus-reference observable at the chart origin is bounded by \(\tau B_{\ast} (x)\), uniformly over the choices above. This is the concrete uniform derivative inequality used by the response construction.

We can now choose the scalar in the insertion receipt explicitly. The polynomial majorant \(P_n\) has nonnegative coefficients, and its arguments, \(\delta\) and the cutoff-tail term are nonnegative. Thus \(B_{\ast} \geqslant 0\) on the common tail where the comparison is applied. Define

\(\displaystyle f (x) = \tfrac{1}{2} B_{\ast} (x), \qquad u (x) = 2 |f (x) | = B_{\ast} (x)\) (18.60)

on that tail, and set both functions to zero before it. Altering a function on an initial interval does not change its eventual rate. Multiplication by a fixed positive constant preserves the strict-power rate: for each \(q > - 1 / 2\), choose \(q' \in (- 1 / 2, q)\) and absorb the constant using \(\beta (x)^{q - q'} \to \infty\). Hence both \(f\) and \(u\) belong to \(\mathcal{R}_{- 1 / 2}\).

For every admissible leaf of weight \(\tau \geqslant 0\) and every ordered list of at most \(n\) allowed chart directions, the inequality already proved is therefore exactly

\(\displaystyle | D^{\mathbf{v}} (\mathcal{O}_{\text{act}} -\mathcal{O}_{\text{ref}}) (0) | \leqslant \tau B_{\ast} (x) = \tau \hspace{0.17em} 2 |f (x) | = \tau |u (x) | .\)

This includes the zeroth derivative and the case \(\tau = 0\); no division by the leaf weight is made. The density and cutoff derivative bounds used in the comparison also give the required differentiability near the chart origin, with the masked ingredient fixed. Thus the bound applies to the actual response test, not just to a formal coefficient of its Taylor expansion.

The function \(f\) may depend on the fixed grade, block, stage profiles, selected kernel, differentiation order and comparison parameters. It is chosen before the period, forest, tags, leaf, mask, read support, base configuration and allowed direction list. Its tail is uniform in all those latter data, because the envelope \(B_{\ast}\) and the two integral identities were uniform in them. There is no additional normalization, positivity or matching condition on the auxiliary scalar \(f\): it is a bound majorant, not one of the local fields. The numerical factor two is therefore realized by the definition in (18.60); it incurs no extra analytic loss.

If no branch patch compatible with the fixed selected kernel and block exists, take \(f = u = 0\) everywhere. Any step to which the receipt could apply would itself supply such a patch, so the universally quantified insertion receipt has no instances in this case. If a compatible patch exists, the actual step uses that selected patch and the preceding construction applies. In either case this supplies the insertion receipt. The finite-test choice and scalar billing of Theorem 18.17 now apply to this concrete \(u\), on the intersection of its common tail with the geometry, cap, probe and cloud tails; no outstanding insertion estimate is assumed.

The package for one transition with block side \(L\) and grade \(d\) is constructed under the following standing conditions, which the scalar theorem above does not see: the guard of the record satisfies \(D. \varepsilon \leqslant 1 / 4\); the two integer parameters of the selected kernel are at least \(4\), and the first of them is at least \(d\); the differentiation order satisfies \(n \geqslant 2\); and the real parameter \(\mu\) of the response estimate is positive. The response receipt with amplitude \(|u (x) |\) is stated there for every leaf whose label is the logarithmic key of a regular activity and whose ingredient is exactly the mask, at the threshold of the representation, of a function that is regular on the support of that activity; for every budget of radius \(b (x)\) and of the power of \(P_0\) that is compatible with the representation; for every read support that is healthy at the base configuration and has cover number at most that radius; and for every list of at most \(n\) directions satisfying the budget's directional bound and the canopy bound \(2 \Theta (1 + R (x))^p\), where \(p\) is the power of \(P_0\). These identities of the leaf, mask, budget and support are what make (18.59) a comparison of one insertion on one Gaussian space. The cap receipt of Theorem 18.17 follows from the scalar budget estimate (18.40) of Subsection 18.6, called at bound \(Q / e\) and with the nonnegative paint term omitted. In fact \(B. \text{threshold} = \Theta = \text{fineCut} (x)\) and \(P. \text{threshold} = \theta \leqslant 2 \Theta\) by (18.52); \(B.B = b (\beta (x)) \leqslant 2 b (x)\) by the comparison proved above; \(P. \text{power}\) is the power of \(P_0\); and the cap's cover bound is at most one, so the support allowance in (18.36) can be chosen to be the constant one. The lower coupling bound is the same in both statements. These verify all the support and budget conditions of that application. The two remaining receipts are proved next for these same local data.

18.8.2The actual probe and cloud bounds

Fix a probe \(p\) of order \(k\) and a frame column \(\alpha\) of amplitude degree \(\ell_{\alpha}\). The constants below depend on these finite choices and the installed kernel and block, but not on the period or on the two thresholds \(w, y \in (0, 1]\) with \(1 / 2 \leqslant y / w \leqslant 2\). Let \(R = \rho (x) = 45 (1 + \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil)\). We apply the cropped-probe calculation of Subsection 10.8 with fine threshold \(w\) and coarse probe threshold \(y\). Its actual curve is exactly the one defining \(U_{\alpha} (V_p (s))\): it crops to radius \(R\) and applies the installed first-order row. The room condition \(2 R < c\) ensures that the crop uses the canonical covering-lattice lifts. The full polynomial curve uses the same row. Absolute convergence of its Fourier kernel identifies the periodized convolution with the covering sum regrouped by residue classes.

In the notation of (10.38), the jets of the two normalized input curves through order \(k\) differ by at most

\(\displaystyle e_R = C \{1 + K (1 + 4 R)^{dk} \} \exp (- \sigma R / 2), \qquad \sigma > 0.\)

Here the polynomial factor bounds the probe's link-log jets outside the crop; inside the crop the two arrays agree. The constants are uniform in \(w, y\): each nonempty chain-rule block contributes a threshold factor \(y^r / w \leqslant y / w \leqslant 2\). The full predicted jets have a fixed bound \(U\) given by the weighted kernel moments. The normalized local-section estimate (10.37) therefore bounds the output jet difference, including the physical factor, by

\(\displaystyle E_{p, \alpha} (x) = L^d C_{k, \alpha, U} (1 + e_R)^{\ell_{\alpha} + k} e_R .\) (18.61)

The full polynomial forecast in that section has exactly the coefficient transport \(\mathcal{L} \textsf{e}_{\alpha}\): its three fine-grade factors multiply to \(L^d w^{- \ell_{\alpha}}\), and its coarse scaling inserts \(y\) in the probe. Thus its reading is \(R_p (\mathcal{L} \textsf{e}_{\alpha})\), with no change of coefficient map. This identifies the difference estimated in (18.61) with the probe-to-run difference in Theorem 18.17. For the unit column the local section is constant and both readings agree exactly.

Since \(R \asymp (\log x)^2\), the logarithm of \(e_R\) is at most \(- c_0 (\log x)^2 + O (\log \log x)\) for some \(c_0 > 0\). Also \(\log \chi (x) = \tfrac{1}{2} (\log \log \beta (x))^2\) and \(\beta (x) = x + c_L\). It follows directly that \(\chi (x) E_{p, \alpha} (x) \to 0\). There are finitely many probes and columns. Taking the maximum of their eventual thresholds gives \(E_{p, \alpha} (x) \leqslant Q / \chi (x)\) simultaneously, uniformly over all the steps, periods and thresholds allowed in the receipt. This proves the probe estimate with its required order of quantifiers.

For the cloud, let \(q_{\ast} < 0\) be the fixed rate of the selected target and \(\epsilon_{\ast} > 0\) its support reserve. The actual cloud response is the quantity in Lemma 18.14, with its coefficient now specified explicitly:

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{M}_x (Z_0) = m_0 \exp (- \epsilon_{\ast} N (Z_0)) \hspace{0.17em} \beta (x)^{q_{\ast} / 4}, \qquad N (Z_0) \geqslant 0.\) (18.62)

The coefficient is a negative power of \(\beta\), so it has the strict-power rate required in that lemma. In particular \(0 \leqslant \mathcal{M}_x (Z_0) \leqslant m_0 \beta (x)^{q_{\ast} / 4}\) uniformly over clouds and representations. Choose one scale threshold with \(2 L^d \beta (x)^{q_{\ast} / 4} \leqslant S = \nu / (2 cTh)\). Multiplying by \(m_0 \geqslant 0\) proves (18.54), including the case \(m_0 = 0\) without division. The same formula gives the stronger statement

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{M}_x (Z_0) \leqslant (v / \chi (x)) m_0\)

for every prescribed \(v > 0\) on a later common tail, since \(\chi (x) \beta (x)^{q_{\ast} / 4} \to 0\). This is the cloud estimate used for regular and primary atoms. Thus the probe, cap, cloud, and insertion estimates are all supplied before their common finite intersection is used to construct the response package.

18.9Fourier tails and local jet estimates

This section proves two quantitative ingredients of the small-support regular-source argument: a weighted convolution bound with an exponentially small tail, and the corresponding multilinear estimate for a local coefficient probe. The remaining hypotheses are named mathematically: a uniform Fourier strip bound, a coefficient-growth receipt, and the local regularity and vanishing-jet receipts. In the construction these come from the selected finite-range kernel, the budget's order bounds, and the regularity of the masked source.

For each natural number \(n\), write \(\mathbb{Z}^4\) for the covering lattice, with \(|x|_1 = \sum_{\nu = 1}^4 |x_{\nu} |\), and put

\(\displaystyle W_n (x) = (1 + |x|_1)^n, \qquad C_n (\sigma) = \left( 1 + \frac{n + 1}{\sigma} \right)^n \quad (\sigma > 0) .\)

The elementary inequality

\(\displaystyle (1 + r)^n \leqslant C_n (\sigma) e^{\sigma r} \qquad (r \geqslant 0) \) (18.63)

follows by setting \(s = \sigma / (n + 1)\). Indeed

\(\displaystyle 1 + r \leqslant \left( 1 + \frac{n + 1}{\sigma} \right) (1 + sr) \leqslant \left( 1 + \frac{n + 1}{\sigma} \right) e^{sr},\)

and taking the \(n\)-th power gives (18.63), since \(ns \leqslant \sigma\). Also \(W_n (x + z) \leqslant W_n (x) W_n (z)\), by the triangle inequality and \(1 + a + b \leqslant (1 + a) (1 + b)\).

Lemma 18.18. (Polynomially weighted convolution) Suppose a scalar kernel \(q : \mathbb{Z}^4 \to \mathbb{R}\) and constants \(\sigma > 0\), \(C \geqslant 0\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \sum_{z \in \mathbb{Z}^4} e^{\sigma |z|_1} |q (z) | \leqslant C \) (18.64)

and a Banach-valued array \(b\) satisfies \(\|b (x)\| \leqslant MW_n (x)\), where \(M \geqslant 0\). Then the convolution

\(\displaystyle (q \ast b) (x) = \sum_z q (z) b (x + z)\)

is absolutely convergent and

\(\displaystyle \|(q \ast b) (x)\| \leqslant MC_n (\sigma) CW_n (x) . \) (18.65)

For a \(4\)-by-\(4\) matrix kernel whose sixteen entries each satisfy (18.64) with the same \(C\), the corresponding vector-row operator obeys

\(\displaystyle \|(\textsf{A} b) (x)\| \leqslant 4 MC_n (\sigma) CW_n (x), \) (18.66)

when the output norm is the maximum of its four component norms.

Proof. For each \(z\),

\(\displaystyle |q (z) | \hspace{0.17em} \|b (x + z)\| \leqslant MW_n (x) |q (z) | W_n (z) \leqslant MW_n (x) C_n (\sigma) e^{\sigma |z|_1} |q (z) |,\)

where (18.63) was applied to \(|z|_1\). Summing proves absolute convergence and (18.65). For a matrix row, apply (18.65) to each of its four entries and sum the four resulting component bounds; this gives (18.66).\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.19. (Agreement on a ball gives an exponential tail) Assume (18.64). Let \(A_1, A_2 : \mathbb{Z}^4 \to E\), where \(E\) is a Banach space, satisfy

\(\displaystyle \|A_1 (y)\| \leqslant d_1 W_n (y), \qquad \|A_2 (y)\| \leqslant d_2 W_n (y),\)

with \(d_1, d_2 \geqslant 0\), and suppose \(A_1 (x + z) = A_2 (x + z)\) whenever \(|z|_1 < b\). Then

\(\displaystyle \left| \sum_z q (z) (A_1 (x + z) - A_2 (x + z)) \right| \leqslant e^{- \sigma b / 2} (d_1 + d_2) C_n (\sigma / 2) CW_n (x) . \) (18.67)

Proof. The difference vanishes for \(|z|_1 < b\), so the triangle inequality and submultiplicativity of \(W_n\) give an upper bound

\(\displaystyle (d_1 + d_2) W_n (x) \sum_{|z|_1 \geqslant b} |q (z) | W_n (z) .\)

Using (18.63) with \(\sigma / 2\), each summand is at most

\(\displaystyle C_n (\sigma / 2) e^{\sigma |z|_1 / 2} |q (z) | \leqslant C_n (\sigma / 2) e^{- \sigma b / 2} e^{\sigma |z|_1} |q (z) | .\)

Summation and (18.64) prove (18.67).\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.20. (Multilinear jet comparison) Here is the passage from that tail estimate to a local coefficient measurement. Let \(P\) be a linear screen onto the allowed edge directions; it is applied only to the direction arrays below. Let \(F\) be a \(C^N\) real-valued local observable. Here \(\text{inc}_1 (U)\) is the configuration obtained by exponentiating the tangent field \(U\) from the identity. Write \(\mathcal{J}_a = D^a (F \circ \text{inc}_1) (0)\) for its symmetric \(a\)-linear derivative at the identity configuration. Assume the following three estimates for \(a \leqslant N\):

  1. for all admissible screened directions \(v_1, \ldots, v_a\), and all nonnegative \(\lambda_i\) with \(\|v_i \| \leqslant \lambda_i\),

    \(\displaystyle |\mathcal{J}_a (v_1, \ldots, v_a) | \leqslant \Phi w \prod_{i = 1}^a \lambda_i ; \) (18.68)

  2. the already-screened reference directions \(A_i = P (\theta y_i)\) satisfy \(\|A_i \| \leqslant K\), with \(K \geqslant 1\), and the screened reference jet vanishes:

    \(\displaystyle \mathcal{J}_a (P (\theta y_1), \ldots, P (\theta y_a)) = 0 ; \) (18.69)

  3. directions \(x_i\) approximate them with \(\|P (\theta x_i) - P (\theta y_i)\| \leqslant \varepsilon\), where \(0 \leqslant \varepsilon \leqslant K\).

Then

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{J}_a (P (\theta x_1), \ldots, P (\theta x_a)) | \leqslant 2^a \varepsilon K^a \Phi w. \) (18.70)

Proof. Set \(U_i = P (\theta x_i)\), \(A_i = P (\theta y_i)\), and \(E_i = U_i - A_i\). By multilinearity,

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{J}_a (U_1, \ldots, U_a) -\mathcal{J}_a (A_1, \ldots, A_a) = \sum_{\varnothing \ne S \subseteq \{1, \ldots, a\}} \mathcal{J}_a (v_1^S, \ldots, v_a^S),\)

where \(v_i^S = E_i\) if \(i \in S\), and \(v_i^S = A_i\) otherwise. The empty-set term is zero by (18.69). In every remaining term at least one factor has norm at most \(\varepsilon\); all other factors have norm at most \(K\), by the stated bounds on the screened reference directions. Applying (18.68) to each term bounds it by \(\varepsilon K^{a - 1} \Phi w \leqslant \varepsilon K^a \Phi w\). There are \(2^a - 1 \leqslant 2^a\) such terms, proving (8).\(\Box\)

Proposition 18.21. (Local probe bound from a strip and a jet gap) Fix natural numbers \(d, N\) with \(d \leqslant N\), a support radius \(b\), a chart radius \(m\), and \(\sigma > 0\), and let \(L \geqslant 1\) be the integer block side. For each \(k \leqslant d\), suppose the four-by-four row kernel \((q_{\nu j})_{\nu, j = 1}^4\) has entries satisfying (18.64) with constant \(M\). Suppose the two arrays \(A^{(0)}_j, A^{(1)}_j\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \|A^{(h)}_j (y)\| \leqslant KW_{dk} (y) \quad (h = 0, 1), \qquad A^{(0)}_j (u + z) = A^{(1)}_j (u + z) \text{if } |z|_1 < b, \) (18.71)

where \(K \geqslant 1\), \(M \geqslant 0\), and each kernel base \(u\) has \(|u|_1 \leqslant 4 m\). Define the order-\(k\) reference and actual directions \(y_k\) and \(x_k\) by applying this matrix row to \(A^{(0)}\) and \(A^{(1)}\), respectively. The screen \(P\) is coordinate restriction to allowed edges. Let \(\theta > 0\) be the threshold and \(\omega (e) \geqslant 1\) the spatial weight of the budget; the norm in (18.68) is the normalized edge norm

\(\displaystyle \|v\| = \sup_e \frac{\|v (e)\|}{\theta \hspace{0.17em} \omega (e)}, \qquad \text{so that} \qquad \|P (\theta v)\| \leqslant \sup_{e \text{allowed}} \|v (e)\|\)

for every direction array \(v\). Thus an entrywise bound for an unscaled direction on the allowed edges is a bound for the normalized norm of its threshold-scaled screen, and no additional screen norm is inserted between the row estimate and the priced jet. Then the entrywise difference of \(x_k\) and \(y_k\) is at most

\(\displaystyle \delta_k = 8 Ke^{- \sigma b / 2} (1 + 4 m)^{dk} C_{dk} (\sigma / 2) M \) (18.72)

and hence so is the normalized norm of \(P (\theta x_k) - P (\theta y_k)\). The reference and actual directions satisfy, entrywise,

\(\displaystyle \|y_k \|, \|x_k \| \leqslant 4 KC_{dk} (\sigma) M (1 + 4 L (m + 2))^{dk} \leqslant C_1,\)

where

\(\displaystyle C_1 = 1 + \sum_{k = 0}^d 4 KC_{dk} (\sigma) M (1 + 4 L (m + 2))^{dk}, \) (18.73)

Assume the local jet satisfies the estimate (18.68), that \(F\) depends only on the allowed-edge components of the configuration, so that \(\mathcal{J}_a (v_1, \ldots, v_a) =\mathcal{J}_a (Pv_1, \ldots, Pv_a)\), and also that \(\varepsilon_d \leqslant C_1\). Assume the weighted vanishing receipt for the screened, threshold-scaled reference directions:

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{J}_a (P (\theta y_{q_1}), \ldots, P (\theta y_{q_a})) = 0 \quad \text{whenever } q_i \geqslant 1, \quad \sum_{i = 1}^a q_i \leqslant d. \) (18.74)

In the application, (18.74) holds because each \(P (\theta y_k)\) lies in the space \(\mathcal{V}_k\) of polynomial directions of degree at most \(k - 1\) on the allowed edges, and the observable has vanishing jets on lists of such directions with positive degrees of sum at most \(d\). No such membership is assumed for the actual directions \(x_k\); it is neither needed below nor available in the application. Assume also the order-zero receipt \(F (\text{id}) = 0\). Let \(\gamma : \mathbb{R} \to \mathcal{X}\) be the actual \(C^d\) probe path in the tangent configuration space, with \(\gamma (0) = 0\) and \(\gamma^{(k)} (0) = \theta x_k\) for \(1 \leqslant k \leqslant d\). The observable is evaluated on \(\text{inc}_1 (\gamma (s))\), so its base point is the identity configuration. Then the probe of total derivative order \(\ell \leqslant d\) obeys

\(\displaystyle \left| \left. \frac{d^{\ell}}{ds^{\ell}} F (\text{inc}_1 (\gamma (s))) \right|_{s = 0} \right| \leqslant | \text{Part}^{\text{ord}} (\ell) | \hspace{0.17em} 2^{\ell} \varepsilon_d C_1^{\ell} \Phi w, \qquad \varepsilon_d = \sum_{k = 0}^d \delta_k . \) (18.75)

Proof. For each of the four entries in an output row, (18.67) with \(d_1 = d_2 = K\) bounds the tail by

\(\displaystyle 2 Ke^{- \sigma b / 2} C_{dk} (\sigma / 2) MW_{dk} (u) .\)

There are four input components, so the row difference is at most \(8 Ke^{- \sigma b / 2} C_{dk} (\sigma / 2) MW_{dk} (u)\). Since \(|u|_1 \leqslant 4 m\), this is (9). Summing over \(k \leqslant d\) gives a common error bound \(\varepsilon_d\). For either direction, (18.66) and the growth bound on its input array give \(4 KC_{dk} (\sigma) MW_{dk} (u)\). In the chart the lifted base index has size at most \(4 L (m + 2)\), giving the order-\(k\) bound above; the extra \(1\) ensures \(C_1 \geqslant 1\).

Here \(\text{Part}^{\text{ord}} (\ell)\) is the finite index set of ordered finite partitions of the \(\ell\) differentiation slots into nonempty blocks, in the canonical ordering used by the chain rule; it is not a sum over arbitrary permutations of each set partition. For (18.75), use the repeated chain rule in that ordered-finpartition form. When \(\ell = 0\), the derivative is \(F (\text{id}) = 0\) by the separate order-zero receipt. For each nonempty-order partition \(\pi\) of the \(\ell\) probe derivatives, the chain-rule term is the \(| \pi |\)-linear jet \(\mathcal{J}_{| \pi |}\) applied to the path derivative \(\theta x_{|B|}\) for each block \(B \in \pi\), and by the locality assumption it is unchanged when each \(\theta x_{|B|}\) is replaced by \(P (\theta x_{|B|})\). The all-reference term, with \(P (\theta x_{|B|})\) replaced by \(P (\theta y_{|B|})\), vanishes: the block degrees are positive and sum to \(\ell \leqslant d\), so the weighted vanishing hypothesis applies. In the normalized norm, each screened reference direction \(P (\theta y_{|B|})\) is bounded by \(C_1\), and each \(P (\theta x_{|B|})\) differs from it by at most \(\delta_{|B|} \leqslant \varepsilon_d\). Since \(\varepsilon_d \leqslant C_1\), the multilinear comparison lemma (18.70), with \(K = C_1\), bounds this chain-rule term by \(2^{| \pi |} \varepsilon_d C_1^{| \pi |} \Phi w\), which is at most \(2^{\ell} \varepsilon_d C_1^{\ell} \Phi w\). Summing over the finite ordered finpartition index set proves (18.75).\(\Box\)

The arrays in the selected construction.
In the selected construction the probe is the composition of the local observable with a routed test path: the logarithmic-link test curve of the coefficient probe is cropped to the chart of radius \(m\) and passed through the first-order row operator of the step. This routed path is \(\theta\) times a real-analytic path \(R (s)\) of direction arrays with \(R (0) = 0\), and the actual direction is \(x_k = R^{(k)} (0)\). The two input arrays, before the row operator is applied, are the following. The reference array \(A^{(0)} = W_k\) is the \(k\)-th derivative at \(s = 0\) of the test script, an array on the covering lattice; the coefficient-growth receipt is \(\|W_k (y)\| \leqslant KW_{dk} (y)\). The actual array is its patched periodic lift
\(\displaystyle A^{(1)} (z) = V_k (z) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} W_k (\text{lift} (\text{wrap} \hspace{0.17em} z)), & \text{sep} (0, \text{wrap} \hspace{0.17em} z) \leqslant m,\\ 0, & \text{otherwise}, \end{array} \right.\)

where \(\text{wrap}\) is the projection of the covering lattice onto the periodic coarse lattice and \(\text{lift}\) its centred section; it satisfies the same growth bound. The placement hypotheses stated below imply \(V_k (u + z) = W_k (u + z)\) for \(|z|_1 < b\) at every kernel base \(u\) that occurs, with \(|u|_1 \leqslant 4 m\). Applying the same row operator to \(W_k\) and \(V_k\) gives the reference direction \(y_k\) and the actual direction \(x_k\). The input arrays agree on the ball of radius \(b\); the two convolved directions need not agree anywhere. The reference direction is, on the allowed edges, a polynomial in the spatial coordinates of degree at most \(k - 1\) when \(k \geqslant 1\); this polynomial property is established for the reference direction only, and is the membership \(P (\theta y_k) \in \mathcal{V}_k\) used for (18.74). The strip bound supplies (18.64), while the coefficient-growth receipt supplies \(KW_{dk}\). Thus the difference is not estimated term by term without cancellation: all terms inside the agreement ball cancel, and only the exponentially weighted exterior tail is paid.

The hypotheses under which Proposition 18.21 is applied in this way are the following; none of them is proved in this section.

  1. The row operator applied to both arrays is literally the first-order row of the actual step. It satisfies the strip bound (18.64) with constants \(\sigma > 0\), \(M \geqslant 0\); it is identical to the row of the selected kernel, and the patch block side of the step equals that kernel's block side \(L\). A strip bound for some other kernel does not suffice.

  2. Placement: with \(c\) the side of the coarse periodic lattice, \(20 (m + b + 1) < c\); the support \(X\) of the observable and the chart region \(Y\) satisfy that the radius-\(b\) halo of the parent of every site of \(X\) is contained in \(Y\), and every site of \(Y\) has separation at most \(m\) from the origin.

  3. The observable is regular on \(X\) for the budget, with all ordered derivatives of order at most the budget's order \(n_B\) along normalized directions bounded by \(w\) on healthy configurations; this yields (6) with \(\Phi\) the fixed jet bound of order \(N\), where \(d \leqslant N \leqslant n_B\).

  4. The observable has the polynomial-jet vanishing property of order \(d\) on \(X\): its jets at the identity vanish on every list of directions whose screens lie in \(\mathcal{V}_{q_1}, \ldots, \mathcal{V}_{q_a}\) with \(q_i \geqslant 1\) and \(\sum_i q_i \leqslant d\). This includes \(F (\text{id}) = 0\) as the case of the empty list.

  5. The growth receipt \(\|W_k (y)\| \leqslant KW_{dk} (y)\) holds for every \(k \leqslant d\) with one \(K \geqslant 1\), the two integer parameters \((p, u)\) of the selected kernel satisfy \(p \geqslant d\) and \(u \geqslant 4\), its real parameter is positive, and \(\varepsilon_d \leqslant C_1\).

Under these hypotheses the routed-path identity gives the path \(\gamma = \theta R\), the tail lemma gives the entrywise error \(\delta_k\) on the allowed edges, the row bound (18.66) gives \(\|y_k \| \leqslant C_1\) on those edges, and the polynomial property of \(y_k\) gives (18.74); the proposition then yields (18.75) with \(L\) the common block side.

The argument above proves the Fourier-tail and multilinear comparison once the three receipts (strip, growth, and vanishing priced jet) are available. It does not prove those upstream receipts, nor does it by itself prove the source-placement or healthy-chart hypotheses in the small-support branch. Those are separate construction inputs.

18.10A paid estimate for one regular source

We spell out how a single regular source term is paid in the selected one-block transition. The source is split into its real and imaginary channels. When the transported support fits inside the receiving budget radius, local coefficient probes control the inserted coefficient. When it does not fit, a direct supremum estimate controls it. The two cases use different fields of the local budget, which is why both the regular and large-support inequalities were selected in Lemma 18.10. The principal insertion attenuation needed in the local estimate is established in Subsection 10.7; the argument below keeps the regular source and both support regimes explicit.

Fix a one-step transition of block length \(L\) between the adjacent rungs \(m\) and \(m + 1\) of the selected history, at fixed volume and period indices, and a coefficient frame of maximum grade \(d\). Both ends of the transition are the effective representations of that one history at these indices; the source, its mask, the cloud estimate and the resulting coefficient all refer to this same pair. Let \(f\) be a complex function of the fine (rung \(m + 1\)) configuration and \(b\) a fine support such that:

  1. \(f\) is regular on \(b\) for the fine budget, that is, local on \(b\), gauge invariant, measurable, and smooth to the order of the budget on configurations healthy on \(b\);

  2. the origin belongs to \(b\), and \(f (\text{id}) = 0\);

  3. if \(b\) is nonwrapping about the origin, then for every local jet the function \(s \mapsto f\) along the corresponding logarithmic-link test curve at the origin is \(C^d\) at \(s = 0\) and all its derivatives of order at most \(d\) vanish there;

  4. there is \(c \geqslant 0\) such that, on every configuration healthy on \(b\), every ordered derivative of \(f\) of order at most the order of the fine budget, along directions satisfying the fine budget's directional bound, has absolute value at most \(c\).

Condition (4) with the empty list of directions is the pointwise bound \(|f| \leqslant c\) on healthy configurations; the full derivative bound, and conditions (1)–(3), are needed for the local estimates below, which do not follow from a value bound. The source term itself is the product of the fine effective density with the mask of \(f\) on \(b\) at the fine threshold. Let \(\alpha_f > 0\) be the fine support exponent and \(q_f \geqslant 0\) the fine cover number of \(b\), and define the charged mass

\(\displaystyle m_0 = e^{\alpha_f q_f} c.\)

Let \(\hat{\beta}_m > 0\) be the hatted coupling of the history at the coarse rung \(m\) (not the actual coupling of the finite-volume representation), let \(\mathcal{M}_f (G, L, \hat{\beta}_m) > 0\) be the fine monitor, and put \(\chi = \sqrt{\mathcal{M}_f (G, L, \hat{\beta}_m)}\). Thus a raw budget \(b_0\) gives the actual channel bound \(b_0 / \chi\). Set \(a = L^d\). Let \(O, H, W \geqslant 0\) be respectively the motion-average norm, local readout, and local test factors. Let raw budgets \(u, v, \xi > 0\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} O (Wu + 2 Hav) & \leqslant \xi, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.76)} \\ 4 OHav & \leqslant \xi . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.77)} \end{array}\)

These are the regular and large-support paid budgets. In the Wilson application, \(m_0\) is the mass of the source support with its sup-bound; \(u, v\) are chosen by Subsection 18.5.

The local estimates needed from the one-step construction have the following precise form. The real and imaginary channels have transported cloud charges \(C_1, C_2\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant C_1, C_2 \leqslant \frac{v}{\chi} m_0 . \) (18.78)

In the application below both charges are the single cloud response of the two-channel source, so \(C_1 = C_2\), and (18.78) is the cloud entry of the selected local record at bound \(v\); it is a separate input, not a consequence of the principal reserved-mass estimate of Subsection 10.7. If the transported support is at most the receiving budget radius, the local probe reconstruction error, after both channels are combined, is at most

\(\displaystyle O (Wu \chi^{- 1} m_0 + Ha (C_1 + C_2)) . \) (18.79)

If the transported support exceeds that radius, each channel admits a uniform chart bound \(S_i \leqslant \frac{v}{\chi} m_0\) for \(i = 1, 2\); the resulting two-channel coefficient error is at most

\(\displaystyle OHa (S_1 + S_2 + C_1 + C_2) . \) (18.80)

Here the coefficient error is measured uniformly over the finite set of lattice motions, and \(O\) pays for this uniformity. In either case the local construction also supplies the following certificate, in which \(\rho = L^{- 2 / 5}\) is the one-step remainder rate. There is one coarse coefficient \(X\), chosen before any motion, such that for every motion \(\omega\) there is a coarse presentation \(P_{\omega}\) with coefficient \(\omega X\) which has the order-zero format of the coarse representation, has remainder cost at most \(\rho m_0\) at the normalization \(\sqrt{\text{forcedAllowance}_m}\), and has the exact pushed density

\(\displaystyle D (P_{\omega}) = \omega \cdot \left[ L^d \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{T}_m \left( \varrho_{m + 1} \hspace{0.17em} \text{mask}_b \hspace{0.17em} f \right) \right], \) (18.81)

where \(\varrho_{m + 1}\) is the fine effective density, \(\text{mask}_b \hspace{0.17em} f\) the mask of \(f\) on \(b\) at the fine threshold, \(\mathcal{T}_m\) the one-step push at the coupling of rung \(m + 1\), and \(\omega \cdot\) the action of the motion on functions. The presentation may depend on the motion; the coefficient \(X\) does not. The estimates (18.79) and (18.80) are bounds for \(\sup_{\omega} \| \omega X\|\), the predicted leading coefficient of a regular source being zero. These are the actual local chart and transport conclusions used below, not consequences of the scalar budget inequalities alone.

Proposition 18.22. (Regular source atom) Assume the local estimates (18.78)– (18.80) and the coarse-format, density, and remainder conclusions just stated. If (18.76)– (18.77) hold, then the source term produces one coarse coefficient \(X\) with \(\| \omega X\| \leqslant \xi \chi^{- 1} m_0\) for every motion \(\omega\), together with presentations \(P_{\omega}\) of \(\omega X\) in the coarse order-zero format having the exact pushed density (18.81) and remainder at most \(\rho m_0\). In the terminology of the finite bound recurrence, this is the regular-source estimate with cost pair \((\xi \chi^{- 1} m_0, \rho m_0)\).

Proof. First suppose the transported support is within the receiving radius. By (18.78),

\(\displaystyle O (Wu \chi^{- 1} m_0 + Ha (C_1 + C_2)) \leqslant O (Wu + 2 Hav) \chi^{- 1} m_0 \leqslant \xi \chi^{- 1} m_0,\)

where the last inequality is (18.76). The local probe reconstruction estimate (18.79) therefore gives the claimed coefficient error.

Now suppose the transported support exceeds the receiving radius. The uniform chart estimate and the two channel bounds give

\(\displaystyle OHa (S_1 + S_2 + C_1 + C_2) \leqslant OHa (4 v \chi^{- 1} m_0) = 4 OHav \chi^{- 1} m_0 \leqslant \xi \chi^{- 1} m_0,\)

by (18.77). Thus the large-support estimate gives the same coefficient error. The two cases exhaust the support sizes. In both cases, the local construction supplies the exact pushed-density identity, coarse format, and remainder bound \(\rho m_0\); these components are unchanged by the numerical comparison. This proves the assertion.\(\Box\)

We now describe how the local hypotheses are realized in the selected construction. The source term is turned into its real and imaginary masked channels. Both channels carry the same root label, the logarithmic key of the regular activity with support \(b\). Their two reserved-mass bounds are combined at the single expansion index \(i_0\) attached to that label, where the assigned cost is \(L^d (c_{\text{re}} + c_{\text{im}})\) with \(c_{\text{re}}, c_{\text{im}}\) the derivative bounds of the two transported channels; every other expansion index is assigned cost zero. The per-channel reserved-mass estimate used for this format and remainder certificate is provided by the regular insertion result of Subsection 10.7; it does not supply the cloud bound (18.78) or the large-support chart bound below, which use two further entries of the selected local record. In the small-support regime, the finite local probes fit inside the chart, and the local reconstruction bound is the test-bound term \(Wu \chi^{- 1} m_0\) plus the two readout terms \(HaC_1\) and \(HaC_2\). In the large-support regime, the direct chart supremum bound replaces the probes, leaving two chart and two cloud readout contributions; this is exactly the factor four in (18.77). The cloud inequality controls each \(C_i\), and the insertion construction carries the same source through the block with its density identity. The finite bound recurrence then sums these single-source costs over the countable regular family using its absolute mass budget.

More explicitly, let \(q_{\tau}\) be the coefficient in Subsection 18.5. In the small-support case, the chart estimate gives, for each recovery probe \(\tau\), a contribution at most \(q_{\tau} v \chi^{- 1} m_0\). The cycle budget gives \(q_{\tau} v \leqslant u\); finite coefficient recovery therefore bounds the probe part by \(Wu \chi^{- 1} m_0\). The two real/imaginary cloud contributions are bounded by \(Ha (C_1 + C_2)\), and the regular budget in (18.76) pays their sum. The chart construction has enough room that the support is nonwrapping and every probe stencil lies in the healthy region; the ordered derivative bounds through order \(d\) are exactly the ones used by the finite recovery map. This is the local probe conclusion of the small-support construction, not an inference from the value bound alone.

For the large-support case, the local field is built by its support reservation. The reservation includes the factor \(\exp ((\alpha_c + 2) q_i) \geqslant 1\), where \(\alpha_c > 0\) is the receiving support exponent and \(q_i \geqslant 0\) the channel's coarse cover number, so its unweighted supremum cost is no larger than its reserved mass. The construction bounds that reserved mass using the one-site talent estimate times the source mass. That estimate is at most \(v / \chi\), hence each channel has \(S_i \leqslant (v / \chi) m_0\). The cloud bounds likewise give \(C_i \leqslant (v / \chi) m_0\). Applying the local readout bound to the two chart bounds and the two cloud terms yields \(OHa (S_1 + S_2 + C_1 + C_2) \leqslant 4 OHavm_0 / \chi\). The large-support budget pays this quantity. In both cases the chosen support labels retain the same fine source and the transition preserves its density, while the one-site reservation and turn-bound inequalities give the remainder \(\rho m_0\). The local chart statements used in these paragraphs are proved for the same insertion in Subsubsection 18.10.1 below, by the actual/reference comparison and the weighted one-site expansion, respectively.

In the small-support construction, the image of the source support is first enlarged by the transport halo. The geometric placement puts this enlarged set in a parent patch and bounds every point of that patch's support by the chart radius. The condition

\(\displaystyle 20 (r_d + R_d + 1) < n_0\)

then makes both the transported source support and the parent patch nonwrapping; \(r_d\) is the chart radius, \(R_d\) the transport reach, and \(n_0\) the parent side length. The local construction gives the actual field \(U_i\) and its smooth reference \(V_i\) on one healthy chart. For each channel weight \(w_i\), and for every ordered list \((A_1, \ldots, A_{\ell})\) of at most \(d\) directions each satisfying the directional bound of the receiving budget at the origin (entrywise at most the receiving threshold times the spatial weight), the ordered derivatives at the identity satisfy

\(\displaystyle | \partial_{A_1} \cdots \partial_{A_{\ell}} (U_i - V_i) (\text{id}) | \leqslant 2 (v / \chi) w_i .\)

This is a bound on these normalized directions only, not an operator-norm bound for the derivative. For every coefficient-recovery probe \(\tau \in \mathcal{T}\), evaluated at parameter zero along its test path, one also has

\(\displaystyle | \partial^{\tau}_0 V_i | \leqslant (v / \chi) w_i .\)

The probe stencil lies in the healthy chart. The first bound controls the local variation correction in the reconstruction, and the second is the probe-bound input. The finite recovery map and its probe-bound sum therefore give the \(Wu \chi^{- 1} m_0\) contribution; applying the same row decomposition to the two real channels gives the separate \(Ha (C_1 + C_2)\) contribution. This supplies the stated small-support estimate with the correct one-block normalization.

In the large-support construction, the transported cover number is larger than the receiving radius. For each real channel, the support-reservation argument constructs a regular field with supremum bound \(S_i\) and a reserved mass

\(\displaystyle R_i = e^{(\alpha_c + 2) q_i} S_i, \qquad R_i \leqslant (v / \chi) m_0,\)

where \(\alpha_c > 0\) is the receiving support exponent and \(q_i \geqslant 0\) is the channel's coarse cover number. The second inequality combines the support-reservation estimate, which bounds \(R_i\) by the tuition of the selected local record times \(m_0\), with the tuition entry of that record, which is at most \(v / \chi\). The exponential factor is at least one, so \(S_i \leqslant R_i \leqslant (v / \chi) m_0\). Independently, the cloud estimate gives \(C_i \leqslant (v / \chi) m_0\). Thus the two chart bounds and two cloud charges together cost at most

\(\displaystyle OHa (S_1 + S_2 + C_1 + C_2) \leqslant 4 OHav \chi^{- 1} m_0,\)

which is paid by the large-support inequality. This is the large-support estimate used in the proposition; it keeps the chart and cloud contributions separate because they come from different source bounds.

18.10.1Construction of the two local estimates

We give the chart argument on the actual insertion, rather than assuming its two conclusions. Fix one real channel \(f_i\), of weight \(w_i\), of the masked source. Use the parent support and the forest/tag variables of the one-block expansion of this insertion. Define \(U_i\) to be its distinguished local row in that expansion. It is the forest/tag average of the normalized actual-sector insertion integral. Define \(G_i\) by replacing that integral with the Gaussian-reference integral, with the same masked ingredient and read set. Finally let \(V_i\) be the source evaluated on the deterministic reference field, after cropping the coarse logarithmic field to radius \(r_d\). All three functions use the same fine source \(f_i\); only the local field on which it is evaluated changes. The cutoff defining the first two integrals is compactly supported in the finite coordinate chart. Differentiation under the integral and the finite or probability averages is justified by its derivative majorants. Locality, gauge covariance of the chart, and regularity of \(f_i\) show that \(U_i\) is regular on its parent support. The reference \(V_i\) is \(C^d\) near the identity by composition.

Suppose first that the cover of the transported support is at most the receiving radius. Its transport halo lies in the parent support; the selected cover placement bounds the latter by radius \(r_d\). For a direction \(A\) replace it by \(A^{\text{loc}}\), equal to \(A\) on that radius and zero outside. Every ordered derivative of \(U_i\) and \(V_i\) at the identity is unchanged by this replacement, by locality, including derivatives of order zero. Moreover \(A^{\text{loc}}\) still satisfies the receiving directional bound and obeys the uniform canopy bound \(\|A^{\text{loc}} \| \leqslant 2 \theta_f (1 + r_d)^{p_c}\). The actual/reference comparison in Subsubsection 18.8.1, at its selected bound, therefore gives

\(\displaystyle | \partial_{A_1} \cdots \partial_{A_{\ell}} (U_i - G_i) (\text{id}) | \leqslant (v / \chi) w_i, \qquad \ell \leqslant d.\)

The forest and tag averages preserve this bound because their weights are nonnegative and sum to one.

Here is the additional comparison from \(G_i\) to \(V_i\). Write \(z\) for the Gaussian coordinates and \(p (z) \geqslant 1\) for their polynomial size envelope. The deterministic crop and fluctuation estimates give a discrepancy \(Jp (z)^2\) in the logarithmic arguments, with \(0 \leqslant J \leqslant 1\). The same quantity bounds the cutoff error; the cutoff is one on the protected read set. Apply the fundamental theorem of calculus between the two arguments, then differentiate in the normalized coarse directions. The finite product rule and chain rule, through order \(\ell \leqslant d < n\), bound the resulting integrand difference by

\(\displaystyle w_i C_n J \hspace{0.17em} p (z)^{4 (n + 1)} .\)

Indeed each differentiated argument contributes at most a constant times \(p (z)^2\); the first difference contributes \(Jp (z)^2\), and the at most \(n\) differentiated factors and their finite partitions are absorbed in \(C_n p (z)^{4 (n + 1)}\). The source derivative bounds through the purchased order supply the factor \(w_i\). Terms differentiating the cutoff obey the same estimate, with its protected-ball discrepancy included in \(J\). The Gaussian moment bound is finite and uniform in the finite-volume choices; denote it by \(M_{4 (n + 1)}\). Here \(J\) is the full response-plus-paint cost \(F (x)\) of (18.37); in particular it includes the deterministic crop term estimated in (18.45). The response cap alone omits that term and would not pay this comparison. The support placement above gives exactly the support and threshold conditions (18.36), with the fine budget as \(B\) and the receiving budget as \(P\). Apply Proposition 18.13 at the same order \(n\), block \(L\), and target \(y = v\). Its clock is linear in the insertion weight, with coefficient \(C_n\). Its conclusion (18.40) gives simultaneously

\(\displaystyle J \leqslant 1, \qquad C_n JM_{4 (n + 1)} \leqslant v / \chi,\)

uniformly on these supports and normalized directions. The threshold for this full cap is included in the selected local record before the volume, period, forest and tags are chosen. Integrating the displayed majorant proves

\(\displaystyle | \partial_{A_1} \cdots \partial_{A_{\ell}} (G_i - V_i) (\text{id}) | \leqslant (v / \chi) w_i .\)

Thus the triangle inequality gives exactly the \(2 (v / \chi) w_i\) normalized directional derivative bound stated above. This factor two combines two comparisons; it does not enlarge the cloud bound.

The support is nonwrapping because \(20 (r_d + R_d + 1) < n_0\). The fine support is also nonwrapping: its distance from its anchor is at most \(L (r_d + 2)\), while its period is \(Ln_0\). Consequently condition (3) on \(f_i\) applies. On a coefficient test path, the deterministic reference field has the screened polynomial jet plus the cropped Fourier-row tail. The all-polynomial term has zero reading by condition (3). Expand each remaining multilinear term by replacing one or more of its arguments by the row tail. The weighted convolution and jet-gap estimate of Proposition 18.21 bounds the finite sum by \((v / \chi) w_i\) at each selected probe. This uses the actual row of the selected history: the reference field was defined using that row, so no new kernel or test direction is substituted here. Finite coefficient recovery now gives the small-support estimate (18.79), with the already displayed cycle bounds \(q_{\tau} v \leqslant u\).

For the large-support estimate no polynomial test or nonwrapping assumption is needed. Let \(q\) be the receiving cover number, \(n\) its order, \(p\) its directional power, and \(\alpha_c\) its support exponent. The same actual expansion assigns its distinguished row an absolute coefficient \(o \geqslant 0\) and an insertion charge \(d_i \geqslant 0\). Its weighted one-site sum, restricted to this single row, gives

\(\displaystyle o \hspace{0.17em} e^{(a + e) q} \leqslant 1, \qquad a \geqslant \alpha_c + 3, \quad e \geqslant 0, \qquad d_i \leqslant \text{goal}^{- 1} \hspace{0.17em} m_0 .\)

The last inequality retains the fine source mass; the additional fine-support attenuation factor is at most one. Differentiating this row in the receiving normalized directions costs at most

\(\displaystyle S_i \leqslant d_i o \hspace{0.17em} A (x)^{np} (1 + q)^{np}, \qquad A (x) = 4 (2 b (x) + 1) .\)

These statements follow by taking one nonnegative term of the absolute one-site expansion bound; thus they apply to its actual distinguished row, rather than to a separately chosen surrogate. Gauge invariance and regularity of that row follow from the same insertion integral as in the small-support case.

The elementary polynomial estimate \((1 + q)^{np} \leqslant P_{1 / 2} (np) e^{q / 2}\) gives, since \(q \geqslant b (x)\),

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} e^{(\alpha_c + 2) q} S_i & \leqslant d_i A (x)^{np} P_{1 / 2} (np) oe^{(\alpha_c + 5 / 2) q}\\ & \leqslant d_i A (x)^{np} P_{1 / 2} (np) e^{- b (x) / 2}\\ & \leqslant \mathcal{T}_Q (x ; n, p) m_0 \leqslant (v / \chi) m_0 . \end{array}\)

The middle step uses the full reserved exponent \(\alpha_c + 2\) and the spare unit in \(a \geqslant \alpha_c + 3\); it would fail if that reserve were silently dropped. The last step is Lemma 18.16. Since the exponential reserve is at least one, this proves the required bound on \(S_i\) itself. The cloud charge is paid separately by Lemma 18.14. Applying coefficient readout to these two contributions in each real channel proves (18.80).

Finally all rows were obtained from the same masked insertion expansion. Recombining its rows therefore gives the exact one-step pushed density. The two real channels carry the same source key; their derivative bounds are combined at that single key. The principal reserved-mass bound of Subsection 10.7, followed by the selected turn bound, pays \(L^d (c_{\text{re}} + c_{\text{im}})\) there and zero at the other keys, with total remainder at most \(\rho m_0\). Motion acts on this constructed coefficient and on its presentation; it does not require a new coefficient choice. This proves the format and density certificate as well as both local estimates used in the proposition.

18.11Atomic responses and the finite-volume root bound

This section records the actual transfer used by the public trace supplier. There are two distinct operations. An atomic response first becomes a finite-volume response with a root-estimate. The estimate is then converted into the two components of a finite-volume response record. We give the conversions and the final finite-volume bounds, while keeping the upstream response, paid-bound, and bound-duality receipts explicit.

18.11.1Preparing the atomic response for transfer

Let \(X\) be an order-zero coefficient at scale \(k\), \(g\) its local field, and \(Z = \hat{\beta}_k\). Write \(x_X\) for its coordinate, \(X^{\text{f}}\) for its forced part, and \(D_X\) for its density. The atomic certificate supplies a nonnegative remainder allowance \(s\), a response \(L\) with attainment cost at most \(e\), and the identities

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \|x_X \|_{\text{coeff}} + s & \leqslant q, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.82)} \\ \text{profile} (X^{\text{f}}) & = \text{hook} (L), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.83)}\\ D_X (U) & = \text{eval} (U) \hspace{0.17em} g (U) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.84)} \end{array}\)

The remainder bound is measured at scale \(k\), at order zero and with tolerance \(\sqrt{\text{forcedAllowance}_k}\). The statement that the response \(L = (L_Y)_Y\), indexed by supports \(Y\), has attainment cost at most \(e\) is a certificate, not a scalar equality: every component \(L_Y\) is measurable and gauge invariant, vanishes at every configuration that is healthy on \(Y\) at the threshold of the scale-\(k\) budget, and takes the same value at two configurations whose links on \(Y\) agree and whose threshold flags in \(Y\) agree; \(L_Y \neq 0\) only if \(Y\) contains the marked origin, and there are numbers \(a_Y \geqslant 0\) with

\(\displaystyle |L_Y (U) | \leqslant a_Y \text{ for all } U, \qquad \sum_Y e^{(\alpha_k + 2) \text{cov}_k (Y)} a_Y \leqslant e,\)

where \(\alpha_k\) is the support exponent and \(\text{cov}_k\) the cover number of that budget. Set \(u = \text{invDil}_{k, d}\), the inverse field dilation, and \(G' = ug\). Multiplying the density identity by \(u\) gives

\(\displaystyle uD_X (U) = \text{eval} (U) G' (U) .\)

Thus the atomic response supplies the input to this transfer, with unchanged format, remainder and forced-profile data.

The selected atomic charge budget satisfies

\(\displaystyle 4 (1 + c_k) q \leqslant Z^3, \) (18.85)

where \(c_k \geqslant 1\) is the selected column allowance of the history at scale \(k\), a real parameter of the stage data and not a cardinality. Hence

\(\displaystyle 4 (1 + c_k) (\|x_X \|_{\text{coeff}} + s) \leqslant Z^3 .\)

The second needed comparison is \(Z \text{gradeDecay}_d (k) \leqslant Z^2\), using \(\text{gradeDecay}_d (k) \leqslant Z\) and \(Z \geqslant 1\). The bound construction chooses the atomic charge allowance so both comparisons hold.

The endpoint bound-duality estimate used below has the following exact meaning. A response duet \(P\) runs from a level \(\ell\) to \(k \leqslant N\) in the same selected history. At each intermediate level its coefficient has admissible order-zero format and remainder bound; its marked site is the spatial origin and these sites are related by the one-step scale map. The coefficient and cost changes satisfy the paid direction inequality, and the densities satisfy the exact one-step transfer identity. Its left endpoint coefficient vector is zero, and its terminal cost obeys the spread estimate

\(\displaystyle \text{cost}_k \leqslant w + c_k (w +\|P_k .x\|_{\text{coeff}}), \qquad w \geqslant 0.\)

Write \(\kappa_k > 0\) for the square root of the level-\(k\) monitor. The endpoint estimate, which is required for all sufficiently large period indices, uniformly in the volume index \(N \geqslant k\), in the paid comparison and in \(w \geqslant 0\), is

\(\displaystyle \kappa_k \hspace{0.17em} \|P_k .x\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant 4 (1 + c_k) w. \) (18.86)

The zero in the left-end condition is a coefficient vector; the marked sites are at the spatial origin, not zero-valued observations. The finite recurrence and adjacent-level estimate are given in Subsection 18.4. The paid-cone estimate is supplied by the canonical argument in Subsection 15.2, under its explicit direction, room, bank, and endpoint-spread assumptions. None of these bounds follows from the atomic response alone.

18.11.2Transfer of the coefficient field

We keep the following parameters distinct. As above, \(c_k \geqslant 1\) is the selected column allowance at level \(k\). Let \(X\) be the input coefficient, \(S\) its remainder budget, \(E\) the paid response and \(e\) its attainment cost, and \(\zeta_i > 0\) its payment allowance. Let \(a \in (0, 1]\) be the mask fraction, \(v' \geqslant 0\) the forced-allowance budget, and \(q, B > 0\) two coefficient budgets. The transfer requires

\(\displaystyle \|x_X \|_{\text{coeff}} + S \leqslant q, \qquad 4 (1 + c_k) q \leqslant B, \qquad e \zeta_k \leqslant v' B. \) (18.87)

They also include exact coefficient format, remainder, payment, density and forced-profile identities; scale growth and one-step response receipts; and the melting condition. If \(B_k\) is the spatial-spacing parameter of the scale budget and \(R_k = \text{siteFactor} (B_k)\), the latter requires support exponent at least four and

\(\displaystyle t = \text{forcedAllowance}_k + R_k \sqrt{\text{forcedAllowance}_k} + a + R_k v', \qquad (2 B_k + 1)^4 t \leqslant 3, \qquad e^t - 1 \leqslant 1.\)

Here \(B_k\) is not the coefficient budget \(B\) in (18.87).

For \(J \leqslant m < k < N\), let \(\text{gain} (k, m) > 0\) denote the scale gain from \(m\) to \(k\). The output weight is

\(\displaystyle w_m = a \hspace{0.17em} \frac{\zeta_m / 2}{\| \text{invDil}_{m, d} \| (B / \text{gain} (k, m))} . \) (18.88)

For clarity, if \(\mathcal{T}_J\) is the set of reflected scale-\(J\) cells in the volume-\(N\) tiling and \(K = |\mathcal{T}_J |\), with \(\mu_{N, p}\) the normalized Wilson measure, the root functional is

\(\displaystyle \text{root}_{J, N, p} (F) = \left| \int \prod_{x \in \mathcal{T}_J} \text{copy}_x (F) (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{N, p} (U) \right|^{1 / K} .\)

Here \(\text{copy}_x (F)\) evaluates \(F\) on the translated reflection half of the configuration; when that reflection reverses orientation it also complex-conjugates the value. This is the reflection-positive tiling functional, with its \(K\)th-root normalization.

Assume \(N > k\) and that the reference level is no larger than \(k\). The response data are an admissible sequence of coefficient coefficient families from the reference level through \(N\), an admissible top coefficient, an exact paid response identity between each adjacent pair, and the terminal forced-profile identity. The input field has the format, remainder, attainment, and density identities stated above. Assume also the melting and payment inequalities and the endpoint estimate (18.86). Put

\(\displaystyle \tilde{G}' = \text{back}_{k \to N} \hspace{0.17em} G' .\)

Under these hypotheses the finite construction returns one public label \(b\), chosen before any window, with the following two properties. First,

\(\displaystyle b' \hspace{0.17em} \|x_X -\mathcal{C}_k (b)\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant 4 (1 + c_k) q. \) (18.89)

Here the window from scale \(J\) to scale \(m\) is called open if

\(\displaystyle J \leqslant m, \qquad \sigma_m^2 \leqslant A_J / A_m,\)

where \(\sigma_m\) is the cover size of the selected clock at level \(m\) and \(A_j\) the field dilation. Second, for the same \(b\) and for every triple \((J, m, l)\) such that the scale-\(J\)-to-\(m\) window is open, the reference level is no larger than \(m\), \(m < k\), the scale \(J\) has room for the radius \(l\) at scale \(k\), and \(G'\) is interior to the window of radius \(l\),

\(\displaystyle \text{root}_{J, N, p} (\tilde{G}' -\mathcal{O}_{N, p} b) \leqslant \frac{\text{volFac} (J, m)}{w_m} . \) (18.90)

The order of quantifiers matters: a label depending on the window would not give a finite-volume response record, whose root estimate is required for all windows with one label. Here \(b'\) is the endpoint bound-duality constant and \(\text{root}_{J, N, p}\) is the reflection-positive root functional on the volume-\(N\) field, defined using the scale-\(J\) tiling. In this application the endpoint constant \(b'\) is \(\kappa_k\). The column \(\mathcal{C}_k (b)\) is defined through the bare reference coordinate of the public label: if \(\textsf{T}\) denotes the terminal identification of the selected response hierarchy, a linear isomorphism of the grade-\(d\) coefficient space onto itself which sends bare reference coordinates \(v\) to public labels, and \(I_k v\) is the coefficient of the calibrated primary field \(\mathcal{P}_k (v)\) at level \(k\), then

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}_k (b) = I_k (\textsf{T}^{- 1} b) .\)

The construction below produces a bare coordinate \(v\) and sets \(b = \textsf{T} v\); the column estimate is proved for \(I_k v\), while the observation \(\mathcal{O}_{N, p} b\) in (18.90) is that of the public label. That the level-\(k\) entries of the selected response hierarchy, read at \(\textsf{T}^{- 1} b\), are these calibrated primary fields is an identity of the selected hierarchy, used here as part of the response data.

For the column estimate, apply the signed reference-difference construction of Subsection 15.3 to the coefficient field being transferred \(X\) at terminal level \(k\), with its remainder bound \(S\) and total charge \(q\). The construction assumes one fixed background history and calibrated primary fields \(\mathcal{P}_i (v)\) whose coefficient is \(I_i v\), together with the bank and column inequalities (15.6) and the exact degree-\(d\) density transition. At each backward step it first transports the current zero-coefficient remainder by the zero-coordinate transition (15.13); it then subtracts the primary field with the resulting coefficient, so that the new remainder again has coefficient zero. Linearity cancels the two primary densities exactly, and the zero-coordinate transition supplies the density of the transported remainder. The paid direction and remainder estimates (15.5) apply to the resulting signed difference, not to an unsigned estimate obtained by subtracting costs.
