The top family has terminal charge at most \(q\), since \(\|x_X \|_{\text{coeff}} + S \leqslant q\). Choose its single bare reference coordinate \(v\) by setting the left endpoint coefficient to zero, and put \(b = \textsf{T} v\). The output is precisely (15.15): for the chosen coordinate, the terminal coefficient is \(x_X - I_k v = x_X -\mathcal{C}_k (b)\), the terminal density is \(D_X - D_{\mathcal{P}_k (v)}\), and the left endpoint coefficient vector vanishes. This choice does not involve any window \((J, m, l)\). Every intermediate field and the selected reference field are evaluated against the same finest volume and background history; this is what permits the exact density cancellation. At the terminal level the reference construction also retains its spread bound \(r_k \leqslant q + c_k (q +\|Z_k .x\|_{\text{coeff}})\), the precise spread condition in the Woke duet. Thus it applies \(\text{SharpRead}\) with budget \(w = q\). The stronger full terminal estimate in (15.15), with \(C_k = c_k\), gives \(r_k + \|Z_k .x\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant 4 (1 + c_k) q\); this is the coarse transport budget used by the subsequent construction. The signed dualization then gives the zero-site paid-response duet required by SharpRead, and its endpoint inequality is exactly (18.89). Here zero refers to the left coefficient vector and to the duet's marked spatial site being the origin. It does not assert that the observed field values vanish.

The planted polynomial and its full moment.
Fix an admitted window \((J, m, l)\) and retain the label \(b\) already chosen. Let \(V\) be its finite set of cells. Work in the square-free polynomial algebra with generators \(\textsf{t}_a\), \(a \in V\); for \(I \subseteq V\) write \([I] Q\) for the coefficient of \(\prod_{a \in I} \textsf{t}_a\). Let \(D =\mathcal{O}_{N, p} b\) and \(F = G'\), and denote their prescribed copies at levels \(N\) and \(k\) by \(D_a\) and \(F_a\). A copy includes complex conjugation when its reflected chamber requires it. Write \(R_N\) for the unnormalized Wilson density, \(\textsf{P}_{v, u}\) for density transfer from level \(u\) to \(v \leqslant u\), and \(\textsf{B}_{v, u}\) for its observable adjoint. Define the following actual polynomials:
\(\displaystyle Q_N = R_N \prod_{a \in V} (1 - \textsf{t}_a D_a), \qquad Q_k = \textsf{P}_{k, N} Q_N, \qquad X_k = Q_k \prod_{a \in V} (1 + \textsf{t}_a F_a), \qquad X_m = \textsf{P}_{m, k} X_k .\) (18.91)

Transfer acts coefficientwise. For bounded measurable \(F\) these coefficients are integrable: \(D\) is a continuous finite-volume field, the configuration spaces are compact, and the selected transition kernels have bounded densities. More generally it suffices that \(\int \prod_{a \in A} |F_a | \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k < \infty\) for every \(A \subseteq V\). Since \(R_N\), the \(D_a\) and the composed kernel density are bounded, this gives absolute integrability of every mixed double integral below.

For every \(I \subseteq V\), multiplication in the square-free algebra gives

\(\displaystyle [I] X_k = \sum_{A \subseteq I} \textsf{P}_{k, N} \left( R_N \prod_{a \in I \setminus A} (- D_a) \right) \prod_{a \in A} F_a .\) (18.92)

Indeed a generator can occur in only one of the two factors in (18.91); choosing the generators taken from the second factor is exactly choosing \(A\). No product of expectations has been substituted for the transferred old product.

The room and interiority assumptions of the window put every propagated read set inside its own chamber at each intervening step. Consequently (13.35), including equivariance under the prescribed copies, gives

\(\displaystyle \textsf{B}_{k, N} \left( \prod_{a \in A} F_a \right) = \prod_{a \in A} \text{copy}_a (\textsf{B}_{k, N} F) .\)

This factorization uses disjoint kernel read sets, not just mass one. For the integrable extension, truncate each local factor, retaining its read set, and pass to the limit by absolute integrability and Fubini. In the bounded case all the following interchanges follow from the kernel-slice bounds and Fubini; in the integrable version they use the absolute integrability just specified. Apply adjointness to each summand of (18.92) and expand the finite product. This gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \int [I] X_k \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_k & = \int R_N \sum_{A \subseteq I} \prod_{a \in I \setminus A} (- D_a) \prod_{a \in A} \text{copy}_a (\textsf{B}_{k, N} F) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_N\\ & = Z_{N, p} \hspace{0.17em} \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_{a \in I} \text{copy}_a (\textsf{B}_{k, N} F - D) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.93)} \end{array}\)

Every further density transfer preserves the integral of every coefficient, by its normalized mass. Therefore the left-hand side can also be written \(\int [I] X_m \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_m\). For \(I = V\) this is exactly \(Z_{N, p} M\), with \(M\) the moment in the root functional. For \(I = \varnothing\) it is \(Z_{N, p}\). This proves the full moment identity, including all mixed terms, with the same partition function. It is stronger than a check of the singleton densities.

Constructing the two support ledgers.
We construct the served representation on the opened centered-cell census using the fixed label \(b\) and the terminal birth in (18.91). Throughout, the same selected history supplies the coefficient identities, density pushes, forced profiles, support rooms, bank and place bounds, migration, separation, and terminal hook. The local response certificate is (18.95); its support and attainment bounds are used below at their actual selected interfaces. The served representation and both support ledgers will be built from these data.

Write \(\tau_i\) for the weight \(w_m\) marched from \(m\) to \(i\), and \(\Omega_i\) for the weight \(w_m / a\) marched by the same positive multipliers. Thus \(\tau_i = a \Omega_i\). Put \(\gamma_i = \text{forcedAllowance}_i\), and retain the column parameter \(c_i \geqslant 1\). We use the paid scale inequalities (15.11), \(\zeta_i c_i \leqslant 1\), and \(\sqrt{\gamma_i} \leqslant 1\). The latter is the small-remainder regime of the selected history. The signed comparison constructed above has

\(\displaystyle r_i + \|Z_i .x\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant B / \text{gain} (k, i), \qquad Z_k .x = x_X - I_k v.\)

Also \(\|I_k v\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant 2 B\): the triangle inequality gives \(q + B \leqslant 2 B\), since \(4 (1 + c_k) q \leqslant B\). The two starting payments are

\(\displaystyle w_m \| \text{invDil}_{m, d} \| \frac{B}{\text{gain} (k, m)} = \frac{a \zeta_m}{2} \leqslant \frac{\zeta_m}{2}, \qquad \frac{w_m}{a} \| \text{invDil}_{m, d} \| \frac{B}{\text{gain} (k, m)} = \frac{\zeta_m}{2} .\) (18.94)

Apply the paid scale induction to both weights. It gives the coefficient-plus-remainder payment for the signed comparison at weight \(\tau_i\), and at level \(k\) gives

\(\displaystyle \Omega_k \| \text{invDil}_{k, d} \| B \leqslant \zeta_k / 2, \qquad \Omega_k \| \text{invDil}_{k, d} \| \|I_k v\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant \zeta_k .\)

The second inequality pays the negative reference label in the terminal birth; the label remains \(- b\) for every window. The larger weight \(\Omega_k\) is essential; payment at \(\tau_k\) alone would not imply it. The first inequality pays the local response because

\(\displaystyle \tau_k \| \text{invDil}_{k, d} \| e \leqslant a \zeta_k e / (2 B) \leqslant v' .\)

Linearity of the reference hierarchy makes the bond of \(- b\) equal to that of \(b\). The opened cell embeddings give occupancy one, migration, and separation; the old-level multiplicity bound is the admitted schedule.

Apply the terminal birth construction and its finite downward induction to this negative label, starting with the second payment. The terminal birth expansion starts with the actual background and linear insertions and transports them through the selected transition identities, retaining the forced hook. At the finest level its polynomial is precisely \(Q_N\) in (18.91). The construction begins with the background box factors and linear terminal insertions, moves forced responses into boxes by connected component closure, and removes the constant and singleton coefficients. This defines the higher arrays, rather than merely bounding proposed arrays. More explicitly, the terminal place bound has a footprint of cover number at most one and section budget \(t_N \geqslant 1\). The bank inequality is

\(\displaystyle e^{\alpha_N + 2} \mathfrak{c}_N (t_N) \zeta_N \leqslant u_N .\)

Together with the paid negative-label bond this gives each terminal response its weighted bound \(u_N\) and its prescribed forced hook. Writing \(n_N\) for the terminal occupancy bound, the terminal budget requires

\(\displaystyle C_N = \gamma_N + n_N \text{siteFactor} (B_N) u_N, \quad (2 B_N + 1)^4 C_N \leqslant 3, \quad e^{C_N} - 1 \leqslant 1, \quad \alpha_N \geqslant 3.\)

The place clause here is the cover-number-at-most-one receipt. The birth polynomial is integrable by the compact-continuity estimate for the terminal cylinder observable. The mixed polynomial is integrable under each transition: on this finite-volume compact configuration space its cylinder factors are bounded, and the selected transition kernels are normalized with bounded densities. Thus the component construction of (7.52) and (7.35), with spare exponent three, gives a flagged higher array of bound at most one at exponent \(\alpha_N - 3\). The ordinary exponent started linear and remains linear after the response shift, so its higher array is zero. The terminal hook and singleton coin identities identify the prescribed singletons; the higher-degree projection retains the exact birth polynomial. At level \(k\), the seeded negative polynomial \(Q_k\) has the negative reference singletons and higher arrays of bound one at weights \(\Omega_k\). Reweighting only degrees at least two from \(\Omega_k\) to \(\tau_k = a \Omega_k\) reduces those bounds to \(a\). Multiply by the positive factors \(\prod_a (1 + \textsf{t}_a F_a)\) and insert their local response, whose singleton coin and forced profile are (18.95). This forms the mixed polynomial \(X_k\), with singleton equal to positive primary plus negative reference plus response. Before component closure, the ordinary higher array still has bound at most \(a\); the flagged background, forced profiles and reweighted old boxes are priced explicitly below. For each nonzero term, connect its old flagged boxes and response supports by support intersection, require an old flagged box, and put the product at the union support with the union flag set. This finite component regrouping preserves the full polynomial. A component with one response contributes exactly its forced hook; components with two or more responses have degree at least two. The resulting representation of \(X_k\) has ordinary and flagged higher arrays \(H^0_Y, K^0_P\), each with site bound at most one at exponent \(\alpha_k - 4\). The flagged bound is the component bound proved below; the ordinary higher array remains at bound \(a\).

Here and below a site bound \(C\) means that the weighted absolute coefficient sums have nonnegative majorants \(h_Y\) satisfying \(\sum_{Y \ni x} h_Y e^{\alpha c_B (Y)} \leqslant C\) for every \(x\); for boxes use their first, support component. This includes a majorant uniform in the configuration. All higher arrays have marker degree at least two. Changing their weights from \(\Omega_k\) to \(\tau_k = a \Omega_k\) multiplies the bound of a degree \(j \geqslant 2\) coefficient by \(a^j \leqslant a\). Consequently both old higher arrays now have site bound at most \(a\).

Let \(\phi_a\) and \(q_a\) denote the negative reference ordinary singleton and forced profile, respectively. The actual atomic response, after the prescribed motion and dilation, gives an ordinary field \(h_a\), a dominated forced family \(y_a\), and a support response \(\psi_{a, Y}\), with

\(\displaystyle \text{coin}_k (h_a, y_a) = \mathscr{R}_k F_a, \qquad \text{profile}_k (y_a) =\mathcal{H}_k (\psi_a) .\) (18.95)

Here \(\mathscr{R}_k\) denotes the evaluation of the level-\(k\) representation, and \(\mathcal{H}_k\) is the response hook defined in Subsection 7.8. These identities follow by applying the same motion and dilation to the input density and forced-profile identities: the Wilson density is invariant under that motion, and both profile and hook are linear. The response has a majorant \(g_{a, Y}\) with

\(\displaystyle \sum_Y \tau_k g_{a, Y} e^{\alpha_k c_{B_k} (Y)} \leqslant v' .\)

Indeed the undilated attainment cost is \(e\), and \(\tau_k \| \text{invDil}_{k, d} \| e \leqslant a \zeta_k e / (2 B) \leqslant v'\), by (18.94) and \(e \zeta_k \leqslant v' B\). The attainment exponent \(\alpha_k + 2\) is stronger than the exponent used here. Each nonzero response contains its mark and vanishes when its support is healthy. The census marks at \(k\) are distinct by the admitted window separation, so occupancy there is one. Equation (7.46), with exponent \(\alpha_k - 1\), therefore bounds the combined response site bound by \(R_k^{\text{site}} v'\), where \(R_k^{\text{site}} = \text{siteFactor} (B_k)\).

To avoid confusing this packing factor with the density, write \(R^{\text{site}} = R_k^{\text{site}}\) for the rest of the gluing step. The forced profiles \(q_a\) of the negative reference field have site bound at most \(R^{\text{site}} \sqrt{\gamma_k}\) at exponent \(\alpha_k - 1\). Here is the payment check. The reference remainder has bound at most \(c_k \|I_k v\|_{\text{coeff}}\); its forced family has cost at most \(\| \text{invDil}_{k, d} \| \sqrt{\gamma_k}\) times that remainder. Multiplication by \(\tau_k\), the reference bond payment, and \(\zeta_k c_k \leqslant 1\) give the per-mark cost \(\sqrt{\gamma_k}\). Apply (7.46) again. The background box activity has site bound \(\gamma_k\). Thus the full old box array, including its background, its linear forced profiles and \(K^0\), has site bound at most

\(\displaystyle W = \gamma_k + R^{\text{site}} \sqrt{\gamma_k} + a \quad \text{at exponent } \alpha_k - 1.\)

Multiplication by \(\prod_a (1 + \textsf{t}_a F_a)\) changes the old ordinary exponent by \(\sum_a \textsf{t}_a F_a\). Put \(J = \sum_{a, Y} \textsf{t}_a \psi_{a, Y}\). Form a new box array \(\bar{K}\) as follows. Take finite sets of distinct old box addresses and response addresses \((a, Y)\), with connected support intersection graph, at least one old box, and pairwise disjoint old box supports. Assign their product to the box whose support is their union and whose flag set is the union of the old flag sets, retaining the indicator that this is the flag set of the union support. Sum the products with the same output box. This is an explicit finite definition on each finite-volume lattice. The component decomposition in (7.52) gives

\(\displaystyle \text{field} (\bar{K}) = \text{field} (K^{\text{old}}) \text{pass} (J) .\)

Subtract \(J\) from the ordinary exponent. The represented polynomial is still exactly \(X_k\); no coefficient has been discarded.

Set \(d_{\ast} = \alpha_k - 4 \geqslant 0\) and \(\eta = W + R^{\text{site}} v'\). The melting hypotheses say \((2 B_k + 1)^4 \eta \leqslant 3\) and \(e^{\eta} - 1 \leqslant 1\). Apply (7.35) with output exponent \(d_{\ast}\) and spare exponent \(3\). Its incoming exponent is \(d_{\ast} + 3 = \alpha_k - 1\), exactly the exponent just paid. The bound of a product is at most the product of its coefficient majorants, and the union box is uniquely determined by its constituent addresses. Restricting the connected-set sum to the admissible sets above only decreases its nonnegative majorant. Hence \(\bar{K}\) has site bound at most \(e^{\eta} - 1 \leqslant 1\) at exponent \(d_{\ast}\). The ordinary higher array remains \(H^0\), since \(J\) and all added ordinary terms are linear; its bound is at most \(a \leqslant 1\) at the same exponent.

The vacuum coefficient of \(\bar{K}\) is the old background. Its singleton at mark \(a\) is \(q_a +\mathcal{H}_k (\psi_a)\): without a response a component is one old box, while a component with exactly one response gives precisely the hook, as proved in Subsection 7.8. Equation (18.95), the singleton identity for \(Q_k\), and the product rule in the square-free algebra identify the singleton of \(X_k\) with the coin of \((\phi_a + h_a, q_a + \text{profile}_k (y_a))\). Positivity of the background density and the singleton uniqueness in (7.45) then identify the ordinary singleton of the new exponent. Remove degrees zero and one from each array; the remaining \(H^k, K^k\) give exactly this prescribed singleton representation of \(X_k\). Degree projection cannot increase the absolute coefficient bound. Products, finite sums and local flag indicators preserve measurability, gauge invariance, locality and containment of each occurring mark in its support. Both higher ledgers therefore have bound one at exponent \(\alpha_k - 4\).

Now descend from \(k\) to \(m\) along the same selected history. At each step the signed singleton expansion in Subsection 15.3 has its exact density push, forced-profile identity and finite support witness on the chosen interface. Transporting the higher arrays through that witness preserves the full polynomial; the newly added response terms have degree at least two, and their connected supports are grouped by union. Thus the descent transports the representation already constructed at \(k\) rather than postulating a new served object. For specificity the required paid local data are

\(\displaystyle 0 < \delta_i \leqslant 1, \quad \alpha_i, \alpha_{i + 1} \geqslant 100, \quad T_i = n_i \text{siteFactor} (B_{i + 1}) \delta_i + 4 \delta_i, \quad T_i + e^{T_i} T_i \leqslant M_i,\)
\(\displaystyle A_i = u_i + n_i \text{siteFactor} (B_i) \delta_i, \qquad (2 B_i + 1)^4 A_i \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}, \qquad e^{A_i} - 1 \leqslant 1.\)

Here \(M_i\) is the available input budget, \(u_i\) is the flagged response bound, and \(n_i\) bounds the two adjacent occupancies. These are the one-step payment inequalities of the selected history, not additional conclusions of melting at \(k\). Here is the singleton payment check. Put \(C_i^{\text{wing}} = \text{orb} (d) (\psi_i + 2 c_{i + 1}) \geqslant 2\). The retained signed expansion has dilated fine cost

\(\displaystyle s_i \leqslant \| \text{invDil}_{i + 1, d} \| (C_i^{\text{wing}} \|Z_{i + 1} .x\|_{\text{coeff}} + 2 r_{i + 1}) \leqslant \| \text{invDil}_{i + 1, d} \| C_i^{\text{wing}} (\|Z_{i + 1} .x\|_{\text{coeff}} + r_{i + 1}) .\)

The paid orbit inequality of the selected history is \(2 C_i^{\text{wing}} \zeta_{i + 1} \leqslant 1\); hence \(2 \tau_{i + 1} s_i \leqslant 1\). Its coarse response cost is at most \(t_i \leqslant \| \text{invDil}_{i, d} \| r_i\). Since the march has \(\tau_{i + 1} \leqslant \tau_i\) and \(\zeta_i \leqslant 1\), the same paid comparison gives \(\tau_{i + 1} t_i \leqslant 1\). These pay the input and output sides of the actual finite-support comparison. The parent-site identity, occupancy and cover-bound comparison are the same migration and separation data as in the centered induction. The adjacent transition preserves the singleton entries; every added response term has marker degree at least two. The component-closure estimate therefore recovers exponent \(\alpha_i\) and separately bounds both higher ledgers by one. At the last level, the forced remainder allowance \(\sqrt{\gamma_m}\) and the marched coefficient-plus-remainder payment give the Vault constants \(2, 1\). The first step accepts \(\alpha_k - 4\); every subsequent step accepts the full exponent already obtained. The actual polynomial at the next level is \(\textsf{P}_{i, i + 1} X_{i + 1}\), so finite induction constructs the required support families for the precise \(X_m\) in (18.91).

Finally the singleton bounds are separate from the higher-array bounds. At \(m\), let \(z_m = \|Z_m .x\|_{\text{coeff}}\). The coefficient format and remainder bounds imply, before dilation and motion,

\(\displaystyle | \text{exponentialEntry} (Z_m) | \leqslant (1 + c_m) z_m + r_m, \qquad \sum_P | \text{profile} (Z_m^{\text{f}})_P | \leqslant r_m .\)

For the first inequality, sum the section bounds against the absolute coefficient vector and add the remainder bound; the constant coordinate contributes the extra \(z_m\). The second is the absolute forced-profile bound, since \(\sqrt{\gamma_m} \leqslant 1\). Motion preserves absolute values. Since \(w_m \| \text{invDil}_{m, d} \| (r_m + z_m) \leqslant \zeta_m\) and \(\zeta_m c_m \leqslant 1\), the ordinary singleton has weighted absolute value at most \(2\) and the summed flagged singleton at most \(1\). These are exactly the two singleton bounds used by the finite-census estimate below. Together with the constructed higher arrays and their site budgets, they give the required served representation and its support bounds at level \(m\).

Thus the following support decomposition has been constructed by the paid same-history transition and its finite-support witnesses. Let \(V\) be the set of census sites, \(K = |V|\), with marks \(o_a\), \(a \in V\), in the level-\(m\) lattice. The polynomial \(\textsf{X} = X_m\) of (18.91), whose integrability and moment identity were just established, has the following structure. There are singleton site amplitudes \(\Phi_a (U)\) and singleton box amplitudes \(\omega_{a, P} (U)\), \(a \in V\), \(P\) a box, with

\(\displaystyle w_a | \Phi_a (U) | \leqslant 2, \qquad \sum_P w_a | \omega_{a, P} (U) | \leqslant 1 \qquad \text{for every } a \text{ and every } U,\)

and two correction families, an ordinary family \(H_Y (U)\) indexed by supports \(Y\) and a flagged family \(K_P (U)\) indexed by boxes \(P\), such that

\(\displaystyle \textsf{X} (U) = e^{E_m (U)} \hspace{0.17em} \text{pass} \left( \sum_a \Phi_a (U) \hspace{0.17em} \textsf{t}_a + \sum_Y H_Y (U) \right) \cdot \text{field} \left( P \mapsto \textsf{b}_P (U) + \sum_a \omega_{a, P} (U) \hspace{0.17em} \textsf{t}_a + K_P (U) \right),\)

where \(E_m\) is the exponent of the level-\(m\) effective density, \(\textsf{t}_a\) is the census monomial of the site \(a\), \(\textsf{b}_P\) is the box activity term of the level-\(m\) representation, and \(\text{field}\) is evaluated on the flagged set of \(U\). Each correction family satisfies the anchored support-cost bound with constant \(1\) at the support exponent \(\alpha_m\) of the level-\(m\) budget: there are numbers \(h_Y \geqslant 0\) bounding the weighted absolute coefficient sums, \(\mathcal{P}_w (H_Y (U)) \leqslant h_Y\) for all \(U\), with \(\sum_{Y \ni x} h_Y e^{\alpha_m \text{cov}_m (Y)} \leqslant 1\) for every site \(x\), and likewise for \(K_P\) with the support of the box \(P\). Every nonzero correction term has a census mark \(o_a\) in its support. Consequently, summing the anchored bound over the \(K\) marks,

\(\displaystyle \sum_Y \mathcal{P}_w (H_Y (U)) \leqslant K, \qquad \sum_P \mathcal{P}_w (K_P (U)) \leqslant K \qquad \text{for every } U. \) (18.96)

The required support-bound structure is a statement about this precise polynomial \(X_m\). Its full moment identity is (18.93), already proved independently of those estimates. The support decomposition and its two bounds were constructed above, using the explicitly stated local-transition inputs.

The polynomial-bound calculation behind the finite-gas estimate is as follows. For a finite census polynomial \(Q\), write

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{P}_w (Q) = \sum_s \omega (w, s) \|Q_s \|\)

for its weighted absolute coefficient sum. The bound is submultiplicative. For box terms \(K_p\), the finite sum over disjoint-support patterns obeys

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{P}_w (\text{field} (K)) \leqslant \prod_p (1 +\mathcal{P}_w (K_p)) \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \sum_p \mathcal{P}_w (K_p) \right),\)

because each pattern's product bound is at most the product of its factors and \(1 + x \leqslant e^x\). The same submultiplicativity, applied term by term to the finite truncated exponential, gives \(\mathcal{P}_w (\text{pass} (Q)) \leqslant e^{\mathcal{P}_w (Q)}\). The singleton site and box amplitudes contribute \(2 K\) and \(K\) to the exponent, by the two singleton bounds just proved. The ordinary and the flagged correction families contribute \(K\) each, by (18.96). The total is \(5 K\); the box activity terms \(\textsf{b}_P\) and the exponent \(E_m\) are paid by the finite-volume denominator bound used next.

By (18.93), the integral of the selected coefficient equals the volume-\(N\) Wilson partition function times \(M\). To specify the denominator estimate, let \(A_m\) be the lawful local representative used in this very expansion, and take \(\rho = \hat{\beta}_m^{- 1 / 2} \leqslant \min \{1, \vartheta_m \}\). The numerator estimate just obtained is the coefficient-estimate of Subsection 4.2, with total mark charge \(5 K\). Its small-ball lower bound for \(Z_{A_m} = \int \text{eval} (A_m)\), followed by division as in (4.10), bounds the quotient by

\(\displaystyle \exp \hspace{-0.17em} (| \Lambda_m | [2 \text{exCost}_m + \kappa_m + \beta_m \text{baseBudget}_m (\rho) + 4 d_G \log (16 / \rho)] + 5 K) .\)

Here \(\kappa_m\) denotes the vacuum bound in that estimate, not the coefficient-recovery constant used later. The treasury receipt at this same radius bounds the bracket by \(\text{vacuumBound} (m)\). The common-trajectory transition-mass identity identifies \(Z_{A_m} = Z_{N, p} > 0\), as in Lemma 4.11; this is also the partition function in the planted moment identity above. No comparison of different local representatives or partition functions is needed. Consequently

\(\displaystyle |M| \hspace{0.17em} \omega (w, \mathfrak{s}) \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} (| \Lambda_m | \text{vacuumBound} (m) + K (2 + 1 + 2)) . \) (18.97)

This is an absolute-value estimate and does not use cancellation. Its hypotheses include the paid local-transition inputs used to construct the support ledgers above.

In this application all site weights equal \(w_m\), so \(\omega (w, \mathfrak{s}) = w_m^K\). The tiling identity is

\(\displaystyle | \Lambda_m | = K \left( \frac{A_J}{A_m} \right)^4 .\)

Taking the positive \(K\)-th root of (18.97) yields

\(\displaystyle |M|^{1 / K} \leqslant \frac{\exp ((A_J / A_m)^4 \text{vacuumBound} (m) + 5)}{w_m} = \frac{\text{volFac} (J, m)}{w_m},\)

which is (18.90). The moment identity is needed to identify this \(M\) with the desired rooted observable.

18.11.3Using the output in the finite-volume response record

The finite-volume response record is evaluated on the inflated field \(G' = ug\). Its root error is \(\tilde{G}' -\mathcal{O}_{N, p} b\), where \(\tilde{G}' = \text{back}_{k \to N} \hspace{0.17em} G'\). In the finite-volume response record specialization, \(a = Z^{- 1}\), \(v' = Z^{- 1}\), \(B = Z^3\), and \(e = Z\), while \(\zeta_i = \text{gradeDecay}_d (i)\) at each scale. Thus the payment condition is \(e \zeta_k = Z \text{gradeDecay}_d (k) \leqslant Z^2 = v' B\). The gas weight from (18.88) is

\(\displaystyle w_{\text{gas}} = Z^{- 1} \frac{\text{gradeDecay}_d (m) / 2}{\| \text{invDil}_{m, d} \| (Z^3 / \text{gain} (k, m))} .\)

The factor \(Z^{- 1}\) is the mask fraction \(a\) in the input to the transfer, so this is exactly the native finite-volume response record denominator.

The melting inequalities also hold eventually for this specialization. To see this directly, write \(x = \hat{\beta}_k\), \(B_x = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil\) and \(R_x = \exp (1) [2 (16 B_x + 9)]^4\) for the spatial radius and site factor. The selected forced allowance is exactly

\(\displaystyle f (x) = \exp \{- \exp [(\log \log x)^2 / 5]\}\)

for sufficiently large \(x\). Indeed, the monitor is \(H (x) = \exp ((\log \log x)^2)\) and the allowance is \(\exp (- H (x)^{1 / 5})\); the power \(1 / 5\) is applied to the value of the monitor, not to its argument. With \(y = \log \log x\), \(\exp (y^2 / 5) / \log x = \exp (y^2 / 5 - y) \to \infty\). Consequently, for every \(A > 0\), \(\sqrt{f (x)} \leqslant x^{- A}\) eventually; also \(0 < f (x) \leqslant \sqrt{f (x)}\) eventually. With \(a = v' = x^{- 1}\), the quantity in the melting condition is

\(\displaystyle t_x = f (x) + R_x \sqrt{f (x)} + x^{- 1} + R_x x^{- 1} .\)

Here \(R_x = O ((\log x)^8)\) and \((2 B_x + 1)^4 = O ((\log x)^8)\), so \((2 B_x + 1)^4 t_x \to 0\) and \(t_x \to 0\). Thus eventually \((2 B_x + 1)^4 t_x \leqslant 3\) and \(e^{t_x} - 1 \leqslant 1\); \(0 < a \leqslant 1\) and \(v' \geqslant 0\) hold as well. The remaining clause is the support exponent bound \(\alpha_k \geqslant 4\), retained among the scale hypotheses. All these tails depend only on the fixed scale data, before periods, volumes, atomic certificates and windows are chosen.

The other two entries of the finite-volume response record use the specific atomic charge. Fix \(u > 0\) and let the charge in (18.82) be

\(\displaystyle q = q_k = u \hspace{0.17em} \vartheta_k^2 \text{Age}_k (1 / 6), \qquad \text{Age}_k (a) = \exp (a (\log \log \hat{\beta}_k)^2),\)

where \(\vartheta_k\) is the threshold of the scale-\(k\) budget; thus \(\kappa_k = \text{Age}_k (1 / 2)\) and \(\text{Age}_k (a) \text{Age}_k (a') = \text{Age}_k (a + a')\). The selected schedule has \(c_k \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_k\). Since \(\vartheta_k^2 \text{Age}_k (1 / 6) = \hat{\beta}_k^{- 1} \text{Age}_k (2 + 1 / 6)\) tends to zero faster than any power of \(\log \hat{\beta}_k\) grows, and \(\log \hat{\beta}_k = \exp (\log \log \hat{\beta}_k)\) is eventually smaller than any positive multiple of \(\text{Age}_k (1 / 12)\), and \(\hat{\beta}_k \to \infty\), for all large \(k\)

\(\displaystyle q_k \leqslant 1, \qquad 4 (1 + \log \hat{\beta}_k) q_k \leqslant Z^3, \qquad 4 (1 + \log \hat{\beta}_k) u \leqslant \text{Age}_k (1 / 12) ;\)

the second inequality gives (18.85). The norm entry of the finite-volume response record is \(\|x_X \|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant 1\); it follows from (18.82), nonnegativity of the remainder allowance and \(q_k \leqslant 1\): \(\|x_X \|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant \|x_X \|_{\text{coeff}} + s \leqslant q_k \leqslant 1\). The bias entry is obtained from (18.89) with \(b' = \kappa_k\) alone:

\(\displaystyle \|x_X -\mathcal{C}_k (b)\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant \frac{4 (1 + c_k) q_k}{\kappa_k} \leqslant \text{Age}_k (1 / 12) \hspace{0.17em} \vartheta_k^2 \text{Age}_k (1 / 6) \text{Age}_k (- 1 / 2) = \vartheta_k^2 \text{Age}_k (- 1 / 4),\)

because \(1 / 12 + 1 / 6 - 1 / 2 = - 1 / 4\). This is the bias bound which Subsection 18.12 takes as a hypothesis on a paid label; that section does not prove it.

The resulting statement has the following order of choices, under the standing hypotheses on the selected family, which include the served response hierarchy and its terminal identification, the common-trajectory finite-menu and atomic response receipts, eventual support exponents at least four, the column bound \(c_k \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_k\), and the endpoint estimate (18.86) with \(b' = \kappa_k\) as a hypothesis. For every fixed \(u > 0\) and grade \(d\): for all sufficiently large \(k\), for all sufficiently large periods \(p\) (depending on \(k\)), for every volume index \(N > k\) and every atomic certificate \((X, g)\) at scale \(k\) with charge \(q_k\) and attainment cost at most \(Z = \hat{\beta}_k\), there is a public label \(b\) such that \((g, x_X, b)\) is a finite-volume response record, with the root estimate for all windows. The period threshold is the one at which the endpoint estimate (18.86) holds with \(\ell\) the reference level.

The remaining analytic dependencies are the atomic response, the endpoint bound-duality recurrence, and the paid elementary integration and singleton transition inputs specified above. The site/box expansion, its two separate correction bounds, and its singleton envelope are now constructed from those inputs; no additional served polynomial is postulated.

18.12The response column and its root-scale error

We compute the quantitative root-estimate attached to a paid finite-volume response. This is the numerical input used for the two component errors in Subsection 18.13. The computation has two parts: a coefficient-size estimate from the paid label and its approximation receipt, and a negative-rate bound on the native root bound.

For each frame index \(i\), set

\(\displaystyle J_i = \text{basin} (i), \qquad K_i = \text{summit} (i), \qquad t_i = \hat{\beta}_{K_i}, \qquad v_i = \left( \frac{A_i}{A_{J_i}} \right)^d, \qquad f_i = \text{gradeDecay}_d (J_i),\)

where \(A_j\) is the field dilation at level \(j\), and the basin, the summit and all scales belong to one fixed selected family and its selected schedule. Let

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} s_i & = \text{volFac} (i, J_i) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \left( \frac{A_i}{A_{J_i}} \right)^4 \text{vacuumBound} (J_i) + 5 \right),\\ \gamma_i & = \text{gain} (K_i, J_i) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{S_{K_i} - S_{J_i}}{8} \right) \end{aligned}\)

be the local volume factor and the gain from level \(K_i\) down to \(J_i\), where \(S_j\) is the scale coordinate. The native root allowance for a grade-\(d\) response is

\(\displaystyle n_{d, i} = \frac{s_i}{t_i^{- 1} \left( \dfrac{f_i / 2}{A_{J_i}^{- d} (t_i^3 / \gamma_i)} \right)} . \) (18.98)

The norm of the inverse grade-\(d\) dilation is \(A_{J_i}^{- d}\), which is the factor appearing in this expression. Simplifying gives

\(\displaystyle A_i^d n_{d, i} = \frac{2 s_i v_i t_i^4}{f_i \gamma_i} . \) (18.99)

Indeed, the denominator in (18.98) is \(f_i \gamma_i A_{J_i}^d / (2 t_i^4)\), and \(A_i^d / A_{J_i}^d = v_i\). Throughout this calculation the scale receipts ensure \(A_i, A_{J_i}, t_i, f_i, \gamma_i > 0\) and \(G_i \to \infty\).

We express the estimates using exponential rates. For a positive sequence \(F_i\), an exponential bound of rate zero means that for every \(\epsilon > 0\), eventually \(0 \leqslant F_i \leqslant \exp (\epsilon G_i)\), where \(G_i = \text{logScale} (\hat{\beta}_i)\). If two sequences satisfy exponential bounds of rates \(\alpha\) and \(\alpha'\), their product has rate \(\alpha + \alpha'\): choose the two bounds at exponent \(\alpha + \eta\) and \(\alpha' + \eta\), multiply them, and then let \(2 \eta\) be smaller than the requested excess over \(\alpha + \alpha'\). Nonnegative sums of equal-rate sequences retain that rate by the same argument and the elementary bound \(u + v \leqslant 2 \max (u, v)\).

Lemma 18.23. (The five scale estimates for the paid response) For the fixed selected history and each fixed grade \(d\), the sequences \(s_i, v_i, t_i^4, f_i^{- 1}\) satisfy bounds of rate zero. Moreover, eventually \(J_i < K_i\) and \(\gamma_i \geqslant \tau_i (500)\), where

\(\displaystyle \tau_i (q) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{q}{10^4} G_i \right) .\)

The eventual cutoff may depend on the grade and the requested exponent, but not on volume, period, observable or window.

Proof. We apply the moving-depth bounds of Lemma 18.58. Its frame \(j\), first opened level \(m\), and summit \(k\) are exactly \(i, J_i, K_i\) here: the opening condition is \((A_i / A_{J_i}) \geqslant \lceil (\log \hat{\beta}_{J_i})^2 \rceil^2\), and the summit is the first level after \(i\) whose scale gap is at least \(G_i\). Its vacuum bound \(\mathcal{T}_{J_i}\) is the same vacuum bound in \(s_i\). No field, paid label, or terminal volume enters those choices. Set \(x = \hat{\beta}_i\) and \(y = \log x\), so \(G_i = y^{48}\). That lemma gives on a common tail

\(\displaystyle \frac{A_i}{A_{J_i}} \leqslant y^6, \qquad \left( \frac{A_i}{A_{J_i}} \right)^4 \text{vacuumBound} (J_i) \leqslant y^{27}, \qquad \hat{\beta}_{K_i} \leqslant 2 x, \qquad S_{K_i} - S_{J_i} \geqslant \tfrac{1}{2} y^{48}, \qquad J_i < K_i .\)

These bounds were proved there from the first-opening minimality, the summit first-crossing bound, and the stage vacuum bound payment; their proof does not use the paid-response estimate of this section. The decay factor at fixed grade is \(f_i = \hat{\beta}_{J_i}^{- w_d}\), where \(w_d > 0\) is the fixed exponent of the selected height schedule. Monotonicity of the hatted coupling and \(J_i < K_i\) give \(f_i^{- 1} \leqslant (2 x)^{w_d}\). Consequently the logarithms of our four positive factors are bounded by

\(\displaystyle y^{27} + 5, \qquad 6 d \log y, \qquad 4 (y + \log 2), \qquad w_d (y + \log 2),\)

respectively. Dividing each bound by \(y^{48}\) gives zero in the limit. For every \(\epsilon > 0\), each factor is therefore at most \(\exp (\epsilon G_i)\) eventually. This proves all four bounds. Finally

\(\displaystyle \gamma_i = \exp ((S_{K_i} - S_{J_i}) / 8) \geqslant \exp (G_i / 16) \geqslant \exp (500 G_i / 10^4) = \tau_i (500),\)

since \(1 / 16 > 500 / 10^4\) and \(G_i \geqslant 0\).\(\Box\)

Taking the product of the four bounds, including the fixed factor \(2\), gives an exponential bound of rate zero for

\(\displaystyle B_i = \frac{2 s_i v_i t_i^4}{f_i} .\)

The exponential bound at exponent \(70 / 10^4\) gives \(B_i \leqslant \tau_i (70)\) eventually. Dividing by the gain and using multiplicativity yields

\(\displaystyle A_i^d n_{d, i} = B_i / \gamma_i \leqslant \tau_i (70) / \tau_i (500) = \tau_i (- 430) . \) (18.100)

This proves the exact negative-rate bound needed below for all sufficiently large \(i\).

Lemma 18.24. (Column size from a paid label) Suppose a grade-\(d\) paid label \(y\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \|y\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant 1, \qquad \|y - \text{Col} (x)\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant \vartheta_K^2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{Age}_K (- 1 / 4),\)

where the selected threshold obeys \(0 < \vartheta_K \leqslant 1\) and \(\text{Age}_K (- 1 / 4) \leqslant 1\). Then

\(\displaystyle \| \text{Col} (x)\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant 2.\)

Proof. The triangle inequality gives

\(\displaystyle \| \text{Col} (x)\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant \|y\|_{\text{coeff}} + \| \text{Col} (x) - y\|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant 1 + \vartheta_K^2 \text{Age}_K (- 1 / 4) \leqslant 2.\)

\(\Box\)

Proposition 18.25. (Coefficient approximation and the scaled root error) Fix the volume index \(N\), the period \(p\) and the grade \(d\), and let \(K = K_i \leqslant N\). Let \(g\) be a scale-\(K\) observable, \(y\) a grade-\(d\) coefficient and \(x\) a public grade-\(d\) label, and let \(\text{Col} (x) =\mathcal{C}_K (x)\) be the scale-\(K\) response column of \(x\) defined in Subsection 18.11 for the same \(N, p, d\). Suppose \((y, x)\) satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma 18.24. Assume its paid root receipt: whenever the window of the selected schedule from \(i\) to \(J_i\) is open, that is, \(i \leqslant J_i\) and \(\sigma_{J_i}^2 \leqslant A_i / A_{J_i}\) with \(\sigma_j\) the cover size of the selected clock at level \(j\), the reference level of grade \(d\) is at most \(J_i\), \(J_i < K_i\), the scale \(i\) has room for a radius \(l\) at scale \(K_i\), and \(g\) is interior to the window of radius \(l\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_{i, p} \hspace{-0.17em} (\mathcal{B} (A_{K_i}^{- d} g) -\mathcal{O}(x)) \leqslant n_{d, i},\)

where \(\mathcal{R}_{i, p}\) is the root functional of the tiling of the volume-\(N\) lattice by scale-\(i\) cells, \(\mathcal{B}\) the extension from scale \(K_i\) to volume \(N\), and \(\mathcal{O} (x)\) the volume-\(N\) observation of the label \(x\) at the origin. Assume that \(i\) is so large that (18.100) holds. Then the paid column has size at most \(2\). For \(q_i = (A_i / A_{K_i})^d\), linearity and homogeneity give the scaled error bound

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_{i, p} \hspace{-0.17em} (\mathcal{B}(q_i g) -\mathcal{O}(A_i^d x)) \leqslant \tau_i (- 430) .\)

For all sufficiently large \(i\), this is the paid-component error used in the probe assembly, uniformly over \(N\), \(p\), the radii \(l\) and the observables satisfying the stated window, room and interiority conditions.

Proof. The coefficient conclusion is Lemma 18.24. The inverse dilation satisfies \(q_i g = A_i^d (A_{K_i}^{- d} g)\). By real-linearity of the finite-volume observation and pullback,

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B} (q_i g) -\mathcal{O} (A_i^d x) = A_i^d (\mathcal{B}(A_{K_i}^{- d} g) -\mathcal{O}(x)) .\)

Since \(A_i > 0\), homogeneity of the root seminorm and the assumed paid receipt bound this by \(A_i^d n_{d, i}\). Equation (18.100) then gives the stated \(\tau_i (- 430)\) bound, uniformly over all allowed windows and observables.\(\Box\)

The label-producing receipt is supplied by the finite-volume response record specialization in Subsection 18.11. Its label is chosen once, before the window tests, from the actual atomic response, density identity and two weight payments. For each fixed inflation and grade, the scale is first chosen sufficiently late, then the period sufficiently large at that scale; the conclusion is uniform over every larger cutoff and every permitted window for that one label. The chosen and reference atomic certificates used here are constructed in Subsection 18.25. Thus the present coefficient and rate arithmetic applies to the same labels, rather than to separately selected witnesses for different tests.

18.13Assembly of the finite-volume response probe

We spell out the finite algebra that turns two separately paid local components and a buffered chamber approximation into the probe used in the mission. The quantitative assumptions below are exactly the estimates the assembly consumes. In particular, the proof establishes every component of the probe conclusion and does not regard the existence of a record with those components as a proof.

Fix a frame level \(j\), its volume level \(k\), and a finite volume \(N > k\). Write

\(\displaystyle t = \hat{\beta}_k, \qquad a > 0, \qquad \beta > 0, \qquad q = (a / a_k)^5,\)

where \(a_k\) is the field scale at the volume level. In the selected construction \(k\) is the summit index of \(j\) (so \(k > j\)), \(a = a_j\) is the field scale at the frame level, and \(\beta\) is the finite-volume coupling at level \(k\) in the volume \(N\). The field scales satisfy \(a_0 = 1\) and decrease strictly, so \(a_j < 1\) for \(j > 0\): only the positivity of \(a\) is available, and only positivity is used below. Let \(\tau_j\) be the error scale

\(\displaystyle \tau_j (s) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{s}{10^4} (\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48} \right), \qquad E = \tau_j (- 430) .\)

Assume the scale bounds

\(\displaystyle t \geqslant 1, \quad 0 < \beta \leqslant 2 t, \quad 1 + 2 t \leqslant \tau_j (230), \quad 6 c \leqslant t, \) (18.101)

where \(c\) is the coefficient cost of the finite symmetry average defined below. The error scales obey \(\tau_j (s) \tau_j (s') = \tau_j (s + s')\).

Let \(\mathfrak{A}= \text{Signed} (4) \times \{0, 1\}\) be the finite motion set; the second factor records whether complex conjugation is applied. For \(\mu = (s, e)\), write \(\rho_{\mu}\) for the real-linear action on grade-five coefficients and \(\sigma_{\mu}\) for the corresponding real-linear action on observables. For \(\mu = (s, e)\) and axis \(d\), define \(\text{prev} (\mu, d) = s. \text{perm}^{- 1} (d)\) and \(\epsilon (\mu, d) = \text{sign} (s, \text{prev} (\mu, d))\); the sign does not depend on the conjugation bit \(e\). Put \(R = |\mathfrak{A}|^{- 1}\). The finite averages have the form

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} (\mathscr{S}_5 V)_d & = R \sum_{\mu \in \mathfrak{A}} \epsilon (\mu, d) \hspace{0.17em} \rho_{\mu} V_{\text{prev} (\mu, d)}, & R \sum_{\mu \in \mathfrak{A}} | \epsilon (\mu, d) | & = 1, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.102)} \\ (\mathscr{S}_0 f)_d & = R \sum_{\mu \in \mathfrak{A}} \epsilon (\mu, d) \hspace{0.17em} \sigma_{\mu} f_{\text{prev} (\mu, d)} . & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.103)} \end{array}\)

This is a real-linear Reynolds average. Reindexing by \(\mu \mapsto g \mu\) for each signed motion \(g\) and using the defining previous-axis and sign formulas gives the exact covariance \(\rho_g Z_d = \text{sign} (g, d) Z_{g. \text{perm} (d)}\). Toggling the second motion coordinate reindexes the conjugation action and proves the reality condition. In particular, central inversion fixes every axis and has sign \(- 1\), so \(\rho_{\text{archV}} Z_d = - Z_d\) for each \(d\). Oddness is a consequence of this vector-valued Reynolds projection, not a claim about arbitrary grade-five labels.

Let \(\text{Col}\) be the finite-volume column map from grade-five labels to coefficient arrays. It is real-linear and commutes with each motion by finite-volume covariance. The paid labels obey \(\| \text{Col} (X_d)\|_1, \| \text{Col} (Y_d)\|_1 \leqslant 2\); this is the transported column bound, including the norm of the observation map, rather than a bound on the raw label alone. Define its averaged coefficient cost by

\(\displaystyle c = R \sup_d \sum_{\mu \in \mathfrak{A}} \| \rho_{\mu} \|_{\text{col}} .\)

The triangle inequality yields \(\| \text{Col} (\mathscr{S}_5 V)_d \|_1 \leqslant c \sup_e \| \text{Col} (V_e)\|_1\). This is the precise finite column estimate used below; the signed or conjugate motion is real-linear, not assumed complex-linear or isometric in the column norm. The observable average has total variation one, so, on chamber-interior observables, the reflection seam makes it preserve a common root-seminorm bound.

Let \(x_d, y_d\) be two arrays of finite-volume observables, each interior to a fixed locality window. Let \(X_d, Y_d\) be their grade-five paid coefficients. The paid receipts and the finite-volume column map give the transported column bounds \(\| \text{Col} (X_d)\|_1 \leqslant 2\) and \(\| \text{Col} (Y_d)\|_1 \leqslant 2\). Write \(\mathcal{O}\) for the linear finite-volume observation into a one-point observable, \(\mathcal{B}\) for pullback from the coarse volume to the reflection chamber at level \(N\), and \(\mathcal{R}\) for the chamber root seminorm. Assume each paid residual, each pulled-back local field, and each observed label below is interior to the same reflection chamber, with the locality window and room conditions required for the finite-volume seam. On this interior class, assume the seam gives root-seminorm subadditivity and invariance under each motion. The exact covariance identities are

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{O} \hspace{0.17em} \mathscr{S}_5 = \mathscr{S}_0 \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{O}, \qquad \mathcal{B} \hspace{0.17em} \mathscr{S}_0 = \mathscr{S}_0 \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{B}. \) (18.104)

These follow term by term from linearity and covariance of the finite-volume observation and pullback under each signed motion.

The paid-component estimates used here are the following explicit inequalities, for every axis \(d\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \mathcal{R} \hspace{-0.17em} (\mathcal{B}(qx_d) -\mathcal{O}(a^5 X_d)) & \leqslant E, & \mathcal{R} \hspace{-0.17em} (\mathcal{B}(qy_d) -\mathcal{O}(a^5 Y_d)) & \leqslant E. \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.105)} \end{array}\)

The buffered chamber estimate is this: if

\(\displaystyle J_d = q (x_d - \beta y_d)\)

and \(g\) is any admissible coarse observable with supremum norm at most \(\tau_j (150000)\), interior to the fixed grade-four locality window of radius \(l_4\) of Proposition 18.7, with room for \(l_4\) at scale \(k\), then at any two distinct coarse sites,

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E} \left[ (\mathcal{B} \mathscr{S}_0 J)_d (x) \hspace{0.17em} (\mathcal{B}g) (z) \right] \right| \leqslant \tau_j (- 30000) . \) (18.106)

The estimate is supplied by the finite-volume chamber buffer; its proof is upstream of this assembly. The scale assumptions and the paid-component bounds are supplied by the construction's budget and response receipts.

Proposition 18.26. (Probe from paid components and a chamber buffer) Under the assumptions above, define

\(\displaystyle Z_d = (\mathscr{S}_5 (X - \beta Y))_d, \qquad J_d = q (x_d - \beta y_d), \qquad P_d =\mathcal{O} (a^5 Z_d), \qquad U_d = (\mathcal{B} \mathscr{S}_0 J)_d .\)

Then \(Z\) is signed-covariant, with each component satisfying \(\rho_{\text{archV}} Z_d = - Z_d\). Moreover, for every axis \(d\),

  1. \(\| \text{Col} (a^5 Z_d)\|_1 \leqslant a^5 t^4\);

  2. \(U_d\) is chamber-interior and \(\mathcal{R} (U_d - P_d) \leqslant \tau_j (- 200)\);

  3. for every admissible bounded local test in (18.106), that is, interior to the grade-four window of radius \(l_4\), the separated-site pairing of \(U_d\) with its pullback is at most \(\tau_j (- 200)\) in absolute value.

Thus \(P_d\) has exactly the coefficient, parity, root-error, and local-bound parts required of the finite-volume probe.

Proof. Real-linearity of the column map and the transported column bounds give \(\| \text{Col} (X_d - \beta Y_d)\|_1 \leqslant 2 + 2 \beta\). Covariance lets the finite-average bound apply to \(Z\), so

\(\displaystyle \| \text{Col} (Z_d)\|_1 \leqslant c (2 + 2 \beta) \leqslant c (2 + 4 t) \leqslant 6 ct \leqslant t^2 .\)

Since \(t \geqslant 1\) we have \(t^2 \leqslant t^4\). The column map is real-linear and the column norm is homogeneous under positive real scalars, so, as \(a^5 > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \| \text{Col} (a^5 Z_d)\|_1 = a^5 \| \text{Col} (Z_d)\|_1 \leqslant a^5 t^2 \leqslant a^5 t^4 .\)

No lower bound on \(a\) enters. The finite signed-motion average has the covariance just established; central inversion fixes each axis with sign \(- 1\), giving the oddness assertion for every component.

By linearity and the covariance identities,

\(\displaystyle U_d - P_d = (\mathscr{S}_0 (\mathcal{B}(qx) -\mathcal{O}(a^5 X)))_d - \beta (\mathscr{S}_0 (\mathcal{B}(qy) -\mathcal{O}(a^5 Y)))_d .\)

The root seminorm is invariant under each signed motion, is subadditive, and the averaging weights have total absolute value one. Hence

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R} (U_d - P_d) \leqslant (1 + \beta) E \leqslant \tau_j (230) \tau_j (- 430) = \tau_j (- 200),\)

where \(1 + \beta \leqslant 1 + 2 t \leqslant \tau_j (230)\) was used. The interiority of \(U_d\) follows because each motion preserves the fixed locality window and finite averaging preserves the interior domain.

Finally, the buffered estimate gives a separated-site bound at most \(\tau_j (- 30000)\), which is at most \(\tau_j (- 200)\) by monotonicity of \(\tau_j\). This proves all three conclusions.\(\Box\)

The proposition isolates the actual finite averaging and error arithmetic in the selected probe producer. The remaining inputs to this local argument, not proved in this section, are the two paid-component estimates (18.105), the finite-volume covariance identities, and the buffered chamber estimate (18.106). For all sufficiently large \(j\), the paid-component estimates and the transported column bounds are the conclusions of Proposition 18.25 with grade \(5\), applied to each observable and paid label \((x_d, X_d)\) and \((y_d, Y_d)\); its actual label and root receipt are supplied by the finite-volume response record specialization of Subsection 18.11, with one label for every allowed window. The buffered estimate is the conclusion of Proposition 18.7, in which \(F_j = \tau_j\), applied to the scale-\(k\) field \(f = (\mathscr{S}_0 J)_d\). That proposition requires a Crown witness for this particular averaged combination, together with its seam, transfer and one-site inputs; this particular grade-five witness is constructed in Subsection 18.24 by the paired-lens expansion and the exact healthy-region identity. It is distinct from the grade-four localizer in that section. Two of the scale bounds (18.101) reduce to inputs already used in Subsection 18.12. Indeed, \(t = \hat{\beta}_k\) with \(k\) the summit index of \(j\), and \(t \geqslant 1\), so \(t \leqslant t^4\). The scale-at-summit receipt is a rate-zero ticket for \(t^4\), with gate \(G_j = (\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48}\). Hence eventually \(1 + 2 t \leqslant 3 t \leqslant 3 \exp (229 \hspace{0.17em} G_j / 10^4) \leqslant \tau_j (230)\), the last step because \(3 \leqslant \tau_j (1)\) for large \(j\). Moreover \(6 c \leqslant t\) for large \(j\), since \(c\) is a fixed constant and \(\hat{\beta}_k \to \infty\) as \(k \geqslant j \to \infty\). For the other two bounds, \(t = \hat{\beta}_k \to \infty\) gives \(t \geqslant 1\) on one scale tail. The finite-volume representation at the same level \(k\) satisfies its coupling band

\(\displaystyle \tfrac{1}{2} \hat{\beta}_k \leqslant \beta_{N, p, k} \leqslant 2 \hat{\beta}_k \qquad (N \geqslant k) .\)

This is the band of the selected effective representations supplied by Lemma 13.27; it holds for all their periods and volume indices. Substituting the actual coupling \(\beta = \beta_{N, p, k}\) gives \(0 < \beta \leqslant 2 t\). This argument uses the finite-volume band, not monotonicity of an unrelated hatted coupling. Together with Lemma 18.23, it supplies all four bounds in (18.101). In particular, this section does not claim that arbitrary local components or an arbitrary chamber record satisfy those paid estimates.

18.14From finite-volume transport to the chart estimate

This section proves the passage from the selected finite-volume mission to its local bound in the limiting table. The finite-volume separation estimate of Proposition 18.57 supplies the numerical bound used by the mileage argument. The mission's funded probe, its local approximations, and the selected chart convergence are stated explicitly below; this section does not claim to produce those analytic receipts.

Write \(i\) for the scale parameter, \(j_i\) for its selected frame level, and \(k_i = \text{summit} (j_i)\) for the associated volume scale. Let \(a_i > 0\) be the field scale, \(b_i > 0\) the zoom factor, and \(r_i = a_i b_i\) the physical insertion scale. Let \(\mathcal{L}\) be the ultrafilter on finite-volume indices \(p = (N, s)\) used by the construction, and define

\(\displaystyle \tau_i (q) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{q}{10^4} (\log \hat{\beta}_{j_i})^{48} \right) .\)

A mission consists of arrays \(t_i (p)\) and \(x_i\) such that, for all sufficiently large \(i\), \(t_i (p) \to x_i\) along \(\mathcal{L}\). Moreover, there is one fixed integer \(m\) such that for all sufficiently large \(i\) and for \(\mathcal{L}\)-almost every \(p = (N, s)\), a probe of complexity at most \(m\), based at level \(j_i\), has marked grade-five field \(a_i^5 t_i (p)\). For such a probe, its measured marker \(P\) has coefficient size at most \(a_i^5 \hat{\beta}_{k_i}^m\) and is odd under the central signed inversion. Its toll receipt supplies a bounded local observable \(U\) whose pullback to the finite-volume chamber is interior and whose root error relative to \(P\) is at most \(\tau_i (- 200)\). For every pair of distinct coarse sites \(x, z\), it also supplies the bound

\(\displaystyle |\mathbb{E}_{i, p} [\tilde{U} (x) \tilde{g} (z)] | \leqslant \tau_i (- 200),\)

where tildes denote pullback to the finite-volume chamber. Here \(g\) is any coarse test whose pointwise absolute value is at most \(\tau_i (150000)\), which is interior to the fixed grade-four locality window, and whose pullback is chamber-interior, provided that the window fits in the available room at frame level \(j_i\) inside the volume at scale \(k_i\). For the selected grade-four insertion, a localization receipt supplies one coarse observable \(g\) with all of the following properties: its pointwise absolute value is at most \(\tau_i (150000)\); it is interior to the fixed grade-four locality window, and that window fits in the available room at frame level \(j_i\) inside the volume at scale \(k_i\); its pullback \(V = \tilde{g}\) is chamber-interior; the root error of \(V\) relative to the measured insertion is at most \(\tau_i (- 300)\); and the measured insertion has root cost at most \(\tau_i (5)\). The first three properties are exactly the admissibility conditions on the test in the probe's separated-site bound, so that bound applies to the pair \((\tilde{U}, V)\) with this particular \(g\); a root approximation alone would not permit this. The measured marker \(P\) has root cost at most \(\tau_i (5)\) by a separate coefficient-to-root estimate, which uses its coefficient bound and its oddness. These are the quantitative inputs consumed by the finite-volume mileage estimate; their construction remains an upstream obligation.

Fix a reference grade-four field \(w\). Let \(H_{i, p} (w)\) be its finite-height insertion approximant, which is a raw grade-four label. It is determined by the fixed reference lift of \(w\) to a raw grade-four label, by the grade-four transfer that the \(i\)th selected chart uses at the finite-volume index \(p\), by the zoom factor \(b_i\) and by the frame level \(j_i\); its formula is not needed here. The mileage estimate is formulated for insertions of exactly this form, under the conditions that this transfer is a tested transfer at level \(j_i\) and that \(1 \leqslant b_i \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j_i)\); both conditions are part of the selected chart receipt for large \(i\). The selected hierarchy receipt asserts \(H_{i, p} (w) \to y_i (w)\) along \(\mathcal{L}\), where \(y_i (w)\) is the limiting raw field and \(\pi_4 y_i (w) = U_i^{(4)} w\). It also ensures, on an \(\mathcal{L}\)-large set, that the finite transfer is admissible, the marker and insertion lie in the same reflection chamber, and the two coarse sites are distinct. Let \(\xi_i\) be the cover vector with first component \(\lceil b_i \rceil\) and every other component \(1\). Set \(z_i = b_i^{- 1} \xi_i\) componentwise. The scale relation \(r_i = a_i b_i\) gives \(r_i z_i = a_i \xi_i\). Thus the physical measurement point has the two cover coordinates

\(\displaystyle \ell_i = (\xi_i, 0) .\)

The separation receipt for these coordinates supplies the distinct-site hypothesis.

Set \(\Theta_i = \tau_i (- 100)\).

Proposition 18.27. (The chart estimate from finite-volume transport) Fix \(w\). Assume the mission properties above. Assume that for all sufficiently large \(i\) and \(\mathcal{L}\)-almost every finite-volume index \(p\), mileage applies to the probe and insertion and gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E}_{i, p} \hspace{-0.17em} \left[ A_{i, p} (a_i^5 t_i (p) ; x_i^{\ast}) \hspace{0.17em} B_{i, p} (H_{i, p} (w) ; y_i^{\ast}) \right] \right| \leqslant \Theta_i . \) (18.107)

Here \(\mathbb{E}_{i, p}\) is the normalized finite-volume expectation, \(A_{i, p}\) and \(B_{i, p}\) are the measured grade-five and grade-four observables at the two distinct sites \(x_i^{\ast}, y_i^{\ast}\) determined by \(\ell_i\). Assume the selected-table limit theorem: whenever \(u_p \to u\) and \(v_p \to v\) along \(\mathcal{L}\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{i, p} [A_{i, p} (u_p ; x_i^{\ast}) B_{i, p} (v_p ; y_i^{\ast})] \longrightarrow \mathcal{M}_i (\pi_5 u, \pi_4 v), \) (18.108)

where \(\pi_d\) is the limiting projection in grade \(d\) and \(\mathcal{M}_i\) is the selected table's two-entry pairing at the scale-\(r_i\) point. Then, for all sufficiently large \(i\) (with threshold allowed to depend on \(w\)),

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathcal{M}_i \hspace{-0.17em} (a_i^5 \pi_5 x_i, U_i^{(4)} w) \right| \leqslant \Theta_i .\)

Proof. Fix a sufficiently large \(i\) for which the mission, mileage, and chart receipts hold. The set on which the probe and finite-volume insertion conditions hold belongs to \(\mathcal{L}\). On this set, (18.107) places every finite expectation in the closed disk of radius \(\Theta_i\). Mission convergence gives \(a_i^5 t_i (p) \to a_i^5 x_i\), and the chart receipt gives \(H_{i, p} (w) \to y_i (w)\) with \(\pi_4 y_i (w) = U_i^{(4)} w\). Applying (18.108) shows that the finite expectations converge to \(\mathcal{M}_i (a_i^5 \pi_5 x_i, U_i^{(4)} w)\); linearity of \(\pi_5\) was used to take the fixed scalar \(a_i^5\) outside. Since the disk is closed, the limit satisfies the same bound. This proves the proposition.\(\Box\)

18.14.1The finite-volume transport estimate

The error scale is multiplicative: \(\tau_i (q) \tau_i (q') = \tau_i (q + q')\), and it is increasing in \(q\). In the finite-volume estimate, both measured markers have root cost at most \(\tau_i (5)\). The probe approximation contributes root error \(\tau_i (- 200)\) and separated-site bound \(\tau_i (- 200)\); the local insertion approximation contributes root error \(\tau_i (- 300)\). The reflection-positive product bound controls the two cross terms by products of root costs. Bilinearity gives the exact decomposition

\(\displaystyle \langle P, Q \rangle = \langle U, V \rangle + \langle P, Q - V \rangle + \langle P - U, V \rangle .\)

The three terms are bounded respectively by

\(\displaystyle \tau_i (- 200), \qquad \tau_i (5) \tau_i (- 300) = \tau_i (- 295), \qquad \tau_i (- 200) (\tau_i (5) + \tau_i (- 300)) = \tau_i (- 195) + \tau_i (- 500) .\)

Thus the sum is at most \(4 \tau_i (- 195)\). The selected growth receipt gives \(4 \leqslant \tau_i (1)\) eventually, hence \(4 \tau_i (- 195) \leqslant \tau_i (- 194) \leqslant \tau_i (- 100)\). Proposition 18.57 proves this three-term estimate from the explicit root and reflection-positive bounds. The remaining hard content is construction of the probe, local approximation, and root-cost receipts at the selected scales.

The mission's fixed-complexity probe is to be produced by a finite-cover construction, and its limiting value by convergence of the selected core field; both are inputs here. The limit transfer above is complete once those receipts are supplied. Producing the funded cover, the probe witness, and the quantitative localization receipts remains part of the larger construction.

18.15Weighted Ward comparison for the trace

Fix one selected family and a gauge group given as a closed subgroup of a finite-dimensional unitary group, whose real Lie algebra is nonabelian and simple. Let \(E\) be the real finite-dimensional space of grade-five vector coefficient arrays obeying the reality and signed covariance conditions, and let \(H\) be the corresponding reference vector table space. The free projection \(f : E \to H\) applies the reference field projection in each vector component. Let \(x \in E\) be the coefficient array of the free equation of motion. The free rank calculation in Subsection 16.1 gives \(x \ne 0\) and \(\ker f =\mathbb{R}x\). To fix the free Ward normalization, write the raw equation-of-motion array as \(J^{(3)}_b = \sum_{i, a} \langle F_{bi}, \partial_a F_{ai} \rangle\), and let

\(\displaystyle (J^{(1)})_b = \sum_i \langle F_{bi}, \partial_b F_{bi} \rangle, \qquad (J^{(2)})_b = \sum_{i, j} \langle F_{ij}, \partial_b F_{ij} \rangle .\)

Here \(F\) and \(\partial F\) are the free curvature jet and its first derivative, and the inner product is the gauge-invariant real color inner product. Each array is real and invariant under the color action. For a coordinate reflection, every contracted index contributes its sign twice, leaving only the sign of the output index \(b\); permutations merely relabel the sums. Thus each array has the required signed-vector covariance. These three arrays form a basis of the raw vector space. To see independence, choose a unit color vector and evaluate the zeroth component on three jets. In each, the only nonzero curvature pair, up to antisymmetry, is respectively \(F_{12}\), \(F_{01}\), and \(F_{01}\). The nonzero derivative entries, again up to antisymmetry, are respectively

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c|l} 1 & \partial_0 F_{12} = 1, \quad \partial_1 F_{20} = - \tfrac{1}{2}, \quad \partial_2 F_{01} = - \tfrac{1}{2},\\ 2 & \partial_0 F_{01} = 1, \quad \partial_2 F_{21} = - 1,\\ 3 & \partial_0 F_{01} = 1. \end{array}\)

Each scalar stands for that scalar times the chosen color vector. These jets satisfy the Bianchi identity; for example a curvature jet \((F_{ab}, K_{cab})\) with \(K_{cab} + K_{abc} + K_{bca} = 0\) is realized by the polynomial potential \(A_b (z) = \tfrac{1}{2} z^a F_{ab} + \tfrac{1}{3} z^a z^c K_{cab}\). It is in radial gauge, since \(z^b A_b (z) = 0\) by antisymmetry in \(a, b\). Its curl at the origin is \(F_{ab}\), and its first curl derivative is

\(\displaystyle \partial_c (\partial_a A_b - \partial_b A_a) (0) = \tfrac{1}{3} (2 K_{cab} - K_{abc} - K_{bca}) = K_{cab},\)

where the last equality uses the cyclic identity. Direct substitution gives the evaluation matrix

\(\displaystyle \left( \begin{array}{ccc} 0 & 2 & 0\\ 1 & 2 & 0\\ 1 & 2 & 1 \end{array} \right) \quad \text{for } (J^{(1)}_0, J^{(2)}_0, J^{(3)}_0) .\)

Its determinant is nonzero. The free-rank calculation gives the matching upper bound three for the raw vector dimension, so the arrays form a basis. The label-coordinate identification carries \(x\) to \(J^{(3)}\). Every raw vector therefore has unique real coordinates \((w_0, w_1, w_2)\) in the form

\(\displaystyle 2 (w_0 - w_2) J^{(1)} + (2 w_1 + \tfrac{1}{2} w_2) J^{(2)} + w_2 J^{(3)} .\)

Indeed, the \(J^{(3)}\) coefficient determines \(w_2\), and the other two coefficients then determine \(w_0\) and \(w_1\). To see the Ward normalization, let \(\mathcal{I}= \sum_{i, j} \langle F_{ij}, F_{ij} \rangle\) and consider the centered stress tensor with diagonal entries \(T_{aa} = w_0 \sum_i \langle F_{ai}, F_{ai} \rangle + w_1 \mathcal{I}\) and off-diagonal entries \(T_{ab} = w_2 \sum_i \langle F_{ai}, F_{bi} \rangle\). Its divergence has the displayed raw-vector coordinates. Indeed the diagonal derivatives contribute \(2 w_0 J^{(1)} + 2 w_1 J^{(2)}\). For the off-diagonal term, first sum over all \(a\). One product-rule term is \(J^{(3)}\). For the other, write \(B = \sum_{a, i} \langle F_{ai}, \partial_a F_{bi} \rangle\). The cyclic Bianchi identity, antisymmetry, and interchange of \(a, i\) give

\(\displaystyle B = \sum_{a, i} \langle F_{ai}, \partial_b F_{ai} \rangle - \sum_{a, i} \langle F_{ai}, \partial_i F_{ab} \rangle = J^{(2)} - B.\)

Hence the two terms sum to \(J^{(3)} + \tfrac{1}{2} J^{(2)}\). Removing \(a = b\) subtracts \(2 J^{(1)}\). Its trace is \((w_0 + 4 w_1) \mathcal{I}\). The scalar normalization is \(\mathcal{I}/ 4\), and the free Ward functional \(\rho : E \to \mathbb{R}\) is minus the coefficient of that scalar label in the trace. The label is nonzero, as seen by taking \(F_{01}\) to be a unit color vector and the other independent curvature components to be zero. Hence \(\rho = - 4 (w_0 + 4 w_1)\) on the above coordinates. In particular \(J^{(3)}\) has coordinates \((1, - \tfrac{1}{4}, 1)\) and \(\rho (x) = 0\).

Let \(k_i\) be the selected initial levels, with \(k_i \to \infty\). Fix the reference level \(j_0\) of the grade-five coefficient space. All finite-volume maps and estimates below are used at levels \(j \geqslant j_0\); in particular, \(k_i \geqslant j_0\) eventually. The running rate is \(\hat{\beta}_n = \bar{\beta} + \gamma \ell_n\), where \(\gamma > 0\), \(\hat{\beta}_n > 1\), and the schedule scale \(\ell_n \to \infty\). For a level \(j\), let \(q (j) > j\) be the first level whose logarithmic scale separation from \(j\) is at least \((\log \hat{\beta}_j)^{48}\), and put \(n_i = q (k_i)\). Consequently \(n_i \to \infty\), \(\hat{\beta}_{n_i} \to \infty\), and the positive weight

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{a}_i = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left\{ \frac{1}{5} (\log \log \hat{\beta}_{n_i})^2 \right\} \longrightarrow \infty . \) (18.109)

This is the \(1 / 5\) age weight at the postselected level \(n_i\); it is not the reciprocal chart scale.

We shall use the following clock and arch inputs. The schedule has natural block sizes \(L_j\) and real increments \(\ell_{j + 1} - \ell_j = \log L_j\), with \(L_j \geqslant 3\) and, eventually, \(L_j \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_j\). The clock offset \(\bar{\beta}\) and the arch scales \(b_j\) are positive, and for every \(\varepsilon > 0\),

\(\displaystyle |b_j - \hat{\beta}_j | \leqslant \varepsilon \ell_j \quad \text{for all sufficiently large } j. \) (18.110)

These are selected-run inputs.

Lemma 18.28. (Arch-scale ratio) Under the clock inputs above, \(b_{k_i} / b_{n_i} \to 1\).

Proof. Put \(\chi = \hat{\beta}_k\) and \(g = (\log \chi)^{48}\), and let \(q (k) = m + 1\). By minimality, \(\ell_m - \ell_k \leqslant g\): if \(m = k\) it is zero, and otherwise the preceding level has not reached the threshold. For large \(k\), \(\gamma g \leqslant \chi\), so \(\hat{\beta}_m \leqslant 2 \chi\). The last increment obeys

\(\displaystyle \ell_{m + 1} - \ell_m = \log L_m \leqslant L_m \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_m \leqslant \log (2 \chi) \leqslant g.\)

Consequently \(0 \leqslant \ell_{q (k)} - \ell_k \leqslant 2 g\), and therefore

\(\displaystyle \frac{\hat{\beta}_{q (k)}}{\hat{\beta}_k} = 1 + \gamma \frac{\ell_{q (k)} - \ell_k}{\hat{\beta}_k} \longrightarrow 1,\)

because \((\log \chi)^{48} / \chi \to 0\). Also \(\hat{\beta}_j = \bar{\beta} + \gamma \ell_j \geqslant \gamma \ell_j\), so (18.110) gives \(b_j / \hat{\beta}_j \to 1\). Since both \(k_i\) and \(n_i = q (k_i)\) tend to infinity,

\(\displaystyle \frac{b_{k_i}}{b_{n_i}} \longrightarrow 1. \) (18.111)

\(\Box\)

Write \(s_i : E \to H\) for the selected, normalized grade-five chart map. For one vector component let \(\textsf{V}\) be the complex coefficient space, \(\mathcal{F}\) the reference field space and \(S_5\) the selected interacting field space. The interacting projection is \(P : \textsf{V} \to S_5\), the scale-\(i\) identification is a linear isomorphism \(X_i : \mathcal{F} \to S_5\), and the selected terminal lift is \(Y_i : \mathcal{F} \to \textsf{V}\). Write \(M_i : \textsf{V} \to \textsf{V}\) for the invertible arch map at \(n_i\), \(t_i > 0\) for the threshold there, and \(\text{core} : \textsf{V} \to \textsf{V}\) for the projection deleting the unit coordinate: if \(e_0\) is the unit coordinate vector, then \(\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v = v - v_0 e_0\). These component maps are complex-linear; \(s_i\) and \(u_i\) act real-linearly on the corresponding covariant arrays. With arch scale \(b_n > 0\) and dilation radius \(r_i > 0\), set \(c_i = b_{n_i} r_i^5\). On an eventual common tail, for each vector component indexed by \(\alpha\),

\(\displaystyle (s_i y)_{\alpha} = X_i^{- 1} P (c_i t_i^2 M_i^{- 1} y_{\alpha}), \qquad (u_i h)_{\alpha} = c_i^{- 1} t_i^{- 2} \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} M_i Y_i h_{\alpha} . \) (18.112)

The second formula is for the selected lift. Its well-definedness as a map into \(E\) follows from the chart symmetries below. On the finitely many indices outside this tail, choose arbitrary real-linear maps \(u_i : H \to E\). The limiting right inverse \(U\) applies the fixed reference lift to each component. For that lift, the reference field is the quotient of the coefficient space by its free equation subspace \(K\). For the finite signed-permutation group \(\Gamma\), write \(gz\) for the action on \(z \in \textsf{V}\) and equip \(\textsf{V}\) with the Hermitian form

\(\displaystyle \langle z, w \rangle_{\ast} = \sum_{g \in \Gamma} \sum_a \overline{(gz)_a} (gw)_a .\)

It is positive definite because the identity term occurs. Reindexing the sum proves invariance under \(\Gamma\); conjugation is antiunitary because it commutes with this action. The free equations are real and signed permutation equivariant; their vanishing subspace \(K\) is therefore stable under these symmetries. Each quotient class has a unique representative in \(K^{\perp}\), and taking that representative is a linear section intertwining the signed action and conjugation. Applied componentwise, it therefore maps \(H\) into \(E\) and satisfies \(fU = \text{id}_H\). These formulas use the same selected history and the same index \(i\); the symbols \(n_i\) and \(b_n\) are as above (with \(b_n\) denoting the arch scale). The following identities hold on a common tail. The first was just proved; the second is a terminal projection hypothesis, and the third follows from the chart symmetries as shown below:

\(\displaystyle fU = \text{id}_H, \qquad PY_i = X_i, \qquad \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} M_i Y_i h_{\alpha} = M_i Y_i h_{\alpha} . \) (18.113)

The last equality holds simultaneously for every \(h \in H\) and every component \(\alpha\). On the same tail, \(Y_i\) and \(M_i\) intertwine signed permutations and conjugation. Thus \(M_i Y_i\) carries real covariant arrays to real covariant arrays. Inversion of all four axes acts by \(- 1\) on each lifted component and fixes the unit coefficient, so that coefficient vanishes; the core deletes only that coefficient. This proves the last identity in (18.113). The scalar \(c_i^{- 1} t_i^{- 2}\) is real, so the second formula in (18.112) also defines a real-linear map \(u_i : H \to E\). The chart identities imply, on this same tail,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} (s_i u_i h)_{\alpha} & = X_i^{- 1} P \hspace{-0.17em} \left( c_i t_i^2 M_i^{- 1} (c_i^{- 1} t_i^{- 2} \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} M_i Y_i h_{\alpha}) \right)\\ & = X_i^{- 1} P (Y_i h_{\alpha}) = h_{\alpha} . \end{array}\)

Thus \(s_i u_i = \text{id}_H\) eventually, uniformly over \(h\). The construction of the chart and its symmetry and terminal projection properties are assumed here, not consequences of the limiting rank alone.

We also record the quantitative inputs behind convergence of the selected right inverses. For any level \(j\), write \(\vartheta_j > 0\) for its threshold, \(M (j)\) for its arch map, and \(W_j = \vartheta_j^{- 2} \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} M (j)\); thus \(M_i = M (n_i)\) and \(t_i = \vartheta_{n_i}\). Let \(| \cdot |_1\) be the sum of absolute values of the finite coefficient coordinates, and set

\(\displaystyle D_i = b_{k_i} r_i^5, \quad \eta_i = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left\{ - \tfrac{1}{2} (\log \log \hat{\beta}_{k_i})^2 \right\}, \quad \delta_i = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left\{ - \tfrac{2}{5} (\log \log \hat{\beta}_{k_i})^2 \right\} .\)

Both \(\eta_i\) and \(\delta_i\) tend to zero. For fixed \(h \in H\) and component \(\alpha\), write \(y_i = Y_i h_{\alpha}\) and \(p = (Uh)_{\alpha}\). The next two estimates follow from the finite-volume inputs listed in (S1)–(S6) below; they are derived consequences, not new Ward comparison hypotheses.

Lemma 18.29. (Initial-level bound) Under those inputs, for each fixed \(h\) and \(\alpha\), eventually

\(\displaystyle |W_{k_i} y_i - D_i p|_1 \leqslant 2 \eta_i D_i |p|_1 . \) (18.114)

Proof. The finite-volume indices are pairs \(\omega = (N, \nu) \in \mathbb{N}^2\), where \(N\) is the mesh cutoff index and \(\nu\) the period index. Fix the selected ultrafilter \(\mathcal{L}\) refining the fused order of these pairs. Write \(\mathcal{F}_{\text{fuse}}\) for its fused-order filter; the refinement is \(\mathcal{L} \leqslant \mathcal{F}_{\text{fuse}}\) in the filter order; it contains both coordinate tails, which are the consequences used below. The initial-level estimate follows from a finite-volume estimate on this ultrafilter. For each sufficiently large \(i\), fixed \(h\) and component \(\alpha\), use the same \(y_i\) and \(p\) as above. Suppose finite-volume coefficients \(Z_i (\omega)\) and normalizations \(B_i (\omega)\), positive on an ultrafilter-large set, satisfy

\(\displaystyle |Z_i (\omega) - B_i (\omega) p|_1 \leqslant 2 \eta_i B_i (\omega) |p|_1 \quad \text{on an ultrafilter-large set}, \) (18.115)

and converge along that ultrafilter to \(W_{k_i} y_i\) and \(D_i\), respectively. These limits have a common source. Let \(\text{Col}_j (\omega)\) be the finite-volume transport at level \(j\). Assume that for each fixed admissible \(j\), whenever \(v (\omega) \to v\) along the selected ultrafilter, \(\text{Col}_j (\omega) v (\omega) \to M (j) v\); also assume the finite-volume running parameter at level \(j\) tends to \(b_j\). Write \(\beta_i (\omega)\) for that parameter at \(k_i\). The selected finite-volume lift satisfies \(\lambda_i (\omega) \to y_i\). The definitions give

\(\displaystyle Z_i (\omega) = \vartheta_{k_i}^{- 2} \text{coreCol}_{k_i} (\omega) \lambda_i (\omega), \qquad B_i (\omega) = \beta_i (\omega) r_i^5 .\)

The two stated limits follow by continuity of \(\text{core}\), with \(i\) fixed. The joint transport and lift convergence remain hypotheses; the finite-volume bound follows from the tested-map estimates below. Continuity of the coefficient norm and the closedness of the inequality give (18.114). The ultrafilter limit is taken for each fixed \(i\) before the limit \(i \to \infty\).

The finite-volume bound has a useful decomposition into its tested-map and calibration inputs. Fix \(i\) and \(\omega = (N, \nu)\) with \(N > k_i\); suppress both indices, and let \(\textsf{A}\) and \(\textsf{T}\) be the transported reference map and its tested approximation. They are complex-linear endomorphisms \(\textsf{A}, \textsf{T} : \textsf{V} \to \textsf{V}\) of the finite complex coefficient space and send the reference unit \(\boldsymbol{1}\) to the unit coordinate vector \(e_0\). Assume that \(\textsf{A}\) is injective and, for every input \(v\),

\(\displaystyle | \textsf{A} v - \textsf{T} v|_1 \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} | \textsf{A} v|_1, \qquad | \textsf{A} v - \textsf{T} v|_1 \leqslant \eta_i | \textsf{A} v|_1 . \) (18.116)

These are separate tested-map and relative-estimates. The first also shows that \(\textsf{T}\) is injective: if \(\textsf{T} v = 0\), then \(| \textsf{A} v|_1 \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} | \textsf{A} v|_1\), so \(\textsf{A} v = 0\) and \(v = 0\). Since \(\textsf{V}\) is finite-dimensional, \(\textsf{T}\) is invertible. For a given \(v\), put \(c = (\textsf{T} v)_0 \in \mathbb{C}\) and \(v^{\circ} = v - c\boldsymbol{1}\). Then \(\textsf{T} v^{\circ} = \text{core} \textsf{T} v\) and \(\text{core} \textsf{A} v^{\circ} = \text{core} \textsf{A} v\). The first bound in (18.116) and the triangle inequality give \(| \textsf{A} v^{\circ} |_1 \leqslant 2 | \textsf{T} v^{\circ} |_1\). Applying \(\text{core}\) to the second bound, and using its nonexpansiveness, yields

\(\displaystyle | \text{core} \textsf{A} v - \text{core} \textsf{T} v|_1 \leqslant 2 \eta_i | \text{core} \textsf{T} v|_1 . \) (18.117)

For the particular selected lift \(v = \lambda_i (\omega)\), its definition identifies the tested image and finite-volume normalization. Write \(d_{k_i} > 0\) for the lattice spacing at \(k_i\), put \(\sigma_i = r_i / d_{k_i}\), and let \(\beta_i (\omega) > 0\) be the finite-volume running parameter. The transported reference map is \(\textsf{A} = d_{k_i}^{- 5} \text{Col}_{k_i} (\omega)\), which explains the normalization of \(Z_i\) below. The vector covariance of \(h\) makes each \(h_{\alpha}\) odd under total inversion; the reference lift intertwines that action, so \(p = (Uh)_{\alpha}\) is odd as well. We recall the short degree argument for the reference frame. Its basis elements are homogeneous in position degree and factor degree, and the only factor-degree-zero basis element is the unit. Each jet factor has position weight at least two; since the frame has degree at most five, a nonunit basis element has at most two factors. A one-factor invariant vanishes by the invariant-linear-form argument in the low-grade label calculation of Subsection 16.1. Thus every nonunit basis element has exactly two factors and position degree four or five. Total inversion acts on a homogeneous position-degree-\(d\) label by \((- 1)^d\): a jet of order \(r\) has both inversion sign \((- 1)^{r + 2}\) and position weight \(r + 2\). Consequently every nonzero nonunit coordinate of the odd vector \(p\) has position degree five and factor degree two. The identities used here are

\(\displaystyle \text{core} \textsf{T} \lambda_i = \beta_i \vartheta_{k_i}^2 \sigma_i^5 p, \qquad Z_i = \vartheta_{k_i}^{- 2} d_{k_i}^5 \text{core} \textsf{A} \lambda_i, \qquad B_i = \beta_i r_i^5 . \) (18.118)

The first identity follows from the definition of the selected finite-volume lift and the grade-five scaling rule. Put \(\xi = (\sqrt{\beta_i})^{- 1} / \vartheta_{k_i}\). For a frame coordinate \(\iota\) with position degree \(d_{\iota}\) and factor degree \(n_{\iota}\), define the position and factor dilations by
