\(\displaystyle (\text{Pos}_t \hspace{0.17em} v)_{\iota} = t^{d_{\iota}} v_{\iota}, \qquad (\text{Dil}_t \hspace{0.17em} v)_{\iota} = t^{n_{\iota}} v_{\iota} .\)

For \(t > 0\), these coordinate formulas give \(\text{Dil}_t^{- 1} = \text{Dil}_{t^{- 1}}\). On the selected tail, where \(i \geqslant 5\), the finite-volume lift is

\(\displaystyle \lambda_i = \textsf{T}^{- 1} \text{Dil}_{\xi^{- 1}} \hspace{0.17em} \text{Pos}_{\sigma_i} \hspace{0.17em} p.\)

Consequently \(\text{Dil}_{\xi} \textsf{T} \lambda_i = \text{Pos}_{\sigma_i} \hspace{0.17em} p\). The reference section \(p\) is odd and has nonunit degree five, so this weight is \(\sigma_i^5 p\). On these nonunit coordinates the inverse coefficient dilation \(\text{Dil}_{\xi^{- 1}}\) acts by \(\xi^{- 2}\). Applying that inverse and \(\text{core}\), with \(\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} p = p\), gives the first identity in (18.118), because \(\xi^{- 2} = \beta_i \vartheta_{k_i}^2\). This is an exact conversion of the lift and grading normalizations. The other two identities specify the actual normalization. Multiplying (18.117) by \(\vartheta_{k_i}^{- 2} d_{k_i}^5\) and using \(r_i = d_{k_i} \sigma_i\) gives exactly (18.115). Thus the tested-map estimates, selected-lift construction and finite-volume limits are the remaining inputs for the initial-level bound, rather than an additional assumed Ward comparison bound.\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.30. (Transport bound) Under (S1)–(S6), for each fixed \(h\) and \(\alpha\), eventually

\(\displaystyle |W_{k_i} y_i - W_{n_i} y_i |_1 \leqslant \delta_i |W_{n_i} y_i |_1 . \) (18.119)

Proof. For the one-step input, fix \(i\), \(h\) and \(\alpha\) and write \(N_i = n_i - k_i > 0\). On a set belonging to the selected ultrafilter, suppose there are finite-volume coefficient-vector functions \(z_j (\omega)\) for \(k_i \leqslant j \leqslant n_i\), all formed from the same terminal label \(y_i\), with

\(\displaystyle |z_j (\omega) - z_{j + 1} (\omega) |_1 \leqslant \frac{\delta_i}{2 N_i} |z_{j + 1} (\omega) |_1 \quad (k_i \leqslant j < n_i) . \) (18.120)

Here \(z_j (\omega) = \vartheta_j^{- 2} \text{coreCol}_j (\omega) y_i\). For each fixed \(j\) in this finite interval, the joint transport input above, applied to the constant label \(y_i\), gives the ultrafilter limit \(W_j y_i\). We derive the one-step bound below from the underlying residual and budget inputs, and now prove (18.119) from it.

Fix \(\omega\) in that set and abbreviate \(z_j = z_j (\omega)\). Put \(a = \delta_i / N_i\). For large \(i\) we have \(\delta_i \leqslant 1\). Backward induction gives, for every \(j\) in the interval,

\(\displaystyle |z_j - z_{n_i} |_1 \leqslant a (n_i - j) |z_{n_i} |_1 .\)

It is immediate at \(j = n_i\). At the induction step, the bound at \(j + 1\) and \(aN_i \leqslant 1\) give \(|z_{j + 1} |_1 \leqslant 2 |z_{n_i} |_1\). The one-step input therefore bounds \(|z_j - z_{j + 1} |_1\) by \(a |z_{n_i} |_1\), and the triangle inequality proves the next step. At \(j = k_i\) this is \(|z_{k_i} - z_{n_i} |_1 \leqslant \delta_i |z_{n_i} |_1\). Passing to the ultrafilter limit in the finite-dimensional coefficient space yields (18.119). The finite intersection over the interval is taken before this limit, for each fixed \(i\); no uniform limit in \(i\) or new choice of history is used.

The one-step bound follows from the following assumptions. Write \(\tilde{z}_j = \text{Col}_j (\omega) y_i\) for the finite-volume coefficient before threshold normalization; thus \(z_j = \vartheta_j^{- 2} \text{core} \tilde{z}_j\). Let the deterministic block transport be \((\text{Block}_j \hspace{0.17em} v)_{\iota} = L_j^{5 - d_{\iota}} v_{\iota}\). For \(a \in \mathbb{R}\) and level \(j\), put

\(\displaystyle A_a (j) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \{ a (\log \log \hat{\beta}_j)^2 \}, \qquad g_j = \frac{2 (1 + C_{j + 1})}{A_{1 / 2} (j + 1)},\)

where \(C_j \geqslant 0\) is the selected real column budget. The budget input is \(C_j \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_j\) eventually. For all levels in the interval, on the same ultrafilter-large set, assume

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \frac{\vartheta_{j + 1}}{\vartheta_j} & \leqslant 2, & | \tilde{z}_j - \text{Dil}_{\vartheta_j / \vartheta_{j + 1}} \hspace{0.17em} \text{Block}_j \tilde{z}_{j + 1} |_1 & \leqslant g_j | \text{core} \tilde{z}_{j + 1} |_1 . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.121)} \end{array}\)

In the selected construction, for each fixed level \(j\) the residual and threshold bounds hold for all sufficiently large \(\nu\), uniformly in \(N > j\) and the terminal label. For fixed \(i\), take the maximum of the finitely many period thresholds for \(k_i \leqslant j < n_i\) and also require \(N \geqslant n_i\); their intersection belongs to the same ultrafilter. This justifies using one large set for the entire finite interval, without a uniform choice over \(i\).

The terminal label \(y_i\) is odd under inversion of all axes, and the finite-volume transport preserves that inversion; hence every \(\tilde{z}_j\) is odd. The reference-frame classification used above therefore shows that a nonzero nonunit coordinate of \(\tilde{z}_{j + 1}\) has position degree five and factor degree two. On a coordinate of position degree \(d\), block transport multiplies by \(L_j^{5 - d}\); on one of factor degree \(n\), coefficient dilation by \(t\) multiplies by \(t^n\). Thus, on every nonunit coordinate of \(\tilde{z}_{j + 1}\), the block factor is one and the dilation factor is \((\vartheta_j / \vartheta_{j + 1})^2\). The core deletes the unit coordinate, so

\(\displaystyle \text{coreDil}_{\vartheta_j / \vartheta_{j + 1}} \hspace{0.17em} \text{Block}_j \tilde{z}_{j + 1} = \left( \frac{\vartheta_j}{\vartheta_{j + 1}} \right)^2 \text{core} \tilde{z}_{j + 1} . \) (18.122)

The core projection is nonexpansive in \(| \cdot |_1\). Applying \(\text{core}\) to the residual and dividing by \(\vartheta_j^2\) gives

\(\displaystyle |z_j - z_{j + 1} |_1 \leqslant g_j \left( \frac{\vartheta_{j + 1}}{\vartheta_j} \right)^2 |z_{j + 1} |_1 \leqslant 4 g_j |z_{j + 1} |_1 .\)

We verify that \(4 g_j \leqslant \delta_i / (2 N_i)\) uniformly for \(k_i \leqslant j < n_i\) when \(i\) is large. Put \(\chi = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}\). The clock estimates already proved imply \(\hat{\beta}_{n_i} \leqslant 2 \chi\) eventually. Since each increment \(\log L_j \geqslant 1\), the overshoot bound gives \(N_i \leqslant 2 (\log \chi)^{48}\). For large \(i\) we have \(\chi \geqslant e\), so \(t \mapsto \exp \{ \tfrac{1}{2} (\log \log t)^2 \}\) is increasing for \(t \geqslant \chi\). Monotonicity of the clock then gives \(C_{j + 1} \leqslant 2 \log \chi\) and \(A_{1 / 2} (j + 1) \geqslant A_{1 / 2} (k_i)\) for large \(i\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle 2 N_i (4 g_j) \leqslant \frac{32 (\log \chi)^{48} (1 + 2 \log \chi)}{A_{1 / 2} (k_i)} \leqslant A_{- 2 / 5} (k_i) = \delta_i .\)

The last inequality follows because every fixed power of \(\log \chi\) is eventually bounded by \(A_{1 / 10} (k_i)\): after taking logarithms this is linear versus quadratic growth in \(\log \log \chi\). This proves (18.120). The residual, preservation of oddness, threshold and column-budget bounds remain hypotheses; the degree classification, grade-scaling identity, bound calculation and telescoping above prove their consequence.\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.31. (Convergence of the selected lifts) Under (S1)–(S6), \(u_i \to U\) in operator norm.

Proof. The preceding estimates prove \(u_i h \to Uh\). Indeed put \(\textsf{L}_i = D_i^{- 1} W_{k_i} y_i\) and \(\textsf{R}_i = D_i^{- 1} W_{n_i} y_i\). The first estimate gives \(| \textsf{L}_i - p|_1 \leqslant 2 \eta_i |p|_1\), and the second gives \(| \textsf{L}_i - \textsf{R}_i |_1 \leqslant \delta_i | \textsf{R}_i |_1\). For \(\delta_i < 1\), the triangle inequality gives \(| \textsf{R}_i |_1 \leqslant (1 + 2 \eta_i) (1 - \delta_i)^{- 1} |p|_1\) and hence

\(\displaystyle | \textsf{R}_i - p|_1 \leqslant \frac{2 \eta_i + \delta_i}{1 - \delta_i} |p|_1 .\)

Since \(c_i = b_{n_i} r_i^5\), the formula for \(u_i\) gives \((u_i h)_{\alpha} = (b_{k_i} / b_{n_i}) \textsf{R}_i\). Thus the component error is at most

\(\displaystyle \left( \left| \frac{b_{k_i}}{b_{n_i}} - 1 \right| + \frac{b_{k_i}}{b_{n_i}} \frac{2 \eta_i + \delta_i}{1 - \delta_i} \right) |p|_1,\)

which tends to zero. There are finitely many components, so \(u_i h \to Uh\) in \(E\); finite dimensionality upgrades this pointwise convergence to operator-norm convergence.\(\Box\)

We collect the inputs used in the preceding lemmas and the proposition. They concern one selected family, one chart and one ultrafilter throughout; none is a new assumption of a Ward comparison estimate.

    (S1)

    The selected levels \(k_i \to \infty\) and the schedule has \(\hat{\beta}_j = \bar{\beta} + \gamma \ell_j > 1\), with \(\bar{\beta}, \gamma > 0\) and \(\ell_j \to \infty\). Its increments satisfy \(\ell_{j + 1} - \ell_j = \log L_j\), with \(L_j \geqslant 3\) and eventually \(L_j \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_j\). The arch scales \(b_j\) are positive and, for every \(\varepsilon > 0\), satisfy \(|b_j - \hat{\beta}_j | \leqslant \varepsilon \ell_j\) eventually in \(j\). The postselected level \(n_i = q (k_i) > k_i\) is the first level at logarithmic separation at least \((\log \hat{\beta}_{k_i})^{48}\), and the weight is (18.109).

    (S2)

    The finite-dimensional real spaces \(E, H\), projection \(f\), nonzero \(x\), \(\ker f =\mathbb{R}x\) and Ward form \(\rho\) are the free data fixed above. The chart maps \(s_i\), \(X_i\), \(Y_i\), \(M_i\) and positive scales use the same selected family and satisfy (18.112) and \(PY_i = X_i\) on a common eventual tail for every field. The chart maps intertwine the signed permutations and conjugation there; the reference section \(U\) has \(fU = \text{id}_H\).

    (S3)

    The selected ultrafilter \(\mathcal{L}\) refines the fused order. For each fixed admissible \(j\), \(\text{Col}_j (\omega) v (\omega) \to M (j) v\) whenever \(v (\omega) \to v\) along \(\mathcal{L}\); the finite running parameter tends to \(b_j\). For each fixed sufficiently large \(i\) and each field component, the selected finite-volume lift \(\lambda_i (\omega)\) converges to \(Y_i h_{\alpha}\) along this same ultrafilter. All these volume limits are taken before \(i \to \infty\).

    (S4)

    On the selected tail, for every period and every \(N > k_i\), the complex-linear endomorphisms \(\textsf{A}, \textsf{T}\) of \(\textsf{V}\) send the reference unit to \(e_0\), \(\textsf{A}\) is injective, and the two bounds (18.116) hold for every coefficient vector. For each fixed \(h\) and component, the selected lift is

    \(\displaystyle \lambda_i = \textsf{T}^{- 1} \text{Dil}_{\xi^{- 1}} \hspace{0.17em} \text{Pos}_{\sigma_i} (Uh)_{\alpha}, \qquad \xi = (\sqrt{\beta_i})^{- 1} / \vartheta_{k_i} .\)

    Further, \(\textsf{A} = d_{k_i}^{- 5} \text{Col}_{k_i}\), with positive \(\beta_i\), \(d_{k_i}\), \(r_i\), and \(\sigma_i = r_i / d_{k_i}\). The calibration identities (18.118) are derived from these data and the grading, as shown in the proof.

    (S5)

    For each fixed level \(j \geqslant j_0\), eventually in the period index \(\nu\), uniformly for every \(N > j\) and every terminal coefficient label, the threshold ratio and residual bounds (18.121) hold with \(\tilde{z}_j = \text{Col}_j (\omega) v\) for that label \(v\). For each fixed \(i\) their finite intersection over \(k_i \leqslant j < n_i\), together with \(N \geqslant n_i\), is taken along the same fused-order ultrafilter; no tail uniform in \(i\) is assumed.

    (S6)

    The selected column budgets obey \(C_j \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_j\) eventually. The reference lift is odd under total inversion and the finite-volume transport preserves oddness; the grade-five frame and block/dilation rules are those used above. These inputs purchase the transport estimate, rather than assuming it.

    (S7)

    The marked sequence \(v_i \in E\) has the three limits in (18.123) below. It is arbitrary subject to those limits; it need not be the later medium example.

Proposition 18.32. (Selected trace Ward comparison) Assume (S1)–(S6). By Lemmas 18.28, 18.29, 18.30 and 18.31, the stated chart has an eventual right inverse and \(u_i \to U\) in operator norm. Suppose a sequence \(v_i \in E\) satisfies, in the indicated norms,

\(\displaystyle v_i \longrightarrow x, \qquad \mathfrak{a}_i s_i (v_i) \longrightarrow 0 \text{in } H, \qquad \mathfrak{a}_i \rho (v_i) \longrightarrow 0 \text{in } \mathbb{R}. \) (18.123)

Then \(s_i \to f\) in operator norm. Moreover there are linear functionals \(\mu_i : H \to \mathbb{R}\) and \(C \geqslant 0\) such that, for all sufficiently large \(i\), \(| \mu_i (h) | \leqslant C \|h\|\) for every \(h \in H\), and for every \(\varepsilon > 0\), for all sufficiently large \(i\),

\(\displaystyle |\mathfrak{a}_i (\mu_i (s_i y) - \rho (y)) | \leqslant \varepsilon \|y\| \quad \text{for every } y \in E. \) (18.124)

The tail in the second assertion may depend on \(\varepsilon\), but is uniform in \(y\).

Proof. Since \(\mathfrak{a}_i > 0\) and \(\mathfrak{a}_i \to \infty\), the first weighted limit in (18.123) implies \(s_i (v_i) \to 0\). Choose a linear functional \(A : E \to \mathbb{R}\) for which \(y = Uf (y) + A (y) x\) for every \(y\): indeed \(y - Uf (y)\) lies in \(\ker f\), and its coefficient along the nonzero \(x\) is linear. Define \(T_i = u_i f + A \otimes v_i\). The chart and marked-vector limits imply \(T_i \to \text{id}_E\) in operator norm. For sufficiently large \(i\), \(T_i\) is invertible and \(T_i^{- 1} \to \text{id}_E\), by the geometric series for \(\text{id}_E - T_i\).

On the common right-inverse tail,

\(\displaystyle s_i T_i y = f (y) + A (y) s_i (v_i) .\)

This also gives a bound without assuming the norms of \(s_i\) in advance. Put \(\epsilon_i = \|T_i - \text{id}_E \|\); for \(\epsilon_i < 1\),

\(\displaystyle \|s_i - f\| \leqslant \frac{\|A\| \hspace{0.17em} \|s_i (v_i) \|+ \|f\| \epsilon_i}{1 - \epsilon_i} .\)

Indeed subtract \(fT_i\) from the displayed identity and multiply on the right by \(T_i^{- 1}\). Both terms in the numerator tend to zero, proving \(s_i \to f\). Now apply the weighted rank-one receiver lemma (Lemma 18.55) with \((v_i, u_i, w_i, e_i)\) there equal to \((s_i, u_i, v_i, \mathfrak{a}_i)\) here. Its two weighted hypotheses are precisely the last two limits in (18.123); its other hypotheses follow from the preceding paragraph and (18.113). It gives \(\mu_i = \rho u_i\) with bounded norms and \(\|\mathfrak{a}_i (\mu_i s_i - \rho)\|_{E^{\ast}} \to 0\). The definition of operator norm then gives (18.124), with one tail uniform in \(y\).\(\Box\)

The marked-vector assumptions are separate analytic inputs. In the selected construction let \(\textsf{q}_j\) and \(\textsf{w}_j\) be the transported chosen and reference responses, and set \(m_j = b_j^{- 1} \textsf{q}_j - \textsf{w}_j\). The scalar assembly in Subsection 18.23, applied at the postselected levels and under its stated response, bound and scale inputs, gives \(m_{n_i} \to x\) and \(\mathfrak{a}_i \rho (m_{n_i}) \to 0\). In particular, the scale comparison above and \(\hat{\beta}_{n_i} \to \infty\) give \(b_{n_i} \geqslant 1\) after discarding finitely many terms; the cited scalar calculation supplies \(\mathfrak{a}_i / b_{n_i} \to 0\). The distinct priced-mission argument in Lemma 18.4 gives the stronger field estimate \(\hat{\beta}_{n_i} s_i (m_{n_i}) \to 0\), under its stated medium-core representation, oddness, response and scaling identities, detector and local-estimates, and scalar rate input. For large \(t\), \(\tfrac{1}{5} (\log \log t)^2 \leqslant \log t\), so \(A_{1 / 5} (n_i) \leqslant \hat{\beta}_{n_i}\) eventually. Therefore

\(\displaystyle \|\mathfrak{a}_i s_i (m_{n_i})\| \leqslant \| \hat{\beta}_{n_i} s_i (m_{n_i})\| \longrightarrow 0.\)

Thus the same selected medium supplies all three marked-vector limits in the proposition, subject to those two separate groups of inputs. For the selected construction, Subsection 18.17 identifies the chart, the tested finite-volume readout and the probe estimates on this same history and applies the proposition.

18.16Branch moments and trace washout

Fix a closed subgroup of a finite-dimensional unitary group whose real Lie algebra is nonabelian and simple; the group itself may be disconnected. Work on one selected history and with the same three tensor families and chart as in the physical stress construction. All spaces below are finite dimensional. The four coordinate axes form the set \(I = \{0, 1, 2, 3\}\). Let \(E_4\) be the complex space of grade-four labels, let \(\mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}}\) be the grade-four field space of the selected interacting table, and let \(\mathcal{F}_4^0\) be that of the standard table. The maps are complex linear unless stated otherwise. Write \(\pi : E_4 \to \mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}}\) and \(\pi_0 : E_4 \to \mathcal{F}_4^0\) for the projections, \(\mathfrak{c}\) and \(\mathfrak{c}_0\) for centering, and \(\boldsymbol{1}_4, \boldsymbol{1}_4^0\) for the corresponding unit fields. Thus \(\mathfrak{c}f = f - \langle f \rangle \boldsymbol{1}_4\). The centering map is idempotent and its range is the space of centered fields, using the table unit law \(\langle \boldsymbol{1}_4 \rangle = 1\); the standard table has the same unit law.

The branch set is \(\{0, -, +\}\): \(0\) is the principal branch, \(-\) is the false correction and \(+\) is the true correction. In the underlying finite-volume construction these are respectively the selected principal field and the two fixed correction fields. To specify the choice, let \(J_{ac}, Q_d, H_{acd}\) be the selected tensor, trace-current and antisymmetry fields at a finite volume. Let a superscript \([r]\) denote the same finite localization of each field and let \(\nabla_d\) be the forward lattice difference. At localization radius \(r\) the principal field is

\(\displaystyle J^0_{ac} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum_d \nabla_d Q_d^{[r]} - \sum_{j \ne 0} J_{jj}^{[r]}, & a = c = 0,\\ J_{ca}^{[r]} - \sum_d \nabla_d H_{cad}^{[r]}, & c < a,\\ J_{ac}^{[r]}, & \text{otherwise} . \end{array} \right.\)

At scale \(k\) the principal field's common localization radius is \(\varrho_k = 1000 \lceil (\log \hat{\beta}_k)^2 \rceil\), where \(\hat{\beta}_k\) is the positive clock introduced below; here \(r = \varrho_k\). In particular \(\sum_a J^0_{aa} = \sum_d \nabla_d Q_d^{[r]}\) exactly. For the two corrections, let \(B_{ad, cd}\) be the real inner product of the two lattice curvatures with the common index \(d\), evaluated at the same lattice site. Their components are

\(\displaystyle J^-_{ac} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 0, & a = c,\\ \sum_d B_{ad, cd}, & a \ne c, \end{array} \right. \qquad J^+_{ac} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{1}{4} \sum_{j, d} B_{jd, jd}, & a = c,\\ 0, & a \ne c. \end{array} \right.\)

These formulas are translated from the origin to the relevant lattice site. The three branches use the same extraction ultrafilter, finite-volume choices and scale history, with no new subsequence; their recorded labels may differ. At scale \(k\), let \(x^b_k (a, c) \in E_4\) be the recorded label of \(J^b_{ac}\), with \(a, c \in I\). Let \(\textsf{R}\) be the real-linear finite signed-symmetry average on arrays of labels and set

\(\displaystyle \text{sym} (x)_{ac} = \tfrac{1}{2} (x_{ac} + x_{ca}), \qquad t^b_k =\mathfrak{c} \hspace{0.17em} \pi \textsf{R} \text{sym} (x^b_k) .\)

The notation means componentwise application of \(\pi\) and \(\mathfrak{c}\). The symmetry average permutes the axes and applies the corresponding label map, with the two tensor signs; it includes the real-structure average. Its normalized weights sum to one. The table projection intertwines these label motions with the corresponding real-linear field motions, and those field motions are fixed, bounded maps. In particular, for a diagonal trace, reindexing the axes cancels the squared signs. Put \(\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} t = \sum_{a \in I} t_{aa}\).

For a triple \(w = (w_1, w_2, w_3) \in \mathbb{R}^3\), define the label-valued material tensor by

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{S} (w)_{ac} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} w_1 \sum_j P_{aj, aj} + w_2 \sum_{i, j} P_{ij, ij}, & a = c,\\ w_3 \sum_j P_{aj, cj}, & a \ne c, \end{array} \right. \qquad M (w)_{ac} = \pi_0 \mathcal{S} (w)_{ac} . \) (18.125)

Here \(P_{ij, kl} \in E_4\) is the coordinate representative of the label whose value on a Taylor jet is the real color inner product \(\langle F_{ij}, F_{kl} \rangle\); no additional factor is included. The axis sums are ordered. There is no scalar-unit term in (18.125); the full four-component coefficient is \((0, w_1, w_2, w_3)\). The map \(w \mapsto \mathcal{S} (w)\) is injective. To see this, let \(v\) be a nonzero real color vector. On a jet with only \(F_{01} = v\) and \(F_{10} = - v\), the \((2, 2)\) and \((0, 0)\) entries are respectively \(2 w_2 \langle v, v \rangle\) and \((w_1 + 2 w_2) \langle v, v \rangle\); if the tensor is zero, \(w_2 = w_1 = 0\). On a jet with \(F_{02} = F_{12} = v\) and their antisymmetric partners, its \((0, 1)\) entry is \(w_3 \langle v, v \rangle\), so \(w_3 = 0\). Exchange of the curvature pairs shows \(\mathcal{S} (w)_{ac} =\mathcal{S} (w)_{ca}\). A signed permutation sends \(F_{ij}\) to \(\epsilon_i \epsilon_j F_{\sigma (i) \sigma (j)}\). In a contracted pair the repeated sign squares to one, and reindexing the sums gives the two tensor signs. The diagonal terms transform likewise, and both the curvature-pair labels and real coefficients are fixed by the real structure. Thus the signed and real symmetry average fixes \(\mathcal{S} (w)\). The branch coefficients are

\(\displaystyle w^0 = (- 1, \tfrac{1}{4}, - 1), \qquad w^- = (0, 0, 1), \qquad w^+ = (0, \tfrac{1}{4}, 0) . \) (18.126)

Define the normalized scalar field \(\mathcal{O}= \pi_0 (\tfrac{1}{4} \sum_{i, j} P_{ij, ij})\). Taking the four diagonal entries in (18.125) gives, for every \(w\),

\(\displaystyle \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M (w) = 4 (w_1 + 4 w_2) \mathcal{O}. \) (18.127)

Consequently

\(\displaystyle \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M (w^0) = \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M (w^-) = 0, \qquad \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M (w^+) = 4\mathcal{O}. \) (18.128)

We use the standard-table one-point fact that each curvature-pair label has expectation zero and the unit has expectation one. In the free table the pair labels are Wick ordered. Thus the expectation of each \(M (w)_{ac}\) is zero because its unit coefficient is zero. Hence \(\mathfrak{c}_0 \pi_0 \mathcal{S} (w) = M (w)\).

Let \(k_i\) be the selected level, tending to infinity with the chart index \(i\), and let \(\hat{\beta}_k > 0\) and \(a_k > 0\) be its inverse-coupling clock and mesh. Denote by \(B_k : E_4 \to E_4\) the selected transport, by \(U_i : \mathcal{F}_4^0 \to \mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}}\) the chart isomorphism, and by \(\omega_i > 0\) its grade-four normalization factor. Explicitly \(\omega_i = \beta_Z (k_i) r_i^4\), where \(r_i\) is the chart radius and \(\beta_Z\) is the associated effective coupling. More explicitly, if \(A_k\) is the selected label automorphism of \(E_4\), \(K\) is the core projection and \(\vartheta_k > 0\) is the threshold, then

\(\displaystyle B_k = \vartheta_k^{- 2} KA_k, \qquad V_{k, c} = \pi \hspace{0.17em} (c \vartheta_k^2 A_k^{- 1}) .\)

The centered moment map and the scaled field map are

\(\displaystyle \textsf{M}_i = U_i^{- 1} \mathfrak{c}V_{k_i, \omega_i} : E_4 \to \mathcal{F}_4^0, \qquad \textsf{P}_i = \omega_i U_i^{- 1} : \mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}} \to \mathcal{F}_4^0 . \) (18.129)

The transport and chart normalization give, for all sufficiently large \(i\),

\(\displaystyle \textsf{M}_i B_{k_i} x = \textsf{P}_i \mathfrak{c} \pi x \quad (x \in E_4) . \) (18.130)

To see the normalization, \(V_{k, c} B_k x = c \pi A_k^{- 1} KA_k x\). The difference between \(A_k^{- 1} KA_k x\) and \(x\) is a multiple of \(A_k^{- 1} e_0\), where \(e_0\) is the scalar coordinate label. At the selected cutoff \(A_k\) sends the reference unit label to \(a_k^4 e_0\). The reference unit projects to \(\boldsymbol{1}_4\), so \(\pi A_k^{- 1} e_0 = a_k^{- 4} \boldsymbol{1}_4\) and centering removes the difference. Applying \(U_i^{- 1}\) yields the identity. The cutoff after which this holds is fixed before taking \(i \to \infty\). Let \(W^b_k = \textsf{R} (B_k x^b_k)\), componentwise, and \(W^b =\mathcal{S} (w^b)\). The following are the incoming chart limits, stated in their actual limit order:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} W^b_k & \longrightarrow W^b & & (k \to \infty, b = 0, -, +), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.131)} \\ \textsf{M}_i & \longrightarrow \textsf{M}_{\infty} := \mathfrak{c}_0 \pi_0 & & \text{in operator norm as } i \to \infty . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.132)} \end{array}\)

These limits depend on the selected finite-volume transport and chart estimates. In the first, the separation-ultrafilter limit is taken at fixed \(k\) before \(k \to \infty\). The existing weighted-estimate construction uses the same reference coefficient limit, and its quantitative inputs remain subject to the gaps recorded there. We record the actual finite-volume passage yielding (18.131). Let \(T : \mathbb{R}^3 \to E_4^{I \times I}\) send \(w\) to \(\mathcal{S} (w)\). For each \(k\) let \(R_k (u)\) be the recorded finite-volume coefficient array before symmetry averaging and \(C^b_k (u)\) the corresponding finite-volume branch coefficient, with \(u\) the separation parameter. Equip these arrays with the finite-product supremum norm induced by the coordinate norm. The incoming estimates say that the nonnegative loss

\(\displaystyle \delta_k = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left[ - \tfrac{1}{4} (\log \log \hat{\beta}_k)^2 \right]\)

satisfies, eventually in \(k\) and on an ultrafilter-large set of \(u\),

\(\displaystyle \|R_k (u) - C^b_k (u)\| \leqslant \delta_k .\)

The threshold normalization explains this loss. For one component, let \(z\) be its local coefficient, \(\xi\) its purchased readout, \(\Gamma\) the finite-volume column map, and \(v\) the purchased label. The local pricing and zero-scalar identities give \(\xi = \vartheta_k^2 z\) and \(Kz = z\); the purchased bias says \(\| \xi - \Gamma v\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant \vartheta_k^2 \delta_k\), with \(\vartheta_k > 0\). The recorded coefficient is \(\vartheta_k^{- 2} K \Gamma v\). Write \(\| \cdot \|_{\text{c}}\) for the sum of the absolute coordinate values. Since \(K\) contracts the coordinate supremum norm and that norm is bounded by \(\| \cdot \|_{\text{c}}\), the bias gives

\(\displaystyle \|z - \vartheta_k^{- 2} K \Gamma v\|_{\infty} \leqslant \vartheta_k^{- 2} \| \xi - \Gamma v\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant \delta_k .\)

Taking the maximum over the finitely many components gives the displayed array estimate. The weighted readout acts on each coordinate by the threshold raised to its color-factor count. The simple-group low-grade classification gives count two at every non-scalar coordinate; the scalar coordinate of \(z\) is its zero-jet evaluation, which equals the observable's value at the identity and vanishes for these branches. Thus the weighted-readout definition gives \(\xi = \vartheta_k^2 z\) coordinate by coordinate. The local identification of this weighted readout, the zero-jet reading, and the purchased bias are inputs here. The selected clock tends to infinity, so \(\delta_k \to 0\): its logarithm is minus one quarter of a quantity tending to infinity. Also, for each \(\varepsilon > 0\), eventually in \(k\) and on an ultrafilter-large set of \(u\) there is \(w = w (k, u)\) with \(\|w - w^b \| \leqslant \varepsilon\) and \(\textsf{R} C^b_k (u) = Tw\). The two correction branches use their displayed coefficient triples exactly; the principal coefficient is controlled only asymptotically. Finally, at every sufficiently large fixed \(k\) the ultrafilter limit of \(\textsf{R} R_k (u)\) is the previously defined \(W^b_k\).

Intersecting the two large sets at fixed \(k\) gives

\(\displaystyle \| \textsf{R} R_k (u) - Tw^b \| \leqslant \| \textsf{R} \| \delta_k + \|T\| \varepsilon .\)

The norm is continuous and the displayed closed ball is preserved by the ultrafilter limit, so the same bound holds with \(W^b_k\) on the left. First let \(k \to \infty\), then let \(\varepsilon \downarrow 0\): this proves (18.131). The purchased bias and the existence of the finite-volume coefficient witnesses are retained inputs; this finite-dimensional passage does not construct them.

The principal witness can be obtained from the local projection and the canonical stress classification, subject to the following local inputs. Assume the coefficient projection is linear on the finite sums below and annihilates each forward difference of the localized trace and antisymmetry currents. Assume also that the local principal coefficients have zero scalar coordinate. The exact finite-volume antisymmetry identity expresses each difference between transposed entries as a sum of those forward differences. Their projected coefficients therefore form a symmetric array. Its finite signed and real-structure average is a real covariant symmetric tensor with zero scalar coordinate. The free stress classification in Subsection 16.1 expresses it as \(Tw\) for a triple \(w\), giving the required witness. The trace-current identity and the same vanishing give \(\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} Tw = 0\); evaluation on a nonzero \(01\) curvature jet yields \(w_1 + 4 w_2 = 0\). This exact local constraint does not give a rate for the recorded field trace. The difference-projection identity follows from the local affine-test identity: translating an affine test changes its potential by an infinitesimal gauge term. The current's first variation at the identity vanishes: global gauge invariance makes its restriction to each link an invariant linear functional on the simple color algebra. Such a functional annihilates all brackets, which span the nonabelian simple algebra, so it is zero. Differentiating gauge invariance at the identity then shows that the Hessian annihilates an infinitesimal gauge direction in either argument: the mixed derivative of the gauge action is multiplied by the already vanishing first variation. This also uses the local realization of the gauge term on the nonwrapping support. The Hessian therefore agrees on the original and translated affine tests. The forward difference consequently has zero Hessian on each such test direction paired with itself. Its value at the identity is also zero, and the grade-four jet-reading identity makes every jet evaluation of its local coefficient zero; the coefficient itself is zero. This application uses the affine translation identity, local regularity and nonwrapping of its designated neighborhood, Taylor order at least four, side length at least \(40\), and the selected local certificates for the antisymmetry currents and their differences. In the selected construction the same finite-volume certificate supplies these local current and difference bounds for every axis on a common ultrafilter-large set at each sufficiently large scale. The existence of those choices and their local bounds remains an input here; the finite-dimensional classification does not itself produce them.

For the principal branch, the closeness of these witnesses has a useful sequential reduction. Let \(t_n \to \infty\) be any sequence of inverse couplings and let the finite-volume data be admissible at each \(t_n\), with side length tending to infinity, the prescribed nonwrapping neighborhood, and Taylor order at least four. The local comparison supplies two Hessian estimates for each fixed pair of the finitely supported elementary test directions of total degree at most four. Fixing a pair of tensor indices, let \(Q_n\) be the corresponding unmodified local observable and \(\tilde{Q}_n\) its principal modification; their second derivatives on those directions differ by at most \(16 t_n^{- 30}\), while the derivative of \(Q_n\) differs from the prescribed limiting bilinear form by at most \(t_n^{- 100}\). For the first bound, write the Hessian of \(Q_n - \tilde{Q}_n\) in lattice coordinates. If \(r_i\) is the distance weight of coordinate \(i\), each elementary direction has \(|v_i | \leqslant 4 (1 + r_i)^2\). Thus the product of coordinates from the two directions is at most \(16 (1 + \max (r_i, r_j))^4\). The weighted Hessian coefficient sum controlling the modification dominates this polynomial weight and is at most \(t_n^{- 30}\); summing the coordinate expansion gives the factor \(16\). The weighted coefficient estimate and the elementary direction bound are the local inputs used here. The canonical quadratic stencil has derivatives converging to that same form as the side length grows. Thus the corresponding derivative of \(\tilde{Q}_n\) and that of the canonical stencil have the same limit. These estimates use the local regularity and comparison inputs and are not consequences of the later field limit.

The resulting Taylor-coefficient convergence follows as follows. A first-order jet produces a lattice test direction which is a finite linear combination of those elementary directions. Bilinearity and the preceding bounds therefore give convergence of the second derivative on this test direction paired with itself. The grade-four jet-reading identity expresses the label evaluated at a jet as the observable's value at the identity plus one half of this second derivative. For the principal observable the identity value is zero, and the same identity for the canonical stencil identifies the limit with the evaluation of the canonical stress label. This uses the jet-reading and canonical-stencil identities under the simple-group, regularity, nonwrapping and order hypotheses; they are external inputs here. The jet evaluations separate the finite-dimensional label space, so finitely many of them already separate it. Their joint linear map has a continuous left inverse onto its image. Consequently the unsymmetrized coefficient array \(C_n\) tends entrywise to \(Tw^0\). The limiting material array is fixed by the finite symmetry average, so \(\textsf{R} C_n \to Tw^0\). If \(w_n\) is any associated coefficient witness with \(\textsf{R} C_n = Tw_n\), then \(w_n \to w^0\). Indeed, the material coefficient map is injective by the curvature-jet calculation above; choose a fixed linear left inverse on its finite-dimensional range and apply it to the convergence.

This sequential statement yields the eventual uniform estimate required above. Otherwise, for some \(\varepsilon > 0\), choose counterexamples with \(t_n \geqslant n\) and \(\|w_n - w^0 \| \geqslant \varepsilon\). Their admissible side length \(N_n\) satisfies \(N_n > 20 (H (t_n) + 10)\), with \(H\) the neighborhood radius defined below. Since \(H (t_n) \to \infty\), the chosen data meet the sequential hypotheses. The preceding convergence is a contradiction. The local projection certificates, weighted Hessian bound, unmodified Hessian comparison, jet-reading identities and neighborhood-size growth are retained inputs. For the two correction branches the coefficient identities follow directly from their finite-volume curvature products. The curvature vanishes at the identity configuration, so for a lattice test direction \(v\),

\(\displaystyle \tfrac{1}{2} D^2 \langle F_{ij}, F_{kl} \rangle_1 [v, v] = \langle DF_{ij} (1) [v], DF_{kl} (1) [v] \rangle .\)

On the affine test associated with a degree-zero radial jet, the two linearized lattice curvatures are exactly the corresponding components of that jet, provided the test stencil is nonwrapping. The grade-four jet-reading identity then identifies this Hessian with \(P_{ij, kl}\). Taking the displayed finite sums gives \(w^- = (0, 0, 1)\) and \(w^+ = (0, \tfrac{1}{4}, 0)\) entry by entry, including their zero entries. Their material tensors are invariant under the finite tensor-symmetry average, so the same identities hold after that average. This uses the local curvature-stencil and jet-reading identities as inputs and does not infer an exact finite-volume equality from a continuum limit.

The same local identities give a useful, but slower, trace estimate. For \(b \in \{0, -\}\) the trace of \(\textsf{R} C^b_k (u)\) is zero on the relevant large set: for \(b = 0\) this is the exact local constraint above, and for \(b = -\) it follows from the zero diagonal. Applying the finite trace map to \(\textsf{R} (R_k (u) - C^b_k (u))\) and then taking the fixed-scale ultrafilter limit gives

\(\displaystyle \| \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} W^b_k \| \leqslant 4 \| \textsf{R} \| \delta_k \qquad (b = 0, -) .\)

The factor four counts the diagonal entries. This does not prove the weighted trace limit: for each fixed \(p > 0\), \(\hat{\beta}_k^p \delta_k \to \infty\). Indeed, for \(y = \log \hat{\beta}_k\), the bound \(\log y \leqslant 4 y^{1 / 4}\) makes the logarithm of this product at least \(py - 4 \sqrt{y} \to \infty\). On the other hand, \((\log \hat{\beta}_k)^m \delta_k \to 0\) for every fixed \(m \in \mathbb{N}\), since its logarithm is \(m \log y - (\log y)^2 / 4\). The fast recording argument below supplies a stronger estimate.

The moment-map convergence is a separate grade-four chart input: the normalized transported lift tends to the core projection, the scalar complement is annihilated by centering, and the chart expectations converge. On the finite-dimensional label space, these statements give operator-norm convergence. We spell out the selected two-scale passage behind the normalized lift. Let \(m_i\) be the selected initial level, so \(m_i \to \infty\), and let \(k_i\) be its prescribed summit. The simple group's grade-four reference projection is an isomorphism. To see this, use the low-grade decomposition in Subsection 16.1: after the invariant linear terms vanish, a grade-four label is a constant plus a quadratic expression depending only on its degree-zero radial jet. Every such jet is realized by a constant-curvature affine Maxwell solution: for prescribed antisymmetric \(F_{ab}\) take \(A_b (x) = \frac{1}{2} \sum_a F_{ab} x_a\). Its curvature is \(F\), and all curvature derivatives, including the Maxwell divergence, vanish. A label in the free equation ideal therefore vanishes on every degree-zero jet and hence on every jet. The standard table identifies the kernel of its projection with that equation ideal, an earlier free-table input. The kernel is zero, and the projection is onto by construction. In particular \(\mathcal{O} \ne 0\): on an affine Maxwell solution with \(F_{01} = v\), \(F_{10} = - v\) and all other curvature components zero, its unprojected label evaluates to \(\tfrac{1}{2} \langle v, v \rangle > 0\) for any nonzero color vector \(v\). Injectivity preserves its nonvanishing. All three material tensors are nonzero: every \(w^b \ne 0\), and both the material coefficient map and \(\pi_0\) are injective. In particular the false material tensor is nonzero although its trace vanishes by (18.128). Write \(\ell = \pi_0^{- 1}\). Let \(Y_i : \mathcal{F}_4^0 \to E_4\) be the selected label lift, and set \(\varpi_i = \beta_Z (m_i) r_i^4 > 0\). For each fixed \(z \in \mathcal{F}_4^0\), the incoming local chart estimates and the scale comparison below give

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \varpi_i^{- 1} B_{m_i} Y_i z & \longrightarrow K \ell z, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.133)} \\ \|B_{m_i} Y_i z - B_{k_i} Y_i z\| & \leqslant \varepsilon_i \|B_{k_i} Y_i z\|, \quad \varepsilon_i \longrightarrow 0, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.134)} \\ \frac{\beta_Z (m_i)}{\beta_Z (k_i)} & \longrightarrow 1. \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.135)} \end{array}\)

The first convergence comes from the fixed-grade local lift estimate after division by \(\varpi_i\): for each fixed \(z\) its error is bounded by a constant depending on \(z\) times \(\exp [- \tfrac{1}{2} (\log \log \hat{\beta}_{m_i})^2]\), which tends to zero. We spell out its finite-volume normalization. At a fixed initial level \(m\), write \(\mathcal{N}\) for the column map normalized by \(a_m^{- 4}\), \(\mathcal{T}\) for the local chart map, and let \(\alpha\) denote the displayed exponential error. The selected chart inputs give \(\mathcal{N}u_{\text{ref}} =\mathcal{T}u_{\text{ref}} = e_0\) and, for every \(y\),

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{N}y -\mathcal{T}y\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant \alpha \|\mathcal{N}y\|_{\text{c}}, \qquad \|\mathcal{N}y -\mathcal{T}y\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} \|\mathcal{N}y\|_{\text{c}} .\)

The second estimate and the triangle inequality give \(\|\mathcal{N}y\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant 2 \|\mathcal{T}y\|_{\text{c}}\). For any \(v\), take \(y = v - (\mathcal{T}v)_0 u_{\text{ref}}\). Then \(\mathcal{T}y = K\mathcal{T}v\) and \(K\mathcal{N}y = K\mathcal{N}v\), so the coefficient norm's contraction under \(K\) gives

\(\displaystyle \|K (\mathcal{N}v -\mathcal{T}v)\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant 2 \alpha \|K\mathcal{T}v\|_{\text{c}} .\)

At grade four the non-scalar coordinates of every label have the required homogeneous grade, by the low-grade simple-group classification. For the actual finite-volume lift \(L\) of a reference label \(v\), the selected lift identity is \(K\mathcal{T}Lv = \beta_{m, u} \vartheta_m^2 h^4 Kv\), where \(\beta_{m, u} > 0\), \(\vartheta_m > 0\), \(h \geqslant 0\) and the physical radius is \(a_m h\). The finite-volume counterpart of \(B_m Lv\) is \(\vartheta_m^{- 2} a_m^4 K\mathcal{N}Lv\). Substituting \(Lv\) into the preceding inequality yields

\(\displaystyle | \vartheta_m^{- 2} a_m^4 K\mathcal{N}Lv - \beta_{m, u} (a_m h)^4 Kv |_{\text{c}} \leqslant 2 \alpha \hspace{0.17em} \beta_{m, u} (a_m h)^4 \|Kv\|_{\text{c}} .\)

At fixed \(i\) the selected lifts and couplings have their common separation-ultrafilter limits, and the selected physical radius is \(r_i\). The steep transport property gives the limit of the finite-volume transport applied to those moving lifts. Passing to these limits gives the stated initial-level estimate; no separate limit of \(\mathcal{T}\) is needed. The two finite-volume chart inequalities, lift identity and selected limits are the inputs used in this reduction.

The second estimate is the comparison over the actual interval from the initial level to its summit. Its coefficient-size error is

\(\displaystyle \text{part} (m_i) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left[ - \tfrac{2}{5} (\log \log \hat{\beta}_{m_i})^2 \right] .\)

The norm is any fixed norm on \(E_4\); equivalence with the coefficient norm transfers the estimate with \(\varepsilon_i\) equal to a fixed positive multiple of \(\text{part} (m_i)\). This tends to zero because the hatted clock at the cofinal initial levels tends to infinity. The finite-volume inputs behind these comparisons belong to the selected history; they do not follow from a generic chart.

The interval comparison follows from the actual one-step column bound and the scheduled sum budget. At one step, let \(w\) and \(v\) be the finite-volume column values at the earlier and later levels, with positive thresholds \(a, b\). The selected one-step input gives \(b / a \leqslant 2\) and a comparison value \(X\) with

\(\displaystyle \|w - X\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant g \|Kv\|_{\text{c}}, \qquad KX = (a / b)^2 Kv.\)

Here \(g\) is the step's nonnegative column error; the second identity uses the grade-four homogeneity just noted. Thus

\(\displaystyle \|a^{- 2} Kw - b^{- 2} Kv\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant a^{- 2} g \|Kv\|_{\text{c}} \leqslant 4 g \|b^{- 2} Kv\|_{\text{c}} .\)

This is the one-step estimate with coefficient \(4 g\); the threshold and column comparison are selected-construction inputs. To see the accumulation, fix a finite-volume label and write \(u_j\) for its transported value at level \(j\) between an initial level \(m\) and its summit \(k\). Put \(N = k - m > 0\) and \(x = \text{part} (m) \leqslant 1\). The selected one-step and schedule inputs state that

\(\displaystyle \|u_j - u_{j + 1} \|_{\text{c}} \leqslant \frac{x}{2 N} \|u_{j + 1} \|_{\text{c}}, \qquad m \leqslant j < k.\)

Backward induction gives, for every \(m \leqslant j \leqslant k\),

\(\displaystyle \|u_j - u_k \|_{\text{c}} \leqslant \frac{x (k - j)}{N} \|u_k \|_{\text{c}} .\)

Indeed, if this is known at \(j + 1\), the triangle inequality and \(x \leqslant 1\) give \(\|u_{j + 1} \|_{\text{c}} \leqslant 2 \|u_k \|_{\text{c}}\). Add the one-step error to the induction bound to obtain the next case; the case \(j = k\) is zero. For fixed \(m, k\) and label, the finite-volume estimates hold together for the finitely many levels on an ultrafilter-large set. Passing to the fixed-scale transport limits gives the stated comparison. The threshold and column comparison and the schedule hypotheses remain selected-construction inputs; the induction does not establish them.

For the scale ratio, the selected finite-volume running estimate says that for every \(\epsilon > 0\), eventually in \(j\),

\(\displaystyle | \beta_{j, u} - \hat{\beta}_j | \leqslant \epsilon s_j\)

on an ultrafilter-large set of finite-volume parameters \(u\). At each fixed \(j\), the same ultrafilter sends \(\beta_{j, u}\) to \(\beta_Z (j)\); passing to the closed interval gives the same estimate for \(\beta_Z (j)\). The affine clock satisfies \(\hat{\beta}_j \geqslant bs_j\) for its positive slope \(b\). For any \(\delta > 0\), choose \(\epsilon = b \delta / 2\) and divide by the positive clock. This proves \(\beta_Z (j) / \hat{\beta}_j \to 1\). The running estimate and the fixed-scale limit are upstream inputs. The summit clock estimate follows from the first-crossing and block-size properties of the selected schedule. Write \(g_j\) for the gap from level \(j\) to its summit and \(b\) for the fixed slope constant. Set \(\text{logScale} (x) = (\log x)^{48}\). For \(x \geqslant 1\), the inequality \(t \leqslant e^t\) with \(t = (\log x) / 96\) gives \((\log x)^{48} / x \leqslant 96^{48} x^{- 1 / 2}\), so the gate ratio tends to zero. The selected clock tends to infinity.

The summit is the first later level at which the scale gap reaches the gate. Let \(q\) be its preceding level and put \(x = \hat{\beta}_j\). By the first-crossing property, \(0 \leqslant s_q - s_j \leqslant \text{logScale} (x)\). The affine clock identity then gives \(\hat{\beta}_q \leqslant x + b \text{logScale} (x) \leqslant 2 x\) eventually. The selected schedule has \(L_q \geqslant 1\) and bounds \(L_q\) by \(\log \hat{\beta}_q\); its scale increment is \(s_{q + 1} - s_q = \log L_q\). Since \(\log L_q \leqslant L_q\), for large \(x\),

\(\displaystyle s_{q + 1} - s_q \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_q \leqslant \log (2 x) \leqslant \text{logScale} (x) .\)

Adding the pre-crossing gap and this last increment gives \(0 \leqslant g_j \leqslant 2 \text{logScale} (\hat{\beta}_j)\). Consequently,

\(\displaystyle \hat{\beta}_{\text{summit} (j)} = \hat{\beta}_j + bg_j, \qquad 0 \leqslant \frac{g_j}{\hat{\beta}_j} \leqslant 2 \frac{\text{logScale} (\hat{\beta}_j)}{\hat{\beta}_j} \longrightarrow 0.\)

The first-crossing definition and block-size schedule bound are the inputs used here. Dividing the identity by the positive \(\hat{\beta}_j\) yields \(\hat{\beta}_{\text{summit} (j)} / \hat{\beta}_j \to 1\), and hence the same limit along \(j = m_i\). Since both levels are cofinal and the denominators are positive,

\(\displaystyle \frac{\beta_Z (m_i)}{\beta_Z (k_i)} = \frac{\beta_Z (m_i) / \hat{\beta}_{m_i}}{(\beta_Z (k_i) / \hat{\beta}_{k_i}) (\hat{\beta}_{k_i} / \hat{\beta}_{m_i})} \longrightarrow 1.\)

Thus (18.135) follows from these two explicit scale estimates.

The same bounds also explain the scheduled budget used in the backward induction. Put \(x = \hat{\beta}_m\) and \(N = k - m\), where \(k\) is the summit. Every scale increment is at least one, so the gap bound gives \(N \leqslant 2 (\log x)^{48}\). For all large \(m\), the summit clock is at most \(2 x\), and the scheduled column factor \(p_j\) at each step satisfies \(p_j \leqslant 2 \log x\). Let \(c_j = \exp [\tfrac{1}{2} (\log \log \hat{\beta}_{j + 1})^2]\); clock monotonicity gives \(c_j \geqslant \exp [\tfrac{1}{2} (\log \log x)^2]\) at these levels. The one-step coefficient in the selected construction is \(8 (1 + p_j) / c_j\). To bound it by \(\text{part} (m) / (2 N)\), it is enough that

\(\displaystyle 16 N (1 + 2 \log x) \leqslant \exp [\tfrac{1}{10} (\log \log x)^2] .\)

Using the bound on \(N\), the left side is at most \(32 (1 + 2 \log x) (\log x)^{48}\). With \(y = \log \log x\), this is at most \(96 e^{49 y}\) for large \(x\), whereas the ratio of the right side to \(96 e^{49 y}\) tends to infinity. This proves the scheduled budget from the clock, block and column bounds. The underlying threshold and column comparison remains an input; it is not a consequence of this numerical calculation.

To combine them, put \(X_i = \varpi_i^{- 1} B_{m_i} Y_i z\) and \(Z_i = \varpi_i^{- 1} B_{k_i} Y_i z\). The stability estimate gives \(\|X_i - Z_i \| \leqslant \varepsilon_i \|Z_i \|\). Since \(X_i\) converges, it is bounded; eventually \(\varepsilon_i \leqslant 1 / 2\), so \(\|Z_i \| \leqslant 2 \|X_i \|\). Consequently \(Z_i - X_i \to 0\). Multiplication by (18.135) changes the denominator from \(\beta_Z (m_i) r_i^4\) to \(\beta_Z (k_i) r_i^4 = \omega_i\), giving

\(\displaystyle \omega_i^{- 1} B_{k_i} Y_i z \longrightarrow K \ell z. \) (18.136)

The same \(Y_i\), \(r_i\), transport and history occur at both levels; the ratio is not a comparison between independently selected charts.

Now put

\(\displaystyle D_i = \omega_i^{- 1} B_{k_i}, \qquad \mathcal{A}_i = D_i Y_i \pi_0 + (1 - K) : E_4 \longrightarrow E_4 .\)

The limit (18.136), \(\ell \pi_0 = 1\), and a finite basis give \(\mathcal{A}_i \to 1\) in operator norm; here \(K\) is the core projection on the fixed label space. Also \(\textsf{M}_i\) annihilates the scalar complement \(1 - K\), and eventually \(\pi Y_i = U_i\). Thus, on \(x \in E_4\), the chart identity gives

\(\displaystyle \textsf{M}_i \mathcal{A}_i x = \pi_0 x - \langle U_i \pi_0 x \rangle \boldsymbol{1}_4^0 \longrightarrow \pi_0 x - \langle \pi_0 x \rangle \boldsymbol{1}_4^0 = \textsf{M}_{\infty} x.\)

The expectation convergence is the one-point case of the selected chart correlation limit. For a fixed standard field \(z\), its one-point function at the origin is \(\langle z \rangle_0\); the rescaled interacting one-point function at the origin is \(\langle U_i z \rangle\). The selected chart convergence at this one-point configuration therefore gives \(\langle U_i z \rangle \to \langle z \rangle_0\). It uses the chart's one-point convergence and the two table hierarchies as inputs. Eventual transport of the unit is another selected-chart input. A finite basis upgrades the displayed pointwise convergence to operator-norm convergence. Eventually \(\|\mathcal{A}_i - 1\| < 1 / 2\), so the geometric series gives \(\|\mathcal{A}_i^{- 1} \| \leqslant 2\) and \(\|\mathcal{A}_i^{- 1} - 1\| \leqslant 2 \|\mathcal{A}_i - 1\| \to 0\). Since \(\textsf{M}_i = (\textsf{M}_i \mathcal{A}_i) \mathcal{A}_i^{- 1}\), this proves (18.132). The normalized local lift and one-point chart convergence remain upstream dependencies. Neither limit alone supplies a trace rate.

Proposition 18.33. (Three branch field limits) Assume (18.131)– (18.132), the chart identity and projection equivariance above, and eventual equivariance of \(B_k\) under every signed motion and the real-structure involution. Then

\(\displaystyle \textsf{P}_i t^0_{k_i} \longrightarrow M (w^0), \qquad \textsf{P}_i t^-_{k_i} \longrightarrow M (w^-), \qquad \textsf{P}_i t^+_{k_i} \longrightarrow M (w^+)\)

in the finite product \((\mathcal{F}_4^0)^{I \times I}\).

Proof. The finite symmetry average commutes with symmetrization. For sufficiently large \(k\), the selected transport intertwines every signed motion and the real-structure involution: this follows by taking its defining fixed-scale separation limit of the corresponding finite-volume transport identities. The core projection also commutes: the scalar label is fixed and its coordinate is preserved, up to conjugation under the real structure. Using equivariance and the chart identity gives, eventually,

\(\displaystyle (\textsf{P}_i t^b_{k_i})_{ac} = \tfrac{1}{2} (\textsf{M}_i W^b_{k_i} (a, c) + \textsf{M}_i W^b_{k_i} (c, a)) . \) (18.137)

Indeed, apply (18.130) to each term of the finite symmetry average; the scalar signs and coefficients are real, so commute with both maps. Since \(k_i \to \infty\), \(W^b_{k_i} (a, c) \to W^b (a, c)\). For any convergent \(v_i \to v\) in \(E_4\),

\(\displaystyle \| \textsf{M}_i v_i - \textsf{M}_{\infty} v\| \leqslant \| \textsf{M}_i - \textsf{M}_{\infty} \| \hspace{0.17em} \|v_i \|+ \| \textsf{M}_{\infty} \| \hspace{0.17em} \|v_i - v\| \longrightarrow 0.\)

Apply this to both entries in (18.137). The limit is \(\text{sym} (\mathfrak{c}_0 \pi_0 \mathcal{S}(w^b))\), which is \(M (w^b)\) by symmetry and zero expectation. There are only sixteen entries, so coordinate convergence is convergence of the entire tensor. This proves all three limits without exchanging an infinite sum and a limit.\(\Box\)

18.16.1The fast trace from localized recordings

We now derive the raw fast-trace statement used in the weighted limit. Let \(s_k \to \infty\) be the logarithmic scale of the selected schedule and \(a_k = e^{- s_k}\) for the mesh above. A sequence is fast if its norm is \(O (e^{- \eta s_k})\) for some \(\eta > 0\); a scalar sequence or operator family is subexponential if its norm is \(O (e^{\epsilon s_k})\) for every \(\epsilon > 0\). A finite sum of fast sequences is fast, with the minimum of finitely many positive decay exponents.

We make the local recording input explicit. Write \(E_3\) and \(\mathcal{F}_3^{\text{int}}\) for the finite-dimensional grade-three label and field spaces of the same interacting table, and \(\pi_d : E_d \to \mathcal{F}_d^{\text{int}}\) for its grade-\(d\) projection, with \(\pi_4 = \pi\). For each admissible separated test \(\sigma\), the table gives linear functionals \(L^{\sigma}_d (z) : \mathcal{F}_d^{\text{int}} \to \mathbb{C}\) for \(d = 3, 4\) and \(z\) near the origin. They are continuous in \(z\) and \(L^{\sigma}_d (0) = \lambda^{\sigma}_d\). In particular,

\(\displaystyle \lim_{z \to 0} \|L^{\sigma}_d (z) - \lambda^{\sigma}_d \| = 0 \quad (d = 3, 4), \qquad \bigcap_{\sigma} \ker \lambda^{\sigma}_4 = \{0\} . \) (18.138)

The convergence is in operator norm by finite dimensionality. Thus finitely many of the functionals \(\lambda^{\sigma}_4\) already separate \(\mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}}\). This follows from the field-separation and smoothness properties of the selected hierarchy: approximate a separated configuration by generic grid configurations at increasingly fine scales. Subtracting the first grid address places the insertion at the origin without changing relative positions. Translation invariance preserves the correlation, and spectator fields have representative tokens, so these are allowed tests. By continuity, a field annihilated by them has all separated correlations zero and is zero by field separation. These hierarchy and grid-density properties are retained inputs; finite dimensionality alone does not imply separation.

Every grade-three field is a multiple of the unit. Indeed, each color-valued jet-coordinate factor has grade at least two, so a grade-three label has at most one factor. Its invariant linear part vanishes by the low-grade color argument of Subsection 16.1; only the scalar label remains. The field projection is onto and sends the scalar label to a nonzero reference-scale multiple of the unit, so the same is true of \(\mathcal{F}_3^{\text{int}}\). Finally, inserting the unit leaves a separated correlation independent of its insertion point. Thus, in a sufficiently small separated neighborhood,

\(\displaystyle L^{\sigma}_3 (z) = L^{\sigma}_3 (0) . \) (18.139)

The grading, quotient projection, unit and field-separation statements here are the ones belonging to the same selected table; they are not inferred from the branch limit.

The incoming recording estimates are the following. For every test \(\sigma\) there is a radius \(c_{\sigma} > 0\). For each \(d \in I\) the localized current \(Q_d^{[r]}\) and its forward difference have recorded fields \(v_{d, k} \in \mathcal{F}_3^{\text{int}}\) and \(w_{d, k} \in \mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}}\). For every branch \(b \in \{0, -\}\), the diagonal tensor recordings are the already defined \(\pi x^b_k (a, a) \in \mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}}\). Their finite-volume tested correlations, denoted respectively by \(F^{\sigma}_{d, k, u} (j)\), \(G^{\sigma}_{d, k, u} (j)\) and \(T^{\sigma, b}_{a, k, u}\), satisfy, with fast nonnegative errors, the following estimates. For all sufficiently large \(k\) and each integer \(j\) with \(|j| a_k \leqslant c_{\sigma}\), the appropriate inequalities hold for \(u\) in an ultrafilter-large set. A common radius is obtained by taking the minimum of the finitely many radii for these current, difference and tensor insertions; the corresponding eventual scale cutoff is the maximum of finitely many cutoffs:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} |F^{\sigma}_{d, k, u} (j) - L^{\sigma}_3 (ja_k e_d) v_{d, k} | & \leqslant E^{\sigma}_{d, k}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.140)} \\ |G^{\sigma}_{d, k, u} (j) - L^{\sigma}_4 (ja_k e_d) w_{d, k} | & \leqslant D^{\sigma}_{d, k}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.141)} \\ |T^{\sigma, b}_{a, k, u} - \lambda^{\sigma}_4 (\pi x^b_k (a, a)) | & \leqslant H^{\sigma, b}_{a, k} . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.142)} \end{array}\)

Here \(e_d\) is the \(d\)th coordinate vector; \(E, D, H\) are fast sequences, possibly with test- and component-dependent exponents. The same separation ultrafilter is used in every line. The last line is at zero displacement. The finite-volume correlation is linear in its inserted observable, and for each \(\sigma\) the forward-difference identity holds for all integers \(j\) on an ultrafilter-large set, for all sufficiently large \(k\):

\(\displaystyle G^{\sigma}_{d, k, u} (j) = a_k^{- 1} (F^{\sigma}_{d, k, u} (j + 1) - F^{\sigma}_{d, k, u} (j)) . \) (18.143)

Here \(a_k^{- 1} = a_k^{- 4} / a_k^{- 3}\), the ratio of the two grade normalizations; translation covariance identifies the displaced current values. The localized current and its difference are recorded using the same finite-volume choices and volumes. The exact finite-volume branch formulas, after applying the same linear tested correlation, give for each test \(\sigma\) and all sufficiently large \(k\), on an ultrafilter-large set of \(u\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_a T^{\sigma, -}_{a, k, u} = 0, \qquad \sum_a T^{\sigma, 0}_{a, k, u} = \sum_d G^{\sigma}_{d, k, u} (0) . \) (18.144)

The construction supplies these estimates using its local recording, selected trace-current localization, field hierarchy and point-limit inputs. The estimates, low-grade unit statement and separating property use the upstream table and hierarchy inputs just stated; the finite-volume bound production behind the estimates is not proved in this subsection. They are more primitive than the assertion that either branch trace is fast.

The following root-bound mechanism identifies a more primitive sufficient input for the three recording estimates. Fix one of the finitely many insertions above, its grade \(d \in \{3, 4\}\), and a separated test \(\sigma\). At scale \(k\) and finite-volume parameter \(u\), let \(A_{k, u}\) be the actual inserted observable and \(O_{k, u} (v_{k, u})\) the observable made from its selected local label \(v_{k, u} \in E_d\). The actual insertion already includes the grade-\(d\) factor \(a_k^{- d}\) and transport to the comparison volume; \(O_{k, u}\) is the corresponding reference-scale observable. The following finite-dimensional selection explains how the same limiting label is obtained for all tests. The purchased finite-volume recording supplies a coefficient \(\xi_{k, u}\), a label \(v_{k, u}\), and the actual column map \(C_{k, u}\) on an ultrafilter-large set, with

\(\displaystyle \| \xi_{k, u} \|_{\text{c}} \leqslant 1, \qquad \| \xi_{k, u} - C_{k, u} v_{k, u} \|_{\text{c}} \leqslant 1, \qquad \|v_{k, u} \| \leqslant M_{d, k} \|C_{k, u} v_{k, u} \|_{\text{c}} . \) (18.145)

Here \(\| \cdot \|_{\text{c}}\) is the fixed coefficient norm, and \(M_{d, k} = \exp (\ell_k + ds_k)\) and \(\ell_k = \sum_{j < k} \log \zeta_{j + 1}\) for the calibrated column factors \(\zeta_{j + 1} \geqslant 1\). The previously established column estimate gives \(\ell_k = o (s_k)\), so \(e^{\ell_k}\) is subexponential. The middle bound is a convenient weakening of the purchased bias bound \(\vartheta_k^2 \exp [- \tfrac{1}{4} (\log \log \hat{\beta}_k)^2]\), using \(\vartheta_k \leqslant 1\). It is not a replacement for the root-bound purchase below. The inverse bound follows by multiplying the calibrated one-step factors \(\zeta_{j + 1} L_j^d\) from the reference level, where the column is the identity. All factors are at least one, \(\sum_{j < k} \log L_j = s_k\), and the label norm is bounded by the coefficient norm. The calibration, reference identity and common choice of column map and label remain inputs.

When constructing a history from these purchased data, for each fixed sufficiently large \(k\) choose \(v_{k, u}\) once together with its full purchase certificate on the ultrafilter-large set where that certificate is available, and define it arbitrarily elsewhere. In particular, (18.145) holds on that set. By the triangle inequality its norm is at most \(2 M_{d, k}\) on that set. The closed ball of that radius is compact because \(E_d\) is finite dimensional, so the ultrafilter has a limit \(x_k \in E_d\) in the ball. The choice is made before any test is selected; this one \(x_k\) works for every fixed test and satisfies \(\|x_k \| \leqslant 2 M_{d, k}\). Since \(\ell_k = o (s_k)\), for every \(\epsilon > 0\) this also gives \(\|x_k \| = O (e^{(d + \epsilon) s_k})\); the bound is grade-dependent and need not be subexponential. For an already selected history, retain its original labels and their existing ultrafilter limit instead: the compactness construction by itself does not identify a newly chosen limit with that history's limit. The root-bound step below uses the labels and limit from one and the same history.

For the root-bound step assume:

  1. for this test, some \(c_{\sigma} > 0\) and \(B_{\sigma} \geqslant 0\) satisfy, for every sufficiently large \(k\), axis \(a \in I\) and integer \(\ell\) with \(| \ell | a_k \leqslant c_{\sigma}\), on an ultrafilter-large set of \(u\),

    \(\displaystyle |\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}_{k, u} (\ell, a ; A_{k, u}) -\mathcal{C}^{\sigma}_{k, u} (\ell, a ; O_{k, u} (v_{k, u})) | \leqslant B_{\sigma} E_k,\)

    where \(E_k\) is fast and \(\mathcal{C}\) is the ordinary finite-volume tested correlation, linear in the insertion;

  2. for each sufficiently large fixed \(k\), axis \(a\), integer \(\ell\) with \(| \ell | a_k \leqslant c_{\sigma}\), and fixed \(v \in E_d\), the correlation of \(O_{k, u} (v)\) converges along the same ultrafilter to \(L^{\sigma}_d (\ell a_k e_a) \pi_d v\).

The first assumption applies the rooted bound to the actual-minus-local insertion, using selected funding, interior and locality estimates. The second is the fixed-label point limit in the same table. To apply it to the moving labels, choose a basis \((e_j)\) of \(E_d\) and write \(v_{k, u} = \sum_j c_j (u) e_j\), with \(c_j (u) \to c_j\) along the ultrafilter. Linearity of the local insertion and correlation gives a finite sum of \(c_j (u)\) times the correlations for the fixed basis vectors. The product and finite-sum limit rules therefore give

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}^{\sigma}_{k, u} (\ell, a ; O_{k, u} (v_{k, u})) \longrightarrow L^{\sigma}_d (\ell a_k e_a) \pi_d x_k .\)

This uses the existing fixed-scale label limit, including for an already chosen history. Fix a lower scale \(h\) at least the test's native scale. A comparison scale is open when \(h \leqslant j\) and \(b_j^2 \leqslant a_h / a_j\), where \(b_j\) is its cover size. Let \(\text{basin} (h)\) be the first open scale and choose \(j = \max \{ \text{basin} (h), \text{ref} (d)\}\). Such a scale exists: the cover-growth and mesh bounds give \(a_{j + 1} b_{j + 1}^2 \leqslant (2 / 3) a_j b_j^2\). Iterating gives \(a_j b_j^2 \leqslant (2 / 3)^{j - h} a_h b_h^2 \to 0\) for \(j \geqslant h\). Opening persists at finer scales, so this choice of \(j\) is open. At each allowed later level \(k > j\), the finite-volume purchase gives the actual-minus-local insertion the rooted bound

\(\displaystyle P_{d, h, j} (k) = \frac{2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{stake}_{h, j} \hspace{0.17em} a_j^{- d}}{\varphi_{d, j}} \hspace{0.17em} \hat{\beta}_k^4 \hspace{0.17em} \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left[ - \frac{s_k - s_j}{8} \right] . \) (18.146)

Here \(\text{stake}_{h, j} = \exp [(a_h / a_j)^4 \mathcal{T}_j + 5]\), where \(\mathcal{T}_j\) is the selected per-site treasury, and \(\varphi_{d, j} = \hat{\beta}_j^{- \nu_d}\), where \(\nu_d\) is the fixed reference-label degree bound plus four. Both are positive and fixed once \(d, h, j\) are chosen. This is the actual purchased bound. Since the hatted clock is affine in \(s_k\), its fourth power is subexponential: for \(s \geqslant 0\) and \(\epsilon > 0\), the exponential series gives \(s^4 \leqslant 24 \epsilon^{- 4} e^{\epsilon s}\). Thus, for every \(0 < \eta < 1 / 8\), \(P_{d, h, j} (k) = O (e^{- \eta s_k})\) and is fast. The containing-window radius has an explicit bound. Put \(r (x) = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil\), \(q (x) = r (x)^{60} + 1100\), and

\(\displaystyle H (x) = 1800 (1001 r (x)) + 4 (1000 r (x) + 2), \qquad N_0 = 4000001^4 .\)

The selected radius is \(R_k = R (\hat{\beta}_k)\), where

\(\displaystyle R (x) = \max \{H (x) + 18 + q (x) (4 r (x) + 2) + 2 r (x), \hspace{0.17em} 2 ((N_0 + q (x)) (4 r (x) + 2) + 10 r (x)) + 1\} .\)

For \(x \geqslant e\), \(r (x) \leqslant 1 + (\log x)^2\), so this expression is at most a constant times \((1 + \log x)^{122}\). Also \(\log x \leqslant 32 x^{1 / 32}\), whence \(R (x) = O (x^{122 / 32}) = o (x^4)\). Consequently \(R_k \leqslant \hat{\beta}_k^4\) eventually and \(a_k R_k \to 0\). This radius includes the support enlargement and is distinct from the principal localization radius \(\varrho_k\): in particular \(R (x) \geqslant H (x) + 18\), so the local regularity neighborhood of radius \(H (x) + 1\) lies in the window. The local symmetry, measurability and size bounds needed for the interior certificate remain inputs. The selected geometric tail is \(\text{tail}_k = 30 a_k (J_k + 5)\), where \(J_k \geqslant 0\) is the schedule's integer clock. The schedule gives \(a_{k + 1} \leqslant a_k / 3\) and \(J_{k + 1} \leqslant J_k + 1\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \text{tail}_{k + 1} \leqslant 10 a_k (J_k + 6) \leqslant \tfrac{2}{3} \text{tail}_k,\)

so the tail tends to zero geometrically. These two limits eventually give the geometric room inequality

\(\displaystyle 2 (a_k R_k + \text{tail}_k) < a_h .\)

It is this inequality that allows the localized observable to sit in the actual fixed test chamber before applying the purchased root bound. To connect that bound to the first assumption, put \(f_{k, u} = A_{k, u} - O_{k, u} (v_{k, u})\). The purchase and localization say that, on an ultrafilter-large set, this difference is interior to the chamber at level \(h\) and its root-test size is at most \(P_{d, h, j} (k)\). The separated spectator estimate supplies a constant \(B_{\sigma} \geqslant 0\), independent of \(k\), and a possibly smaller displacement radius, such that

\(\displaystyle | \mathcal{C}^{\sigma}_{k, u} (\ell, a ; f_{k, u}) | \leqslant B_{\sigma} \text{root}_{h, u} (f_{k, u}) .\)

The spectator input places the \(n\) translated factors in distinct cells and bounds each root-test size by a common \(A \geqslant 0\), uniformly in \(k\) and displacements of size at most \(a_h / 8\). The rooted moment inequality gives the bound with \(B_{\sigma} = A^n\); its integrability and separation conditions are part of that input. To see the room for the local representatives, choose a finer level \(j' \geqslant h + 1\) with \(24 a_{j'} < a_h\) at which the spectator input supplies a common bound for its finitely many tokens and frames. It follows from the centered-representative hypothesis, using exponent and error allowances \(1\), then summing the finitely many bounds and intersecting their eventual sets. The displacement and three fine-mesh widths together are less than \(a_h / 4\), inside the coarse cell's one-third margin. For sufficiently large period the spectator addresses and the insertion address occupy distinct cells. By correlation linearity, the first assumption follows with \(E_k = P_{d, h, j} (k)\) after intersecting the relevant large sets. The local representative and its root bound still use the spectator input. The cover-growth bound, purchase, localization, spectator estimate and the displayed mesh and integer-clock bounds remain inputs from the selected construction; this argument does not establish those purchases.

For each fixed \(k\) and displacement, the moving-label limit makes the local correlation differ from its limit by at most \(a_k\) on an ultrafilter-large set. Intersect that set with the root-bound set and use the triangle inequality. The resulting recording error is at most \(B_{\sigma} E_k + a_k\), which is fast because \(a_k = e^{- s_k}\). This proves the corresponding instance of (18.140)–(18.142) from the displayed root-bound and point-limit inputs. It does not construct the underlying purchased root bound. The radius can be reduced so that all moved test configurations remain separated.

Proposition 18.34. (Localized current and raw trace decay) Assume the recording bounds (18.140)– (18.142), the finite-volume linearity and identities (18.143) and (18.144), and the test separation, continuity and grade-three unit properties (18.138) and (18.139). Then for each \(d\) the recorded difference \(w_{d, k}\) is fast, and so are both unaveraged projected traces

\(\displaystyle y^0_k = \sum_a \pi x^0_k (a, a), \qquad y^-_k = \sum_a \pi x^-_k (a, a) .\)

Proof. We first record the finite-dimensional detection step. Choose finitely many \(\lambda^{\sigma}_4\) whose joint map is injective. If for each such \(\sigma\) linear functionals \(\Lambda^{\sigma}_k\) converge pointwise to \(\lambda^{\sigma}_4\), finite dimensionality gives convergence in operator norm. If \(\mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}} = \{0\}\) the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise the joint limiting map has a positive minimum on the unit sphere. Consequently, for all large \(k\) and every \(z \in \mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}}\),

\(\displaystyle \|z\| \leqslant C \max_{\sigma} | \Lambda^{\sigma}_k z| . \) (18.147)

Thus fast bounds on these finitely many moving tests imply a fast field bound, even when their exponents depend on \(\sigma\).

Fix \(d\) and a test \(\sigma\). Decrease the decay exponent if necessary so that \(0 < \eta < 1\) and \(E^{\sigma}_{d, k} = O (e^{- \eta s_k})\). Choose \(0 < \tau < \eta\) and set

\(\displaystyle n_k = \lceil e^{(1 - \tau) s_k} \rceil, \qquad h_k = a_k n_k .\)

The ceiling bound gives

\(\displaystyle e^{- \tau s_k} \leqslant h_k \leqslant e^{- \tau s_k} + e^{- s_k} .\)

Thus \(n_k \geqslant 1\), \(h_k \to 0\), and \(E^{\sigma}_{d, k} / h_k = O (e^{- (\eta - \tau) s_k})\). For large \(k\) all the points \(ja_k e_d\), \(0 \leqslant j \leqslant n_k\), lie inside the recording neighborhood. Define the moving functional

\(\displaystyle \Lambda^{\sigma}_k = \frac{1}{n_k} \sum_{j = 0}^{n_k - 1} L^{\sigma}_4 (ja_k e_d) .\)

Continuity at zero implies \(\Lambda^{\sigma}_k z \to \lambda^{\sigma}_4 z\) for every fixed \(z\): every term in the average lies within distance \(h_k\) of zero. At each fixed \(k\) intersect the finitely many ultrafilter-large sets needed for the two endpoint current estimates, the \(n_k\) difference estimates, and the exact difference identity. This intersection is nonempty. On it (18.139) gives a common value \(C_{d, k}^{\sigma} = L^{\sigma}_3 (0) v_{d, k}\) at both endpoints. Telescoping (18.143) and using the recording errors therefore yields

\(\displaystyle | \Lambda^{\sigma}_k w_{d, k} | \leqslant D^{\sigma}_{d, k} + \frac{2 E^{\sigma}_{d, k}}{a_k n_k} .\)

The right side is fast. This construction may use a different \(n_k\) for each of the finitely many tests; applying (18.147) proves that \(w_{d, k}\) is fast. The finite intersection at fixed \(k\) is essential: no common infinite intersection of ultrafilter-large sets is used.

For the false branch the first identity in (18.144) is exactly zero. For a fixed separating test, intersect the four sets in (18.142) with that exact identity and use linearity. The triangle inequality gives \(| \lambda^{\sigma}_4 (y^-_k) | \leqslant \sum_a H^{\sigma, -}_{a, k}\), which is fast. The fixed-test version of (18.147) proves the false trace is fast.

For the principal branch, the second identity in (18.144) gives the sum of the four forward differences. Intersect the four tensor-recording sets with the four difference-recording sets at \(j = 0\) and with this identity. Linearity gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \lambda^{\sigma}_4 \left( y^0_k - \sum_d w_{d, k} \right) \right| \leqslant \sum_a H^{\sigma, 0}_{a, k} + \sum_d D^{\sigma}_{d, k} .\)

The detector bound makes the difference fast. The already proved fastness of each \(w_{d, k}\) and finiteness of \(I\) now imply that \(y^0_k\) is fast.\(\Box\)

Under the displayed fixed-scale root-bound inputs, the same proof gives the more explicit bound

\(\displaystyle \|y^0_k \| + \|y^-_k \| = O ((1 + s_k)^5 e^{- s_k / 8}) . \) (18.148)

Indeed, for the finitely many required tests and insertions, each recording error is \(O ((1 + s_k)^4 e^{- s_k / 8})\): the fourth power of the affine clock has polynomial growth and the point-limit tolerance is \(a_k = e^{- s_k}\). In the sampling argument choose instead

\(\displaystyle n_k = \left\lceil \frac{e^{s_k}}{1 + s_k} \right\rceil, \qquad h_k = a_k n_k .\)

Then \((1 + s_k)^{- 1} \leqslant h_k \leqslant (1 + s_k)^{- 1} + e^{- s_k}\), so \(h_k \to 0\) and the endpoint error divided by \(h_k\) is \(O ((1 + s_k)^5 e^{- s_k / 8})\). The moving detectors and finite trace comparisons now give (18.148). In particular, the bound is \(O (e^{- \kappa s_k})\) for every \(0 < \kappa < 1 / 8\). Its constants depend on the finite tests; the bound and point-limit statements remain inputs.

For the remaining weighted step, let \(S_{k, u}\) be the finite-volume transport whose fixed-\(k\) separation limit is \(B_k\). With the same accumulated column logarithm \(\ell_k\) as above, the selected calibration gives a fixed cutoff \(k_0\) such that, for every \(k \geqslant k_0\) and every \(v \in E_4\), the bound

\(\displaystyle \|S_{k, u} v\| \leqslant C \hat{\beta}_k e^{\ell_k} \|v\|\)

on an ultrafilter-large set of \(u\). Here \(C\) depends only on the fixed finite-dimensional label coordinates. The closed-ball limit gives the same bound for \(B_k v\), and taking the supremum over \(\|v\| \leqslant 1\) gives

\(\displaystyle \|B_k \| \leqslant C \hat{\beta}_k e^{\ell_k} \qquad \text{for all sufficiently large } k. \) (18.149)

This passage fixes \(k\) and \(v\) before taking the separation limit; it does not require a common ultrafilter-large set for infinitely many \(v\). The accumulated-column estimate gives \(\ell_k = o (s_k)\), so \(e^{\ell_k}\) is subexponential. The clock is affine in \(s_k\); since \(s \leqslant \epsilon^{- 1} e^{\epsilon s}\) for \(s \geqslant 0\) and \(\epsilon > 0\), \(\hat{\beta}_k\) and hence \(\|B_k \|\) are subexponential as well. The finite-volume column calibration and fixed-scale transport limit are upstream inputs. The Crown, Eve and weighted-estimate chapters supply parts of the selected local bounds under their construction hypotheses.

Proposition 18.35. (Principal and false trace washout) Under the recording and test inputs of Proposition 18.34, the transport bound (18.149) with \(\ell_k = o (s_k)\) and the affine clock, (18.132), the chart identity (18.130) and the stated projection equivariance, for each fixed \(p \in \mathbb{N}\) and each \(b \in \{0, -\}\),

\(\displaystyle \hat{\beta}_{k_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} p} \hspace{0.17em} \text{tr} (\textsf{P}_i t^b_{k_i}) \longrightarrow 0 \quad \text{in } \mathcal{F}_4^0 . \) (18.150)

In particular the assertion holds for \(p = 1\). The true branch is treated separately below.

Proof. First, diagonal symmetrization changes nothing. Let \(\mathcal{G}\) be the finite signed and real-structure symmetry set, and let \(\rho_g\) denote the corresponding real-linear field action. In each term of the symmetry average the square of the tensor sign is one and the axis permutation is bijective. Using projection equivariance gives

\(\displaystyle q^b_k := \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} t^b_k = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|} \sum_{g \in \mathcal{G}} \mathfrak{c} \hspace{0.17em} \rho_g (y^b_k) .\)

These fixed real-linear maps are bounded in finite dimension. Thus a constant \(C\) independent of \(k\) satisfies \(\|q^b_k \| \leqslant C \|y^b_k \|\), and Proposition 18.34 makes \(q^b_k\) fast. It is centered, because each \(t^b_k (a, c)\) is centered and centering is linear.

Choose a fixed linear representative \(R : \mathcal{F}_4^{\text{int}} \to E_4\) with \(\pi R = \text{id}\) and set \(r^b_k = Rq^b_k\). It is also fast. For the fixed integer \(p\), (18.149) and the clock give subexponential growth of \(\hat{\beta}^p_k B_k\) and hence

\(\displaystyle \hat{\beta}_k^{\hspace{0.17em} p} B_k r^b_k \longrightarrow 0.\)

For example, if \(\|r^b_k \| \leqslant Ce^{- \eta s_k}\), use the subexponential bound with exponent \(\eta / 2\) on the combined factor. Composing with the cofinal sequence \(k_i\) preserves the limit. The norms of \(\textsf{M}_i\) are eventually bounded by (18.132). Hence their images of the last sequence tend to zero. Since \(q^b_k\) is centered and \(\pi Rq^b_k = q^b_k\), the chart identity yields, for all sufficiently large \(i\),

\(\displaystyle \textsf{M}_i (\hat{\beta}_{k_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} p} B_{k_i} r^b_{k_i}) = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} p} \textsf{P}_i q^b_{k_i} = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} p} \text{tr} (\textsf{P}_i t^b_{k_i}) .\)

This proves (18.150). The estimate is for each fixed \(p\); it is not a uniform assertion over a growing exponent.\(\Box\)

With the explicit root-bound and point-limit inputs, the quantitative raw rate gives more: for each fixed \(p \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(b \in \{0, -\}\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \hat{\beta}_{k_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} p} \text{tr} (\textsf{P}_i t^b_{k_i}) \right| = O \hspace{-0.17em} \left( (1 + s_{k_i})^{p + 6} e^{\ell_{k_i} - s_{k_i} / 8} \right) .\)

Indeed, the raw bound contributes \((1 + s)^5 e^{- s / 8}\), while \(\hat{\beta}^p \|B\|\) is bounded by a constant times \((1 + s)^{p + 1} e^{\ell}\); the moment maps are eventually bounded. Since \(\ell_k = o (s_k)\), the right side is \(O (e^{- \kappa s_{k_i}})\) for every \(0 < \kappa < 1 / 8\). The constants can depend on \(p\) and \(\kappa\).

The true branch instead has the unweighted limiting trace \(4\mathcal{O}\) by Proposition 18.33 and (18.128). This limit is nonzero, so the unweighted trace norm is eventually at least \(2 \|\mathcal{O}\| > 0\). Since \(\hat{\beta}_{k_i} \to \infty\), the norm of the true-branch trace multiplied by \(\hat{\beta}_{k_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} p}\) diverges for each fixed \(p \geqslant 1\). These consequences do not construct the physical tensor or close the quantitative chart inputs.

18.17The common-trajectory physical stress transfer

This section proves the physical stress and flux passage used by the selected trace chart family from the explicit response, Ward comparison, fourth-order, and weighted trace receipts displayed below.
