Their same-history producers are identified and applied below; the arguments also show how the resulting estimates combine. All limits below use one selected history, one ultrafilter, one hierarchy, and one scaling. In particular, the physical stress, the correction tensors, and the comparison observables are never reselected on separate subsequences. The quantitative estimates entering the construction include the paid response and comparison estimates from Sections 18.23, 18.3, and 18.8. The additional Ward comparison and branch receipts are stated explicitly below. Given these inputs, this section proves how the estimates produce the conserved tensor sequence and transfer its physical normalization to the selected table.

Write \(\kappa = s_G > 0\) for the slope of the actual clock. The chosen charged response uses this same constant: its target payment is \(- 12 s_G\), and division by the flux normalization factor \(12\) gives \(- s_G\). Thus the principal Ward limit below and the clock slope have exactly the same \(\kappa\). The final logarithmic trace coefficient is independent of its numerical value, as shown below; no identification with a separately normalized one-loop constant is needed here. Let \(1\) denote the unit field of the reference grade-four table, and let \(\mathcal{O}\) be the normalized curvature-square field of the reference table: the image in that table of one quarter of the sum, over all ordered pairs \((a, b)\) of coordinate directions, of the labels representing the color inner products \(\langle F_{ab}, F_{ab} \rangle\) of the curvature with itself. It is the field denoted \(\mathcal{O}\) in Subsection 18.16 and \(O\) in Subsection 18.20. It has expectation zero in the reference table, it is nonzero, and it is not a multiple of the unit field. The physical coefficient is \(d_2 = - \kappa / 4\).

18.17.1The two-direction conserved correction

Let \(E\) be the finite-dimensional real space of centered, signed-equivariant, spin-symmetric grade-four tensors for the selected table (before imposing the divergence equation), let \(V_{\text{int}}\) be the real space of grade-five vector arrays of the selected table in which divergences lie, let \(V\) be the finite-dimensional response space, which is the grade-five vector table of the reference table, and let \(\mathcal{B}\) be the grade-five profile space. Let \(D : E \to V_{\text{int}}\) be the divergence map. Its codomain belongs to the selected table, whereas the comparison and response maps below take values in the reference space \(V\). The conserved-line construction in Subsection 17.3, applied to the completed maps of Theorem 17.5, gives \(\dim \ker D = 1\) by (17.28). Its hypotheses are checked for these same maps in the normalized-energy application below: coherent unit-preserving maps, uniform short-test convergence, and the reference rank calculation. In particular, rank one does not require the correction sequence about to be constructed. The interacting divergence remains fixed; the comparison varies the optical map, and its positive scale and invertible chart preserve the kernel.

The construction solves the correction equations at each large index, rather than taking an unspecified kernel vector. Let \(\textsf{K}_i : E \to V\) be the selected comparison map, let \(s_i : \mathcal{B} \to V\) be the paid response map, and let \(L_i, \Omega_i, \Delta_i\) be the principal and two correction directions. The correction map is

\(\displaystyle \textsf{S}_i (c_0, c_1) = c_0 \Omega_i + c_1 \Delta_i, \qquad \textsf{B}_i = \textsf{K}_i \textsf{S}_i .\)

Let \(q_i > 0\) be the selected arch scale at the summit level (the quantity \(\zeta (k_i)\) of the clock paragraph below; it is compared with the branch unit's effective coupling in Lemma 18.36) and \(\rho_i\) its positive response-size bound; these are the scales in the paid bounds below. The optical comparison identity, which is an input here, states that on the selected tail the comparison map is the divergence followed by the scaled inverse chart: for every \(Q \in E\) and every axis \(a\),

\(\displaystyle (\textsf{K}_i Q)_a = q_i r_i^5 \hspace{0.17em} U_{i, 5}^{- 1} ((DQ)_a), \) (18.151)

where \(r_i > 0\) is the chart radius and \(U_{i, 5}\) is the grade-five chart isomorphism from reference fields to fields of the selected table. Since \(q_i r_i^5 > 0\) and \(U_{i, 5}\) is invertible, \(\textsf{K}_i Q = 0\) if and only if \(DQ = 0\) on that tail. For \(a = 0, 1\), the corrected response estimates compare the corresponding column with the common-trajectory linear response on a paid forecast \(w_i^a \in \mathcal{B}\):

\(\displaystyle \| \textsf{B}_i e_a - s_i (w_i^a)\| \longrightarrow 0, \qquad w_i^a \longrightarrow v_a, \qquad s_i \longrightarrow \textsf{V} .\)

The profiles \(v_0, v_1\) are the two free correction directions, and \(\textsf{W} (c_0, c_1) = c_0 v_0 + c_1 v_1\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \textsf{B}_i \longrightarrow \textsf{B}_{\infty} = \textsf{VW}, \qquad \textsf{VW} (c_0, c_1) = c_0 \textsf{V} (v_0) + c_1 \textsf{V} (v_1),\)

where \(\textsf{V}\) is the free vector-response map and \(\textsf{W}\) is the two-column reference correction. Indeed, for each \(a\), the displayed response estimate and continuity of the limiting response give \(\textsf{B}_i e_a \to \textsf{V} (v_a)\). Since the domain has basis \(e_0, e_1\), convergence of both columns implies operator-norm convergence. The map \(\textsf{B}_{\infty}\) is an isomorphism. To see this, use the real coordinates \(w = (w_0, w_1, w_2) \in \mathbb{R}^3\) that parametrize \(\mathcal{B}\) bijectively and in which the branch profiles are defined. In these coordinates

\(\displaystyle v_0 = (0, 0, 1), \qquad v_1 = (0, \tfrac{1}{4}, 0),\)

and two facts from the reference computation of Subsection 16.1 are used as inputs: \(\textsf{V}\) maps \(\mathcal{B}\) onto \(V\), and its kernel is the line spanned by the equation-of-motion direction, whose coordinates are \(\epsilon = (1, - \tfrac{1}{4}, 1)\). If \(\textsf{VW} (c_0, c_1) = 0\), then \(c_0 v_0 + c_1 v_1 = \gamma \epsilon\) for some real \(\gamma\); the coordinate \(w_0\) gives \(\gamma = 0\), then \(w_2\) gives \(c_0 = 0\) and \(w_1\) gives \(c_1 = 0\). So \(\textsf{VW}\) is injective. It is onto: an arbitrary element of \(V\) is \(\textsf{V} (w)\) for some \(w\), and

\(\displaystyle w - w_0 \epsilon = (w_2 - w_0) v_0 + (4 w_1 + w_0) v_1,\)

so \(\textsf{V} (w) = \textsf{VW} (w_2 - w_0, \hspace{0.17em} 4 w_1 + w_0)\) because \(\textsf{V} (\epsilon) = 0\). In finite dimension, if \(\| \textsf{B}_i - \textsf{B}_{\infty} \| < 1 / (2\| \textsf{B}_{\infty}^{- 1} \|)\), then for every \(c\),

\(\displaystyle \| \textsf{B}_i c\| \geqslant \| \textsf{B}_{\infty}^{- 1} \|^{- 1} \|c\|- \|(\textsf{B}_i - \textsf{B}_{\infty}) c\| \geqslant \frac{\|c\|}{2 \| \textsf{B}_{\infty}^{- 1} \|} .\)

Thus \(\textsf{B}_i\) is injective and hence bijective, and its inverse norms are uniformly bounded on a tail. Let \(X_i = q_i \textsf{K}_i (L_i)\) be the uncorrected forcing. The paid response-size estimate gives \(\|X_i \| \leqslant C \rho_i\), where \(\rho_i\) is the positive response-size bound. Define

\(\displaystyle c_i = - \textsf{B}_i^{- 1} X_i, \qquad T_i = q_i L_i + \textsf{S}_i (c_i) .\)

Then \(\textsf{K}_i (T_i) = 0\), and the optical comparison identity (18.151) gives \(DT_i = 0\): on the selected tail, \(\textsf{K}_i (Q) = 0\) is equivalent to \(DQ = 0\) for every \(Q \in E\). The inverse bound gives \(\|c_i \| \leqslant C' \rho_i\).

The scalar calibration uses the same paid functional \(\mu_i \in V^{\ast}\) that controls the response bound. Let \(\Lambda \in \mathcal{B}^{\ast}\) be the free Ward functional. At chart level \(k_i\), write \(t_i\) for its positive Ward comparison scale and \(z_i = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}\) for its clock weight. The bound comparison gives \(t_i \to \infty\), \(\rho_i \leqslant t_i \leqslant z_i\). The Ward comparison inputs include a constant \(C_{\mu}\) with \(\| \mu_i \| \leqslant C_{\mu}\) eventually, and, for every \(\delta > 0\), the eventual estimate

\(\displaystyle t_i | \mu_i (s_i y) - \Lambda (y) | \leqslant \delta \|y\| \quad \text{for all } y \in \mathcal{B}.\)

These Ward comparison inputs are the conclusion of Proposition 18.32. We use it under the following identification of its notation with the present one. Its spaces \(E\) and \(H\) are \(\mathcal{B}\) and \(V\); its Ward comparison maps \(s_i\) are the present \(s_i\), defined by the selected chart formula in (18.112) and not merely maps with the same limit; its free projection \(f\) is \(\textsf{V}\); its Ward functional \(\rho\) is \(\Lambda\); its postselected level \(n_i\) is the present \(k_i\), the summit index of the frame level of the \(i\)th chart; and its weight \(\mathfrak{a}_i\) is \(t_i\). In the notation of Subsection 18.23, with \(p_i = z_i\), one has \(t_i = A_{1 / 5} (i)\) and \(\rho_i = A_{1 / 6} (i)\), and \(q_i\) is the arch scale \(b_i\) at the level \(k_i\). The required arch ratios are verified below from the running estimate and the first-crossing schedule. The additional schedule and column bounds in the Ward comparison proposition's hypotheses (S1) and (S6) follow from (17.20), taking \(\mathfrak{C}_j\) to dominate the grade-five column parameters of the fixed stage profiles. These are the same column budgets in the raw-step estimate, not operator norms of a coupling-dependent response. The stage entry is purchased before selecting the history and hence before its charts. For its marked vector use the actual medium \(m_{k_i} = q_i^{- 1} \textsf{q}_{k_i} - \textsf{w}_{k_i}\), with the chosen and reference responses of Subsection 18.23. That section's weighted assembly gives \(m_{k_i} \to x\) and \(t_i \Lambda (m_{k_i}) \to 0\); its chart-field decay gives \(z_i s_i (m_{k_i}) \to 0\). Since \(t_i \leqslant z_i\), the latter implies \(t_i s_i (m_{k_i}) \to 0\). These are exactly the three marked-vector limits of the Ward comparison proposition, with the same normalized chart map. The selected-lift formulas there use the same finite-volume inverse column and the same fixed-level ultrafilter limit as this construction; thus their identity \(PY_i = X_i\) holds before and after projection, not merely asymptotically. Apply its initial-level bound and transport bound to this lift, taking the finite intersection of the levelwise ultrafilter sets between the frame level and its summit. This is legitimate at each fixed chart index; no tail uniform over all chart indices is required. Its convergence-of-selected-lifts lemma therefore applies to the same lift. The actual tested-bound and raw-step bounds (S4) and (S5) for this lift are proved in the tested-bound and adjacent-column arguments below. The identity and finite intersection only synchronize them. With these quantitative bounds, the Ward comparison proposition supplies the bounded functionals \(\mu_i\) and the estimate uniform over every \(y \in \mathcal{B}\) used above. We spell out how these conclusions give the three limits needed for calibration. Let \(Y_i\) be the principal forecast. The paid response comparison, penumbra estimate, and principal Ward limit are

\(\displaystyle q_i \textsf{K}_i (L_i) - s_i (Y_i) \longrightarrow 0, \qquad \|Y_i \| \leqslant C \rho_i \leqslant Ct_i, \qquad \Lambda (Y_i) \longrightarrow - \kappa . \) (18.152)

The first convergence and the uniform bound on \(\mu_i\) imply \(\mu_i (X_i) - \mu_i (s_i (Y_i)) \to 0\). For any \(\delta > 0\), the Ward comparison estimate then gives eventually

\(\displaystyle | \mu_i (s_i (Y_i)) - \Lambda (Y_i) | \leqslant \delta \|Y_i \| / t_i \leqslant C \delta .\)

Taking the limit superior and then \(\delta \downarrow 0\) proves \(\mu_i (X_i) \to - \kappa\); no norm convergence of the principal forecast is needed.

For a correction branch \(P \in \{\Omega, \Delta\}\), let \(U_i^P \in \mathcal{B}\) be its finite-volume profile and \(v_P \in \mathcal{B}\) its reference profile. The comparison and Ward estimates needed are

\(\displaystyle U_i^P \to v_P, \qquad z_i \| \textsf{K}_i (P_i) - s_i (U_i^P)\| \to 0, \qquad t_i | \Lambda (U_i^P) - \Lambda (v_P) | \to 0, \) (18.153)

where \(P_i\) denotes the corresponding correction tensor. Since \(U_i^P\) is bounded, the uniform Ward comparison estimate implies \(t_i | \mu_i (s_i (U_i^P)) - \Lambda (U_i^P) | \to 0\). Using \(t_i \leqslant z_i\) and the uniform operator bound for \(\mu_i\) in the middle estimate of (18.153) gives

\(\displaystyle t_i | \mu_i (\textsf{K}_i (P_i)) - \Lambda (v_P) | \longrightarrow 0.\)

The reference Ward values are \(\Lambda (v_{\Omega}) = 0\) and \(\Lambda (v_{\Delta}) = - 4\). Thus the principal and two correction limits are now established from the explicitly stated paid inputs:

\(\displaystyle \mu_i (X_i) \to - \kappa, \qquad t_i \mu_i (\textsf{K}_i \Omega_i) \to 0, \qquad t_i (\mu_i (\textsf{K}_i \Delta_i) + 4) \to 0. \) (18.154)

Applying \(\mu_i\) to the exact cancellation \(X_i + \textsf{B}_i c_i = 0\) gives

\(\displaystyle \mu_i (X_i) + c_i^0 \mu_i (\textsf{K}_i \Omega_i) + c_i^1 \mu_i (\textsf{K}_i \Delta_i) = 0.\)

Set \(\alpha_i = t_i \mu_i (\textsf{K}_i \Omega_i)\) and \(\beta_i = t_i (\mu_i (\textsf{K}_i \Delta_i) + 4)\). Since \(\|c_i \| \leqslant C \rho_i \leqslant Ct_i\), both \(c_i^0 / t_i\) and \(c_i^1 / t_i\) are bounded. The exact equation rewrites as

\(\displaystyle 0 = \mu_i (X_i) - 4 c_i^1 + \frac{c_i^0}{t_i} \alpha_i + \frac{c_i^1}{t_i} \beta_i .\)

The last two terms tend to zero by (18.154), so \(\mu_i (X_i) \to - \kappa\) implies

\(\displaystyle c_i^1 \longrightarrow - \frac{\kappa}{4} .\)

This is the stable-inversion and coefficient-extraction argument of Subsection 17.1.

The correction is built from three paid tensor families, the same at every index: a principal tensor \(L_i\) and two correction tensors \(\Omega_i, \Delta_i\). These families and the coefficients \(c_i^0, c_i^1\) below are the tensors of that selected construction. Their field and trace properties follow from the common-trajectory branch application below. Let \(\mathcal{P}_i\) be the scaled pullback of a stress tensor, and put \(\mathcal{Y}_i = q_i^{- 1} \mathcal{P}_i\). The scaled pullback is the chart map multiplied by its positive crown normalization; by definition, \(\mathcal{P}_i = q_i \mathcal{Y}_i\) on every tensor. Here \(q_i = \zeta (k_i)\) is the arch scale at the selected level (see the clock paragraph below), and the crown normalization is \(q_i\) times the fourth power of the chart radius. The defining-limit identification (18.157) below identifies \(q_i\) with the branch unit's effective coupling at \(k_i\); the subsequent ratio lemma also records the transfer for asymptotically equal normalizations. The defining corrected tensor is

\(\displaystyle T_i = q_i L_i + c_i^0 \Omega_i + c_i^1 \Delta_i . \) (18.155)

For the three tensor sequences \(Q_i \in \{L_i, \Omega_i, \Delta_i \}\), let their reference branches be \(\nu_L = 0\), \(\nu_{\Omega} = -\), and \(\nu_{\Delta} = +\). Let \(W_i^Q (a, b)\) be the ordered grade-four label for the two tensor indices \(a, b\) obtained by the finite-volume response of the corresponding sequence. These ordered labels are the entries of a finite array; no spatial sum is hidden in this notation. Let \(\textsf{M}_i\) be the finite-volume fourth-order coefficient map, obtained by applying the centered chart pullback to a grade-four label. It is a continuous linear map \(\textsf{M}_i : \mathcal{L}_4 \to \mathcal{F}_4\), not a scalar moment matrix. Each \(W_i^Q (a, b)\) belongs to \(\mathcal{L}_4\). For every sufficiently large \(i\) (so the selected level exceeds the fixed reference cutoff), symmetrizing the two ordered indices gives the chart formula

\(\displaystyle [\mathcal{P}_i (Q_i)]_{ab} = \frac{1}{2} (\textsf{M}_i (W_i^Q (a, b)) + \textsf{M}_i (W_i^Q (b, a))) . \) (18.156)

The selected coefficient convergence gives \(W_i^Q (a, b) \to W_{\nu_Q} (a, b)\), and the fourth-order maps converge in operator norm, \(\textsf{M}_i \to \textsf{M}_{\infty}\); for the present normalization this uses \(\vartheta_i \to 1\) of Lemma 18.36, since \(\textsf{M}_i\) is \(\vartheta_i^{- 1}\) times the branch unit's moment map. Thus, entry by entry in the finite stress array,

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{P}_i (Q_i) \longrightarrow \mathcal{B}_{\nu_Q}, \qquad (\mathcal{B}_{\nu})_{ab} := \frac{1}{2} (\textsf{M}_{\infty} (W_{\nu} (a, b)) + \textsf{M}_{\infty} (W_{\nu} (b, a))) .\)

This follows directly from continuity of the finite bilinear expression in (18.156); no limit is interchanged with an infinite sum. The explicit free tensor calculation identifies these arrays with the reference material tensors. Write

\(\displaystyle M_0 =\mathcal{B}_0, \qquad M_{\Omega} =\mathcal{B}_-, \qquad M_1 =\mathcal{B}_+ .\)

The false-branch tensor need not vanish; it is its coefficient \(c_i^0 / q_i \to 0\) that removes it from the normalized field limit. The three branch limits are on the same history.

The three branch limits, and the trace statements used below, are the conclusions of Subsection 18.16, under the following identification. The tensors are \(L_i = t^0_{k_i}\), \(\Omega_i = t^-_{k_i}\) and \(\Delta_i = t^+_{k_i}\), at the same level \(k_i\) as above. That section uses the map \(\textsf{P}_i = \omega_i U_i^{- 1}\) with \(\omega_i = \beta_Z (k_i) r_i^4\), where \(\beta_Z\) is its effective coupling, whereas the present crown normalization uses the arch scale \(q_i\) in place of \(\beta_Z (k_i)\). Both normalizations use the same fixed-level limit of the running coupling. Indeed the arch is constructed by taking, for each level \(j\), the limit of \(\beta_{N, p, j}\) along the selected ultrafilter; this defines \(\zeta (j)\). The branch unit's \(\beta_Z (j)\) is the limit of this same real-valued function along this same ultrafilter. Uniqueness of a limit in \(\mathbb{R}\) therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \beta_Z (j) = \zeta (j) \qquad \text{for every } j.\) (18.157)

No further extraction or adjustment of the coupling is involved. With

\(\displaystyle \vartheta_i = \frac{\beta_Z (k_i)}{q_i} > 0,\)

one has \(\vartheta_i = 1\) for the actual constructed arch. More generally, the scaled pullback \(\mathcal{P}_i\) is the entrywise application of \(\vartheta_i^{- 1} \textsf{P}_i\), and \(\textsf{M}_i\) is its centered moment map, which is \(\vartheta_i^{- 1}\) times that of \(\textsf{P}_i\). The ordered labels are \(W_i^Q = W^b_{k_i}\) for the branch \(b = \nu_Q\), and \(M_0 = M (w^0)\), \(M_{\Omega} = M (w^-)\), \(M_1 = M (w^+)\). Proposition 18.33 gives the three field limits in (18.158); Proposition 18.35 with exponent \(p = 1\) gives the weighted trace limits for the principal and the false branch that are used below, and gives nothing for the true branch; and the trace formula (18.128) gives \(\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M_1 = 4\mathcal{O}\). Their recording, transport and chart estimates concern exactly this supplied history: at fixed \(i\), use the same finite-volume tensors and the same ultrafilter before passing to \(i \to \infty\). The three branch choices and sixteen tensor entries form a finite set, so their eventual identities and estimates hold on one common tail. This finite intersection does not prove the actual branch coefficient bias or local-current identities, nor the three recording estimates in (18.140), (18.141), and (18.142). Those estimates are required at these actual labels. No separate history is selected for a branch. The finite-array argument written out above repeats the proof of the first of them under the same hypotheses, together with \(\vartheta_i \to 1\) of Lemma 18.36.

Those conclusions are stated for \(\textsf{P}_i\). They transfer to \(\mathcal{P}_i = \vartheta_i^{- 1} \textsf{P}_i\) because of the following lemma.

Lemma 18.36. (The two normalizations agree asymptotically) Use the scale ratio \(\beta_Z (j) / \hat{\beta}_j \to 1\) from Subsection 18.16 (proved in its scale-ratio paragraph, after the display (18.135), from its running estimate and fixed-scale limit), and the first clock limit \(\zeta (j) / \hat{\beta}_j \to 1\) of (18.172), proved below from Lemma 16.17. Here \(\hat{\beta}_j\) is the same clock coupling in both statements: the branch unit and this section use the hatted coupling of the one selected clock. Then \(\vartheta_i \to 1\). Consequently, for \(Q_i \in \{L_i, \Omega_i, \Delta_i \}\) with reference tensors \(M_0, M_{\Omega}, M_1\),

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} \textsf{P}_i (Q_i) \to M \Longrightarrow \mathcal{P}_i (Q_i) \to M, \qquad \textsf{P}_i (\Delta_i) \to M_1 \Longrightarrow \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i) \to 4\mathcal{O},\\ z_i \text{tr} \textsf{P}_i (Q_i) \to 0 \Longrightarrow z_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (Q_i) \to 0. \end{array}\)

Proof. The summit index \(k_i\) is a level not below the frame level \(j_i\), and \(j_i \to \infty\), so \(k_i \to \infty\). Hence both ratio limits hold along \(j = k_i\). The quantities \(\beta_Z (k_i)\), \(q_i = \zeta (k_i)\) and \(\hat{\beta}_{k_i}\) are positive, and

\(\displaystyle \vartheta_i = \frac{\beta_Z (k_i) / \hat{\beta}_{k_i}}{\zeta (k_i) / \hat{\beta}_{k_i}} \longrightarrow \frac{1}{1} = 1.\)

In particular \(\vartheta_i^{- 1} \to 1\), and \(\vartheta_i^{- 1} \leqslant 2\) for all large \(i\). Entrywise, \(\mathcal{P}_i (Q_i) = \vartheta_i^{- 1} \textsf{P}_i (Q_i)\) in the finite product of copies of \(\mathcal{F}_4^0\); the product of a convergent scalar sequence and a convergent vector sequence converges to the product of the limits, which gives the first implication. The trace is linear and continuous on that finite product, so if \(\textsf{P}_i (\Delta_i) \to M_1\), then \(\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i) = \vartheta_i^{- 1} \text{tr} \textsf{P}_i (\Delta_i) \to \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M_1 = 4\mathcal{O}\) by the trace formula of the branch unit. Finally \(\|z_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (Q_i)\| = \vartheta_i^{- 1} \|z_i \text{tr} \textsf{P}_i (Q_i)\| \leqslant 2 \|z_i \text{tr} \textsf{P}_i (Q_i)\|\) for large \(i\), which tends to zero when the right side does.\(\Box\)

Since \(z_i = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}\), the washout limits of the branch unit with \(p = 1\) are exactly the hypotheses \(z_i \text{tr} \textsf{P}_i (Q_i) \to 0\) for \(Q_i \in \{L_i, \Omega_i \}\). By the lemma, the field limits and the exponent-one principal and false trace limits used below hold for \(\mathcal{P}_i\) on this same history. For the actual arch this also follows directly from (18.157), since \(\mathcal{P}_i = \textsf{P}_i\). The exact identity for \(\mathcal{Y}_i (T_i)\) below uses only \(\mathcal{P}_i = q_i \mathcal{Y}_i\) and linearity, so it is unaffected.

These are the field receipts used below:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlllllll} \mathcal{P}_i (L_i) & \longrightarrow M_0, & \mathcal{P}_i (\Omega_i) & \longrightarrow M_{\Omega}, & \mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i) & \longrightarrow M_1, & \frac{\rho_i}{q_i} & \longrightarrow 0, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.158)} \end{array}\)

The displayed ratio is the selected-scale washout input: the response bound is bounded by the one-sixth subpower clock bound, whose ratio to the effective coupling tends to zero along this scale. Since \(M_0\) is the prescribed material tensor and \(|c_i^0 | + |c_i^1 | \leqslant C \rho_i\), it follows that \(c_i^a / q_i \to 0\) for \(a = 0, 1\). Linearity gives the exact identity

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_i (T_i) =\mathcal{P}_i (L_i) + \frac{c_i^0}{q_i} \mathcal{P}_i (\Omega_i) + \frac{c_i^1}{q_i} \mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i) .\)

The principal, false-correction, and true-correction rescaled tensors converge to \(M_0, M_{\Omega}, M_1\), respectively. In particular the two correction phase tensors are bounded, and the exact identity proves

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_i (T_i) \longrightarrow M_0 . \) (18.159)

For the scalar trace, the true correction profile is the true material correction, whose diagonal trace is \(4\mathcal{O}\) by the trace formula of the branch-moment unit recalled above: the diagonal sum is \(4 (0 + 4 \cdot \frac{1}{4}) \mathcal{O}= 4\mathcal{O}\). We also use the scale bounds \(q_i \leqslant 2 z_i\) and \(\rho_i \leqslant t_i \leqslant z_i\) and the limits

\(\displaystyle z_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (L_i) \to 0, \quad z_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (\Omega_i) \to 0, \quad |c_i^0 | \leqslant C \rho_i, \quad \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i) \to 4\mathcal{O}, \quad c_i^1 \to - \kappa / 4.\)

The first two limits are the exponent-one principal and false trace limits of the branch-moment unit recalled above. The bound on \(c_i^0\) is contained in the coefficient bound \(|c_i^0 | + |c_i^1 | \leqslant C \rho_i\) used above. The fourth limit follows from the field receipt \(\mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i) \to M_1\) and \(\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M_1 = 4\mathcal{O}\), by continuity of the trace. The coefficient limit \(c_i^1 \to - \kappa / 4\) was proved above, after (18.154), from the exact cancellation \(X_i + \textsf{B}_i c_i = 0\), the Ward comparison limits (18.154), the coefficient bound \(\|c_i \| \leqslant C \rho_i\) and the scale bound \(\rho_i \leqslant t_i\); it is therefore conditional on the same inputs. These scale inequalities need no additional receipt. In the notation of Subsection 18.23, \(\rho_i \leqslant \exp ((\log \log z_i)^2 / 6)\) and \(t_i = \exp ((\log \log z_i)^2 / 5)\), so \(\rho_i \leqslant t_i\) and \(t_i \to \infty\). Since \((\log \log z)^2 = o (\log z)\), \(t_i / z_i \to 0\). Finally the first clock input in (18.172), applied at \(k_i\), gives \(q_i / z_i \to 1\), hence \(q_i \leqslant 2 z_i\) eventually and \(\rho_i / q_i \to 0\). These consequences still depend on the stated clock and response-size inputs. To see exactly how the weighted estimates enter, use \(\mathcal{P}_i (T_i) = q_i \mathcal{P}_i (L_i) + c_i^0 \mathcal{P}_i (\Omega_i) + c_i^1 \mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i)\). The principal contribution tends to zero since \(q_i \leqslant 2 z_i\) and the exponent-one principal trace estimate gives \(z_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (L_i) \to 0\). For the false correction, \(|c_i^0 | \leqslant C \rho_i \leqslant Cz_i\) and the exponent-one weighted false-sector estimate gives \(z_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (\Omega_i) \to 0\). Finally \(c_i^1 \to - \kappa / 4\) and \(\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (\Delta_i) \to 4\mathcal{O}\). Thus applying the trace to (18.155) and using these three contributions gives

\(\displaystyle \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (T_i) \longrightarrow - \kappa \mathcal{O}. \) (18.160)

Indeed, the principal term tends to zero, the first correction term is bounded by \(Cz_i | \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (\Omega_i) |\), and the last term tends to \((- \kappa / 4) (4\mathcal{O})\). This establishes the physical field and flux limits from the exact three-term correction, with no interchange of limits or choice of history.

18.17.2Proportionality transfers the physical normalization

We now show that the corrected physical tensor and the selected table stress have the same limiting normalization. Let \(A\) be the selected algebra, and let \(R\) be its centered table stress \(R = \text{reCenter}_S (A. \text{energy}) \in E\), whose entries are conserved and whose flux is the physical Ward flux. Let \(g_i = T_i\) be the explicit conserved correction sequence just constructed, viewed in the same one-dimensional kernel of \(D\). Its normalized field and flux limits are (18.159) and (18.160). We prove the transfer, spelling out the nonzero test and the scalar ratio.

The argument uses the following inputs, all for the same selected table, reference table, derivative maps, chart and scaling as above; the derivative maps of the two tables are assumed to obey the derivative and orientation laws of their tables. None of these inputs is a consequence of rank one or of the field and flux limits.

  1. Physical stress laws. The tensor \(R\) lies in \(E\), satisfies \(DR = 0\), and obeys the translation Ward identity of the selected table: for every separated configuration consisting of a distinguished point and spectator points, every tuple of spectator labels, every cutoff that separates the distinguished point from the spectator points, every axis \(b\) and every field \(B\), the shell measurement of \(R\) with that cutoff and with \(B\) at the distinguished point equals the correlation at that configuration with the derivative field \(D_b^S B\) at the distinguished point and the same spectators.

  2. Reference stress laws. The material tensor \(M_0\) is a centered, conserved stress tensor of the reference table and obeys the translation Ward identity of the reference table.

  3. A detecting derivative. There are an axis \(a\), a reference observer \(o\), and a reference grade-four field \(v\) whose derivative in direction \(a\) has nonzero value \(y\) on the grade-five test of \(o\).

  4. Tested convergence and jet comparison. The following four statements hold for the selected chart maps \(U_{i, d}\) and radii \(r_i\).

      (4a)

      For every integrated reference test, that is, a compactly supported test function with fixed spectator labels and one distinguished slot, and every fixed reference field \(g\) of the grade of that slot, the correspondingly rescaled tested correlation of the selected table with \(U_{i, d} g\) in the distinguished slot converges to the reference tested correlation with \(g\) in that slot. This holds also for the test obtained by differentiating the test function in the distinguished variable.

      (4b)

      There are finitely many integrated reference tests with a grade-five distinguished slot whose reference functionals have zero common kernel on the reference grade-five field space.

      (4c)

      In both tables a tested correlation with the derivative field \(D_a g\) in the distinguished slot equals the derivative in direction \(a\), in the distinguished variable, of the correlation with \(g\) in that slot.

      (4d)

      Pointwise jet comparison for the observer \(o\). For every fixed reference grade-five field \(g\), the selected correlation at the configuration \(r_i x\) with \(U_{i, 5} g\) at the distinguished point and the spectator labels \(\text{pins}_i \circ q\) converges to the observer's reference value \(o.test_5 (g)\). For every fixed reference stress tensor \(H\), the rescaled shell measurement \(\mathcal{L}_i (H) = \mathscr{F}_i (r_i^{- 4} U_{i, 4} H)\) defined below, in which the chart is applied entrywise, converges to the reference shell measurement \(\text{shellGrad} (H, \chi, a, x, q, v)\). Both are obtained below from the comparison-of-jets estimate for the finitely many grades and arities fixed by the observer, on a comparison window containing the support of the gradient of \(\chi\) together with the spectator points. The deduction from that compact comparison is given below; the comparison must concern these same chart maps.

The physical stress in item 1 is supplied by the following choice of the energy in the selected algebra and the centering argument after it. The reference laws and detector in items 2–3 are verified below for the actual Wick comparator used by this chart family. For the completed charts of the selected chart family, the comparison statements in item 4 follow below from their uniform short-test estimate and the derivative laws of the two tables.

Identifying the actual reference fields and their normalization.
We verify the reference identifications needed here before using the Ward calculation. Keep the frame register of the supplied family fixed. Let \(c_d\) be its coordinate isomorphism from invariant jet polynomials of grade at most \(d\) to coordinate labels, let \(\pi_d^0\) be projection to reference fields, and let \(\text{obs}_d\) be passage to the on-shell Wick polynomial. The actual reference table is defined by
\(\displaystyle S_0 (\pi_{d_1}^0 c_{d_1} p_1, \ldots, \pi_{d_n}^0 c_{d_n} p_n ; x) = \text{Wick}_x \left( \text{obs}_{d_1} \hspace{0.17em} p_1, \ldots, \text{obs}_{d_n} \hspace{0.17em} p_n \right) .\) (18.161)

Indeed its coordinate correlation is the Wick correlation composed in each slot with \(c_d^{- 1}\); projection quotients precisely the labels annihilated by all separated spectator correlations. Thus this identity uses the reference table of the supplied family, not a replacement table. Its Wick covariance is the curvature covariance (16.12), obtained by taking the two curls of the potential kernel \((4 \pi^2 |x|^2)^{- 1}\); this normalization is part of the defining free table. Write \(\Phi_d p = \pi_d^0 c_d p\). The on-shell polynomial address of \(\Phi_d p\) is \(\text{obs}_d \hspace{0.17em} p\). The free separation result of Subsection 16.4 makes this address an isomorphism onto the invariant on-shell sector: its kernel is exactly the correlation null space. In particular the reference is faithful.

Its derivative plan is obtained by conjugating polynomial translation through this address. On a curvature jet, translation raises the jet in the specified direction; on products it obeys the Leibniz rule. The rotation action is obtained by conjugating rotation of the same jets, including their tensor indices. Consequently, writing \(\delta_a\) and \(\mathscr{D}_A\) for these polynomial derivations,

\(\displaystyle D_a^0 \Phi_d p = \Phi_{d + 1} (\delta_a p), \qquad K_A^0 \Phi_d p = \Phi_d (\mathscr{D}_A p) .\) (18.162)

These identities follow by applying the injective polynomial address to either side. They are compatible with grade inclusions since those inclusions preserve the addressed polynomial. They also prove the positional derivative identity by differentiating the finite Wick sum in (18.161). The reference data installed before the comparison curve have exactly these derivative and rotation maps; fixing the stress normalization preserves both maps and the vacuum. Even if a derivative plan is specified instead by its positional derivative law, it must be this one: subtracting the two laws gives zero separated correlations for their difference on every label, so faithfulness makes the difference zero. This verifies (R1) and (R2) of Subsection 16.2 on these same data.

For the stress identification there is a sign to retain. The coordinate pair \(P_{ab, cd}\) in (18.125) is the representative of \(\langle F_{ab}, F_{cd} \rangle\), with no extra factor. The canonical positive Maxwell tensor is therefore the image of (16.19); this verifies (R3). But the branch coefficients \(w^0 = (- 1, 1 / 4, - 1)\) in (18.126) give

\(\displaystyle (M_0)_{ab} = - \Phi_4 \left( \sum_c \langle F_{ac}, F_{bc} \rangle - \frac{\delta_{ab}}{4} \sum_{c, e} \langle F_{ce}, F_{ce} \rangle \right) = - J^0_{ab} .\) (18.163)

The products inside \(\Phi_4\) are Wick ordered by the defining observable map. Hence \(M_0\) is centered and conserved. Applying the proved polynomial Ward identity (16.20) and (18.162), the outward collar flux of \(J^0\) is minus the derivative insertion, and that of \(M_0 = - J^0\) is the derivative insertion with coefficient one. The same sign gives coefficient one for rotations. This proves input 2; no choice depending on the comparison sequence has entered it.

Finally the definition of the scalar label in the branch table gives

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{O}= \Phi_4 \left( \frac{1}{4} \sum_{a, b} \langle F_{ab}, F_{ab} \rangle \right) .\) (18.164)

Here the sum is over ordered pairs, just as in (18.163). Applying (18.161) to two such labels, the explicit Wick computation (18.189) proves (18.190) for this actual \(\mathcal{O}\): \(S_0 (\mathcal{O}, \mathcal{O}; x, y) = 3 \dim \mathfrak{g}/ (\pi^4 |x - y|^8)\). Its expectation is zero by Wick ordering. For \(\dim \mathfrak{g}> 0\) this two-point function is nonzero, so \(\mathcal{O} \ne 0\); its zero expectation also excludes a nonzero multiple of the unit. The differentiated two-point test below now supplies input 3 without an additional reference-normalization assumption.

The tested-bound input for this lift.
The Ward comparison's (S4) is supplied by the quantitative invoice of Lemma 17.8, not merely by the convergence of tested correlations. Indeed take its grade-five reading \(T\) at the same station, finite-volume cutoff and period as the selected lift. Its calibrated map is \(A = d_k^{- 5} \text{Col}_k\). Both maps send the reference unit to the coefficient unit, and \(A\) is injective by the calibrated column isomorphism. The invoice gives, for every coefficient vector \(v\),
\(\displaystyle |Av - Tv|_1 \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2} |Av|_1 .\)

Its threshold is uniform over the finite volumes and the chart offset; it was paid in the same joint chart purchase. Enlarging the selected tail gives the half-accuracy estimate as well. Thus the two estimates in (18.116) hold with the actual invoice bound \(\eta_i = H^{- 1 / 2}\). In particular \(T\) is invertible, and the selected finite-volume lift is exactly \(T^{- 1} \text{Dil}_{\xi^{- 1}} \hspace{0.17em} \text{Pos}_{\sigma_i} (Uh)_{\alpha}\). To check that the invoice remains valid after deleting the vacuum coefficient, apply both bounds to \(v^{\circ} = v - (Tv)_0 e_{\text{ref}}\). Then \(Tv^{\circ} = \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} Tv\) and \(\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} Av^{\circ} = \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} Av\), while \(|Av^{\circ} |_1 \leqslant 2 |Tv^{\circ} |_1\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle | \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} Av - \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} Tv|_1 \leqslant 2 \eta_i | \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} Tv|_1 .\)

This is precisely the centered coefficient estimate needed for the Ward comparison's initial-level bound, with the same inverse reading and lift.

The adjacent-column receipt.
Fix a level \(m\) beyond the grade-five entrance, and retain the actual calibrated columns at levels \(m, m + 1\) of one finite-volume hierarchy. Write \(L = L_m\), \(B = C_m\), \(U = C_{m + 1}\), and let \(k \geqslant 1\) be the selected local forecast bound and \(r > 0\) the fixed local remainder transport bound at that transition. Put
\(\displaystyle z = | \text{core} \tilde{z}_{m + 1} |_1, \qquad y = | \text{core} \tilde{z}_m |_1 .\)

Deleting the vacuum coefficient before applying the calibrated front estimate gives \(y \leqslant UL^5 z\). The block map obeys \(| \text{Block}_m \hspace{0.17em} \text{core} \tilde{z}_{m + 1} |_1 \leqslant L^5 z\).

Apply the actual insertion and local uniqueness comparison of Subsection 18.4, specifically (18.22) and (18.26), to these adjacent calibrated fields. Their format and remainder bounds are the calibrated field certificates, and their exact density transfer is the defining recursion of this same hierarchy. The joint estimate takes the following form. For every \(\epsilon > 0\), on a sufficiently large period tail, uniformly over \(N > m\) and over the terminal label,

\(\displaystyle | \tilde{z}_m -\mathcal{L}_m \tilde{z}_{m + 1} |_1 \leqslant s_m (z + Uz) + \epsilon \{By + k| \text{Block}_m \hspace{0.17em} \text{core} \tilde{z}_{m + 1} |_1 + rUz\}, \qquad s_m \leqslant A_{- 1 / 2} (m + 1) .\)

Here \(\mathcal{L}_m\) is the actual local forecast, not a freely chosen comparison map. To see the period quantifier, compare the calibrated coarse density with the forecast of the calibrated fine density. Both are exact representations of the same transferred density. Cancel the common nonzero vacuum density and combine the two absolutely summable regular remainders, changing the sign of one family. The local section of their coefficient difference is exactly this combined remainder. Its nonwrapping terms have zero local projection. Subtract a finite partial sum of those terms before applying a member of the finite separating probe family. The corresponding finite probe is exactly zero. The probe comparison estimate bounds the remaining error by its fixed derivative bound times the sum of the omitted nonwrapping weights and the weights of wrapping supports. If the period exceeds \(10 (h (4 B_m + 2) + 2 B_m)\), every wrapping support anchored at the insertion has enough weighted support mass to pay \(e^{- ah}\), where \(a > 0\) is the fixed support exponent. Send the finite partial sum through all nonwrapping terms: its omitted weights tend to zero by absolute summability. This finite-partial-sum argument avoids differentiating an uncontrolled infinite series. Sum the finitely many probe bounds and apply the fixed coefficient recovery matrix. The resulting bound on the coefficient difference is \(Ce^{- ah}\) times the sum of the two remainder bounds. Choose \(h\) so that \(Ce^{- ah} \leqslant \epsilon\), then require the period to contain that collar and the fixed forecast support. The constants and the period threshold are independent of \(N\) and the terminal label. The forecast remainder is bounded by \(k| \text{Block}_m \hspace{0.17em} \text{core} \tilde{z}_{m + 1} |_1 + rUz\); its coefficient error is \(s_m (z + Uz)\). These two bounds give the displayed inequality by the triangle inequality. The selected transition bound is \(s_m = w_m A_{- 1 / 2} (m + 1)\) with \(0 < w_m \leqslant 1\), which gives its stated bound.

The forecast map is exactly the primary map of Subsection 10.8, not a matrix selected from its bounds. To verify its normalization, let \(R\) be that section's unscaled coefficient recovery, put \(f = \vartheta_{m + 1}\), \(c = \vartheta_m\), and write \((g_i, \ell_i)\) for the two grades of its fixed coordinate basis. The local map before block transport is

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{B} = \mathscr{S}_c R \mathscr{D}_L \mathscr{S}_{f^{- 1}}, \qquad \mathscr{B}_{bi} = c^{\ell_b} f^{- \ell_i} L^{g_i} R_{bi} .\)

This is the same full-matrix identity as in the primary calibration; Subsection 10.9 explicitly uses that same \(R\). Its proved identity (10.42), with \(u = L^{- 1}\), gives

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{B}_{bi} = (c / f)^{\ell_i} \delta_{bi} \qquad (g_b \leqslant g_i) .\)

There is no error term in this identity: the polynomial transport of a weight-\(a\) potential is \(u^a\) times that potential plus a term of weight less than \(a\); the local section's lower-weight cancellation annihilates every chain-rule term using the latter. The section then reads the original homogeneous coordinate exactly. This is precisely the argument proving the cited triangular receipt on the actual kernel.

The same section proves that \(R\), and hence \(\mathscr{B}\), commutes with signed coordinate motions: change variables in the absolutely convergent kernel sum, use the local section's covariance, and take its finite jet. This establishes the symmetry for this very matrix, rather than requiring a separate symmetric approximation. Every nonunit invariant coordinate through grade five has factor degree two and position degree four or five: each curvature factor costs at least two units of position degree, and a linear invariant functional on a simple Lie algebra vanishes. The only possible off-diagonal matrix entry left by triangularity therefore raises position degree four to five. Total inversion acts on degree \(g\) by \((- 1)^g\), so commutation forces that entry to equal its negative and hence to vanish. The unit is fixed exactly by the local map. Thus on the entire grade-five frame \(\mathscr{B} = \text{Dil}_{c / f}\).

The full raw forecast is this same \(\mathscr{B}\) composed with \(\text{Block}_m\), whose coordinate weights are \(L^{5 - g_i}\). Consequently \(\mathcal{L}_m = \text{Dil}_{\vartheta_m / \vartheta_{m + 1}} \hspace{0.17em} \text{Block}_m\). Its unit weight is \(L^5\), consistently with \(\text{Col}_m (e_{\text{ref}}) = a_m^5 e_0\) and \(a_m = La_{m + 1}\); only the pre-block local map fixes the unit. The selected threshold comparison gives \(\vartheta_{m + 1} / \vartheta_m \leqslant 2\). Set \(g_m = 2 (1 + U) A_{- 1 / 2} (m + 1)\) and \(P = BUL^5 + kL^5 + rU > 0\). Choosing \(\epsilon = g_m / (2 P)\) in the preceding period estimate bounds its second term by \((g_m / 2) z\); its first term is also at most \((g_m / 2) z\). This proves exactly (18.121), with its original calibrated columns. The period tail is chosen separately for each fixed \(m\), as required in (S5). Intersecting finitely many such tails between the chart level and its summit gives the single ultrafilter-large set used in the Ward comparison's transport proof.

The actual branch recording family.
The recording estimates in the branch unit are applications of one finite-volume purchase to a finite family of local observables. We specify that family to keep its coefficient and rooted bounds attached to the same functions. The principal tensor is the localized stress field, the false correction is its off-diagonal curvature-product array, and the true correction is the diagonal quarter-square array. Include every tensor entry, its directional forward differences and divergence, and the trace and antisymmetry currents of the localized stress together with their forward differences. Tensor entries have grade four, their differences and divergence grade five, the currents grade three and their differences grade four. There are finitely many branches, axes and pairs of tensor indices. Translations are applied to the already chosen local functions and their labels; they do not require a new purchase for each test point.

The local analytic construction and healthy derivative calculation of Subsubsection 18.25.7 apply to precisely these entries, currents and differences. Subsubsection 18.25.5 supplies the regular atomic certificate uniformly over this finite family in grades at most five; its support-tail estimate applies to the original full observable, including its healthy mask. Apply Subsection 18.11 using the same selected coffer for every member, and choose its coefficient label once for each scale and finite-volume index, together with the full purchase certificate. Its coefficient bias is \(\vartheta_k^2 A_{- 1 / 4} (k)\). For the rooted estimate substitute \(a = \hat{\beta}_k^{- 1}\), \(B = \hat{\beta}_k^3\), \(\zeta_j = \varphi_{d, j}\), \(\| \text{invDil}_{j, d} \| = a_j^{- d}\) and \(\text{gain} (k, j) = e^{(s_k - s_j) / 8}\) into (18.88) and (18.90). This gives exactly

\(\displaystyle \frac{2 \text{stake}_{h, j} \hspace{0.17em} a_j^{- d}}{\varphi_{d, j}} \hat{\beta}_k^4 e^{- (s_k - s_j) / 8},\)

which is the actual-minus-local bound in (18.146). The reference-frame column bound gives a fixed-scale compact ball for the chosen labels. Hence one ultrafilter limit \(x_k\) exists for each member, before any spectator test is chosen. This is the actual recording selection, not a selection of a different label for each test.

Here is how the rooted receipt yields all three recorded inequalities. Fix a spectator test and choose a native scale small enough that its fixed points and a fixed neighborhood of the moving insertion are separated. Its centered spectator bounds are bounded on that neighborhood. Multiplying the actual-minus-local rooted bound by the fixed spectator bound gives a bound uniform over the allowed integer translations. At every fixed scale the point-limit identity for a moving chosen label follows from its finite basis expansion: its coefficients converge and the correlations for the fixed basis vectors converge along the same ultrafilter. For each fixed \(k\) and allowed displacement, choose the ultrafilter-large set so that this convergence error is at most \(a_k\). The allowed integer displacements at that fixed level form a finite set, so their intersection is still large. Adding \(a_k\) to the rooted bound gives the recorded current and difference inequalities; the tensor inequality is the same argument at zero displacement. Their errors are fast: with the native and comparison scales fixed, the rooted bound has the form \(C \hat{\beta}_k^4 e^{- s_k / 8}\), so it is at most \(e^{- s_k / 16}\) on a tail, since \(\log \hat{\beta}_k = o (s_k)\). Multiplication by any fixed spectator constant preserves such an exponential bound after a further tail. Finite maxima over the family give one common tail. This proves the actual instances of the three inequalities labelled (18.140), (18.141), and (18.142) in Subsection 18.16.

The current observable is normalized by \(a_k^{- 3}\) and its raw first difference by \(a_k^{- 4}\). Hence the physical difference is \(a_k^{- 1}\) times the difference of normalized current observables, which is the factor in the external branch identity. The atomic certificate applies to the raw local difference before this grade normalization; no exponentially enlarged raw certificate is assumed. The finite-volume identities are preserved in this application. The trace of the principal tensor is the sum of the designated current differences, and the false correction has exactly zero diagonal sum. The trace and antisymmetry current identities and vanishing of their projected forward differences are the local identities proved in Subsection 18.25; the correction entries are the actual curvature products used in its local calibration. Taking the finite sums before the ultrafilter limit therefore gives exactly the current/difference relation and the principal and false trace relations used by the branch washout proof. Its grade-three unit argument, finite-difference estimate and moving finite detector then apply to these recorded labels. Consequently the three branch field limits and the principal and false weighted trace washout are supplied for this very history and these very chart maps.

Installing the normalized energy in the selected algebra.
Keep the hierarchy, positional derivatives and orthogonal field action constructed in Sections 17.3, 17.9 and 17.10. Here is the same-table application. Use the selected table \(S\) as the physical hierarchy \(S^1\) of the first section, and use the actual comparator identified above as \(S^0\). The completed maps are those of the selected trace chart family; their uniform test bound and derivative transport are verified below for these same maps. The reference divergence is onto with one-dimensional kernel by Subsection 16.1. Thus (17.28) produces the conserved line in this very selected table. Its choice of a nonzero tensor and sign uses a subsequence only to establish existence of a fixed tensor \(J\); it does not change the table or its finite-volume extraction.

The annular-field construction of Proposition 17.22 applied to this \(J\) gives its translation and angular response maps. The physical packet bounds and their finite-row limits in Subsection 17.9 concern precisely these response maps, so Corollary 17.30 applies. Its two free tests are supplied for the actual comparator by Theorems 16.15, 16.16 and 16.7. No change of reference derivatives is needed, by (18.162). The corollary gives the global Ward laws with one nonzero scalar on the same \(S\); the inclusion argument makes that scalar common to all grades. Finally Subsection 17.10 integrates those very angular maps and makes them compatible with the selected derivatives and inclusions. For this application, use the shell-node receipt at each opened pair required by the finite watches. The actual schedule, lightness, finite-stencil chamber placement and birth quota are verified in the selected-family placement argument. The finite-mask identification is the actual fine finite-mask payment, and the coarse and fine transfer duties follow from (17.20) and its ensuing estimates. These use the same selected service and interfaces as the two-channel construction. The tab bounds follow from the adjacent clock ratio; centered spectator bounds come from the one-field centered service. For every fixed finite family of watches, the slotwise estimate (17.48) supplies the required separated-point derivatives with the enlarged spectator allowance. It precedes the Ward construction and therefore introduces no use of rotation covariance in proving that covariance.

The finite-row comparisons then use exactly this annular response, the fixed normalization (17.29) and the same-curve compact comparison. Their translation and rotation readout limits are Proposition 17.28; its reference flux identity is the one identified above. The packet estimates are those of Subsection 17.6, with the stress-cone estimate of Subsection 17.7. Their polynomial derivative input follows from the same pre-Ward slotwise bound. Thus the shell, finite-row and packet arguments use one selected hierarchy and its already fixed tensor throughout. Let \(J\) be the single conserved tensor in that construction and let \(c \ne 0\) be its common charge scalar: its translation response is \(c \partial\) and its rotation response is \(cD\), where \(D\) is the Lie algebra action of the constructed orthogonal representation \(R_d\). The energy of the selected algebra is chosen to be

\(\displaystyle E_{ab} = \hat{J}_{ab}, \qquad \hat{J} = \int_{O (4)} \mathcal{R}_K (c^{- 1} J) \hspace{0.17em} dK, \qquad (\mathcal{R}_K Q)_{ab} = \sum_{i, j} K_{ai} K_{bj} R_4 (K) Q_{ij} .\) (18.165)

The measure is probability Haar measure. This choice is made once, together with the derivatives and the action, before choosing a trace chart or a null sequence. It specifies the energy component of that selected algebra; it is not an identification of an arbitrarily preassigned energy with a generator of the conserved line.

Here is the same-data verification. The common scalar is independent of grade by the inclusion argument of Subsection 17.9. Multiplying the stress by \(c^{- 1}\) multiplies each collar flux by \(c^{- 1}\), so its charges become exactly \(\partial\) and \(D\). The averaging argument in the subsection “Normalizing and averaging the stress” of Subsection 17.10 applies to these very maps. It proves that the tensor in (18.165) is real, symmetric, tensor covariant and conserved, and that both Ward identities retain coefficient one. Explicitly, for each \(K\) the change of variables \(y = Kz\) carries its collar integral to that of \(c^{- 1} J\) with cutoff \(\chi \circ K\), transformed spectators and vector field \(K^{- 1} W (Kz)\). Covariance of the same derivative and rotation action returns the original Ward right-hand side. Each collar flux is a continuous linear functional on the finite-dimensional space of stress arrays; it commutes with the Haar integral. The right-hand side is independent of \(K\), so probability normalization leaves it unchanged. Thus the energy component \(E\) has the raw coefficient-one laws needed below.

No scale-dependent rescaling or second tensor is introduced in this assembly. In the remainder of this section, the raw energy is exactly (18.165), and \(R\) is its centered version. With those producer instances, the energy is installed on the selected table with the raw Ward laws just obtained. No identification with an independently chosen stress tensor is needed. In particular the auxiliary comparison subsequence used to construct \(J\) does not restrict the later trace scalings: once constructed, \(J\), its scalar and all its Ward identities are fixed statements on \(S\).

Centering preserves the physical Ward normalization.
There is no scalar-normalization loss in passing from the raw energy to \(R\). More precisely, suppose the raw energy \(E_{ab} = A. \text{energy}_{ab}\) is real, symmetric and tensor covariant, is conserved, and satisfies the coefficient-one translation Ward identity of input 1 on this same table, whose derivative annihilates the unit and whose unit insertion removes that slot. Write \(c_{ab} = \langle E_{ab} \rangle_S\), so \(R_{ab} = E_{ab} - c_{ab} \boldsymbol{1}\). Then \(\sum_a D_a^S R_{ab} = \sum_a D_a^S E_{ab} = 0\). For fixed spectator fields, the contribution of the subtracted tensor to a translation shell is a sum of terms
\(\displaystyle - c_{ab} \hspace{0.17em} S (\text{spectator fields}) \int_{\mathbb{R}^4} \partial_a \chi (z) \hspace{0.17em} dz = 0.\)

The last equality follows by integrating a derivative of a compactly supported smooth function, one coordinate at a time. Thus the shell identity for \(R\) has exactly the same coefficient one and the same derivative insertion as the identity for \(E\). Centering is immediate; symmetry, reality and tensor covariance are preserved because expectation and the unit respect those structures. This proves the stress laws in input 1 from the raw energy laws of the preceding construction, under the shell and packet hypotheses specified there. The scalar comparison with the explicit corrected tensor sequence remains necessary and is proved below on the original trace scaling.

The detecting derivative in item 3 can also be fixed explicitly. Take \(v =\mathcal{O}\), the grade-four curvature-square field defined at the start of this section, and choose any coordinate axis \(a\). Let \(o\) be the reference observer which tests a grade-five field \(g\) by the two-point correlation \(S_0 (g, \mathcal{O}; 0, e_a)\). By the reference-field normalization (18.190), the Wick calculation gives, with \(N = \dim \mathfrak{g}> 0\),

\(\displaystyle S_0 (\mathcal{O}, \mathcal{O}; x, e_a) = \frac{3 N}{\pi^4 |x - e_a |^8}, \qquad o.test_5 (D_a \mathcal{O}) = \left. \partial_{x_a} \frac{3 N}{\pi^4 |x - e_a |^8} \right|_{x = 0} = \frac{24 N}{\pi^4} \ne 0.\)

The derivative identity is the reference derivative law; both points are separated, so ordinary differentiation is legitimate. This fixes a single observer and a single nonzero value before any chart is selected. It uses the actual reference Wick covariance and the identification (18.164) proved above. Thus the detector is fixed without using the interacting trace asymptotic or retaining an additional reference-identification premise.

From chart comparison to the tested and shell limits.
The tested approach property of Subsection 17.2, on the same scaling, supplies (4a): a derivative of a fixed compactly supported smooth test is another such test, with support in the same separated window. That section's finite separating-test argument, applied in grade five, supplies (4b). More explicitly, a nonzero field has a nonzero separated reference evaluation. Continuity and a nonnegative smooth bump around that evaluation give a nonzero integrated functional. These functionals separate the grade-five space; finitely many suffice, since adjoining a functional nonzero on the current common kernel strictly decreases its dimension until it is zero. The derivative laws (4c) remain the laws of the selected and reference tables.

We now check the stronger compact comparison needed in (4d). Use exactly the completed corrected maps of Theorem 17.5 and its completion paragraph: the index \(k\) there is the present \(i\), the selected table is \(S\), the reference table is \(S_0\), and the radii and every grade map are the present \(r_i, U_{i, d}\). The raw lifts are those of these same supplied charts and use the fixed extraction and ultrafilter. This is the choice of chart family throughout the transfer, not a replacement by a second curve. Any subsequent subsequence is applied to all these data together.

For a fixed word \(v\) and compact set \(Q\) in its noncoincident configuration space, the finite-slot expansion in that theorem gives an error bound

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} f \in C_c^{\infty}, \text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} f \subset Q\\ \|f\|_{\infty} \leqslant M \end{array}$}}} \left| \int f (x) \{ S (U_i v ; r_i x) - S_0 (v ; x) \} \hspace{0.17em} dx \right| \leqslant \frac{MK_v}{i + 1} \longrightarrow 0\) (18.166)

on a tail, where \(K_v\) is a finite constant depending on the word. Indeed the corrected discrepancy is a finite sum of raw discrepancies at fixed words, each bounded by the short-test estimate (17.12). Their number and label norms do not depend on \(f\) or \(i\). The expanding cutoff window contains the whole compact set \(Q\) eventually. Completion changes none of the finitely many grade maps on a further tail independent of \(f\). This proves the supremum estimate, rather than merely convergence for each individual test. Along a further subsequence \(i \mapsto n_i\), the same argument retains the vanishing bound \(MK_v / (n_i + 1)\).

The compact-jet argument in the subsection “Derivative transport and convergence on compact sets” of Subsection 17.3 now applies to these very data. Its hypothesis (17.26) is precisely (18.166). Its spaces \(E_d, F_d\) are the reference and selected grade spaces here; their finite dimension, smooth multilinear correlations, separating tests, and derivative identities are the same hierarchy and derivative laws already used in (4a)–(4c). No stress-normalization conclusion of that section is used in its compact-jet argument.

To make the application explicit, its transported derivative is \(r_i U_{i, d + 1}^{- 1} D_a^S U_{i, d}\). The finitely many separating tests in grade \(d + 1\) give measurement maps \(L_i \to L\), with \(L\) injective. Integration by parts and (18.166) imply \(L_i (r_i U_{i, d + 1}^{- 1} D_a^S U_{i, d}) v \to LD_a^0 v\). The eventual lower bound for \(L_i\) therefore gives convergence of the transported derivative to \(D_a^0\) in operator norm, as proved there. Finite-basis multilinearity transfers the uniform test estimate to converging moving labels. Position differentiation replaces one label by its transported derivative, so this class of discrepancies is closed under every fixed number of position derivatives. For a compactly supported cutoff \(\eta\) in configuration space and any such discrepancy \(h_i\), the tests \(\eta e^{- 2 \pi \text{i} \hspace{0.17em} \xi \cdot x}\) have one common supremum bound. The same is true with derivatives of \(\eta\) and descendants of \(h_i\). The integration-by-parts calculation in that section consequently bounds \(| \widehat{\eta h_i} (\xi) |\) by \(C \epsilon_i (1 + | \xi |)^{- b}\), with \(\epsilon_i \to 0\) and any fixed \(b > 4 n\) at arity \(n > 0\). Fourier inversion yields uniform convergence on a compact set where \(\eta = 1\); applying this to the finitely many derivatives of a fixed order gives convergence of that jet. At arity zero the assertion is the scalar short-test limit. This also explains why no estimate at collision configurations is needed.

Thus, for each fixed finite word of reference labels \(h_1, \ldots, h_s\), with grades \(d_1, \ldots, d_s\), and each compact set \(K\) of pairwise distinct configurations,

\(\displaystyle \sup_{z \in K} | S (U_{i, d_1} h_1, \ldots, U_{i, d_s} h_s ; r_i z) - S_0 (h_1, \ldots, h_s ; z) | \longrightarrow 0.\) (18.167)

The required finite grade bounds include the finitely many descendants used for the desired jet order. They and the fixed arity are eventually within the selected chart cutoff. Equation (18.167) is therefore a proved consequence of the uniform short-test estimate and the derivative laws, not an independent compact-comparison premise. Taking \(K\) to be the observer's one configuration gives the first assertion of (4d).

For the shell assertion, let \(x\) denote the distinguished and spectator points and choose the cutoff \(\chi\) so that the compact set \(\text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} \nabla \chi\) avoids all these points. The configurations \((z, x)\) with \(z \in \text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} \nabla \chi\) then form a compact subset of the noncoincident configuration space: all pair distances have a positive minimum there. Thus one separated window contains the entire integration set. Write \(C_i^c (z ; H)\) for the selected correlation with the entry \(U_{i, 4} H_{ca}\) at \(r_i z\), the distinguished field \(U_{i, 4} v\) at its rescaled point, and the chart-transformed spectators. Let \(C_0^c (z ; H)\) be its reference counterpart. With the translation-shell convention, the flux is minus the sum of these correlations against \(\partial_c \chi\). Changing variables \(y = r_i z\) in its four-dimensional integral gives exactly

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}_i (H) = - \sum_{c = 1}^4 \int (\partial_c \chi) (z) C_i^c (z ; H) \hspace{0.17em} dz.\) (18.168)

Indeed, the prefactor \(r_i\) in \(\mathscr{F}_i\), the stress multiplier \(r_i^{- 4}\), the cutoff derivative \(r_i^{- 1}\) and the Jacobian \(r_i^4\) multiply to one. The spectator maps are exactly the chart maps used in (18.167); no extra scaling of them is introduced. That estimate on the compact shell therefore yields

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{L}_i (H) - \text{shellGrad} (H, \chi, a, x, q, v) | \leqslant \sum_{c = 1}^4 \| \partial_c \chi \|_{L^1} \sup_{z \in \text{supp} \hspace{0.17em} \nabla \chi} |C_i^c (z ; H) - C_0^c (z ; H) | \longrightarrow 0.\)

This proves the shell deduction in (4d). Expanding in a finite basis of stress arrays also proves operator-norm convergence of these shell functionals, which is the form used below for moving tensors.

By input 3 fix an axis \(a\), an observer \(o\), and a reference grade-four field direction \(v\) such that \(y = o.test_5 (D_a v) \ne 0\), where \(D_a\) is the reference derivative in direction \(a\). Let \(x\) be the separated configuration of this same observer, consisting of the point of the distinguished insertion and the points of its spectators, let \(q\) be its fixed tuple of spectator labels, and let \(\chi\) be its cut, a cutoff separating the distinguished point from the spectator points. The reference translation Ward identity of input 2, applied to this cut, identifies this number with \(\text{shellGrad} (M_0, \chi, a, x, q, v)\). We keep this exact observer and cut for both stress sequences; no new test or window is selected in the limit argument.

For a stress tensor \(Q\), write the exact selected shell measurement as

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{F}_i (Q) = r_i \text{shellGrad}_S \hspace{-0.17em} (Q, \text{stretch} (r_i, \chi), a, r_i x, \text{pins}_i \circ q, U_i v),\)

where \(r_i\) is the chart radius, \(q\) is the observer's tuple of spectator labels, transported by the chart maps \(\text{pins}_i\), and \(U_i\) is the selected grade-four chart isomorphism. We claim

\(\displaystyle f_i := \mathscr{F}_i (R) \to y, \qquad \mathscr{F}_i (g_i) \to y. \) (18.169)

The two limits have different sources, and both involve labels that move with \(i\), so that convergence for fixed labels cannot be applied directly.

First limit. Write \(D_a^S\) for the derivative map of the selected table and define the real-linear maps from reference grade-four fields to reference grade-five fields

\(\displaystyle \textsf{T}_i = r_i \hspace{0.17em} U_{i, 5}^{- 1} D_a^S \hspace{0.17em} U_{i, 4} .\)

We first show \(\textsf{T}_i \to D_a\) in operator norm. Let \(\lambda^1, \ldots, \lambda^m\) be the reference functionals of the tests of (4b) and let \(\lambda^1_i, \ldots, \lambda^m_i\) be the rescaled functionals \(g \mapsto\) (tested correlation of the selected table with \(U_{i, 5} g\) in the distinguished slot). By (4a), \(\lambda^r_i \to \lambda^r\) pointwise, hence in operator norm on the finite-dimensional reference grade-five space. Fix a reference grade-four field \(u\). By (4c) for the selected table and integration by parts in the distinguished variable, \(\lambda^r_i (\textsf{T}_i u)\) is minus the rescaled tested correlation of \(U_{i, 4} u\) against the test with differentiated test function; the factor \(r_i\) in \(\textsf{T}_i\) is exactly the one produced by rescaling the derivative. By (4a) for the differentiated test this converges to minus the reference tested correlation of \(u\) against the differentiated test, which equals \(\lambda^r (D_a u)\) by (4c) for the reference table and integration by parts. Thus \(\Lambda_i (\textsf{T}_i u) \to \Lambda (D_a u)\), where \(\Lambda = (\lambda^r)_r\) and \(\Lambda_i = (\lambda^r_i)_r\). Since \(\Lambda\) is injective on a finite-dimensional space there is \(c > 0\) with \(\| \Lambda w\| \geqslant c \|w\|\), and \(\| \Lambda_i w\| \geqslant (c / 2) \|w\|\) for large \(i\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \| \textsf{T}_i u - D_a u\| \leqslant \frac{2}{c} (\| \Lambda_i \textsf{T}_i u - \Lambda D_a u\|+\|(\Lambda - \Lambda_i) D_a u\|) \longrightarrow 0,\)

and pointwise convergence on the finite-dimensional grade-four space is operator-norm convergence. Next let \(A_i (g)\) denote the selected correlation at \(r_i x\) with \(U_{i, 5} g\) at the distinguished point and spectator labels \(\text{pins}_i \circ q\). By the first part of (4d), \(A_i \to o.test_5\) pointwise, hence in operator norm. The physical Ward identity of input 1, applied at the separated configuration \(r_i x\) with the stretched cut, and linearity give

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{F}_i (R) = r_i \hspace{0.17em} (\text{correlation at } r_i x \text{ with } D_a^S (U_{i, 4} v) \text{ at the distinguished point}) = A_i (\textsf{T}_i v) .\)

Since \(A_i \to o.test_5\) in operator norm and \(\textsf{T}_i v \to D_a v\), we get \(\mathscr{F}_i (R) \to o.test_5 (D_a v) = y\).

Second limit. No Ward identity for \(g_i\) is used. For a reference stress tensor \(H\) put \(\mathcal{L}_i (H) = \mathscr{F}_i (r_i^{- 4} U_{i, 4} H)\), with the chart applied to each entry. This is a linear functional on the finite-dimensional space of reference stress tensors, and by the definition of \(\mathcal{Y}_i\) and linearity of \(\mathscr{F}_i\),

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{F}_i (g_i) =\mathcal{L}_i (\mathcal{Y}_i (g_i))\)

exactly. By the second part of (4d), \(\mathcal{L}_i (H) \to \mathcal{L}_0 (H) := \text{shellGrad} (H, \chi, a, x, q, v)\) for every fixed \(H\), hence in operator norm. The field limit (18.159) gives \(\mathcal{Y}_i (g_i) \to M_0\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{F}_i (g_i) \longrightarrow \mathcal{L}_0 (M_0) = \text{shellGrad} (M_0, \chi, a, x, q, v) = y,\)

the last equality being the reference Ward identity used above. The argument uses the same selected scale and the same observer test in both limits. The physical flux excludes \(R = 0\): if \(R\) vanished, linearity of shell differentiation would force \(f_i = 0\) at every index, contrary to \(y \ne 0\).

Since \(\ker D\) is one-dimensional, \(g_i = \lambda_i R\) for all sufficiently large \(i\). The second limit in (18.169) therefore reads \(\lambda_i f_i \to y\), while the first reads \(f_i \to y\). As \(y \ne 0\), choose a scalar coordinate functional \(\ell\) with \(\ell (y) \ne 0\). Then

\(\displaystyle \lambda_i = \frac{\ell (\lambda_i f_i)}{\ell (f_i)} \longrightarrow \frac{\ell (y)}{\ell (y)} = 1.\)

Thus \(g_i = \lambda_i R\) and \(\lambda_i \to 1\). On a late tail \(\lambda_i \ne 0\) and \(\lambda_i^{- 1} \to 1\). Since \(\mathcal{Y}_i\) and the weighted trace are linear,

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_i (R) = \lambda_i^{- 1} \mathcal{Y}_i (g_i), \qquad \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (R) = \lambda_i^{- 1} \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (g_i)\)

on that tail. This proves the physical field and flux limits. In particular,

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_i (R) \longrightarrow M_0, \qquad \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P}_i (R) \longrightarrow - \kappa \mathcal{O}. \) (18.170)

This is the physical-array step used by the chart family. The dimension-one kernel alone would not fix the scalar; the common nonzero shell test fixes it.

18.17.3Comparison tables and the complete trace chart data

Let \(T\) be any table on the same label book as the selected table \(S\); for the limit proved in this paragraph no hierarchy on \(T\) and no compatibility of \(T\) with \(S\) is required. Let \(\Theta_T\) be the trace of \(A. \text{energy}\) centered by its one-point value in \(T\). Its exact centered decomposition is

\(\displaystyle \Theta_T = \text{tr} (\text{reCenter}_S (A. \text{energy})) - c_T \hspace{0.17em} 1_S, \qquad c_T \in \mathbb{C},\)

where \(1_S\) is the grade-four unit field of \(S\): both sides differ from the uncentered trace by a multiple of \(1_S\). The physical-transfer limit (18.170) applies to the first term. The scalar-unit term vanishes after zooming: the selected scale satisfies

\(\displaystyle q_i r_i^4 \longrightarrow 0, \qquad q_i > 0, \) (18.171)

as is proved from the clock in the next paragraph, and the selected chart eventually identifies the reference unit with the unit of \(S\), that is, \(U_i 1 = 1_S\) for large \(i\). Hence \(q_i r_i^4 U_i^{- 1} (c_T 1_S) = c_T q_i r_i^4 1 \to 0\). Therefore the zoomed comparison observable has the same limit as the trace of the physical stress,

\(\displaystyle q_i r_i^4 U_i^{- 1} \Theta_T \longrightarrow - \kappa \mathcal{O}= 4 d_2 \mathcal{O}.\)

The limit is a multiple of the curvature-square field \(\mathcal{O}\), not of the unit. This argument is uniform in the algebraic identity for \(T\); only the two scalar sequences used below to represent its component in the distinguished two-dimensional stress sector depend on \(T\).

The clock. Write \(j_i\) for the frame level of the \(i\)th chart, so that \(k_i\) is its summit index, let \(\textsf{a}_j > 0\) be the field scale at level \(j\) and \(\textsf{b}_i\) the zoom factor of the \(i\)th chart, and let \(\zeta (j) > 0\) be the arch scale at level \(j\); thus \(q_i = \zeta (k_i)\). The chart radius is \(r_i = \textsf{a}_{j_i} \textsf{b}_i\), the running rate is \(\hat{\beta}_j = \bar{\beta} + \kappa \log (\textsf{a}_j^{- 1})\) with a fixed constant \(\bar{\beta}\), and the selected run has \(j_i \to \infty\), \(\hat{\beta}_j \to \infty\) and \(r_i \to 0\). The first two clock limits below follow from the actual arch construction. For each fixed \(j\), take the fixed-level ultrafilter limit in the running bound of Lemma 16.17. Closedness of the real interval gives, for every \(\eta > 0\) and every sufficiently large \(j\),

\(\displaystyle | \zeta (j) - \hat{\beta}_j | \leqslant c_R + \eta s_j, \qquad \hat{\beta}_j = \bar{\beta} + \kappa s_j, \qquad s_j = \log (\textsf{a}_j^{- 1}) .\)

The fixed constant \(c_R\) does not depend on \(j\). Division by the clock and then \(\eta \downarrow 0\) proves \(\zeta (j) / \hat{\beta}_j \to 1\). For completeness, let \(m + 1 = \text{summit} (j)\) and \(x = \hat{\beta}_j\). The selected schedule defines this summit as the first crossing of the gap \((\log x)^{48}\). Thus \(0 \leqslant s_m - s_j \leqslant (\log x)^{48}\) and \(\hat{\beta}_m \leqslant x + \kappa (\log x)^{48} \leqslant 2 x\) eventually. The actual schedule gives the weaker bound that is sufficient here: by (19.7),

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant s_{m + 1} - s_m = \log L_{J (m + 1)} \leqslant s_m / J (m + 1) .\)

As \(j \to \infty\), also \(m \to \infty\) and \(J (m + 1) \to \infty\). Since \(s_m / x \leqslant 2 / \kappa\) eventually, this last increment is \(o (x)\). The pre-crossing gap is \((\log x)^{48} = o (x)\), so \(\hat{\beta}_{\text{summit} (j)} / \hat{\beta}_j \to 1\). In particular this clock-ratio proof does not need the stronger block-size bound used in the external Ward comparison unit. Since both levels tend to infinity,

\(\displaystyle \frac{\zeta (j)}{\zeta (\text{summit} (j))} = \frac{\zeta (j) / \hat{\beta}_j}{(\zeta (\text{summit} (j)) / \hat{\beta}_{\text{summit} (j)}) (\hat{\beta}_{\text{summit} (j)} / \hat{\beta}_j)} \longrightarrow 1.\)

The third estimate is proved in Lemma 18.3 from the chart receipt \(1 \leqslant \textsf{b}_i \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j_i)\) of (18.2):

\(\displaystyle \frac{\zeta (j)}{\hat{\beta}_j} \longrightarrow 1, \qquad \frac{\zeta (j)}{\zeta (\text{summit} (j))} \longrightarrow 1 \quad (j \to \infty), \qquad 0 \leqslant \log \textsf{b}_i \leqslant (\log \hat{\beta}_{j_i})^{48} \quad \text{eventually} . \) (18.172)

The definition of the rate gives

\(\displaystyle \frac{\log (r_i^{- 1})}{\hat{\beta}_{j_i}} = \frac{\log (\textsf{a}_{j_i}^{- 1})}{\hat{\beta}_{j_i}} - \frac{\log \textsf{b}_i}{\hat{\beta}_{j_i}} = \frac{1}{\kappa} \left( 1 - \frac{\bar{\beta}}{\hat{\beta}_{j_i}} \right) - \frac{\log \textsf{b}_i}{\hat{\beta}_{j_i}} \longrightarrow \frac{1}{\kappa},\)

because \((\log t)^{48} / t \to 0\) as \(t \to \infty\). Dividing by \(\zeta (j_i) / \hat{\beta}_{j_i} \to 1\) and multiplying by \(\zeta (j_i) / \zeta (k_i) \to 1\) gives

\(\displaystyle \frac{\log (r_i^{- 1})}{q_i} \longrightarrow \frac{1}{\kappa} . \) (18.173)

In particular the slope cancels in the logarithmically normalized trace. For the selected phase let \(Z_i = r_i^4 U_{i, 4}^{- 1} \Theta\) be its pulled-back centered trace. The physical transfer gives \(q_i Z_i \to 4 d_2 \mathcal{O}= - \kappa \mathcal{O}\), whereas (18.173) gives \(\log (r_i^{- 1}) / q_i \to 1 / \kappa\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \log (r_i^{- 1}) Z_i \longrightarrow -\mathcal{O}.\) (18.174)

At two separated points, the moving-label correlation comparison and (18.190) consequently give the coefficient \(S_0 (-\mathcal{O}, -\mathcal{O}) = 3 \dim \mathfrak{g}/ \pi^4\) at unit separation. This is the coefficient in the trace asymptotic; using the same positive slope in the two factors is sufficient.

Put \(\ell_i = \log (r_i^{- 1})\). Since \(r_i \to 0\), \(\ell_i \to \infty\), and \(\ell_i r_i^4 = \ell_i e^{- 4 \ell_i} \to 0\). For large \(i\), \(\ell_i > 0\) and

\(\displaystyle q_i r_i^4 = \frac{\ell_i r_i^4}{\ell_i / q_i} \longrightarrow \frac{0}{1 / \kappa} = 0,\)

which is (18.171). Finally \(q_i \to \infty\), since \(q_i = \ell_i / (\ell_i / q_i)\) and \(\ell_i \to \infty\).

More explicitly, the scalar representation is

\(\displaystyle \iota (e, d) = e \hspace{0.17em} 1 + 4 d \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{O}.\)

The finite reference calculation shows that \(1\) and \(\mathcal{O}\) are linearly independent, so \(\iota\) is injective and has a continuous left inverse on its range. The limiting comparison tensor is \(4 d_2 \mathcal{O}\), where \(d_2 = - \kappa / 4\), so its coordinates are \((0, d_2)\). Put \(Z_i = q_i r_i^4 U_i^{- 1} \Theta_T\). For the coordinate representation, and only for it, assume in addition that \(T\) carries a hierarchy and respects \(S\), and that the selected algebra has the local Ward structure (conservation, symmetry and reality of its energy tensor and its tensor transformation law). Then \(Z_i \in \text{range} (\iota)\) eventually, separately from the limit \(Z_i \to 4 d_2 \mathcal{O}\). Indeed, under these assumptions \(\Theta_T\) is fixed by the real structure of \(S\) and by every signed permutation of the axes. On the tail where the grade-four chart is coherent, \(U_i^{- 1}\) intertwines the real structures and the signed-permutation actions of the two tables, and multiplication by the positive real number \(q_i r_i^4\) preserves both conditions; so \(Z_i\) is a real, signed-invariant grade-four field of the reference table. The diagonal tensor with all four diagonal entries equal to \(Z_i\) and vanishing off-diagonal entries is then a real, symmetric, signed-covariant stress tensor of the reference table, hence a real combination of the four basis tensors of Lemma 16.2. Taking its trace, and using that the trace of each of those basis tensors lies in \(\mathbb{R}1 +\mathbb{R}\mathcal{O}\), shows \(4 Z_i \in \mathbb{R}1 +\mathbb{R}\mathcal{O}\). On that tail let \((a_i, b_i) = \iota^{- 1} (Z_i)\); continuity of the fixed left inverse gives \(a_i \to 0\) and \(b_i \to d_2\). Define \(e_i = a_i / q_i\) and \(d_i = b_i / q_i\). Thus, eventually,

\(\displaystyle r_i^4 U_i^{- 1} \Theta_T = \iota (e_i, d_i), \qquad q_i e_i \to 0, \qquad q_i d_i \to d_2 .\)

The definition of the zoom then gives \(q_i r_i^4 U_i^{- 1} \Theta_T \to 4 d_2 \mathcal{O}\).

The exact record contains \(q_i \to \infty\) and the clock limit (18.173), in which the scale is the reciprocal \(r_i^{- 1}\) of the chart radius and not the zoom factor; the same finite-volume chart and hierarchy; the physical stress limit; for every table \(T\) the zoomed trace limit; and, for every table \(T\) that carries a hierarchy and respects \(S\), the late scalar representation and two coefficient limits just proved. The fixed-scale case uses the selected chart directly. For arbitrary positive null sequences the order of choices is as follows.
