The ultrafilter, the arch scale and the reference hierarchy are fixed first. Then, for every positive sequence \(s_n \to 0\), the paid scale selection chooses a strictly increasing sequence of indices \(n_i\) and a scaling whose radii are exactly \(r_i = s_{n_i}\) (Lemma 17.15, using the joint chart purchase proved in that section); the same history is then passed to the finite-volume chart producer, and the physical transfer above applies without another extraction. With the actual shell, Ward comparison-bound and branch-recording instances specified above, applying the response, Ward comparison, branch and scale-selection producers to this same history gives the complete trace chart data. The Ward comparison's marked-vector limits, the actual arch identification and clock limits, and the physical-energy normalization have been proved above on that history. The tested-bound, adjacent-column and actual branch-recording arguments supply the additional quantitative instances for the external Ward comparison and branch theorems. The compact comparison and the shell limit are consequences of the selected chart family's uniform short-test bound and the derivative laws, as proved above; they are not additional independent inputs.

18.18The spectral measure of a single field

The joint translation measure acts on vectors in Hilbert space. A point field applied to the vacuum need not be such a vector. To obtain the spectral measure of its two-point function, we first keep a positive heat height, take the measure of the resulting vector, and then remove the exponential damping. The resulting measure can have infinite total mass.

Throughout this section one selected phase is fixed, and every object belongs to the single reconstruction built from it in Sections 6.1–6.10, whose hypotheses are retained. These objects are: the Euclidean Hilbert space \(\mathcal{H}_E\) with vacuum \(\Omega_E\), its heat semigroup \(E_t\), the complex heat operators \(E (z)\) and the unitary group \(V (t)\) of Proposition 6.1, and the joint translations \(T_E (a)\); the slab insertions \(B_p (v)\) and the point heat vectors (6.15); the continued correlations \(F_n\) of Lemma 6.3 and their boundary distributions \(W_n\) of Theorem 6.5; the involution \(J\) on labels; and the field operators of Proposition 6.22, written \(\phi (v, f)\) in this section, with vacuum \(\Omega\) and the representation \(U\) of Proposition 6.24. Nothing below selects a second phase, continuation, or reconstruction.

18.18.1Exact damping of vector measures

We first record a uniqueness fact for bounded holomorphic functions that will identify heat evolution inside the spectral integral. Suppose \(h\) is bounded and continuous on the closed upper half-plane, holomorphic in its interior, and zero on the real axis. Fix \(x \in \mathbb{R}\), \(s \geqslant 0\) and apply the Gaussian contour argument from Lemma 6.32 to \(h (z + x)\) with \(b = - 2 \varepsilon s\). The bottom integral vanishes. On the top edge,

\(\displaystyle e^{- ib (t + is) - \varepsilon (t + is)^2} = e^{- \varepsilon s^2} e^{- \varepsilon t^2} .\)

The nonzero constant factor can be cancelled, giving \(\int e^{- \varepsilon t^2} h (x + t + is) dt = 0\). Set \(\varepsilon = R^{- 2}\) and \(t = Ru\). After cancelling \(R > 0\), let \(R \downarrow 0\). Boundedness and the Gaussian majorant justify dominated convergence and give \(\sqrt{\pi} h (x + is) = 0\). Thus \(h = 0\). In particular, two such bounded holomorphic functions with equal continuous boundary values agree throughout the half-plane.

Let \(\nu_v\) be the finite joint spectral measure of a vector \(v \in \mathcal{H}_E\), and retain \(T_E\) and \(E_t\) from the earlier construction. Equation (6.23) gives \(p_0 \geqslant 0\) almost everywhere for \(\nu_v\). For fixed \(a \in \mathbb{R}^4\), the function

\(\displaystyle G (z) = \int e^{ip \cdot a} e^{izp_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_v (p)\)

is bounded and continuous on the closed upper half-plane. On a neighborhood where \(\text{Im} \hspace{0.17em} z \geqslant \eta > 0\), its derivative is dominated by \(p_0 e^{- \eta p_0} \leqslant \eta^{- 1}\). Since \(\nu_v\) is finite, dominated differentiation proves holomorphy. The bounded holomorphic function

\(\displaystyle F (z) = \langle v, T_E (a) E (- iz) v \rangle\)

in the open half-plane has the continuous boundary \(\langle v, T_E (a) V (t) v \rangle\). By the translation spectral formula these boundary values equal \(G (t)\). The uniqueness just proved therefore identifies \(F = G\).

The operators \(E_t\) are self-adjoint and commute with translations. For \(t \geqslant 0\), the preceding identity at \(z = 2 it\) gives

\(\displaystyle \langle E_t v, T_E (a) E_t v \rangle = \langle v, T_E (a) E_{2 t} v \rangle = \int e^{ip \cdot a} e^{- 2 tp_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_v (p) .\)

Both measures here are finite, because \(p_0 \geqslant 0\). Uniqueness of finite measures from their characteristic functions, proved in Subsection 6.10, yields

\(\displaystyle d \nu_{E_t v} (p) = e^{- 2 tp_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_v (p) .\) (18.175)

This proof uses the continuous bounded half-plane boundary of a vector matrix coefficient, not the generally distributional boundary of a point correlation.

18.18.2Point vectors and removal of damping

Fix a label \(v\) and, for \(s > 0\), define

\(\displaystyle q_s (v) = Q_{p, (s)} (0 ; v) = E_{s - p} B_p (v) \Omega_E, \qquad 0 < p < 1, \quad 4 p < s.\)

The vector is independent of the admissible width \(p\). Indeed, the mixed Gram identity (Lemma 6.18), together with the width independence of the continued correlations (Lemma 6.3), gives the same number for the scalar product of either of two such vectors with either one of them. Expanding the squared norm of their difference therefore gives zero. If \(t \geqslant 0\), a width admissible at \(s\) is also admissible at \(s + t\). Using that common width and the semigroup law gives

\(\displaystyle q_{s + t} (v) = E_t q_s (v) .\) (18.176)

Let \(\nu_s\) be the finite spectral measure of \(q_s (v)\). Define a positive Borel measure by

\(\displaystyle d \mu_v (p) = e^{2 p_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_1 (p) .\) (18.177)

This is a well-defined sigma-finite measure: the density is finite and positive, and is bounded on each set \(\{|p_0 | \leqslant n\}\). Multiplication of densities and (18.175)–(18.176) imply

\(\displaystyle e^{2 (s + t) p_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_{s + t} = e^{2 sp_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_s .\)

Compare \(s\) and \(1\) through their common larger height \(s + 1\). It follows that for every \(s > 0\),

\(\displaystyle d \mu_v = e^{2 sp_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_s, \qquad e^{- 2 sp_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_v = d \nu_s .\) (18.178)

In particular \(\mu_v\) is supported in the time half-space \(p_0 \geqslant 0\), and every positive exponential damping has finite mass.

The single-insertion case of the mixed Gram identity and translation covariance of the point vectors now gives

\(\displaystyle F_2 (x_0^0 - is, x_1^0 + is ; \mathbf{x}_0, \mathbf{x}_1 ; Jv, v) = \int e^{- 2 sp_0} e^{ip \cdot (x_1 - x_0)} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_v (p) .\) (18.179)

To see the relative sign explicitly, the two vectors are \(T_E (x_0) q_s (v)\) and \(T_E (x_1) q_s (v)\). Unitarity moves the first translation to the second, producing \(T_E (x_1 - x_0)\); the spectral formula and (18.178) give the integral above.

For a self-adjoint Euclidean scalar label \(v\), taking both real positions equal to zero identifies the left side with its Euclidean two-point function at separation \(2 s\). Thus

\(\displaystyle T_v (t) = \int e^{- tp_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_v (p) \quad (t > 0) .\) (18.180)

It follows already that \(T_v\) is real, nonnegative and decreasing. The stronger forward-cone conclusion below is obtained after identifying the measure with the actual reconstructed fields.

18.18.3Polynomial growth and the full boundary distribution

The fixed-label gap bound (6.5), applied at heights \((- s, s)\) and zero real positions, gives constants \(C \geqslant 0\) and \(k \in \mathbb{N}\) such that

\(\displaystyle \nu_s (\mathbb{R}^4) = \|q_s (v)\|^2 \leqslant Cs^{- k}, \qquad 0 < s \leqslant 1.\)

For \(R \geqslant 1\) use \(s = R^{- 1}\) in (18.178). On \(\{p : p_0 \leqslant R\}\) one has \(e^{- 2 sp_0} \geqslant e^{- 2}\), and hence

\(\displaystyle \mu_v \{p : p_0 \leqslant R\} \leqslant e^2 CR^k .\) (18.181)

This controls an entire energy sublevel set, not just a Euclidean ball. In particular \(\mu_v (\{|p| \leqslant R\}) \leqslant AR^k\) with \(A = e^2 C\). The measure is locally finite and therefore is a Radon measure on \(\mathbb{R}^4\).

Here is a quantitative rapid-weight consequence. Cover \(\mathbb{R}^4\) by the closed shells \(2^j \leqslant 1 + |p| \leqslant 2^{j + 1}\), \(j \geqslant 0\). On the \(j\)th shell the weight \((1 + |p|)^{- k - 1}\) is at most \(2^{- j (k + 1)}\), and its mass is at most \(A 2^{(j + 1) k}\). Summing, with overlaps causing only an upper bound, gives

\(\displaystyle \int (1 + |p|)^{- k - 1} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_v (p) \leqslant A 2^{k + 1} < \infty .\) (18.182)

Thus every Schwartz function is absolutely integrable against \(\mu_v\), and integration is a continuous functional on Schwartz space, bounded by a weighted supremum seminorm times the finite integral in (18.182).

Use the unnormalized plus Fourier convention

\(\displaystyle f_+ (p) = \int e^{ip \cdot x} f (x) \hspace{0.17em} dx, \qquad \mathcal{W} \Phi (p) = \int e^{ip \cdot (x_1 - x_0)} \Phi (x_0, x_1) \hspace{0.17em} dx_0 dx_1 .\)

Both are Schwartz functions when their arguments are Schwartz. For the second assertion take the joint plus Fourier transform and restrict it to the injective linear subspace \(p \mapsto (- p, p)\). The chain rule controls derivatives of the restriction, and \(| (- p, p) | = \sqrt{2} |p|\) controls its weighted supremum seminorms.

At each height \(s > 0\), Fubini in (18.179) is justified by \(\nu_s (\mathbb{R}^4) \| \Phi \|_{L^1} < \infty\). It identifies the joint tested correlation with \(\int e^{- 2 sp_0} \mathcal{W} \Phi (p) d \mu_v (p)\). As \(s \downarrow 0\), the exponential has absolute value at most one almost everywhere and tends to one. The integrable majorant \(|\mathcal{W} \Phi |\) and the joint boundary theorem (Theorem 6.5, at the strictly ordered heights \((- s, s)\)) give

\(\displaystyle W_2 (Jv, v) (\Phi) = \int \mathcal{W} \Phi (p) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_v (p) .\) (18.183)

This is an identity on all joint Schwartz tests, including those meeting the coincidence set or the null cone. No restriction of a distribution to such a set has been used.

18.18.4Identification with field vectors and the forward cone

Let \(\xi_v (f) = \phi (v, f) \Omega\) be the one-field vector in the reconstructed Hilbert space. With \(\Phi (x_0, x_1) = \overline{f (x_0)} g (x_1)\), Fubini in the ordinary Fourier integral gives \(\mathcal{W} \Phi = \overline{f_+} g_+\). By the adjoint identity (6.19) and the vacuum expectation formula of Proposition 6.22, the scalar product \(\langle \xi_v (f), \xi_v (g) \rangle\) is the value of \(W_2 (Jv, v)\) on this product test. This and (18.183) therefore give

\(\displaystyle \langle \xi_v (f), \xi_v (g) \rangle = \int \overline{f_+ (p)} g_+ (p) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_v (p) .\) (18.184)

The product is integrable by the rapid-weight bound. Translation covariance of the fields (Proposition 6.24) gives \(U (a) \xi_v (f) = \xi_v (f (\hspace{0.17em} \cdot - a\))), and changing variables in the Fourier integral gives \((f (\hspace{0.17em} \cdot - a))_+ (p) = e^{ip \cdot a} f_+ (p)\). It follows from (18.184) that the characteristic function of the finite PVM measure of \(\xi_v (f)\) equals that of \(|f_+ |^2 \mu_v\). The latter is finite by (18.182). Finite-measure uniqueness gives

\(\displaystyle d \mu^{\text{field}}_{\xi_v (f)} = |f_+ |^2 \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_v .\) (18.185)

The weights in this identity determine \(\mu_v\) uniquely. Given a closed ball of radius \(n\), choose a smooth compactly supported function \(h\) equal to one on that ball and take a Schwartz function \(f\) with \(f_+ = h\). Such an \(f\) exists by Fourier inversion, with the factor \((2 \pi)^{- 4}\) in the inverse transform. The weight \(|f_+ |^2\) is then exactly one on the ball. Equality of all the weighted measures implies equality on every measurable subset of each ball; increasing the radius proves equality on all Borel sets by continuity from below.

By Theorem 6.33, every measure on the left of (18.185) vanishes outside the forward cone. Apply the preceding weights on each ball to conclude

\(\displaystyle \mu_v (\mathbb{R}^4 \setminus \bar{V}_+) = 0.\) (18.186)

Thus the forward-cone statement is inherited from the reconstructed field representation, while the initial undamping argument required only positivity of the time coordinate in the Euclidean chamber.

For completeness the same identification gives Lorentz covariance of the point measure. Write \(A = \Lambda^{- T}\) for the dual momentum action and \(g_{\Lambda} (x) = g (\Lambda^{- 1} x)\). Since \(\det \Lambda = 1\), \((g_{\Lambda})_+ (Ap) = g_+ (p)\). Covariance of the field vectors and of their PVM measures then gives

\(\displaystyle | (g_{\Lambda})_+ |^2 \mu_{\rho (\Lambda) v} = A_{\ast} (|g_+ |^2 \mu_v) = | (g_{\Lambda})_+ |^2 A_{\ast} \mu_v .\)

The transformed tests run through Schwartz space, so uniqueness by the weights proves \(\mu_{\rho (\Lambda) v} = A_{\ast} \mu_v\). In particular a Lorentz scalar label has a Lorentz-invariant point measure, with the same dual convention as the joint translation measure.

18.18.5Application to the centered trace

Let \(T_{ab}\) be the physical Euclidean stress tensor of the same construction, a family of grade-four labels, and let \(\chi\) be the selected phase fixed above, with one-point functional \(\langle \hspace{0.17em} \cdot \hspace{0.17em} \rangle_{\chi}\). We use the following properties of \(T_{ab}\) as hypotheses; they are produced by the stress and Ward part of the construction and not in this section: its entries are real labels, \(T_{ab} = T_{ba}\), and it transforms as a rank-two tensor under the action \(R\) of the Euclidean orthogonal group on labels. Put \(\tau = \sum_a T_{aa}\). Orthogonality gives

\(\displaystyle R (K) \tau = \sum_{a, i, j} K_{ia} K_{ja} T_{ij} = \sum_{i, j} \delta_{ij} T_{ij} = \tau .\)

Its one-point value \(c = \langle \tau \rangle_{\chi}\) is independent of position by translation invariance, and real by conjugation symmetry. The centered label \(\Theta = \tau - c\boldsymbol{1}\) is consequently real, invariant under all Euclidean orthogonal transformations, and has zero one-point function. Since the adjoint label operation is conjugation followed by time reflection, \(J \Theta = \Theta\).

It is also a Lorentz scalar. Differentiating its Euclidean invariance shows that every Euclidean rotation generator annihilates it. The corresponding boost generator is \(- i\) times that generator, so also annihilates it. The constant curve \(\Theta\) and its boost orbit solve the same finite-dimensional constant-coefficient differential equation with the same initial value; uniqueness makes the orbit constant. Spatial rotations already fix it, and generation by boosts and spatial rotations proves full Lorentz invariance.

The one-point holomorphic continuation of \(\Theta\) is zero: on positive imaginary times it is the zero Euclidean one-point function, and the one-variable identity theorem extends that equality to the half-plane for each spatial position. Its tested boundary is therefore zero. The word formula gives \(\langle \Omega, \phi (\Theta, f) \Omega \rangle = 0\) for every Schwartz \(f\). Finally (18.180) supplies the positive measure and Laplace formula for the very same centered trace used in Subsection 18.20. No additional positive-measure input is needed there once the earlier reconstruction hypotheses and this stress tensor are available.

18.19Excluding finite mass-shell profiles

The centered trace has a short-distance behavior that can distinguish it from finite polynomial expressions in free fields. The relevant distinction is the negative power of the logarithm. We prove the exclusion directly for the mass-shell profiles appearing in the statement. The proof proceeds through finite power–logarithm approximations to Laplace integrals; it does not assume a convergent asymptotic series.

18.19.1An algebra of finite asymptotic approximations

Let \(\mathcal{P}\) be the real vector space of finite sums of the functions \(s^a (\log s)^k\), where \(a \in \mathbb{Z}\), \(k \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(s > 0\). It is an algebra containing the constants. Say that \(f\) has an approximation of order \(b \in \mathbb{Z}\) if

\(\displaystyle f (s) = P (s) + O (s^b) \quad (s \downarrow 0), \qquad P \in \mathcal{P}.\)

Say that \(f\) has power–logarithm approximations if it has an approximation of every integer order. Different orders may use different finite sums. An approximation of order \(b\) is also one of any order \(a \leqslant b\), since \(s^b \leqslant s^a\) for \(0 < s < 1\).

Lemma 18.37. Functions with power–logarithm approximations form a real algebra. Every such function, and every element of \(\mathcal{P}\), is \(O (s^u)\) for some integer \(u\). Multiplication by \(s^a\) shifts the order of an approximation by \(a\).

Proof. For \(0 < s < 1\), \(| \log s| \leqslant s^{- 1}\). Thus each monomial is bounded by \(s^{a - k}\), and a finite sum is \(O (s^u)\) for the smallest of the finitely many exponents \(a - k\). An order-zero approximation then gives the growth claim for \(f\), after replacing \(u\) by \(\min (u, 0)\). The assertions about addition, real scalar multiplication, and shifts are immediate from the definition and closure of \(\mathcal{P}\) under these operations.

For multiplication fix a desired order \(b\). Choose \(\ell\) with \(g = O (s^{\ell})\) and an approximation \(f = P + O (s^{b - \ell})\). Choose \(u\) with \(P = O (s^u)\) and then \(g = Q + O (s^{b - u})\). The exact identity

\(\displaystyle fg - PQ = (f - P) g + P (g - Q)\)

shows that both terms are \(O (s^b)\). Since \(PQ \in \mathcal{P}\), this proves the required approximation. In particular it proves closure under every finite product, including the empty product \(1\).\(\Box\)

18.19.2Radial Laplace integrals

For \(m \geqslant 0\) and \(j, k \in \mathbb{N}\), put

\(\displaystyle M_{j, k} (s) = \int_0^{\infty} r^{j + 2} (m^2 + r^2)^{(k - 1) / 2} e^{- s \sqrt{m^2 + r^2}} \hspace{0.17em} dr, \qquad s > 0.\)

Proposition 18.38. Every \(M_{j, k}\) has power–logarithm approximations.

Proof. If \(m = 0\) the integral is \((j + k + 1) !s^{- (j + k + 2)}\). If \(m > 0\), set \(t = \sqrt{1 + (r / m)^2}\) and \(\nu = (j + 2) / 2\). The integral for \(k = 0\) becomes \(m^{j + 2} \int_1^{\infty} e^{- mst} (t^2 - 1)^{\nu - 1 / 2} \hspace{0.17em} dt\). Since \(ms > 0\) and \(\nu > - 1 / 2\), the Bessel integral [O+, Eq. 10.32.8] gives

\(\displaystyle M_{j, 0} (s) = \frac{m^{j + 2} \Gamma (\nu + 1 / 2)}{\sqrt{\pi}} \left( \frac{2}{ms} \right)^{\nu} K_{\nu} (ms) .\)

For integer \(\nu\), Eq. 10.31.1 of [O+] writes \(z^{- \nu} K_{\nu} (z)\) as a Laurent series with finitely many negative powers plus \(\log z\) times a power series. For noninteger \(\nu\), Eqs. 10.25.2 and 10.27.4 express it as a power series plus \(z^{- 2 \nu}\) times a power series. Here \(2 \nu = j + 2\) is integral. To control derivatives of any fixed order, truncate the analytic factors sufficiently far. Differentiating an analytic tail \(s^a h (s)\) lowers its order by at most the number of derivatives. A log factor costs at most one further power in the bound. Replacing \(z\) by \(ms\) changes \(\log z\) to \(\log s + \log m\). Finally \(M_{j, k} = (- \partial_s)^k M_{j, 0}\) by dominated differentiation on compact subsets of \(s > 0\), since \(m > 0\) and the differentiated integrands decay exponentially at infinity. This supplies the approximations of every integer order.\(\Box\)

18.19.3From modes to the logarithmic obstruction

A mode consists of a mass \(m \geqslant 0\), integers \(j, k \geqslant 0\), and a continuous real function \(P\) on \(\mathbb{R}^3\) homogeneous of degree \(j\) under positive scalings. Its Euclidean profile is

\(\displaystyle K_{m, P, k} (s) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^3} \frac{\omega_m (p)^k P (p)}{2 \omega_m (p)} e^{- s \omega_m (p)} \hspace{0.17em} dp, \qquad \omega_m (p) = \sqrt{m^2 + |p|^2} .\) (18.187)

Let \(d \sigma\) denote ordinary surface area measure on the unit sphere. The maximum of \(|P|\) on that sphere is finite. In polar coordinates, \(|P (r \theta) | \leqslant Cr^j\), so absolute integrability follows from that of \(M_{j, k}\). Fubini's theorem and homogeneity therefore give the exact normalization

\(\displaystyle K_{m, P, k} (s) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \int_{S^2} P (\theta) \hspace{0.17em} d \sigma (\theta) \right) M_{j, k} (s) .\) (18.188)

A track is a finite product of mode profiles; the empty track is \(1\). By Proposition 18.38 and Lemma 18.37, every track has power–logarithm approximations. The same is true of the real part of a finite complex linear combination of tracks, because all the individual tracks are real.

Lemma 18.39. (No inverse logarithmic leading term) If a real function \(f\) has an approximation of order zero and

\(\displaystyle \lim_{s \downarrow 0} s^8 (\log s^{- 1})^2 f (s) = A \in \mathbb{R},\)

then \(A = 0\).

Proof. Write \(f = P + O (1)\) with \(P \in \mathcal{P}\). Since \(s^8 (\log s^{- 1})^2 \to 0\), the same weighted limit holds for \(P\). Restrict to \(s_n = 2^{- n}\). A monomial \(c_{a, k} s^a (\log s)^k\) contributes

\(\displaystyle c_{a, k} (\log (1 / 2))^{k + 2} (2^{- (8 + a)})^n n^{k + 2} .\)

Combine equal pairs \((a, k)\) and discard zero coefficients. The remaining finite sum has distinct pairs \((\lambda, q) = (2^{- (8 + a)}, k + 2)\), with \(\lambda > 0\) and \(q \geqslant 2\). Order them lexicographically and choose the largest pair \((\lambda_{\ast}, q_{\ast})\). If the sum is empty, its limit is zero. If \(\lambda_{\ast} < 1\), every term tends to zero: writing \(\lambda = e^{- c}\) with \(c > 0\) reduces this to \(n^q e^{- cn} \to 0\). For completeness, \(e^{cn} \geqslant (cn)^{q + 1} / (q + 1)\)! proves that limit.

If \(\lambda_{\ast} \geqslant 1\), then \(D_n = \lambda_{\ast}^n n^{q_{\ast}} \to \infty\). Each smaller term divided by \(D_n\) tends to zero. With a smaller base, exponential decay dominates any remaining power of \(n\) by the same estimate; with equal base, the ratio is a negative power of \(n\). Thus the finite sum divided by \(D_n\) tends to its nonzero leading coefficient. But the sum has the finite limit \(A\), and division by \(D_n \to \infty\) makes its limit zero, a contradiction. Only the first case is possible, and it has \(A = 0\).\(\Box\)

Theorem 18.40. Let \(T : (0, \infty) \to \mathbb{C}\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \lim_{s \downarrow 0} s^8 (\log s^{- 1})^2 T (s) = A, \qquad A > 0.\)

Then \(T\) cannot agree on any interval \((0, \delta)\) with a finite complex linear combination of tracks built from (18.187).

Proof. If such agreement held, the real part of that combination would have an order-zero approximation. Taking real parts in the displayed limit and using the agreement would contradict Lemma 18.39.\(\Box\)

This proves the exclusion once the positive trace asymptotic has been established for the constructed theory. It imposes no restriction on an infinite sum or a continuous distribution of masses, and no such exclusion is asserted here. The construction of \(T\) and its positive coefficient \(A\) is a separate quantitative step.

18.20The trace coefficient and the passage to all radii

For the perturbative trace anomaly in non-Abelian gauge theory, see Collins, Duncan and Joglekar [CDJ77]. The short-distance two-point function in the selected continuum phase requires the estimates on the Wilson hierarchy given here.

Two different normalizations enter the trace asymptotic. Translation Ward identities fix the amplitude of the stress tensor, while the running Wilson coefficient fixes the logarithmic scale. After those normalizations, the numerical coefficient comes from a free curvature contraction. Finally one must remove the rounded positions and the chosen sequence of scales. We separate these operations because none can be replaced by choosing a convenient normalization of the trace after the fact.

18.20.1The free scalar contraction

Put \(N = \dim_{\mathbb{R}} \mathfrak{g}\) and use an orthonormal color basis. The reference potential covariance in four Euclidean dimensions is \(\delta^{AB} \delta_{ab} D (x)\), where

\(\displaystyle D (x) = \frac{1}{4 \pi^2 |x|^2}, \qquad x \neq 0.\)

Indices \(a, b, i, j\) range over the four coordinate directions and \(A, B\) over the \(N\) colors. The curvature covariance obtained by differentiating this potential is \(\delta^{AB} C_{abij} (x)\). Explicitly,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} C_{abij} (x) = & - \delta_{bj} \partial_a \partial_i D (x) + \delta_{bi} \partial_a \partial_j D (x)\\ & + \delta_{aj} \partial_b \partial_i D (x) - \delta_{ai} \partial_b \partial_j D (x) . \end{array}\)

Write \(r = |x|\) and \(H_{ai} = \delta_{ai} - 2 x_a x_i / r^2\). Differentiating \(D\) gives

\(\displaystyle \partial_a \partial_i D (x) = \frac{1}{4 \pi^2} (- 2 \delta_{ai} r^{- 4} + 8 x_a x_i r^{- 6}), \qquad C_{abij} (x) = \frac{H_{ai} H_{bj} - H_{aj} H_{bi}}{\pi^2 r^4} .\)

For the second identity expand the products of \(H\). Their quartic terms cancel, leaving exactly the four second-derivative terms above. Moreover \(H = I - 2 uu^{\textsf{T}}\) for the unit vector \(u = x / r\), hence \(HH^{\textsf{T}} = I\). It follows that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \sum_{i, j} (H_{ai} H_{bj} - H_{aj} H_{bi})^2 & = 2 \left( \sum_i H_{ai}^2 \right) \left( \sum_j H_{bj}^2 \right) - 2 \left( \sum_i H_{ai} H_{bi} \right)^2\\ & = 2 - 2 \delta_{ab} . \end{array}\)

Summing over the sixteen ordered pairs \((a, b)\) gives \(24\).

The reference scalar is

\(\displaystyle O (x) = \frac{1}{4} \sum_{A, a, b} : F_{ab}^A (x) F_{ab}^A (x) :,\)

where the sum is over ordered coordinate pairs. Here the colons mean the quadratic Wick field: in its two-point function only contractions between the two different positions are included. There are exactly two such pairings. Color contraction gives \(\sum_{A, B} (\delta^{AB})^2 = N\), and therefore

\(\displaystyle \langle O (x) O (0) \rangle_0 = \frac{2 N}{16} \sum_{a, b, i, j} C_{abij} (x)^2 = \frac{3 N}{\pi^4 |x|^8} .\) (18.189)

This computation applies to the actual reference table used in the selected comparison. Indeed, (18.161) identifies its correlations with Wick contractions for the potential kernel \(D = (4 \pi^2 |x|^2)^{- 1}\), and (18.164) identifies its field \(\mathcal{O}\) with precisely the ordered quarter-square field \(O\) above. Thus the reference normalization is a consequence of that same-table dictionary and the calculation, rather than an independent choice of a free comparison model:

\(\displaystyle S_0 (O, O ; x_0, x_1) = \frac{3 N}{\pi^4 |x_0 - x_1 |^8} \qquad (x_0 \neq x_1) .\) (18.190)

The factor \(1 / 4\) and the use of ordered pairs are essential to the coefficient.

18.20.2Fixing the physical stress amplitude

Here is the finite-dimensional step that prevents an arbitrary scalar multiple of the stress tensor from entering the answer. All vector spaces in this paragraph are real, including the real spaces of self-adjoint tensor fields. Let \(\mathcal{K}\) be the space of centered symmetric tensors with the required rotation symmetry, let \(\mathcal{V}\) be the space in which their divergences lie, and let \(D : \mathcal{K} \to \mathcal{V}\) be the divergence map. The reference theory has corresponding spaces \(\mathcal{K}_0, \mathcal{V}_0\) and divergence \(D_0\). The following precise finite-dimensional data are used; they are the properties under which Lemma 17.1 is proved: \(D_0\) is onto, \(\dim \ker D_0 = 1\), \(\dim \mathcal{K}= \dim \mathcal{K}_0\), and there is a surjective map \(Q : \mathcal{K} \to \mathcal{V}_0\) with \(\ker Q = \ker D\). Rank–nullity then gives

\(\displaystyle \dim \ker D = \dim \mathcal{K}- \dim \mathcal{V}_0 = \dim \mathcal{K}_0 - \dim \mathcal{V}_0 = 1.\) (18.191)

In the construction the surjectivity of \(Q\) is obtained from a surjective finite-dimensional comparison composed with a test map into \(\mathcal{K}\); a surjective composition is sufficient to make \(Q\) itself surjective. The statements about \(D_0\) are proved in Proposition 16.3. The other properties are supplied by the conserved-line construction of Subsection 17.3, on the completed maps of the selected trace chart family, as follows. The map \(C_i B = \text{center} (U_{i, 4} B)\) is an isomorphism of the two centered tensor spaces: its inverse centers \(U_{i, 4}^{- 1}\), and the unit corrections cancel because \(U_{i, 4} 1 = 1\). This proves the dimension identity. With \(V_i\) the induced isomorphism of vector arrays, \(V_i^{- 1} r_i DC_i\) converges to \(D_0\) by the derivative-transport argument there. If \(R^{\text{right}}\) is any linear right inverse of \(D_0\), then \(V_i^{- 1} r_i DC_i R^{\text{right}} \to I\) and is invertible for large \(i\). Thus \(Q_i = V_i^{- 1} r_i D\) is onto and, since \(r_i > 0\) and \(V_i\) is invertible, \(\ker Q_i = \ker D\). Choose any one such large \(i\) for \(Q\). This construction uses only the uniform short-test estimate and coherence of the chart family, not the trace asymptotic or its normalization.

Let \(R \in \ker D\) be the physical centered stress tensor. At scales \(r_i > 0\) let \(U_i\) be the calibrated isomorphism from reference grade-four fields to interacting grade-four fields. On tensors set

\(\displaystyle Y_i (T)_{ab} = r_i^4 U_i^{- 1} (T_{ab}) .\)

Suppose the comparison construction supplies eventually conserved tensors \(T_i \in \mathcal{K}\) and positive numbers \(B_i\) with

\(\displaystyle Y_i (T_i) \longrightarrow R_0, \qquad B_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} Y_i (T_i) \longrightarrow 4 d_2 O.\) (18.192)

In the selected construction these are the corrected tensors \(T_i\) and the arch scales \(B_i = q_i\) of Subsection 18.17, with \(R_0 = M_0\) and \(4 d_2 = - \kappa\) there; the two limits are (18.159) and (18.160), which are conditional on the inputs of that section. The normalization test is a shell flux functional \(\ell_i\) on tensors. For example, fixing a separated spectator configuration \(x\), a smooth cutoff \(\chi\) equal to one near that configuration, a direction \(b\), and one distinguished field \(v\), its form is

\(\displaystyle \ell_i (T) = - r_i \sum_a \int (\partial_a \chi_i) (z) S_{T_{ab}, v_i, \boldsymbol{q}_i} (z, r_i x) \hspace{0.17em} dz, \qquad \chi_i (z) = \chi (z / r_i) .\)

The derivatives of \(\chi_i\) are supported away from the spectators, so the integrals involve separated correlations. The translation Ward identity for the physical tensor identifies this with a derivative insertion. The needed convergence of that insertion and of the comparison shell integral has the following exact consequence:

\(\displaystyle \ell_i (R) \longrightarrow y \neq 0, \qquad \ell_i (T_i) \longrightarrow y.\) (18.193)

The same nonzero \(y\) occurs because \(R_0\) has the reference translation Ward identity. The detecting reference derivative field and the jet estimates justifying these two limits are proved for the actual reference table and the completed chart family charts in the proportionality argument of Subsection 18.17, where the two limits appear as (18.169).

Lemma 18.41. Equations (18.191)–(18.193) imply

\(\displaystyle Y_i (R) \longrightarrow R_0, \qquad B_i \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} Y_i (R) \longrightarrow 4 d_2 O.\)

Proof. The first limit in (18.193) implies \(R \neq 0\). Since the conserved space is one-dimensional, eventually \(T_i = c_i R\) for a real scalar \(c_i\). Linearity of \(\ell_i\) gives \(c_i \ell_i (R) = \ell_i (T_i)\). For large \(i\) the denominator \(\ell_i (R)\) is nonzero, and the two limits give \(c_i \to y / y = 1\). In particular \(c_i\) is eventually nonzero and \(c_i^{- 1} \to 1\). Multiply both limits in (18.192) by \(c_i^{- 1}\) and use linearity of \(Y_i\) and of the trace. This yields exactly the asserted limits.\(\Box\)

Centering the trace in the chosen phase can differ from tracing the mixed-state-centered tensor \(R\) by a fixed multiple \(c\boldsymbol{1}\) of the unit. If \(U_i \boldsymbol{1}_0 =\boldsymbol{1}\) eventually and \(B_i r_i^4 \to 0\) (for the selected data the latter is (18.171)), then

\(\displaystyle U_i^{- 1} (B_i r_i^4 c\boldsymbol{1}) = B_i r_i^4 c\boldsymbol{1}_0 \longrightarrow 0.\)

Consequently the selected centered trace \(\Theta\) has the same limit

\(\displaystyle a_i := U_i^{- 1} (B_i r_i^4 \Theta) \longrightarrow 4 d_2 O.\) (18.194)

The identities above explain how the physical Ward normalization, rather than a later rescaling of \(\Theta\), determines this coefficient.

18.20.3Rounded correlations and moving labels

We next state exactly the approximation data used to pass from (18.194) to the trace asymptotic. Write \(S_{\chi} (v, w ; x_0, x_1)\) for the selected two-point function and \(S_0 (v, w ; x_0, x_1)\) for the reference one. Both are bilinear in the field labels. The selected centered trace is a scalar under Euclidean rotations, and translations preserve its correlations. Thus its two-point function has the form

\(\displaystyle S_{\chi} (\Theta, \Theta ; x_0, x_1) = T (|x_0 - x_1 |) \quad (x_0 \neq x_1) .\) (18.195)

To justify this reduction, an orthogonal map sends the nonzero difference \(x_0 - x_1\) to \(- |x_0 - x_1 | e_0\); equal-length vectors are related by a reflection, with the identity covering equality. A translation then sends the two points to \(- te_0 / 2, te_0 / 2\), where \(t = |x_0 - x_1 |\). Covariance and the scalar transformation law of \(\Theta\) give (18.195).

Input 18.42. (Trace comparison along scales) For every sequence \(s_n > 0\) tending to zero there is a subsequence \(r_i = s_{n_i}\), with \(n_i\) strictly increasing, and comparison data with the following properties. The maps \(U_i\) and numbers \(B_i > 0\) satisfy (18.194) and

\(\displaystyle \frac{\log (r_i^{- 1})}{B_i} \longrightarrow \frac{1}{\kappa}, \qquad \kappa > 0, \qquad d_2 = - \frac{\kappa}{4},\)

where \(\kappa\) is the slope of the selected running clock and the same constant in its local response payment. Only this equality of the two constants, not their separate numerical evaluation, enters the trace coefficient. There are rounded configurations \(q_i (x) \to x\) and errors \(\varepsilon_i \to 0\). Let \(E_0\) be the reference grade-four field space and fix its finite basis \((e_{\alpha})\). At every coordinatewise separated configuration \(x\) in the interior of the comparison window, eventually

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} & | S_{\chi} (U_i e_{\alpha}, U_i e_{\beta} ; r_i q_i (x)) - S_0 (e_{\alpha}, e_{\beta} ; q_i (x)) |\\ & \hspace{30mm} \leqslant \varepsilon_i (1 + M_{\alpha \beta} (q_i (x))), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.196)} \end{array}\)

where \(M_{\alpha \beta}\) is continuous near \(x\). The reference correlations are continuous there. The comparison window includes a neighborhood of \(x (t) = (0, (t / 2, t / 2, t / 2, t / 2))\) for each \(1 / 2 < t < 2\).

Proposition 18.43. (The common-trajectory scale supplier) Fix the common selected history and its extraction before choosing any sequence of radii. Use its completed chart family, its physical stress and centered trace, and the physical transfer (18.170) with the clock limit (18.173). Then these objects supply Input 18.42 for the same selected phase.

Proof. The scale estimates for the paid response, including the gain bound, are supplied by Lemma 18.23. The actual coupling band is verified in Subsection 18.13. Its local atom bounds are supplied by Subsection 18.5.1, using the probe and cloud estimates of Subsection 18.8.2; their thresholds are included before the common history is chosen. Its grade-five buffer uses the actual Crown witness. The Ward comparison used in the stress transfer is the one on these completed charts: its estimate is uniform over all vectors, so it applies to the growing principal forecast of norm \(O (t_i)\), not just to fixed reference labels. The three actual branch tensors are recorded along the same fixed-level ultrafilter. The principal and false traces use the exponent-one washout of the branch theorem; the true trace instead uses its exact normalization \(4\mathcal{O}\). These are distinct conclusions, as verified in the Ward comparison and branch application of Subsection 18.17. Its arch identity (18.157) identifies the branch coupling with the summit arch scale; alternatively the ratio lemma there transfers all three conclusions without assuming an unsupported equality. The conserved-line dimension is (17.28), with the same-table application explained above. Thus the local estimates in the physical transfer are those of the selected objects, not additional freely postulated responses or maps.

Let \(s_n > 0\) tend to zero. Apply Lemma 17.15 to this sequence. It extracts strictly increasing indices \(n_i\) and charts with radii exactly \(r_i = s_{n_i}\). The background history and its extraction remain the already fixed ones; only charts and their indices are selected after the sequence is given. All finite-grade requirements use tails of these charts. Apply the threshold refinement of Proposition 18.50 in this selection: Corollary 18.51 supplies its allowance and level budget on the same funded family. Thus (G3) holds at the selected root depth with cost \((i + 1)^{- 1}\), and the chamber lemma supplies (G4) at that very depth.

Take \(U_i\) to be the completed grade-four map and \(B_i = q_i\) to be the actual summit arch scale. The physical transfer gives \(U_i^{- 1} (B_i r_i^4 \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} R) \to - \kappa O\). The phase-centered trace differs from \(\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} R\) by one fixed scalar multiple of the unit. Unit preservation and (18.171) remove this difference after pullback. Hence (18.194) holds with \(4 d_2 = - \kappa\). The clock limit uses this same arch and same positive slope \(\kappa\), giving the required logarithmic ratio. Neither the tensor nor its scalar normalization is reselected along this subsequence.

Corollary 18.47 supplies the rounded configurations, pairwise basis errors and common comparison window for these maps and this phase. Its rounding bound tends to zero; the reference kernels are smooth off the diagonals by their explicit Wick formula. In particular they are continuous at every eligible configuration, and the control functions there are bounded in a neighborhood of that configuration. The window contains a neighborhood of every \(x (t)\) with \(1 / 2 < t < 2\). These are all the remaining clauses of Input 18.42.\(\Box\)

The physical transfer in this proposition is the output of the same-history response, Ward comparison, branch and shell constructions of Subsection 18.17. The external Ward comparison and branch units are used there as distinct statements, with their actual inputs matched separately. The proposition records the order of choices needed at the endpoint; it does not replace any of those quantitative constructions by compactness. The positive trace spectral measure is constructed in Subsection 18.18 from this same phase and centered tensor.

Lemma 18.44. Assume Input 18.42, the radial form (18.195) and the reference normalization (18.190). Then, along each comparison subsequence of the input and for each eligible fixed \(x\),

\(\displaystyle r_i^8 \log^2 (r_i^{- 1}) T (r_i |q_i (x)_0 - q_i (x)_1 |) \longrightarrow \frac{A}{|x_0 - x_1 |^8}, \qquad A = \frac{3 N}{\pi^4} .\)

Proof. The right side of (18.196) tends to zero, since \(M_{\alpha \beta} (q_i (x))\) converges and is therefore bounded. Continuity of \(S_0\) gives convergence of the left correlation to \(S_0 (e_{\alpha}, e_{\beta} ; x)\). This also holds for convergent, varying labels: expand each of them in the fixed finite basis. Every coordinate converges, and bilinearity expresses the correlation as a finite sum of products of two coordinates and one of the convergent basis correlations. In particular, (18.194) gives

\(\displaystyle S_{\chi} (U_i a_i, U_i a_i ; r_i q_i (x)) \longrightarrow (4 d_2)^2 S_0 (O, O ; x) .\)

The definition of \(a_i\), bilinearity and (18.195) identify the expression on the left with \(B_i^2 r_i^8 T (r_i |q_i (x)_0 - q_i (x)_1 |)\). Here (18.195) applies because the two points of \(r_i q_i (x)\) are distinct for large \(i\): \(q_i (x) \to x\) and the two points of \(x\) are distinct. Multiply by \((\log (r_i^{- 1}) / B_i)^2\) and use (18.190). The limiting coefficient is

\(\displaystyle \frac{(4 d_2)^2}{\kappa^2} \frac{3 N}{\pi^4} = \frac{(- \kappa)^2}{\kappa^2} \frac{3 N}{\pi^4} = \frac{3 N}{\pi^4} .\)

All divisions are legitimate since \(\kappa > 0\) and \(B_i > 0\). The common slope cancels before its numerical value is needed; this is also the cancellation in (18.174).\(\Box\)

18.21The pointwise comparison at rounded configurations

We now derive the pointwise clause (18.196) of Input 18.42, together with its window and continuity clauses, from the selected chart family data of Subsection 17.2, the pairwise comparison receipt contained in those data, and the phase construction of Sections 5.2–5.8. The derivation is conditional on the inputs (G1)–(G4) and (Ph1)–(Ph3) listed below; the only quantitative estimate it uses is the pairwise receipt (G3). For the selected funded family, (G3) is supplied by Corollary 18.51. All other steps are proved here or are results proved earlier in the text.

Configurations and the comparison window.
For \(n \geqslant 1\) a configuration is
\(\displaystyle x = (x_0, \ldots, x_{n - 1}) \in (\mathbb{R}^4)^n\)

, with the Euclidean norm of \(\mathbb{R}^{4 n}\). It is coordinatewise separated if for every coordinate direction \(a \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}\) the numbers \(x_{0, a}, \ldots, x_{n - 1, a}\) are pairwise distinct; such a configuration has distinct points. These configurations form an open set. The comparison window is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}= \{x \in (\mathbb{R}^4)^2 : |x_0 - x_1 | \geqslant 10^{- 2}, |x| \leqslant 100\} .\)
Rounding.
For \(m > 0\) and \(x \in (\mathbb{R}^4)^n\) write \(\lceil mx \rceil\) for the coordinatewise ceiling, an element of \((\mathbb{Z}^4)^n\), and put
\(\displaystyle \rho_m (x) = m^{- 1} \lceil mx \rceil .\)

Each coordinate of \(\rho_m (x) - x\) lies in \([0, m^{- 1})\), so

\(\displaystyle | \rho_m (x) - x| \leqslant \frac{2 \sqrt{n}}{m} .\) (18.197)

Rounding is idempotent: \(\lceil m \rho_m (x) \rceil = \lceil mx \rceil\), because \(m \rho_m (x)\) already has integer coordinates.

The selected chart family data.
We use the following data, indexed by \(i \in \mathbb{N}\), together with an extraction order and an ultrafilter \(\mathcal{L}\) on mesh–period pairs \((N, p)\) finer than its fused filter, along which the mesh \(N\) tends to infinity.
    (G1)

    Levels and radii. A chart level \(k_i\) with \(k_i \to \infty\), a base arc \(b_i > 5\), and the radius

    \(\displaystyle r_i = a_{k_i} m_i, \qquad m_i = \mu_{k_i}^{\hspace{0.17em} b_i},\)

    where \(a_k > 0\) is the lattice spacing at depth \(k\) and \(\mu_k \geqslant 1\) is the clock monitor, so that \(r_i = g_{b_i} (k_i)\) in the notation of (17.22). The clock satisfies \(\mu_k^5 \to \infty\) as \(k \to \infty\).

    (G2)

    Chart readings and their limits. For each grade \(d\) and each \(i \geqslant d\), the grade-\(d\) chart of level \(i\) provides linear maps \(X^{(i)}_{N, p}\) from the reference grade-\(d\) field space \(E_d\) into the raw grade-\(d\) label space, one for every \((N, p)\). For each fixed \(d\), eventually in \(i\), these converge along \(\mathcal{L}\) to a linear map \(Y_i\), and the moving chart map of Input 18.42 at grade \(d\) is \(U_i = \textsf{P}_d Y_i\), where \(\textsf{P}_d\) is the quotient projection of the selected table. (This is the finite-dimensional extraction of Subsection 17.2.)

    (G3)

    Pairwise comparison receipt at cost \(\varepsilon_i = (i + 1)^{- 1}\). This is part of the one-step chart output recorded in Subsection 17.2; its content is stated precisely in the next paragraph.

    (G4)

    Chamber interiority. There is a root depth \(w_i \leqslant k_i\) such that, if \(x\) is fit at level \(i\) (defined below), then for all sufficiently large meshes \(N\) and every period \(p\), every grade-\(d\) insertion at a site \(a_{k_i} \lceil m_i x \rceil_c\) belongs to the chamber-local domain of the centered tiling at depth \(w_i\).

A configuration \(x \in (\mathbb{R}^4)^2\) is fit at level \(i\) if the grid configuration \(a_{k_i} \lceil m_i x \rceil\) is coordinatewise separated and each of its coordinates has absolute value at most one eighth of the spacing of the middle cell of the chart. Fitness depends on \(x\) only through \(\lceil m_i x \rceil\); by idempotence of rounding, \(x\) is fit if and only if \(\rho_{m_i} (x)\) is fit.

The pairwise receipt.
Fix \(i\), a grade \(d \leqslant i\), a configuration \(x\) fit at level \(i\), and two vectors \(e, e' \in E_d\). Write \(q = \rho_{m_i} (x)\). Put
\(\displaystyle j_0 = S_0 (e ; q_0), \qquad j_1 = S_0 (e' ; q_1), \qquad \kappa = S_0 (e, e' ; q_0, q_1) - j_0 j_1,\)

the reference one-point values and the reference connected two-point value at the rounded points; these are defined because \(q\) has distinct points. For \((N, p)\) let \(f_0^{N, p}\) and \(f_1^{N, p}\) be the finite-volume insertions of the labels \(X^{(i)}_{N, p} e\) and \(X^{(i)}_{N, p} e'\) at the two lattice sites \(a_{k_i} \lceil m_i x \rceil_0\) and \(a_{k_i} \lceil m_i x \rceil_1\). Let \(\| \cdot \|_{\text{root}}\) denote the root test of the centered tiling at depth \(w_i\) for the Wilson law at \((N, p)\), as in Subsection 16.9. The receipt (G3) asserts: for every \(i\), every \(d \leqslant i\), all basis vectors \(e, e'\) of \(E_d\), all \(N > k_i\) and all \(p\), and every \(x\) that is fit at level \(i\) with \(q \in \mathcal{W}\),

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \|f_c^{N, p} - j_c \|_{\text{root}} & \leqslant \varepsilon_i \qquad (c = 0, 1), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.198)} \\ \|(f_0^{N, p} - j_0) (f_1^{N, p} - j_1) - \kappa \|_{\text{root}} & \leqslant \varepsilon_i . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.199)} \end{array}\)

It also asserts the following width clause: for every \(t \in (\mathbb{Z}^4)^2\) such that \(y = m_i^{- 1} t\) is coordinatewise separated and \(|y| \leqslant 100\), the configuration \(y\) is fit at level \(i\).

The receipt is formulated with a cumulant-corrected polynomial, and we record why its three nonempty parts are exactly the left sides above. Let \(F\) be the reference moment profile at \(q\), with \(F_{\varnothing} = 1\), \(F_{\{c\}} = j_c\) and \(F_{\{0, 1\}} = S_0 (e, e' ; q_0, q_1)\). The corrected polynomial \(\Phi\) is the unique family \((\Phi_s)_{s \subseteq \{0, 1\}}\) with \(\Phi_{\varnothing} = 1\) and

\(\displaystyle \prod_{c \in s} f_c^{N, p} = \sum_{u \subseteq s} F_u \hspace{0.17em} \Phi_{s \setminus u} \qquad (s \subseteq \{0, 1\}) .\)

Uniqueness and existence follow by induction on \(|s|\), since the term \(u = \varnothing\) is \(\Phi_s\). For \(s = \{c\}\) the identity reads \(f_c^{N, p} = \Phi_{\{c\}} + j_c\). For \(s = \{0, 1\}\) it reads

\(\displaystyle f_0^{N, p} f_1^{N, p} = \Phi_{\{0, 1\}} + j_0 \Phi_{\{1\}} + j_1 \Phi_{\{0\}} + S_0 (e, e' ; q_0, q_1),\)

and substituting the one-point parts gives \(\Phi_{\{0, 1\}} = (f_0^{N, p} - j_0) (f_1^{N, p} - j_1) - \kappa\).

Phase inputs.
Let \(\mathcal{A}\), \(\Omega\), \(\Xi\) and \(\mu\) be the commutative ground algebra, the vacuum, the character space and the representing measure of Sections 5.2 and 5.5, built from the limiting (unselected) table of the same extraction order, and let \(S_{\chi} (w) = \chi (M (w))\) be as in (5.23). We assume:
    (Ph1)

    the hypotheses of that construction, in particular the separated-cluster limit of Lemma 5.9, the representation (5.22) with full support, and the separating property of \(\Omega\) for \(\mathcal{A}\) used in the proof of Proposition 5.14;

    (Ph2)

    the finite-volume hypotheses of the polynomial-packet argument of Subsection 16.9, from (16.75) to (16.77): the planted inequality for the Wilson law at every \((N, p)\), the point-limit theorem for words with convergent labels along the selected order, and admission of the fixed reference depth; and

    (Ph3)

    reality and null relations of the phases: for every \(\chi \in \Xi\) and every distinct-point word \(w\), \(S_{\chi} (\bar{w}) = \overline{S_{\chi} (w)}\), where \(\bar{w}\) conjugates all labels without moving the points; and for the selected character \(\chi\) the phase correlations respect the null relations of the selected table, so that for raw labels \(y, y'\) and distinct points \(z_0, z_1\),

    \(\displaystyle S_{\chi} (\textsf{P}_d y, \textsf{P}_d y' ; z_0, z_1) = \chi \left( M (y@z_0 \hspace{0.17em} y' @z_1) \right) .\)

    (By Proposition 5.29, the null relations are respected under the detector hypotheses stated there.)

Lemma 18.45. (Conjugate words) Under (Ph1) and the reality clause of (Ph3), \(M (\bar{w}) = M (w)^{\ast}\) for every distinct-point word \(w\).

Proof. Put \(Z = M (\bar{w}) - M (w)^{\ast} \in \mathcal{A}\). Every character is a star homomorphism, so \(\chi (Z) = S_{\chi} (\bar{w}) - \overline{S_{\chi} (w)} = 0\) for every \(\chi \in \Xi\). By (5.22) applied to \(Z^{\ast} Z\),

\(\displaystyle \|Z \Omega \|^2 = \langle \Omega, Z^{\ast} Z \Omega \rangle = \int_{\Xi} | \chi (Z) |^2 \hspace{0.17em} d \mu (\chi) = 0.\)

Since \(\Omega\) is separating for \(\mathcal{A}\), \(Z = 0\).\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.45 supplies the identity (16.72), which Subsection 16.9 takes as an input, in the present setting; there it is used only to identify the limits of the conjugated packets.

Proposition 18.46. (Pointwise rounded comparison) Assume (G1)–(G4) and (Ph1)–(Ph3), and let \(\chi\) be the selected character. Fix a grade \(d\). There is \(i_0\) such that for all \(i \geqslant i_0\), all basis vectors \(e, e'\) of \(E_d\), and every configuration \(x\) that is fit at level \(i\) with \(q = \rho_{m_i} (x) \in \mathcal{W}\),

\(\displaystyle | S_{\chi} (U_i e, U_i e' ; r_i q) - S_0 (e, e' ; q) | \leqslant \varepsilon_i (1 + |S_0 (e ; q_0) | + |S_0 (e' ; q_1) |) .\) (18.200)

Proof. Choose \(i_0 \geqslant d\) such that the convergence in (G2) holds at grade \(d\) for all \(i \geqslant i_0\). Fix such \(i\), the vectors \(e, e'\) and \(x\). Put \(y_0 = Y_i e\), \(y_1 = Y_i e'\), so that \(X^{(i)}_{N, p} e \to y_0\) and \(X^{(i)}_{N, p} e' \to y_1\) along \(\mathcal{L}\), and \(\textsf{P}_d y_0 = U_i e\), \(\textsf{P}_d y_1 = U_i e'\). Let \(z\) be the grid configuration \(a_{k_i} \lceil m_i x \rceil\). By (G1),

\(\displaystyle z = a_{k_i} m_i \hspace{0.17em} \rho_{m_i} (x) = r_i q.\)

It is coordinatewise separated because \(x\) is fit; in particular its two points are distinct and lie on the depth-\(k_i\) grid. Let \(w\) be the two-slot word carrying \(y_0\) at \(z_0\) and \(y_1\) at \(z_1\), and \(w_c\) its one-slot subwords.

Step 1: the two corrected packets. Consider the polynomial packets, in the sense of Subsection 16.9,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} & \text{words } [w_c], \varnothing \text{ with coefficients } 1, - j_c & & (c = 0, 1),\\ & \text{words } [w], [w_1], [w_0], \varnothing \text{ with coefficients } 1, - j_0, - j_1, j_0 j_1 - \kappa . & & \end{array}\)

Their cutoff polynomials at \((N, p)\) are respectively \(f_c^{N, p} - j_c\) and

\(\displaystyle f_0^{N, p} f_1^{N, p} - j_0 f_1^{N, p} - j_1 f_0^{N, p} + j_0 j_1 - \kappa = (f_0^{N, p} - j_0) (f_1^{N, p} - j_1) - \kappa,\)

and their compressed limits are

\(\displaystyle D_c = M (w_c) - j_c I, \qquad D_{01} = M (w) - j_0 M (w_1) - j_1 M (w_0) + (j_0 j_1 - \kappa) I.\)

We check the hypotheses of the packet argument. The sites of every word lie on the depth-\(k_i\) grid and are coordinatewise separated, since \(z\) is. The coefficients do not depend on \((N, p)\). The cutoff labels \(X^{(i)}_{N, p} e\) and \(X^{(i)}_{N, p} e'\) converge along \(\mathcal{L}\) to the labels \(y_0, y_1\) of the limiting words. The root depth satisfies \(w_i \leqslant k_i\). Since \(N \to \infty\) along \(\mathcal{L}\), the set of \((N, p)\) with \(N > k_i\) and \(N\) beyond the threshold of (G4) belongs to \(\mathcal{L}\). On that set, (G4) puts \(f_0^{N, p}\) and \(f_1^{N, p}\) in the chamber-local domain; constants, sums and products preserve that domain, so all three cutoff polynomials belong to it. On the same set the receipt (18.198)–(18.199) bounds their root tests by \(\varepsilon_i\). This is the root-bound hypothesis of the packet argument with \(g = \varepsilon_i\) (and holds with \(g + \varepsilon\) for every \(\varepsilon > 0\) a fortiori).

By (Ph2) and Lemma 18.45, which supplies (16.72), the packet argument applies to each of the three packets and gives, by (16.77),

\(\displaystyle \langle \Omega, (D^{\ast} D)^n \Omega \rangle \leqslant \varepsilon_i^{2 n} \qquad (n = 0, 1, 2, \ldots ; D = D_0, D_1, D_{01}) .\)

Step 2: every character. Lemma 5.28, which uses the full-support representation of (Ph1), gives \(| \chi (D) | \leqslant \varepsilon_i\) for each of the three elements and every \(\chi \in \Xi\). The two-point calculation following that lemma, with \(a = w_0\), \(b = w_1\), \(ab = w\), then gives

\(\displaystyle | S_{\chi} (w) - (\kappa + j_0 j_1) | \leqslant \varepsilon_i (1 + |j_0 | + |j_1 |) .\)

For completeness: evaluating \(\chi\) on \(D_{01}\) and rearranging, \(S_{\chi} (w) - (\kappa + j_0 j_1) = \chi (D_{01}) + j_0 \chi (D_1) + j_1 \chi (D_0)\), and the triangle inequality gives the bound.

Step 3: identification. By the definition of \(\kappa\), \(\kappa + j_0 j_1 = S_0 (e, e' ; q_0, q_1)\). By the null-relation clause of (Ph3) and Step 1,

\(\displaystyle S_{\chi} (w) = \chi (M (y_0 @z_0 \hspace{0.17em} y_1 @z_1)) = S_{\chi} (U_i e, U_i e' ; z_0, z_1) = S_{\chi} (U_i e, U_i e' ; r_i q) .\)

Substituting these identities and the definitions of \(j_0, j_1\) proves (18.200). The threshold \(i_0\) depends only on \(d\).\(\Box\)

Corollary 18.47. (The pointwise clause of the trace input) Assume (G1)–(G4) and (Ph1)–(Ph3), and that the selected chart sequence realizes the comparison subsequence \(r_i\) of Input 18.42 at the grade \(d = 4\) of the reference field space \(E_0 = E_4\). Assume also that the reference correlations are continuous on configurations with distinct points. Put \(q_i (x) = \rho_{m_i} (x)\) and, for a basis \((e_{\alpha})\) of \(E_0\),

\(\displaystyle M_{\alpha \beta} (q) = |S_0 (e_{\alpha} ; q_0) | + |S_0 (e_{\beta} ; q_1) | .\)

Then the pointwise clause of Input 18.42 holds with these rounded configurations, with \(\varepsilon_i = (i + 1)^{- 1}\), with this \(M_{\alpha \beta}\), and with comparison window \(\mathcal{W}\): \(q_i (x) \to x\) for every \(x\), and at every coordinatewise separated \(x\) in the interior of \(\mathcal{W}\), eventually in \(i\), (18.196) holds for all \(\alpha, \beta\). Moreover \(M_{\alpha \beta}\) and the reference correlations are continuous near such \(x\), and the interior of \(\mathcal{W}\) contains a neighborhood of \(x (t) = (0, (t / 2, t / 2, t / 2, t / 2))\) for every \(1 / 2 < t < 2\).

Proof. Convergence of the rounding. Since \(b_i > 5\) and \(\mu_k \geqslant 1\), \(m_i = \mu_{k_i}^{b_i} \geqslant \mu_{k_i}^5\), which tends to infinity because \(k_i \to \infty\) (G1). Hence (18.197) gives \(|q_i (x) - x| \leqslant 2 \sqrt{n} / m_i \to 0\) for every configuration \(x\) of \(n\) points.

The estimate. Let \(x\) be coordinatewise separated and interior to \(\mathcal{W}\). The interior of \(\mathcal{W}\) and the set of coordinatewise separated configurations are open neighborhoods of \(x\). Since \(q_i (x) \to x\), there is \(i_1\) with \(q_i (x)\) in both for \(i \geqslant i_1\). For such \(i\) write \(q_i (x) = m_i^{- 1} t\) with \(t = \lceil m_i x \rceil\). Then \(q_i (x)\) is coordinatewise separated and \(|q_i (x) | \leqslant 100\), so the width clause of (G3) shows that \(q_i (x)\) is fit at level \(i\); hence \(x\) is fit, by idempotence of rounding. Proposition 18.46 applies for \(i \geqslant \max (i_0, i_1)\) to every pair of basis vectors and gives (18.196) with the stated \(\varepsilon_i\) and \(M_{\alpha \beta}\); note \(\varepsilon_i \to 0\).

Continuity. The one-point functions \(q \mapsto S_0 (e_{\alpha} ; q_0)\) and the two-point functions \(q \mapsto S_0 (e_{\alpha}, e_{\beta} ; q)\) are reference correlations of configurations with distinct points, hence continuous on that open set by assumption; so is \(M_{\alpha \beta}\), as a sum of absolute values of continuous functions. The configuration \(x\) has distinct points, so these functions are continuous near \(x\).

The window. For \(1 / 2 < t < 2\), the two points of \(x (t)\) are at distance \(|x (t)_0 - x (t)_1 | = t > 10^{- 2}\), and \(|x (t) | = t < 100\). Both functions \(x \mapsto |x_0 - x_1 |\) and \(x \mapsto |x|\) are continuous, so the strict inequalities persist on a neighborhood of \(x (t)\), which is therefore contained in \(\mathcal{W}\); it lies in the interior of \(\mathcal{W}\). Moreover \(x (t)\) is coordinatewise separated, its coordinates being \(0\) and \(t / 2 \ne 0\) in each direction.\(\Box\)

Status.
Corollary 18.47 produces the pointwise, continuity and window clauses of Input 18.42 for a given comparison subsequence, conditional on (G1)–(G4) and (Ph1)–(Ph3). The quantitative content is the pairwise receipt (G3) at cost \((i + 1)^{- 1}\). Subsection 17.2 records it as part of the one-step chart output and attributes the quantitative estimates of that output to Subsection 14.20. The root-test estimates themselves are supplied by Corollary 18.51 below: it attaches the actual marked propagation and lawful representation at the root depth to the level-budget estimate, and then purchases the cost \((i + 1)^{- 1}\) and width clause by the selection thresholds. The identification of the chart sequence with the comparison subsequence of the input, the moving-label and logarithmic clauses, and the reference normalization are separate inputs, discussed after Input 18.42.

18.22The pairwise receipt from the level budget

We now derive the receipt (G3) of Subsection 18.21 from two properties of the funded chart witnesses of Subsection 17.2: a scale allowance, stated in (A) below, and a root-test bound at each level whose size is an explicit negative power of the clock monitor, stated in (B) below. The width clause uses only (A) and the growth of the monitor. The two root-test bounds (18.198)–(18.199) use only (B) and the growth of the monitor. The selection of the chart levels then supplies (G3) at the cost \(\varepsilon_i = (i + 1)^{- 1}\). At the end of the subsection we show how the replica-root estimate of Subsection 14.19 yields (B), and attach its inputs to the selected funded family.

Charts at a fixed level.
Throughout, \(\mu_k \geqslant 1\) is the clock monitor of (G1), with \(\mu_k \to \infty\); it is the diverging monitor \(\mathcal{M}_k\) of Subsection 14.19. Fix a grade cutoff \(d\) and let
\(\displaystyle M = M_d = d + 2\)

be the marker allowance of the funded chart family at grade \(d\): the maximal number of markers per cell in the replica estimate of Subsection 14.19. (It is not the arity cap \(l\) of Subsection 17.2.) Consider a chart of the family at level \(k\) with base arc \(b > 0\). Put

\(\displaystyle m = \mu_k^{\hspace{0.17em} b}, \qquad r = a_k m,\)

so that \(r = g_b (k)\) in the notation of (17.22). Let \(a_{\ell}\) be the spacing of the middle cell of the chart. Let \(w\) be the root depth of the chart: the depth of the centered tiling on which the root tests of its comparison receipt are taken, and at which its chamber condition is asserted. A configuration \(x \in (\mathbb{R}^4)^2\) is fit for the chart if the grid configuration \(a_k \lceil mx \rceil\) is coordinatewise separated and each of its coordinates has absolute value at most \(a_{\ell} / 8\). For the chart selected at index \(i\), with \((k, b) = (k_i, b_i)\), this is exactly fitness at level \(i\) as defined in Subsection 18.21. As there, for a fit \(x\) and \(e, e' \in E_{d'}\) with \(d' \leqslant d\), we write \(f_0^{N, p}\), \(f_1^{N, p}\) for the finite-volume insertions of the chart labels of \(e\) and \(e'\) at the two sites of \(a_k \lceil mx \rceil\). We write \(j_0, j_1, \kappa\) for the reference values at the rounded configuration \(\rho_m (x)\), and \(\Phi_s\) (\(s \subseteq \{0, 1\}\)) for the parts of the corrected polynomial defined there. Thus \(\Phi_{\{c\}} = f_c^{N, p} - j_c\) and \(\Phi_{\{0, 1\}} = (f_0^{N, p} - j_0) (f_1^{N, p} - j_1) - \kappa\).

We use the following two properties of the funded chart witnesses. In both, the level threshold is fixed before the chart is chosen.

    (A)

    Allowance. There is \(K_A = K_A (d)\) such that every chart of the family at a level \(k \geqslant K_A\) with base arc \(b\) satisfies

    \(\displaystyle m \hspace{0.17em} \mu_k^2 \leqslant \frac{a_{\ell}}{a_k}, \qquad \text{equivalently} \qquad r \hspace{0.17em} \mu_k^2 \leqslant a_{\ell} .\) (18.201)
    (B)

    Level budget. There is \(K_B = K_B (d)\) with the following property. Let \(k \geqslant K_B\) and consider any chart of the family at level \(k\), any grade \(d' \leqslant d\), any basis vectors \(e, e'\) of \(E_{d'}\), any \(N > k\), any period \(p\), and any \(x\) fit for the chart with \(\rho_m (x) \in \mathcal{W}\). Then, for every nonempty \(s \subseteq \{0, 1\}\), the root test of the centered tiling at the root depth \(w\) of the chart, for the Wilson law at \((N, p)\), satisfies

    \(\displaystyle \| \Phi_s \|_{\text{root}} \leqslant \mu_k^{- 1 / (16 M)} .\) (18.202)

The thresholds \(K_A\) and \(K_B\) may depend on the grade cutoff, the chosen bases and the window \(\mathcal{W}\). They do not depend on the chart, on \((N, p)\), or on \(x\). Since there are finitely many grades \(d' \leqslant d\) and finitely many pairs of basis vectors, a threshold for each fixed pair yields one for all of them by taking the maximum. This chart-uniform form is used in the proof of Proposition 18.50, where the threshold must be known before the producer is called. A weaker form, with a threshold allowed to depend on the level curve returned by the producer, would suffice for the first assertion of that proposition, but not for the argument given for its second assertion.

Lemma 18.48. (The width clause) Let \(R > 0\). Consider a chart at a level \(k \geqslant K_A\) with base arc \(b\), and suppose \(\mu_k^2 \geqslant 8 R\). Let \(t \in (\mathbb{Z}^4)^2\) be such that \(y = m^{- 1} t\) is coordinatewise separated and \(|y| \leqslant R\). Then \(y\) is fit for the chart. In particular, if \(\mu_k^2 \geqslant 800\), the width clause of (G3) holds for that chart.

Proof. Since \(my = t\) has integer coordinates, \(\lceil my \rceil = t\), and the grid configuration of \(y\) is

\(\displaystyle z = a_k \lceil my \rceil = a_k t = a_k my = ry.\)

Multiplication by \(r > 0\) preserves the strict order of real numbers, so \(z\) is coordinatewise separated because \(y\) is. Each coordinate satisfies \(|z_{c, \nu} | = r |y_{c, \nu} | \leqslant r |y| \leqslant rR\), because a coordinate of a vector in \(\mathbb{R}^8\) is bounded by its Euclidean norm. By (18.201), which holds because \(k \geqslant K_A\), and by \(\mu_k^2 \geqslant 8 R\),

\(\displaystyle rR \leqslant \frac{a_{\ell}}{\mu_k^2} \hspace{0.17em} R \leqslant \frac{a_{\ell}}{8 R} \hspace{0.17em} R = \frac{a_{\ell}}{8} .\)

Hence \(y\) is fit. The width clause of (G3) is the case \(R = 100\), and there \(8 R = 800\).\(\Box\)

Lemma 18.49. (Cost of the root tests) Assume (B), and let \(0 < \varepsilon \leqslant 1\). If \(k \geqslant K_B\) and \(\mu_k \geqslant \varepsilon^{- 16 M}\), then for every chart of the family at level \(k\) the bounds (18.198)–(18.199), with the root test taken at the root depth \(w\) of the chart, hold with \(\varepsilon\) in place of \(\varepsilon_i\). This holds for every grade \(d' \leqslant d\), all basis vectors \(e, e'\) of \(E_{d'}\), all \(N > k\), all \(p\), and every \(x\) fit for the chart with \(\rho_m (x) \in \mathcal{W}\).

Proof. The function \(t \mapsto t^{- 1 / (16 M)}\) is decreasing on \((0, \infty)\), so \(\mu_k \geqslant \varepsilon^{- 16 M}\) gives \(\mu_k^{- 1 / (16 M)} \leqslant (\varepsilon^{- 16 M})^{- 1 / (16 M)} = \varepsilon\). By (B), \(\| \Phi_s \|_{\text{root}} \leqslant \varepsilon\) for each nonempty \(s \subseteq \{0, 1\}\). By the identities recalled above, the left sides of (18.198) and (18.199) are exactly \(\| \Phi_{\{c\}} \|_{\text{root}}\) and \(\| \Phi_{\{0, 1\}} \|_{\text{root}}\).\(\Box\)

Since \(\mu_k \to \infty\), the conditions

\(\displaystyle k \geqslant K_A (d), \qquad k \geqslant K_B (d), \qquad \mu_k^2 \geqslant 800, \qquad \mu_k \geqslant \varepsilon^{- 16 M_d}\)

hold for all sufficiently large \(k\). We write \(K (d, \varepsilon)\) for the least \(K\) such that they hold for every \(k \geqslant K\). (The monitor is not assumed monotone, so this is the right form of the threshold.)

Proposition 18.50. (Producer of the pairwise receipt) Assume (A) and (B) at every grade cutoff. Identify the root depth \(w_i\) of (G4) with the root depth \(w\) of the chart selected at index \(i\); that is, (G4) is required at that depth. Suppose that for every index \(i\) the chart family selection of Subsection 17.2, with grade cutoff \(d = i\) and cost index \(l = i\), takes its chart at a level

\(\displaystyle k_i \geqslant K (i, (i + 1)^{- 1}) .\) (18.203)

Then the selected data satisfy (G3) at the cost \(\varepsilon_i = (i + 1)^{- 1}\), including its width clause. Moreover the selection can always be made so that (18.203) holds for every \(i\), without changing the conclusions of Lemma 17.15.

Proof. Fix \(i\), and let \(M = M_i = i + 2\). The selected chart has level \(k_i \geqslant K (i, (i + 1)^{- 1})\) and base arc \(b_i\), so \(m = \mu_{k_i}^{b_i} = m_i\) and the fitness of the previous paragraphs is fitness at level \(i\). By the identification in the statement, the root test of (G3) at index \(i\) is taken at the depth \(w_i = w\) of the selected chart. Since \(k_i \geqslant K_A (i)\) and \(\mu_{k_i}^2 \geqslant 800\), Lemma 18.48 with \(R = 100\) gives the width clause: every \(y = m_i^{- 1} t\) that is coordinatewise separated with \(|y| \leqslant 100\) is fit at level \(i\). Since \(k_i \geqslant K_B (i)\) and \(\mu_{k_i} \geqslant (i + 1)^{16 M} = \varepsilon_i^{- 16 M}\), Lemma 18.49 with \(\varepsilon = \varepsilon_i\) gives (18.198)–(18.199) at depth \(w_i\). These hold for every grade \(d' \leqslant i\), all basis vectors of \(E_{d'}\), all \(N > k_i\), all \(p\), and every \(x\) fit at level \(i\) with \(\rho_{m_i} (x) \in \mathcal{W}\). As \(i\) was arbitrary, this is (G3).

For the second assertion we use only the output of the one-step producer as stated in Subsection 17.2. The chart it returns at level \(k\) and base arc \(b > 5\) has exact radius equal to the clock gap \(g_b (k) = a_k \mu_k^b\) of (17.22), and the producer accepts every threshold \(\theta > 0\) for that radius. Fix \(i\) and put \(K = K (i, (i + 1)^{- 1})\); by (A) and (B) as stated, \(K\) is determined before the producer is called. Since \(\mu_k \geqslant 1\) and \(b > 0\), the radius satisfies \(g_b (k) \geqslant a_k\). Given the threshold \(\theta > 0\) required by the selection at step \(i\), call the producer instead with

\(\displaystyle \theta' = \min (\theta, \min_{j < K} a_j) > 0\)

(with \(\theta' = \theta\) if \(K = 0\)); this minimum is positive because there are finitely many \(j < K\) and each \(a_j > 0\). If the returned level \(k\) satisfied \(k < K\), the returned radius would satisfy \(g_b (k) \geqslant a_k \geqslant \theta'\), contradicting \(g_b (k) < \theta'\). Hence \(k \geqslant K\), which is (18.203). Since \(\theta' \leqslant \theta\), the returned output also meets the threshold \(\theta\) required in the proof of Lemma 17.15, and every other property of the output is unchanged. Thus that lemma applies verbatim with the reduced thresholds, and its conclusions hold for the resulting selection.\(\Box\)

Supply of the level budget.
The bound (18.202) is the replica-root estimate of Subsection 14.19 at marker allowance \(M\). Write \(q_0\) for the number of replica cells, which is also the site count of the selected output torus. With
\(\displaystyle I_k = \frac{\log \mu_k}{16 M}, \qquad a = e^{2 I_k} = \mu_k^{1 / (8 M)} \geqslant 1,\)

a coefficient whose pushed marked density has the local estimate with coefficient bound \(a^{q_0 |I|}\) and per-site expense \(X \leqslant 1 + M\) satisfies (14.133), whose right side is \(\exp \{P_R (X) - 4 D \log g / q_0 \} / a^{|I|}\). The harmonic term \(- 4 D \log g / q_0\) is nonnegative and must be paid for. That section shows

\(\displaystyle P_R (X) - 4 D \log g / q_0 \leqslant T_M\)

under the following conditions, all at the output level \(m\) of the clock: the coupling bound \(\beta \leqslant 4 \hat{\beta}_m\); the harmonic condition \(8 D \hat{\beta}_m / q_0 \leqslant 1\), which the clock supplies eventually; the same clip and row bound \(A\) as the clock stage, with \(\theta_R \leqslant \theta_{\text{clock}}\), \(\Lambda \leqslant \Lambda_{\text{clock}}\) and \(\delta \leqslant 1\); and the late-scale purchases \(\hat{\beta}_m \theta_{\text{clock}}^3 \leqslant 1\), \(\hat{\beta}_m \theta_{\text{clock}}^4 \leqslant 1\) and \(\Lambda_{\text{clock}} \theta_{\text{clock}}^2 \leqslant 1\). If moreover the stage ticket satisfies \(T_M \leqslant I_k\), then the right side of (14.133) is at most \(e^{I_k} / a^{|I|}\). For a nonempty marker subset \(|I| \geqslant 1\) and \(a \geqslant 1\), so this is at most \(e^{I_k} / a = e^{- I_k} = \mu_k^{- 1 / (16 M)}\). The marker subsets \(I\) are the nonempty \(s \subseteq \{0, 1\}\); since \(M \geqslant 2\), they are admissible. The coefficients are the one-point and corrected two-point parts \(\Phi_s\) themselves, as Subsection 14.19 requires.

The coefficient bound \(a^{q_0 |I|}\) with \(X \leqslant 1 + M\) on the same pushed density is Proposition 14.34, with \(u = M\). It is obtained from the scalar envelope (14.123) at the marker weight (14.116). That weight is used from the launch at the middle level down to the output torus. The leading interval above the middle level is run at a smaller weight. These inputs are verified on the selected family by Proposition 14.49. We use that result below only to derive the pairwise geometric receipt; its marked propagation proof is not repeated.

Corollary 18.51. (The receipt on the selected chart family) Use the common history and the funded panels of Proposition 17.10, with the ticket requests (14.135) included before the history is chosen. For each fixed grade cutoff \(d\), restrict to its three panels and their offsets \(t \in [0, 1]\). Then (A) and (B) hold, with thresholds uniform over those charts, all \(N > k\), all periods, and all the fit configurations in the stated window. The root test in (B) is at the same depth \(w\) as the chamber statement Lemma 17.13. Consequently the selected chart family can satisfy (G3) at every index, without changing its background history or extraction.

Proof. Fix \(d\), put \(M = d + 2\), and bound the three panel arcs and their offsets by \(\bar{b} = \max_{0 \leqslant h \leqslant 2} (b_{d, h} + 1)\). Apply Proposition 14.49 with \(n = 2\) marks per replica cell and \(u = M\), to the labels solving (17.23) and their signed copies. Its common-history proof supplies the complete marked endpoint, the joint reader, the middle launch, the lower propagation and the lawful output on precisely these charts. Its large weight is \(a = \mu_k^{1 / (8 M)}\) and its output is the root depth \(w\), not the middle level \(\ell\). The scalar inverse reference-moment factor commutes with the full marked push; Lemma 14.32 consequently identifies its nonempty coefficients with the \(\Phi_s\) above, with the original partition denominator. Its root conclusion is exactly (B).

The constants in that application are uniform on the bounded window \(\mathcal{W}\), where the two points have distance at least \(10^{- 2}\). They require no positive lower bound on each coordinate difference; nonzero coordinate differences are used only for fitness and chamber ordering. Take the maximum over the finitely many grades and basis pairs. The joint stage purchase includes that maximum and both adjacent-stage data before the history is fixed. Thus the resulting level threshold is independent of the offset, \(N, p\) and fit configuration.

Lemma 17.14 gives (A) and the bracket \(J (w) \geqslant J (k) - 1\). Since \(J (k) \to \infty\), this also gives \(w \to \infty\) uniformly over these charts: for fixed \(m_0\), taking \(J (k) - 1 > J (m_0)\) forces \(w > m_0\). The late output purchases in Proposition 14.50 therefore use one common tail. The chamber lemma uses this very centered tiling at \(w\). Finally Proposition 18.50 refines the radius thresholds to obtain (G3) at cost \((i + 1)^{- 1}\), preserving the exact prescribed radii, the background history and its extraction.\(\Box\)

Status.
The conditional producer Proposition 18.50 continues to apply to any family satisfying (A) and (B). Corollary 18.51 verifies those properties on the selected funded family and identifies its root depth. Thus (G3) is supplied for that family's rounded comparison. This application retains the canonical selected-history and representation constructions; it does not assert the receipt for an arbitrary chart with merely the same numerical scales.

18.22.1Removing rounding and subsequences

The rounded separation need not equal the intended radius. Pointwise convergence at rounded arguments alone does not identify \(T (r_i)\). The positive spectral representation resolves this: \(T\) is real, nonnegative and decreasing, because \(e^{- tp_0}\) has those properties for each \(p_0 \geqslant 0\). Integrability at the smaller positive time permits the comparison under the integral.

Theorem 18.52. Let \(\Theta\) be the centered trace of Subsection 18.18, let \(T\) be its two-point function in the selected phase as in (18.195), and let \(N = \dim_{\mathbb{R}} \mathfrak{g}\). Assume:

  1. the reconstruction and stress hypotheses of Subsection 18.18, under which \(T\) has the positive Laplace representation (18.180);

  2. invariance of the selected correlations under Euclidean translations and orthogonal transformations, together with the scalar law of \(\Theta\), which give the radial form (18.195);

  3. the reference normalization (18.190);

  4. Input 18.42.

Then

\(\displaystyle \lim_{r \downarrow 0} r^8 \log^2 (r^{- 1}) T (r) = \frac{3 N}{\pi^4} > 0.\)

The reconstruction hypotheses and the selected-scale comparison are matched to their producers below; the reference normalization has just been proved for the actual comparator. The proof of the limiting passage uses these statements separately.

Proof. First fix any one comparison subsequence \(r_i\). Let \(c_i = r_i^8 \log^2 (r_i^{- 1}) \geqslant 0\). For \(1 / 2 < t < 2\) take the configuration \(x (t)\) in the input. Its Euclidean separation is exactly \(t\), all four coordinate separations are nonzero, and \(w_i (t) := |q_i (x (t))_0 - q_i (x (t))_1 | \to t\). Lemma 18.44 gives

\(\displaystyle c_i T (r_i w_i (t)) \longrightarrow A / t^8 .\)

If \(t > 1\), eventually \(w_i (t) > 1\), so monotonicity yields \(c_i T (r_i) \geqslant c_i T (r_i w_i (t))\). If \(t < 1\), eventually \(0 < w_i (t) < 1\) and the reverse inequality holds. Given \(\epsilon > 0\), choose \(t_+ > 1\) and \(t_- < 1\) sufficiently close to one that \(|A / t_{\pm}^8 - A| < \epsilon / 2\). For all sufficiently large \(i\), the two comparison values are within \(\epsilon / 2\) of their limits. They bound \(c_i T (r_i)\) between \(A - \epsilon\) and \(A + \epsilon\). Thus \(c_i T (r_i) \to A\).

To obtain the limit at all positive radii, suppose it failed. There would be \(\epsilon > 0\) and a sequence \(s_n \in (0, 1 / n)\), \(n \geqslant 1\), for which \(|s_n^8 \log^2 (s_n^{- 1}) T (s_n) - A| \geqslant \epsilon\) for every \(n\). Input 18.42 supplies a comparison subsequence of this same sequence. The preceding argument makes its weighted values converge to \(A\), contradicting that inequality. Finally \(N > 0\) for the nonabelian simple Lie algebra under consideration, so \(A > 0\).\(\Box\)

18.22.2The trace requirement on the fixed hierarchy

Theorem 18.53. (The selected hierarchy satisfies the trace requirement) Fix the selected funded family, its ordered limiting hierarchy \(S\), and its extraction and arch. Let \(\chi_{\ast}\) be its selected phase, and take the reconstruction of this phase from Sections 6.1–6.10, retaining the hypotheses of those constructions. Use the normalized physical stress \(T_{ab}\) of Subsection 17.10 on this hierarchy, with its proved Ward and symmetry properties. Define in the grade-four field space

\(\displaystyle \tau = \sum_a T_{aa}, \qquad \Theta = \tau - \langle \tau \rangle_{\chi_{\ast}} \boldsymbol{1}, \qquad T (r) = S^{\chi_{\ast}}_{\Theta, \Theta} ((\boldsymbol{0}, - r / 2), (\boldsymbol{0}, r / 2)) .\)

Then the vacuum one-point functional of the reconstructed field \(\phi (\Theta, f)\) vanishes for every Schwartz test \(f\), and

\(\displaystyle \lim_{r \downarrow 0} r^8 \log^2 (r^{- 1}) T (r) = \frac{3 \dim_{\mathbb{R}} \mathfrak{g}}{\pi^4} > 0.\)

Moreover \(T\) is not equal on any interval \((0, \delta)\) to a finite complex linear combination of finite products of the mode profiles (18.187), including empty products. Thus these objects satisfy the trace requirement (19.32) and its profile exclusion on this very hierarchy. No further choice of hierarchy, phase, stress normalization or extraction is needed for the trace assertion.

Proof. The physical stress is real, symmetric and covariant under orthogonal transformations on the selected label space. By the centered-trace application in Subsection 18.18, the displayed \(\Theta\) is a self-adjoint Euclidean and Lorentz scalar with zero vacuum one-point functional. That construction also gives \(T (r) = \int e^{- rp_0} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{\Theta} (p)\) with a positive measure supported on \(p_0 \geqslant 0\). It uses the point vectors, continued correlations and field operators of this same phase reconstruction. Consequently this \(T\) is real, nonnegative and decreasing. Its argument is the separation \(r\) of the two time-axis points, not the heat height \(r / 2\).

The mixed-state centering in the stress transfer introduces no different physical field. If

\(\displaystyle R = \text{reCenter}_S (T),\)

then

\(\displaystyle \Theta = \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} R - \langle \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} R \rangle_{\chi_{\ast}} \boldsymbol{1}.\)

Indeed both centerings subtract constant multiples of the same unit, and the constants cancel in the displayed identity. The phase mean is fixed before any radius sequence is chosen. Unit preservation of the charts and \(q_i r_i^4 \to 0\) remove this fixed scalar after scaled pullback, as in (18.194). The coefficient of \(\mathcal{O}\) remains \(- \kappa\), fixed by the nonzero shell test for the Ward-normalized \(R\); no rescaling of \(\Theta\) is made here.

Apply Proposition 18.43 with this family, extraction, phase and stress. The optical, Ward comparison, branch, shell and rounded comparison data it uses are on the same charts.
