In its rounded notation \(k_i\) is the frame level \(j_i\) of the stress transfer; the stress transfer's \(k_i = \text{summit} (j_i)\) is the different level at which \(B_i = q_i = \zeta (k_i)\) is evaluated. These notational roles do not change \(U_i\) or \(r_i\). For every positive null sequence the supplier selects strictly increasing indices with exact radii \(r_i = s_{n_i}\), while the hierarchy, filter and phase remain fixed. The supremum over finite basis pairs is taken on those charts, rather than by selecting another subsequence for a basis pair or a branch.

Thus all four hypotheses of Theorem 18.52 have been matched: the positive Laplace representation above; Euclidean covariance and the scalar law of \(\Theta\); the reference normalization (18.190) for the actual comparison table; and the common-trajectory scale supplier. That theorem removes rounding by monotonicity and then removes the subsequence. Its result is the displayed limit at all positive radii tending to zero. The constant is positive because the nonabelian simple Lie algebra has positive real dimension. Real convergence here also gives the same limit after the usual inclusion into \(\mathbb{C}\).

Finally the mode in (18.187) has mass \(m \geqslant 0\), natural orders \(j, k\), and a continuous real numerator homogeneous of degree \(j\) under positive scaling. It uses Lebesgue momentum measure and factor \(1 / (2 \sqrt{m^2 + |p|^2})\), exactly the mode normalization in the trace requirement. Finite products are the tracks of Subsection 18.19; a zero-factor product is one and a zero-term linear combination is zero. Theorem 18.40 applies with the just-proved strictly positive coefficient and rules out eventual equality with every such finite complex combination. This is equality on a punctured positive neighborhood of zero, which is exactly equality on some \((0, \delta)\). It proves the last claim without selecting another trace field.\(\Box\)

Remark 18.54. (The dilation current at short separation) Let \(T_{ab}\), \(\tau\) and \(\Theta = \tau - c\boldsymbol{1}\) be as in Subsection 18.18, let \(T\) be the two-point function of (18.195), and let \(V\) be a continuously differentiable vector field on \(\mathbb{R}^4\) with \(\partial_a V_b + \partial_b V_a = 2 \varphi \delta_{ab}\) for a real function \(\varphi\). Assume in addition, in the selected phase and for \(x_0 \neq x_1\), that \(x_0 \mapsto S_{\chi} (T_{ab}, \Theta ; x_0, x_1)\) is continuously differentiable with \(\sum_a \partial_{(x_0)_a} S_{\chi} (T_{ab}, \Theta ; x_0, x_1) = 0\) for each \(b\) (the stress is conserved at separated points), and that \(S_{\chi} (\boldsymbol{1}, \Theta ; x_0, x_1) = \langle \Theta \rangle_{\chi}\), as used in Remark 5.17. Subsection 18.18 does not assume the first; for the normalized stress of Subsection 17.10 it follows, under their hypotheses, from Propositions 17.31 (vary the cutoff away from \(x_1\)) and 5.20. Put \(j_a (x_0) = \sum_b V_b (x_0) S_{\chi} (T_{ab}, \Theta ; x_0, x_1)\), the current \(\sum_b T_{ab} V_b\) at \(x_0\) against the centered trace at \(x_1\). Then

\(\displaystyle \sum_a \partial_{(x_0)_a} j_a (x_0) = \varphi (x_0) S_{\chi} (\tau, \Theta ; x_0, x_1) = \varphi (x_0) T (|x_0 - x_1 |) \quad (x_0 \neq x_1) .\)

Indeed, the product rule gives two sums. The one containing the derivatives of \(S_{\chi} (T_{ab}, \Theta ; x_0, x_1)\) vanishes by conservation. The other is \(\sum_{a, b} \partial_a V_b \hspace{0.17em} S_{\chi} (T_{ab}, \Theta ; x_0, x_1)\); since \(T_{ab} = T_{ba}\), \(\partial_a V_b\) may be replaced by \(\frac{1}{2} (\partial_a V_b + \partial_b V_a) = \varphi \delta_{ab}\), and bilinearity leaves \(\varphi S_{\chi} (\tau, \Theta ; x_0, x_1)\). Finally \(\tau = \Theta + c\boldsymbol{1}\), so bilinearity, the second assumption and \(\langle \Theta \rangle_{\chi} = 0\) give \(S_{\chi} (\tau, \Theta ; x_0, x_1) = S_{\chi} (\Theta, \Theta ; x_0, x_1)\), which is \(T (|x_0 - x_1 |)\) by (18.195).

For the dilation field \(V (z) = z\) one has \(\varphi = 1\), so for a pair at distance \(r\) the divergence of \(j\) is \(T (r)\). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 18.52, its conclusion \(r^8 \log^2 (r^{- 1}) T (r) \to 3 N / \pi^4 > 0\) as \(r \downarrow 0\), the limit required in (19.32), gives \(r_0 > 0\) with \(T (r) > 0\) for \(0 < r < r_0\). Thus the divergence of \(j\) is positive whenever \(0 < |x_0 - x_1 | < r_0\): against the centered trace, the dilation current is not divergence free at short separation. In fact \(T (r) > 0\) for every \(r > 0\): in (18.180) the measure is positive, and nonzero since the limit is positive. Like the theorem, this conclusion for a general tensor retains the four hypotheses of Theorem 18.52 and the two assumptions above. For the canonical selected stress, the trace hypotheses are matched in Theorem 18.53; the additional conservation and unit identities are supplied by the Ward and phase statements cited at the start of this remark. In the free Maxwell setting of Subsection 16.3 the polarized stress of two closed two-forms \(f, g\) has zero trace, and its dilation current is divergence free wherever \(f\) and \(g\) are Maxwell waves.

18.23Weighted bound control for the trace receiver

This section constructs the normalized response estimates and their scalar combination, which feeds the rank-one receiver lemma 18.55. The chosen and reference fields use the same finite-volume response columns, arch and separation ultrafilter. We keep the field decay separate from bound convergence: the former uses the priced mission and its detector.

Lemma 18.55. (Weighted bound from a rank-one approximate kernel) Let \(E\) and \(H\) be finite-dimensional real normed spaces. Let \(f : E \to H\) and \(U : H \to E\) be linear, with \(fU = \text{id}_H\) and \(\ker f =\mathbb{R}x\) for some \(x \ne 0\). Suppose linear maps \(v_i : E \to H\) and \(u_i : H \to E\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle v_i \longrightarrow f, \qquad u_i \longrightarrow U, \qquad v_i u_i = \text{id}_H \quad \text{eventually} .\)

Let \(w_i \to x\), let \(e_i \in \mathbb{R}\), and let \(\rho : E \to \mathbb{R}\) be a fixed linear functional. If

\(\displaystyle e_i v_i (w_i) \longrightarrow 0 \quad \text{in } H, \qquad e_i \rho (w_i) \longrightarrow 0,\)

then there are linear functionals \(\mu_i : H \to \mathbb{R}\) with uniformly bounded operator norms and

\(\displaystyle | e_i (\mu_i v_i - \rho) |_{E^{\ast}} \longrightarrow 0.\)

Proof. Choose a linear functional \(\lambda : E \to \mathbb{R}\) with \(\lambda (x) = 1\), by extending \(x\) to a basis. Define

\(\displaystyle A (y) = \lambda (y - Uf (y)) .\)

Since \(f (y - Uf (y)) = 0\), the kernel assumption gives \(y - Uf (y) = ax\) for some scalar \(a\). Applying \(\lambda\) shows \(a = A (y)\), and therefore

\(\displaystyle y = Uf (y) + A (y) x. \) (18.204)

Set \(V_i = u_i f + A \otimes w_i\), where \((A \otimes w_i) (y) = A (y) w_i\). The convergence of \(u_i\) and \(w_i\) implies \(V_i - \text{id}_E = (u_i - U) f + A \otimes (w_i - x)\), by (*). The right side tends to zero in operator norm. Once \(\|V_i - \text{id}_E \| < 1\), the Neumann series gives an inverse and

\(\displaystyle \|V_i^{- 1} - \text{id}_E \| \leqslant \frac{\|V_i - \text{id}_E \|}{1 - \|V_i - \text{id}_E \|},\)

so \(V_i^{- 1} \to \text{id}_E\).

Put \(\mu_i = \rho u_i\). These functionals have bounded norms because \(u_i \to U\). For \(y \in E\), the exact identity \(v_i u_i = \text{id}_H\) gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} e_i (\mu_i v_i - \rho) (V_i y) & = e_i \rho (u_i v_i (u_i f (y) + A (y) w_i) - u_i f (y) - A (y) w_i)\\ & = A (y) \hspace{0.17em} e_i (\rho (u_i v_i (w_i)) - \rho (w_i)) . \end{array}\)

The first term inside the final difference vanishes after weighting, because

\(\displaystyle e_i \rho (u_i v_i (w_i)) = \rho (u_i (e_i v_i (w_i)))\)

and \(\rho u_i\) is uniformly bounded. The second term vanishes by hypothesis. Thus the left side tends to zero for every fixed \(y\). In finite dimension, convergence on a basis implies operator-norm convergence, so \(e_i (\mu_i v_i - \rho) V_i \to 0\). Multiplication by \(V_i^{- 1} \to \text{id}_E\) gives \(e_i (\mu_i v_i - \rho) \to 0\) in operator norm.\(\Box\)

Let \(E\) be a finite-dimensional real normed space, \(H\) a finite-dimensional real normed space, \(s_i : E \to H\) linear maps, and \(\rho : E \to \mathbb{R}\) a fixed linear functional. Let \(x \in E\) satisfy \(\rho (x) = 0\). For each \(i\), let \(b_i \geqslant 1\) with \(b_i \to \infty\), let \(q_i, r_i \in E\), and let \(e_i \geqslant 0\). Define

\(\displaystyle m_i = b_i^{- 1} q_i - r_i .\)

Assume

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlllll} b_i^{- 1} q_i & \longrightarrow 0, & \sup_i | \rho (q_i) | & < \infty, & r_i & \longrightarrow - x, \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.205)} \\ e_i / b_i & \longrightarrow 0, & e_i \rho (r_i + x) & \longrightarrow 0, & b_i s_i (m_i) & \longrightarrow 0 \quad \text{in } H. \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.206)} \end{array}\)

Lemma 18.56. (The medium has both weighted null limits) Under (18.205)– (18.206),

\(\displaystyle m_i \longrightarrow x, \qquad e_i \rho (m_i) \longrightarrow 0, \qquad e_i s_i (m_i) \longrightarrow 0.\)

Proof. Since \(b_i^{- 1} q_i \to 0\) and \(r_i \to - x\), \(m_i = b_i^{- 1} q_i - r_i \to x\). Since \(\rho (x) = 0\),

\(\displaystyle e_i \rho (m_i) = (e_i / b_i) \rho (q_i) - e_i \rho (r_i + x) .\)

The first term tends to zero by boundedness of \(\rho (q_i)\) and \(e_i / b_i \to 0\), and the second by hypothesis. Also \(e_i / b_i \leqslant 1\) eventually, so

\(\displaystyle \|e_i s_i (m_i)\| = (e_i / b_i) \|b_i s_i (m_i)\| \leqslant \|b_i s_i (m_i)\| \longrightarrow 0.\)

\(\Box\)

All sequences in the application below are already reindexed by a selected level \(k_i \to \infty\): \(b_i\) is the arch scale at level \(k_i\), \(q_i\) and \(r_i\) are the chosen and reference responses there, and \(s_i\) is the chart at index \(i\). Precisely, \(k_i\) is the summit index of the frame level of the \(i\)th selected chart. It is the level called the postselected level and denoted \(n_i\) in Subsection 18.15; it is not the initial level denoted \(k_i\) there. This reindexing matters: the field-decay estimate is assumed, and used, only at the reindexed levels, not at every level. Write \(p_i = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}\) and \(A_a (i) = \exp (a (\log \log p_i)^2)\). The weight used is \(e_i = A_{1 / 5} (i)\) at this selected level. The next subsection proves \(e_i / b_i \to 0\) and \(e_i \leqslant p_i\). The field input is supplied with weight \(p_i\); since \(b_i \leqslant 2 p_i\), this gives \(b_i s_i (m_i) \to 0\) as required above.

In the selected construction, \(E\) is the real signed-equivariant space of grade-five coefficient arrays, \(x\) is its nonzero equation-of-motion direction, and \(\rho\) is the free scalar bound. The vectors \(q_i\) and \(r_i\) are respectively the transported chosen response and the transported reference response. Their combination \(m_i = b_i^{- 1} q_i - r_i\) is the medium used to build a response functional. The scale \(b_i\) is the positive arch scale along the selected chart. Boundedness of \(\rho (q_i)\) follows from the unweighted Ward-bound limit; the weighted estimate on \(\rho (r_i + x)\) is the scale-resolved reference-bound comparison; and the last estimate is the field decay of the medium.

Combining Lemma 18.56 with Lemma 18.55, under the chart hypotheses \(s_i \to f\), \(u_i \to U\), \(fU = \text{id}\), \(\ker f =\mathbb{R}x\) with \(x \ne 0\), and \(s_i u_i = \text{id}\) eventually, gives bounded linear functionals \(\mu_i : H \to \mathbb{R}\) and

\(\displaystyle \sup_i \| \mu_i \| < \infty, \qquad | e_i (\mu_i s_i - \rho) |_{E^{\ast}} \longrightarrow 0.\)

This is the response-estimate used by the two-direction trace correction. The displayed deduction is complete as a deduction from its hypotheses. In the selected construction it is carried out in Proposition 18.32: that proposition takes the three limits of Lemma 18.56 as its marked-vector hypotheses, derives \(s_i \to f\) from them together with its own chart inputs, and then applies Lemma 18.55. The convergence \(s_i \to f\) is therefore not a consequence of the present paragraph, and the chart inputs of that proposition are distinct from the response estimates proved below. The field estimate uses the separately priced mission, with the exact same-medium identification at the end of this section.

18.23.1The rate and medium calculation

For the already selected levels \(k_i \to \infty\), write \(p_i = \hat{\beta}_{k_i}\) for the running positive rate and \(b_i\) for the arch scale at \(k_i\). The arch is the fixed-level ultrafilter limit of the running couplings \(\beta_{N, \nu, k}\). On the ultrafilter-large set \(N \geqslant k\), the selected Wilson history gives \(\hat{\beta}_k / 2 \leqslant \beta_{N, \nu, k} \leqslant 2 \hat{\beta}_k\). The interval is closed, so its ultrafilter limit \(b (k)\) belongs to it. In particular \(b (k) > 0\). The clock tends to infinity; substitution \(k = k_i \to \infty\) proves the scale comparison

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{2} p_i \leqslant b_i \leqslant 2 p_i, \qquad p_i \longrightarrow \infty . \) (18.207)

The age weights are \(A_a (i) = \exp (a (\log \log p_i)^2)\). They satisfy

\(\displaystyle A_a (i) A_c (i) = A_{a + c} (i), \qquad A_a (i) \leqslant p_i^{\delta} \quad \text{eventually for every fixed } a \in \mathbb{R} \text{ and every } \delta > 0. \) (18.208)

For the second relation, take logarithms. Since \((\log \log p)^2 = o (\log p)\), eventually \(|a| (\log \log p_i)^2 \leqslant \delta \log p_i\). Exponentiation gives the bound. Also \(A_a (i) \to 0\) directly for \(a < 0\), since \((\log \log p_i)^2 \to \infty\).

In particular, for \(a \in \{1 / 6, 1 / 5\}\),

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant \frac{A_a (i)}{b_i} \leqslant 2 p_i^{a - 1} \longrightarrow 0. \) (18.209)

The same argument gives \(A_a (i) \leqslant p_i\) eventually, so an estimate with weight \(p_i\) implies the corresponding estimate with weight \(A_{1 / 5} (i)\).

Here is the scalar deduction from the response and reference receipts. Let \(q_i\) be the transported chosen response and \(w_i\) the transported reference response. The local response estimate and the separated flux limit have the form

\(\displaystyle \|q_i \| \leqslant CA_{1 / 6} (i), \qquad \rho (q_i) \longrightarrow - \kappa, \) (18.210)

while the reference chart and the reference-bound comparison give

\(\displaystyle w_i \longrightarrow - x, \qquad \rho (x) = 0, \qquad A_{1 / 5} (i) \rho (w_i) \longrightarrow 0. \) (18.211)

Define \(m_i = b_i^{- 1} q_i - w_i\). Then

\(\displaystyle \|b_i^{- 1} q_i \| \leqslant 2 Cp_i^{- 5 / 6} \longrightarrow 0,\)

so \(m_i \to x\). Since \(\rho (q_i)\) converges it is bounded, and

\(\displaystyle A_{1 / 5} (i) \rho (m_i) = \frac{A_{1 / 5} (i)}{b_i} \rho (q_i) - A_{1 / 5} (i) \rho (w_i) \longrightarrow 0\)

by (18.209) and (18.211). Finally, if the local field estimate is \(p_i \hspace{0.17em} s_i (m_i) \to 0\), then \(A_{1 / 5} (i) \leqslant p_i\) gives \(A_{1 / 5} (i) s_i (m_i) \to 0\). These are exactly the three limits consumed by the rank-one approximate-kernel lemma; the remaining bound-receiver construction is its finite-dimensional argument, not another analytic estimate.

The response and reference estimates are proved below from the local charged coefficient and the paid response column of the same finite-volume history. In particular the arch comparison is a closed-interval limit, and the transport error is the normalized bias in Subsection 18.11; neither requires an additional rate assumption on the arch.

18.23.2How the two weighted receipts are assembled

The weighted bound receipt for the medium uses only the response-bound limit, the reference-bound cancellation, and the age-rate comparison just proved. It is therefore useful to distinguish it from the stronger estimate that supplies the weighted field image. If \(\rho (q_i) \to - \kappa\), then \(| \rho (q_i) | \leqslant M\) eventually for some \(M\); thus

\(\displaystyle \left| \frac{A_{1 / 5} (i)}{b_i} \rho (q_i) \right| \leqslant 2 Mp_i^{- 4 / 5} \longrightarrow 0.\)

No weighted convergence rate for the response flux itself is needed here. The separate reference-chart estimate is the cancellation

\(\displaystyle A_{1 / 5} (i) \rho (w_i) \to 0.\)

This identifies the precise roles of the two limits and avoids conflating a field-size estimate with a bound estimate.

For the field image, the construction proves the stronger statement \(p_i s_i (m_i) \to 0\) in the finite-dimensional chart norm. The transfer to the weaker age weight is the monotonicity estimate \(A_{1 / 5} (i) \leqslant p_i\); there is no loss from summing over a basis because the chart is a fixed finite-dimensional operator. The bound \(\|q_i \| = O (A_{1 / 6} (i))\) alone proves only \(m_i \to x\) and does not imply this field estimate. The latter is supplied by the local pricing argument and its finite-volume passage.

18.23.3The response limits and the remaining estimates

The response-size and response-flux limits above follow from a concrete separation argument. Let \(\mathcal{T}\) be the linear map from the transported finite-volume coefficient field to the grade-five response, and let \(\lambda\) be the flux functional on the transported fields. On the limiting field, their compatibility is \(\rho (\mathcal{T}v_i) = \lambda (v_i)\). At fixed level \(i\), write \(v_i (\nu)\) for the finite-volume transported field of the chosen response, indexed by the finite-volume index \(\nu\) (a different object from the rate \(p_i\)). The local charged-configuration estimate gives, on a set in the separation ultrafilter,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \|\mathcal{T}v_i (\nu)\| & \leqslant C_T A_{1 / 6} (i), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.212)}\\ | \lambda (v_i (\nu)) + \kappa | & \leqslant C_1 A_{- 1 / 4} (i) + C_2 A_{- 1} (i) + \frac{p_i^{- 30} + p_i^{- 100}}{12} . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.213)} \end{array}\)

Here \(C_T, C_1, C_2\) are fixed constants from the finite-dimensional coefficient, response, and flux maps. The first line is the field-cost part of the estimate; the second is the flux-bound part. The transported field converges along that ultrafilter to the infinite volume field \(v_i\). Since \(\mathcal{T}\) and \(\lambda\) are continuous linear maps, the inequalities pass to their limits. Therefore \(q_i =\mathcal{T}v_i\) satisfies the response norm bound and \(\rho (q_i) = \lambda (v_i) \to - \kappa\). Every term on the right tends to zero by (18.208), proving (18.210).

Here is the finite-volume derivation, including its normalization. Work in the fixed coefficient frame, with its supremum norm, and let \(P\) delete the unit coordinate. Thus \(\|Pz\| \leqslant \|z\|\). The map \(\mathcal{T}\) is signed averaging followed by the fixed identification with real covariant arrays. Put \(\lambda = \rho \mathcal{T}\). Since the unit has zero vector flux, \(\lambda P = \lambda\). These are identities of these maps, not a norm assertion about the nonlinear local coefficient construction.

At the fixed level \(k_i\) write \(\vartheta_i\) for the budget threshold, \(X_i (\nu)\) for the chosen coefficient array, and \(B_i (\nu)\) for the public labels produced from its atomic certificate. Its charge is \(q = \vartheta_i^2 A_{1 / 6} (i)\), and its attainment cost is at most \(p_i\). Apply the finite-volume response record specialization of Subsection 18.11, with \(u = 1\), separately to the four entries. It gives the actual columns \(\mathcal{C}_{k_i, \nu}\) of those labels and

\(\displaystyle \|X_i (\nu) -\mathcal{C}_{k_i, \nu} B_i (\nu)\|_{\infty} \leqslant \vartheta_i^2 A_{- 1 / 4} (i) . \) (18.214)

Indeed its column error is at most \(4 (1 + c_{k_i}) \vartheta_i^2 A_{1 / 6} (i) / A_{1 / 2} (i)\), and \(4 (1 + c_{k_i}) \leqslant A_{1 / 12} (i)\) eventually. The exponent is exactly \(1 / 12 + 1 / 6 - 1 / 2 = - 1 / 4\). The tail is chosen before the period, volume and atomic certificate; taking a maximum over the four entries preserves it.

The transported field and its local comparison are, by definition,

\(\displaystyle v_i (\nu) = \vartheta_i^{- 2} P\mathcal{C}_{k_i, \nu} B_i (\nu), \qquad y_i (\nu) = P (\vartheta_i^{- 2} X_i (\nu)) .\)

The column here is exactly the one defining that bias, so

\(\displaystyle \|v_i (\nu) - y_i (\nu)\|_{\infty} \leqslant A_{- 1 / 4} (i) . \) (18.215)

No bound for \(\vartheta_i^{- 2}\) is lost: it cancels the factor \(\vartheta_i^2\) in (18.214). Proposition 18.60 and the payment accuracy bound following it give

\(\displaystyle \|y_i (\nu)\| \leqslant A_{1 / 6} (i), \qquad | \lambda (y_i (\nu)) + \kappa | \leqslant 4 \| \lambda \| A_{- 1} (i) + (p_i^{- 30} + p_i^{- 100}) / 12.\)

Since \(A_{- 1 / 4} \leqslant A_{1 / 6}\), the triangle inequality now yields

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{T}v_i (\nu)\| \leqslant 2 \|\mathcal{T}\| A_{1 / 6} (i),\)

and

\(\displaystyle | \lambda (v_i (\nu)) + \kappa | \leqslant \| \lambda \| A_{- 1 / 4} (i) + 4 \| \lambda \| A_{- 1} (i) + (p_i^{- 30} + p_i^{- 100}) / 12.\)

Thus (18.213) holds with \(C_T = 2 \|\mathcal{T}\|\), \(C_1 = \| \lambda \|\) and \(C_2 = 4 \| \lambda \|\). The maximum norm on direction arrays is why no factor four occurs in the transport error.

These fields really admit the fixed-level limit used above. Here is the uniform bound needed before invoking compactness. Write \(c_{j + 1}\) for the stage column allowance and \(L_j\) for the block side. The adjacent calibrated-column inequality is

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{C}_{j, \nu} b|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant c_{j + 1} L_j^d |\mathcal{C}_{j + 1, \nu} b|_{\text{coeff}} .\)

At the reference level the column is \(b\) itself. Induction over the finitely many levels up to \(k\) gives

\(\displaystyle |b|_{\text{coeff}} \leqslant B_{d, k} |\mathcal{C}_{k, \nu} b|_{\text{coeff}}, \qquad B_{d, k} = \prod_{0 \leqslant j < k} c_{j + 1} L_j^d . \) (18.216)

The factors below the reference level only enlarge the bound, since they are at least one. This constant depends on \(d, k\) but not on the volume, period or label. The finite-volume response record column has coefficient size at most two by Lemma 18.24, so its public label has norm at most \(2 B_{d, k}\). A finite-dimensional closed ball is compact; the fixed separation ultrafilter therefore gives a limit of these public labels. Changing labels outside its large set has no effect. The joint moving-label convergence defining the arch column sends this limit to the limit of \(\mathcal{C}_{k_i, \nu} B_i (\nu)\). Applying the fixed maps \(\vartheta_i^{- 2} P\) and \(\mathcal{T}\) gives precisely \(q_i\), not a second response chosen after taking the limit.

The same argument applies to all three reference profiles, not just the one whose limit is the equation-of-motion vector. Write \(P \in \{\ast, 0, 1\}\) for the stress profile and the two correction profiles. Denote their finite-volume local probes by \(c_i^P (\nu)\) and the corresponding transported fields by \(v_i^P (\nu)\). In the local radial chart, the canonical probe has zero unit coordinate and its paid coefficient is exactly \(\vartheta_i^2 c_i^P (\nu)\). The first assertion follows by evaluating the probe at the zero field; the second is the definition of its weighted local projection. Subsection 18.25.5 supplies this full observable with charge \(\vartheta_i^2 A_{1 / 6} (i)\) and attainment bound at most \(p_i\). Applying (18.214) to this atomic coefficient and then deleting the unit coordinate gives

\(\displaystyle \|v_i^P (\nu) - c_i^P (\nu)\| \leqslant A_{- 1 / 4} (i) . \) (18.217)

Thus the reference uses its own probe and its own paid public label; it is not compared to the chosen-response field \(v_i (\nu)\).

Here is the precise finite-dimensional calibration needed for this passage. Let \(a^P \in \mathbb{R}^3\) be the coefficient triple of the limiting profile and \(V : \mathbb{R}^3 \to E\) the fixed raw vector map. The local probe classification proved in Subsection 18.25.3 gives triples \(a_i^P (\nu)\) with

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{T}c_i^P (\nu) = Va_i^P (\nu), \qquad \rho (Va_i^P (\nu)) = \rho (Va^P) . \) (18.218)

For the two elementary correction profiles the triples are exactly \(a^0, a^1\). For the stress profile the local stress probe tends to the negative Maxwell tensor. The injective grade-four stress-coordinate map of Lemma 16.2 has a continuous left inverse, so its coefficient triples tend to \(a^{\ast} = (- 1, 1 / 4, - 1)\). This convergence is uniform in the local configurations on the late-level, sufficiently large-period sets: otherwise choose a violating sequence with levels and periods tending to infinity and apply the same local-probe limit and left inverse to that sequence. Consequently, for every \(\epsilon > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \|a_i^P (\nu) - a^P \| \leqslant \epsilon\)

on an ultrafilter-large set for every sufficiently large \(i\).

Put \(U_i^P = \lim_{\nu \to \mathcal{L}} \mathcal{T}v_i^P (\nu)\) and \(v_P = Va^P\). By (18.217),

\(\displaystyle \|U_i^P - v_P \| \leqslant \|\mathcal{T}\| A_{- 1 / 4} (i) + \|V\| \epsilon\)

for every sufficiently large \(i\). First let \(i \to \infty\), then \(\epsilon \downarrow 0\), to obtain \(U_i^P \to v_P\). For the bound, use the exact identity (18.218), rather than the qualitative convergence of the triples. It gives directly

\(\displaystyle | \rho (U_i^P) - \rho (v_P) | \leqslant \| \rho \| \hspace{0.17em} \|\mathcal{T}\| A_{- 1 / 4} (i), \qquad A_{1 / 5} (i) | \rho (U_i^P) - \rho (v_P) | \leqslant \| \rho \| \hspace{0.17em} \|\mathcal{T}\| A_{- 1 / 20} (i) \longrightarrow 0.\)

For \(P = \ast\), the equation-of-motion identification is \(v_{\ast} = - x\) and \(\rho (x) = 0\); taking \(w_i = U_i^{\ast}\) proves (18.211). The other two cases are the weighted correction-profile estimates used by the stress transfer. No rate of convergence of \(a_i^{\ast} (\nu)\) to \(a^{\ast}\) has been assumed.

The remaining chart-field input required by the general lemma is the exact limit

\(\displaystyle p_i \hspace{0.17em} s_i (m_i) \longrightarrow 0. \) (18.219)

It is independent of the response-size and flux estimates above. It is the conclusion of Lemma 18.4 with exponent \(1\), applied to the present medium: in the notation of that lemma, \(z_i = p_i\), \(\zeta_i = b_i\), and its medium \(v_i\) is the present \(m_i\). The hypotheses of that lemma are the scale relations, the core representation (18.4) of this medium, the response and scaling identities, the local estimate for finitely many detector fields, the detector lower bound (18.8), and the scalar rate (18.9). The detector lower bound is essential. Bounds for the pairings of \(s_i (m_i)\) with finitely many tested fields give a bound for its norm only because those tests separate the finite-dimensional response space with a lower bound uniform in \(i\); finite dimensionality alone does not provide this. All the hypotheses listed are inputs to this step, and no claim that they follow from the scalar rate calculation is made here.

For completeness the medium identity uses the same arch column as these limits. Let \(R\) denote signed averaging, \(\mathcal{E}_k\) the normalized core column of the arch, and \(a_k, a_k^{\ast}\) the limiting public chosen and stress labels. Signed equivariance of the common-trajectory columns gives \(R\mathcal{E}_k =\mathcal{E}_k R\), componentwise. Hence, with \(b = b (k) > 0\),

\(\displaystyle b^{- 1} R\mathcal{E}_k a_k - R\mathcal{E}_k a_k^{\ast} = b^{- 1} \mathcal{E}_k R (a_k - ba_k^{\ast}) .\)

The left side is exactly the medium already constructed, and the right side is the core representation (18.4), with \(x_i = R (a_{k_i} - b_i a_{k_i}^{\ast})\). Both terms in this identity use the same arch: replacing one by a separately chosen column limit would invalidate it. The response identity in the small-means lemma is the defining normalization of \(s_i\). Central inversion acts as minus the identity on these vector arrays, so its odd-grade scaling identity applies. Thus, when the same core is inserted in the priced mission of Proposition 18.27, the resulting field is the present \(s_i (m_i)\). The detector and the bound used there remain the substantive field estimates, rather than consequences of the coefficient-size bound just proved.

18.24A finite-volume separation estimate

We prove the separation estimate that converts a localized probe into a small two-site correlation. The proof is a three-term bilinear expansion; all quantitative inputs specific to the selected construction are written as precise hypotheses.

Fix a finite periodic gauge configuration space \(\Omega\) with normalized Wilson expectation \(\mathbb{E}_{\beta}\), and a reflection-positive cell tiling at selected level \(i\) with \(n \geqslant 1\) cells. Its admissible observables are bounded measurable gauge-invariant functions in the chamber-local domain. For each cell \(a\), its copy \(C_a F\) is the translated pullback of \(F\) under that cell's reflection; if the reflection has odd sign parity, the copy is complex-conjugated. Define the root bound by

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_i (F) = \left| \mathbb{E}_{\beta} \prod_{a = 1}^n C_a F \right|^{1 / n} .\)

The local positivity estimate supplies the following inequalities for observables in the chamber-local domain:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} |\mathbb{E}_{\beta} [F (\tau_{a (x)} \omega) G (\tau_{a (y)} \omega)] | & \leqslant \mathcal{R}_i (F) \mathcal{R}_i (G) \quad (x \ne y), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.220)} \\ \mathcal{R}_i (F + G) & \leqslant \mathcal{R}_i (F) +\mathcal{R}_i (G) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.221)} \end{array}\)

Here \(x, y\) are coarse sites in the tiling, \(a (x), a (y)\) are their fine anchor sites, and \(\omega \in \Omega\) is a gauge configuration. The anchor map is injective on the two sites under consideration. The first estimate is the reflected-cell product bound; the second is the root-bound triangle inequality. We use them only when the observables and all indicated sums and differences remain in the bounded measurable, gauge-invariant, chamber-local domain. The local error scale at selected level \(i\) is

\(\displaystyle t_i (a) = \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{a}{10^4} (\log p_i)^{48} \right), \qquad p_i \longrightarrow \infty .\)

It satisfies \(t_i (a) t_i (c) = t_i (a + c)\) and is increasing in \(a\) once \(p_i > 1\). Since \(p_i \to \infty\), also \((\log p_i)^{48} \to \infty\) and hence \(t_i (1) \to \infty\); in particular \(t_i (1) \geqslant 4\) for all sufficiently large \(i\).

Proposition 18.57. (Finite-volume two-site error) Let \(P, Q, U, V\) be observables in the chamber-local domain at selected level \(i\), and let \(x \ne y\) be coarse sites whose fine anchor sites are distinct. Suppose

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \mathcal{R}_i (P) & \leqslant t_i (5), & \mathcal{R}_i (Q) & \leqslant t_i (5), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.222)} \\ \mathcal{R}_i (Q - V) & \leqslant t_i (- 300), & \mathcal{R}_i (P - U) & \leqslant t_i (- 200), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.223)} \\ |\mathbb{E}_{\beta} [U (\tau_{a (x)} \omega) V (\tau_{a (y)} \omega)] | & \leqslant t_i (- 200) . & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.224)} \end{array}\)

Then

\(\displaystyle |\mathbb{E}_{\beta} [P (\tau_{a (x)} \omega) Q (\tau_{a (y)} \omega)] | \leqslant t_i (- 100)\)

for all sufficiently large \(i\).

Proof. Write \(\langle F, G \rangle_{x, y} =\mathbb{E}_{\beta} [F (\tau_{a (x)} \omega) G (\tau_{a (y)} \omega)]\). By (18.221) and the assumptions,

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_i (V) \leqslant \mathcal{R}_i (Q) +\mathcal{R}_i (V - Q) \leqslant t_i (5) + t_i (- 300) .\)

The definition gives \(\mathcal{R}_i (- F) =\mathcal{R}_i (F)\), since negating \(F\) changes the product of its \(n\) cell copies only by the overall sign \((- 1)^n\). Hence the assumed bound for \(Q - V\) also bounds \(V - Q\). Bilinearity gives the exact identity

\(\displaystyle \langle P, Q \rangle_{x, y} = \langle U, V \rangle_{x, y} + \langle P, Q - V \rangle_{x, y} + \langle P - U, V \rangle_{x, y} . \) (18.225)

Indeed, the right hand side equals \(\langle U, V \rangle + \langle P, Q \rangle - \langle P, V \rangle + \langle P, V \rangle - \langle U, V \rangle\). Using (18.220) on the last two terms and (18.222)–(18.224) therefore yields

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} | \langle P, Q \rangle_{x, y} | & \leqslant t_i (- 200) + t_i (5) t_i (- 300) + t_i (- 200) (t_i (5) + t_i (- 300))\\ & = t_i (- 200) + t_i (- 295) + t_i (- 195) + t_i (- 500)\\ & \leqslant 4 t_i (- 195) . \end{array}\)

The last inequality uses monotonicity in the exponent. Since \(t_i (1) \geqslant 4\),

\(\displaystyle 4 t_i (- 195) \leqslant t_i (1) t_i (- 195) = t_i (- 194) \leqslant t_i (- 100),\)

again by monotonicity. This proves the conclusion.\(\Box\)

18.24.1Application to a selected response insertion

For the selected construction, the proposition applies with \(P\) equal to the measured grade-five marker, that is, the one-point observation of the probe's grade-five label; \(Q\) the one-point observation of the selected grade-four insertion label, which is a separately measured grade-four field and is not obtained from the marker; \(U\) the pullback of the localized coarse witness supplied by the probe's receipt; and \(V\) the pullback of the bounded coarse approximation supplied, at the same finite volume and frame level, for that grade-four insertion. The hypotheses of the proposition then take the following form: both measured markers have root bound at most \(t_i (5)\); the local approximation satisfies \(\mathcal{R}_i (Q - V) \leqslant t_i (- 300)\); the probe-to-marker difference satisfies \(\mathcal{R}_i (P - U) \leqslant t_i (- 200)\); and the distant-site probe correlation satisfies \(| \langle U, V \rangle_{x, y} | \leqslant t_i (- 200)\). The last bound is the probe's separated-site estimate applied to the coarse approximation of the insertion; this is legitimate because that approximation also satisfies the supremum bound \(t_i (150000)\), the grade-four window condition and the room condition required of a test in that estimate. With these four receipts, the proposition proves the required two-site estimate \(t_i (- 100)\).

The proof above discharges the finite-volume assembly estimate. The coefficient-to-root and moving-depth localization arguments are supplied below. The probe witness and its separated-site bound still require the actual Crown construction described at the end of this section. No continuum or infinite-volume limit is used in this proposition.

18.24.2Production at the moving frame and localization depths

We now supply the quantitative root and localization bounds used above. All coefficient maps and pullbacks in this subsection belong to the one history at \((N, p)\); the estimates are uniform in \(N > k\) and \(p\). Write \(j\) for the frame depth, \(m\) for its first opened depth, and \(k = \text{summit} (j)\). Put

\(\displaystyle x = \hat{\beta}_j, \quad y = \log x, \quad H = y^{48}, \qquad T (a) = \exp (aH / 10000), \qquad \Delta (r, s) = \log (a_r / a_s) .\)

Thus \(T\) is evaluated at the frame, not at the summit. The summit is the least \(k > j\) with \(\Delta (j, k) \geqslant H\). The opened depth \(m \geqslant j\) is the least depth with \(a_j / a_m \geqslant B_m^2\), where \(B_m = \lceil (\log \hat{\beta}_m)^2 \rceil\) is the cover size. These choices are made on the common clock, before any terminal volume or label is chosen.

Here are the clock bounds needed in the argument, including their payment. We choose the numerical stage delays before extracting the common history. At a fixed stage, write \(r\) for its loop-path bound, \(c_{\text{dir}}\) for its direct quadratic-action bound, and \(D_G\) for the fixed real dimension of the matrix coordinate space of the gauge group. Let \(n_{\text{plaq}}\) be the number of plaquette orientations. The vacuum majorant at coupling \(z > 1\) is

\(\displaystyle F_{\text{stage}} (z) = 2 (1 + 4 r + 1) + 1 + n_{\text{plaq}} (c_{\text{dir}} + r^2) + 4 D_G \left( \log 16 + \tfrac{1}{2} \log z \right) = C_{\text{stage}} + 2 D_G \log z.\)

We verify the majorization, rather than assume a bound for the treasury. Its terms are twice the ordinary allowance plus twice the loop bound, the forced allowance, the quadratic action bound, and the logarithmic small-ball cost. The ordinary and forced allowances are at most one. With \(\vartheta\) the selected threshold, \(\Lambda\) the allowed loop cutoff, and \(h = z^{- 1 / 2}\), the loop term is \(2 z (r \vartheta^3 + r \vartheta^4) + \Lambda \vartheta^2\). The clock has \(\vartheta \leqslant z^{- 1 / 2} \exp ((\log \log z)^2)\) and \(\Lambda \vartheta^2 \leqslant 1\) on the late-scale tail of Lemma 14.51, applied to that same clock. The lemma's stage thresholds are among the common entry requests. Hence \(z \vartheta^3 \leqslant 1\) and \(z \vartheta^4 \leqslant 1\) eventually, independently of the stage constants, and the loop bound is at most \(4 r + 1\). The action term is exactly \(n_{\text{plaq}} (c_{\text{dir}} + r^2)\), since \(zh^2 = 1\). Finally \(\log (16 / h) = \log 16 + \tfrac{1}{2} \log z\). Adding the four bounds proves \(\mathcal{T} \leqslant F_{\text{stage}} (z)\) on a fixed coupling tail.

Now let \(L\) and \(c\) be the block and column constants at that stage. For all sufficiently large \(z\), impose

\(\displaystyle \log z \geqslant \max \{1, L, c, C_{\text{stage}}, 2 D_G + 1\} .\)

Writing \(u = \log z\), we have \(C_{\text{stage}} + 2 D_G u \leqslant u + 2 D_G u \leqslant u^2\). Thus these eventual requests give, at every rung \(r\) in that stage,

\(\displaystyle L_r \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_r, \qquad c_r \leqslant \log \hat{\beta}_r, \qquad \mathcal{T}_r \leqslant (\log \hat{\beta}_r)^2 .\)

At each stage entry there are only finitely many active and next-stage requests. Include the displayed bounds and their adjacent-stage versions in the finite maximum defining the entry threshold of Proposition 17.10, and then take prefix maxima to preserve earlier entries. Each request holds on an entire coupling tail with that stage fixed, so this operation is legitimate even though the constants vary from stage to stage. These bounds therefore hold on the one history selected with these delays; no history is changed after the extraction, and no uniform fixed-stage constant is asserted across all stages. The block lower bound \(L_r \geqslant 1024 (J_{r + 1} + 10)\) is a choice of the stage block before its entry threshold is paid. These purchases involve no new finite-volume analytic estimate.

Lemma 18.58. (Moving-depth bounds) For each fixed grade \(d\), eventually

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} j \leqslant m < k, \qquad H \leqslant \Delta (j, k) \leqslant 2 H, \qquad \hat{\beta}_k \leqslant 2 x,\\ a_j / a_m \leqslant y^6, \qquad (a_j / a_m)^4 \mathcal{T}_m \leqslant y^{27}, \qquad \Delta (m, k) \geqslant H / 2. \end{array}\)

Put \(S = \exp ((a_j / a_m)^4 \mathcal{T}_m + 5)\), \(E_k = 4 (1 + c_k)\) and \(G = \exp (\Delta (m, k) / 8)\). With the fixed-grade height \(\zeta_{d, r} = \hat{\beta}_r^{- w_d}\), where \(w_d\) is the fixed exponent of that height schedule, these imply

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} 2 E_k / \zeta_{d, m} \leqslant T (100), \qquad 2 (a_j / a_m)^d S \leqslant T (99), \qquad G \geqslant T (500), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.226)} \\ c_m (a_j / a_m)^d / \zeta_{d, m} \leqslant T (2), \qquad eS \leqslant T (2), \qquad 2 (1 + c_k) \leqslant T (1) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.227)} \end{array}\)

Proof. The coupling clock has increments \(\hat{\beta}_s - \hat{\beta}_r = \gamma \Delta (r, s)\) with fixed \(\gamma > 0\). Just before the first crossing of \(H\), the coupling is at most \(x + \gamma H \leqslant 2 x\). The last block adds at most \(\log L_{k - 1} \leqslant \log (2 x) \leqslant H\) to the gap. Thus the gap is at most \(2 H\), and \(x + 2 \gamma H \leqslant 2 x\) eventually gives the stated summit coupling bound. In particular \(\log \hat{\beta}_r \leqslant 2 y\) and \(B_r \leqslant 5 y^2\) for \(j \leqslant r \leqslant k\). At \(k - 1\) the gap is at least \(H - 2 y\); hence \(\exp \Delta (j, k - 1) \geqslant (5 y^2)^2\) eventually. This proves \(m < k\). Eventually \(B_j > 1\), so \(a_j / a_j = 1 < B_j^2\) and \(m > j\). By minimality of \(m\), the preceding ratio is less than \(B_{m - 1}^2\); the last block is at most \(2 y\). Consequently \(a_j / a_m \leqslant 2 y (5 y^2)^2 \leqslant y^6\) for large \(y\). The treasury bound is at most \(y^{24} (2 y)^2 \leqslant y^{27}\). Also \(\Delta (j, m) \leqslant y^6 = o (H)\), which gives the last gap bound.

Every fixed product of \(c_m, c_k, (a_j / a_m)^d\) and \(\zeta_{d, m}^{- 1}\) is bounded by a fixed power of \(x\) and \(y\). Its logarithm is \(O (y + \log y) = o (H)\). Multiplication by \(S\) adds at most \(y^{27} + 5 = o (H)\). Thus each such product is eventually bounded by \(\exp (\epsilon H)\) for every \(\epsilon > 0\). Choosing respectively \(\epsilon = 100 / 10000, 99 / 10000, 2 / 10000\) and \(1 / 10000\) proves the displayed bounds. Finally \(G \geqslant \exp (H / 16) \geqslant T (500)\).\(\Box\)

Proposition 18.59. (Root and bounded localizer from actual coefficients) Let \(L\) be a grade-\(d\) label in the actual finite-volume history, with its measured field in the frame chamber. If

\(\displaystyle \| \text{col}_m (L)\|_1 \leqslant a_j^d T (1),\)

then \(\mathcal{R}_j (O_{N, p} L) \leqslant T (5)\) eventually. For localization use the same-interface served representation constructed below. If \(\| \text{col}_k (L)\|_1 \leqslant a_j^d T (1)\), there is a bounded coarse field \(B\) at \(k\), in the window of radius \(l_d = R_d + 4\), such that

\(\displaystyle \|B\|_{\infty} \leqslant T (25000 d + 10000), \qquad \mathcal{R}_j (\textsf{B}_{k, N} B - O_{N, p} L) \leqslant T (- 300) .\)

The window has room inside the frame chamber, and its pullback is chamber-local. In particular the supremum is at most \(T (150000)\) for \(d \leqslant 5\).

Proof. For the first assertion use insertion weight \(z = T (- 3)\). The actual exponential insertion money is \(c_m a_m^{- d} \| \text{col}_m (L)\|_1\); by (18.227) its product with \(z\) is at most \(\zeta_{d, m} T (- 3 + 1 + 2) = \zeta_{d, m}\). The opened full-family exponential estimate therefore bounds its root by

\(\displaystyle \frac{\exp ((a_j / a_m)^4 \mathcal{T}_m + 6)}{z} = \frac{eS}{T (- 3)} \leqslant T (5) .\)

The extra factor \(e\) accounts for the difference between the single insertion bound \(6\) and the discrepancy bound \(5\).

For localization use the actual phase-grid construction of Subsection 13.11 with \(w = a_m^d T (400)\). Its bound condition is satisfied, since

\(\displaystyle \frac{2 wa_m^{- d} E_k}{\zeta_{d, m}} \leqslant T (400 + 100) \leqslant G.\)

The coefficient basis in Lemma 13.37 is within \(1 / 4\) of the identity. Inverting that same basis therefore expresses \(L\) as a linear combination of its terminal labels with

\(\displaystyle \sum_s |v_s | \leqslant 2 \| \text{col}_k (L)\|_1 .\)

We retain this actual coefficient bound; replacing it by an unspecified operator norm times \(\|L\|\) would lose the moving-depth estimate. Each primary masked field has supremum at most \(a_k^{- d} (1 + c_k)\) and discrepancy root at most \(S / w\). Linear combination, pullback linearity and the root triangle inequality give a field \(B\) with error

\(\displaystyle 2 a_j^d T (1) S / (a_m^d T (400)) \leqslant T (99 + 1 - 400) = T (- 300) .\)

Its supremum is at most

\(\displaystyle 2 a_j^d T (1) a_k^{- d} (1 + c_k) \leqslant T (2 + 20000 d) \leqslant T (25000 d + 10000),\)

because \(a_j / a_k \leqslant \exp (2 H) = T (20000)\). The same finite combination preserves bounded measurability, gauge invariance and the window radius \(l_d\).

For completeness, the room inequality is \(2 a_k [l_d + 30 (J_k + 5)] < a_j\). The block bounds give \(J_k \leqslant 2 y\), while \(a_j / a_k \geqslant e^H\); hence this inequality holds eventually. More generally the same proof works for any radius bounded by a fixed power of \(\hat{\beta}_k\), since such a radius and \(J_k\) are \(o (e^H)\). The pullback locality and room inclusion then put \(B\) in the required frame chamber. All constructions used the same \((N, p)\), transport and gauge interfaces.\(\Box\)

The actual labels.
For a fixed raw reference label \(v\) of grade \(d \leqslant 5\), the tested finite-height lift at frame \(j\) has coefficient bound
\(\displaystyle \| \text{col}_j (Lv)\|_1 \leqslant a_j^d \hspace{0.17em} 2 (2\mathcal{M}_j)^2 b^d \|v\|, \qquad 1 \leqslant b \leqslant \text{zoomCap} (j) .\)

Indeed the tested half-accuracy bound gives an inverse cost at most two; the frame shrink inverse costs at most \(2\mathcal{M}_j\), the nonconstant polynomial components in these grades have degree at most two, and the dilation weight costs at most \(b^d\). For coefficient transport from \(j\) to \(r \leqslant k\), the paid per-step logarithmic column bound divided by the log block is at most \(J_s^{- 2}\). Summing gives a log bound at most \(\sup_{s \geqslant j} J_s^{- 2} \Delta (j, r) = o (H)\). The logarithm of the prefactor above is also \(o (H)\), because \(\log \mathcal{M}_j = (\log \log x)^2\) and \(\log \text{zoomCap} (j) = (\log \log x)^3\). Allocate half of \(H / 10000\) to each bound. Uniformly for \(j \leqslant r \leqslant k\),

\(\displaystyle \| \text{col}_r (Lv)\|_1 \leqslant a_j^d T (1) .\)

This supplies both hypotheses of Proposition 18.59 for the selected grade-four insertion, with the same tested map at each finite height. Finitely many fixed labels require only a finite intersection of the resulting tails.

The measured grade-five marker uses a different argument. Its odd axial sector has homogeneous factor two, so its spine coefficients at \(r\) equal \(\vartheta_r^{- 2} \text{col}_r (P)\). The actual spine transport from \(k\) back to \(m\) costs at most two. Since \(\vartheta_m \leqslant 1\) and \(\vartheta_k^{- 2} \leqslant \hat{\beta}_k\), a summit bound \(\| \text{col}_k (P)\|_1 \leqslant a_j^5 \hat{\beta}_k^M\) gives

\(\displaystyle \| \text{col}_m (P)\|_1 \leqslant 2 a_j^5 \hat{\beta}_k^{M + 1} \leqslant a_j^5 T (1) .\)

Here the last step follows from \(\hat{\beta}_k \leqslant 2 x\) and \(\log x = o (H)\). Thus its root is also at most \(T (5)\). This application uses the actual odd-sector spine transport; a summit coefficient bound for an unrelated label would not suffice.

The intermediate insertion and the served-representation seam.
There is one representation issue in the localization proof that must not be hidden by its numerical estimates. At depth \(k\) the served negative played family is augmented by the positive primary factors. For the finite set of cell markers \(I\), its coefficient is the sum over all subsets \(A \subset I\): cells in \(A\) carry the local positive primary, and the complementary cells carry the marched negative insertion. This is the finite product expansion, with no truncation of its higher marker coefficients. At singleton level the sum is exactly the exponential entry of the backward coefficient family \(Z_k\); its forced profile is unchanged, because the primary has zero forced profile. Below \(k\) use that coefficient family and the same sound gauge interfaces. The two transition profiles have bounds
\(\displaystyle 2 \tau_{r + 1} a_{r + 1}^{- d} D_r (g_{r + 1} +\|Z_{r + 1} .x\|_1) \leqslant 1, \qquad \tau_{r + 1} a_r^{- d} g_r \leqslant 1,\)

as verified in Subsection 13.11. The centers remain the opened migrating centers, with occupancy one; finite linear sums preserve the prescribed finite supports. These are precisely the numerical and geometric data for the same-interface offer in Subsection 3.16. The latter invokes the whole polynomial push, including its flagged part, of Subsection 7.8; it does not permit replacing the augmented polynomial by its singleton part.

Here is the representation construction for this augmented family. Let \(A_N\) be the negative terminal birth polynomial, and let \(P_{k, N}\) be the actual iterated push from \(N\) to \(k\). The terminal observables are continuous on the compact gauge configuration space, so every coefficient of \(A_N\) is integrable. The actual Markov push preserves integrability. The local primary is bounded and measurable. Thus every coefficient of

\(\displaystyle A_k = (P_{k, N} A_N) \exp_{\text{nil}} \left( \sum_{v \in I} z_v F_v \right)\)

is integrable: it is a finite sum of an integrable pushed coefficient times a bounded product of \(F_v\)'s. Here the exponential is in the finite nilpotent marker algebra, and \(F_v\) is the reflected copy of the positive primary. This proves integrability for the whole polynomial, not only for its singleton coefficients.

The negative played insertion service supplies a representation of \(P_{k, N} A_N\) with regular entries \(f_v\), forced entries \(\lambda_v\), and ordinary and flagged higher arrays \(H, K\). Keep \(H, K\) unchanged and replace \(f_v\) by \(f_v + F_v\). This represents \(A_k\) exactly: in the commutative nilpotent algebra, \(\exp_{\text{nil}} (u + v) = \exp_{\text{nil}} u \hspace{0.17em} \exp_{\text{nil}} v\) for zero-constant polynomials. Consequently all higher-array support and estimates are unchanged, and the forced entries are unchanged. The identities for \(Z_k\) above identify the new regular entries and the old forced entries with the backward coefficient family. There is no fresh choice of response, interface, or primary coefficient.

At the next step the actual adjacent comparison of that coefficient family supplies the finite elementary list: its regular difference is the difference of the two exponential entries, and its flagged difference is the finite-support forced-profile comparison transported through the same sound interface. Their two bounds are the ones displayed above. Their single-insertion identities follow from these exact adjacent identities, while their supports and parent centers are the migrated supports and centers already used in the negative service. The actual sound-interface response of Subsection 3.16 applies to this prepared list; its whole-polynomial push is the transition of \(A_k\), with both higher arrays retained. The integrability just proved permits that push. Induction from \(k\) down to \(m\) gives a served representation of \(P_{m, k} A_k\). The weighted local entry and forced bounds at \(m\) are at most \(2\) and \(1\), respectively, by the backward coefficient family bounds and the purchased height inequality. Together with action allowance \(1\), the full-family estimate therefore costs \(2 + 1 + 2 = 5\) per cell, yielding exactly \(S / w\).

Finally, coefficientwise push preserves the integral, and expansion of \(A_k\) over subsets of \(I\) gives, at its full mode, the product of the reflected differences \(\textsf{B}_{k, N} F_v - O_{N, p} (T_N b_s)\). Pullback locality and the room inequality identify these with copies in the same frame tiling. This proves the moment identity needed for the root bound. It supplies the intermediate representation omitted from the generic phase-grid discussion for the primary insertions used here.

Assembly of the actual Crown witness.
The localizer just constructed is the grade-four test of the probe; it is not the probe's grade-five Crown witness. The source construction of that witness has the following explicit quantitative form. At the same summit \(k\), let \(X_e\) be the actual corrected primary entry minus \(\beta_{N, p, k}\) times its climate field, and let \(Y_e\) be its local finite-difference witness. Their common window must have room. If the finite lens construction gives each summand root at most \(T (- 1900000)\) and total absolute coefficient mass at most \(\hat{\beta}_k^2 \leqslant T (100000)\), the root triangle inequality gives \(\mathcal{R}_j (\textsf{B}_{k, N} Y_e) \leqslant T (- 1800000)\). Suppose its actual healthy-region identity gives
\(\displaystyle |X_e - Y_e + c_e | \leqslant e^{- \text{prism} (\hat{\beta}_k)}, \qquad |X_e - Y_e | \leqslant \hat{\beta}_k^M\)

with a fixed \(M\) and a polynomial-size exceptional set. Multiply by \(q = (a_j / a_k)^5 \leqslant T (100000)\) and apply the finite signed axial average. This average is contractive for these root and supremum bounds and annihilates the constant vector \((c_e)\). The polynomial bound is at most \(T (1000)\) eventually; the healthy error is at most \(T (- 600000)\) by the prism growth. Thus the averaged witness has root at most \(T (- 1700000)\), global difference at most \(T (101000) \leqslant T (500000)\), and healthy error at most \(T (- 500000)\). These are the Crown bounds used in Proposition 18.7.

The paired estimate and healthy identity used here are constructed below for the actual local current and the corrected-primary terms. The finite summation, scaling and signed-average argument above then produces the witness to which the Crown buffer applies.

Payment of the paired local lens estimate.
The moving-depth bound of each local lens can also be made explicit. Write \(t = \hat{\beta}_k\), \(p = \text{prism} (t)\) and set
\(\displaystyle w_r = T (1000000) t^{2 (r - j)}, \qquad j \leqslant r \leqslant k.\)

Since \(\hat{\beta}_{r + 1} \leqslant t\), these weights obey \(w_r \leqslant \hat{\beta}_{r + 1}^{- 2} w_{r + 1}\), exactly the two-marker height recurrence. The number of steps is at most \(\Delta (j, k) \leqslant 2 H\), so \(\log w_k \leqslant 100 H + 4 H \log t \leqslant p / 32\) eventually. The last inequality follows from \(p = B (t)^{40} + 1100\), \(B (t) = \lceil (\log t)^2 \rceil\), whereas \(H \leqslant (\log t)^{48}\). In particular \(1 \leqslant w_k \leqslant e^{p / 32}\).

Here is the finite-stage local payment behind this permitted weight. Let \(q = B (t)^{60} + 1100\) be the lens truncation depth, and let \(l\) be its fixed cover count. Put \(c_0 = \exp ((s - 3) l)\), where \(s\) is the stage support exponent, \(c = e^{p / 2}\) and \(\epsilon = e^{- p / 8}\). The local lease tariff is at most \(t^3\) at a late fixed-stage entry. If \(u\) is that tariff times \(e^{- q}\), then

\(\displaystyle 8 e^p u \leqslant \epsilon, \qquad 4 cc_0 \leqslant e^p, \qquad tc_0 e^2 w_k^2 / c \leqslant \epsilon .\)

Indeed \(q \geqslant 4 p\) eventually, while every fixed multiple of a fixed power of \(t\) is at most \(e^{p / 32}\). The forced allowance also satisfies \(2 e^p b \leqslant \epsilon\) by its selected small-field payment. Consequently the local summed activity bound is at most \(3 \epsilon\); \((2 B (t) + 1)^4 (3 \epsilon) \leqslant 1\) and \(e^{3 \epsilon} - 1 \leqslant 1\). These inequalities verify the local paired-marker expense with the actual weight \(w_k\). The remaining wing tariff is the one already paid by the local finite-difference construction, at most one. All bounds are uniform in the torus and the actual representation within its selected parameter band.

For each reflected cell take two marker slots with the same anchor. This has occupancy two, independently of the number of cells; the paid occupancy schedule is eventually at least two. The anchor separation, after displacement to the lens center, exceeds both the lens diameter and \(64 B (t) p + 2 B (t)\), since these are polynomial in \(t\) and \(a_j / a_k \geqslant e^H\). The local lens expansion with the expense just verified therefore forms the paired marked polynomial. Its full-mode coefficient is the product of the reflected lens fields. The same-interface whole-polynomial transport descends it to \(m\) with the recurrence for \(w_r\) above. Its regular and forced singleton entries vanish, and its action allowance is one per marker. The full-family bound consequently has per-cell action cost four and weight \(w_m^2\), giving

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_j (\textsf{B}_{k, N} \text{lens}) \leqslant \frac{\exp ((a_j / a_m)^4 \mathcal{T}_m + 4)}{w_m^2} \leqslant \exp (y^{27} + 4 - 200 H) \leqslant T (- 1900000) .\)

The division by a square is essential: the construction uses two markers per cell. Thus neither the moving-depth choice nor the large negative exponent is an additional lens smallness assumption. The local lens expansion, its wing tariff and the healthy identity are derived in the following subsection.

18.24.3The local finite-difference witness and its healthy identity

We describe the actual fields in the Crown construction, including the sign and the finite approximation used there. Write \(h = \text{peace} (t) > 0\) for the finite-difference step and \(Y_e\) for the localized geometric current in direction \(e\). Its support is the inner box \(\mathcal{B}\). For a link \(a\) in this box and a color direction obtained by conjugating a unit color by \(g \in G\), let \(U_{a, g, s}\) denote multiplication of that link by the Lie exponential of \(s\) times the color. Set

\(\displaystyle J_a (U, g) =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{U \text{ healthy at } a \text{ at threshold } \vartheta / 4\}} \langle Y_e (U)_a, \text{Ad}_g \hspace{0.17em} v_a \rangle .\)

This is the cutoff used in the actual witness. It is bounded measurable, local in the union of the current support and the inspected links at \(a\), and gauge covariant. Gauge covariance follows by changing \(g\) by the gauge transformation at the starting vertex; Haar measure is unchanged. For a scalar action \(F\) define

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}_{a, s} (F) (U) = \int_G [J_a (U_{a, g, s}, g) e^{F (U_{a, g, s}) - F (U)} - J_a (U, g)] dg.\)

The actual finite-difference witness is

\(\displaystyle W_e (U) = h^{- 1} \sum_{a, \nu} \omega_{\nu} \mathcal{L}_{a, \lambda_{\nu} h} (F_q) (U),\)

where the finite nodes \(\lambda_{\nu}\) and weights \(\omega_{\nu}\) are those of the selected order-\(m\) derivative formula, and \(F_q\) is the actual local action with support-cover cutoff \(q\).

The change of a link stays within the local healthy chart. Indeed smoothness of the Lie exponential on the unit ball gives \(\text{chord} (\exp v) \leqslant C_G \|v\|\) there. For the finitely many nodes take \(Z = C_G \max_{\nu} | \lambda_{\nu} |\). Since \(h = \vartheta t^{- 1 / 24}\), eventually \(64 Zh \leqslant \vartheta\). Thus on the support of the cutoff the moved configurations have the same large-field flags, and the current and its inspected links remain inside the prescribed enlarged box. This is why one may use the same local action and the same local response in every term.

To see the precise local expansion, write \(F_q\) as its constant plus its support activities \(f_P\) with cover at most \(q\). Activities whose support misses \(a\) have zero difference under the link change. Hence on the support of \(J_a (U_{a, g, s}, g)\),

\(\displaystyle e^{F_q (U_{a, g, s}) - F_q (U)} = \prod_{P \ni a} (1 + e^{f_P (U_{a, g, s}) - f_P (U)} - 1) .\)

Expanding this product gives the empty-set term and every nonempty cluster of changed activities, without discarding any term. The empty term is the local directional finite difference; the other terms are exactly the finite response corrections used to construct the corrected primary. Locality follows by taking the union of the supports in each term. The support-weighted sum of their absolute bounds is bounded by the exponential of the sum of the individual difference bounds. This is the elementary inequality \(\sum_{A \ne \varnothing} \prod_{P \in A} b_P = \prod_P (1 + b_P) - 1 \leqslant e^{\sum_P b_P} - 1\) for \(b_P \geqslant 0\). It also proves convergence and preserves the supports when the finite activity cutoff is enlarged.

The individual action-difference bounds follow by the fundamental theorem of calculus along the changed link. In the actual selected parameter band their weighted sum is bounded, up to fixed stage constants and subpower geometric factors, by

\(\displaystyle t \vartheta \hspace{0.17em} Zh + t (\vartheta^3 + \vartheta^4) + \vartheta^2 \text{pathBound} (t) + \text{hue} (t) .\)

Here \(\vartheta = t^{- 1 / 2} \mathcal{M} (t)\), \(h = \vartheta t^{- 1 / 24}\), and the monitor and the displayed geometric factors are subpowers of \(t\). The first term is \(Z\mathcal{M} (t)^2 t^{- 1 / 24}\); the second and third have strictly negative powers as well. Thus this sum tends to zero, and is eventually at most one. This is the wing tariff used in the paired-lens payment, now for these actual link changes. The bound is uniform in the torus because the bound is a support-weighted sum based at \(a\), not a sum over all translated cells. The cutoff current is at most \(t\) eventually: its derivative bound is a fixed subpower current bound. The same expansion bounds each lens by \(t (e^2 + 1)\), and therefore the finite weighted sum defining \(W_e\) is at most \(t^2\) after paying its fixed node weights, the subpower number of links and \(h^{- 1} \leqslant t^{2 / 3}\) eventually. These are the global bounds required above.

Choose a finite activity cabinet \(F_{q, K}\) using the very same activities as \(F_q\). Their uniformly summable bounds imply uniform convergence of the action differences and hence of their exponentials. More explicitly, if the omitted absolute difference bound is \(\eta_K \to 0\) and all full difference bounds are at most \(C\), then the difference of exponentials is at most \(e^C (e^{\eta_K} - 1)\), uniformly in \(U\) and \(g\). Multiplication by the bounded cutoff current and integration over Haar measure preserve this convergence. If \(D_m = \sum_{a, \nu} | \omega_{\nu} |\), choose the common cabinet sufficiently large that each lens error is at most \(he^{- p} / (1 + D_m)\). Then

\(\displaystyle \left| W_e - h^{- 1} \sum_{a, \nu} \omega_{\nu} \mathcal{L}_{a, \lambda_{\nu} h} (F_{q, K}) \right| \leqslant e^{- p} .\)

There are finitely many directions, so one cabinet suffices for all of them. Enlarging it to contain the finite action and loop cutoffs used by the corrected primary preserves these tail bounds. The cabinet is chosen at the given finite volume; no volume-uniform bound on its number of terms is needed.

On the common healthy region the cutoff is one for every moved configuration. For the healthy identity we refactor the finite cabinet, keeping its short main and loop action in the core. Put \(F = - \beta M - L\), where \(M\) and \(L\) are the actual finite main-action and loop-action sums. The exact local action identity is

\(\displaystyle F_{q, K} (U_{a, g, s}) - F_{q, K} (U) = F (U_{a, g, s}) - F (U) + \sum_{P \in \mathcal{A}_a} \delta_P (U, g, s),\)

where \(\mathcal{A}_a\) contains only the residual changed activities, excluding those already placed in \(F\). Thus its exponential factors as \(e^{F (U_{a, g, s}) - F (U)} \prod_{P \in \mathcal{A}_a} (1 + (e^{\delta_P} - 1))\). This differs from the expansion of the whole action used for the lens bound: here the core exponential stays outside the product. Let \(D_Y\) denote the current directional derivative. The empty residual cluster is

\(\displaystyle h^{- 1} \mathcal{D}_m (F, Y) = \text{div} \hspace{0.17em} Y + D_Y F + K_m (F, Y),\)

where \(K_m\) is the exact finite-difference remainder (defined by this identity). Write \(R_{a, A}\) for the nonempty-cluster corrections from the product above and put \(\mathcal{C}= h^{- 1} \sum_{a, \nu, A \ne \varnothing} \omega_{\nu} R_{a, A}\). The corrected primary is precisely the sum

\(\displaystyle Z_e = \beta (B_e - D_Y M) + (\text{div} \hspace{0.17em} Y_e - \text{div} \hspace{0.17em} Y_e (1)) - D_Y L + K_m (F, Y_e) +\mathcal{C}.\)

These are the actual residual, centered divergence, loop, finite-difference and nonempty-cluster terms; each is constructed with the same current, finite cabinet and finite action sums. Combining the empty and nonempty terms and using \(D_Y F = - \beta D_Y M - D_Y L\) gives the exact identity

\(\displaystyle h^{- 1} \sum_{a, \nu} \omega_{\nu} \mathcal{L}_{a, \lambda_{\nu} h} (F_{q, K}) = Z_e - \beta B_e + \text{div} \hspace{0.17em} Y_e (1) .\)

Thus for \(X_e = Z_e - \beta B_e\) and \(c_e = \text{div} \hspace{0.17em} Y_e (1)\) the cabinet estimate proves \(|X_e - W_e + c_e | \leqslant e^{- p}\) on the common healthy region. This supplies the healthy identity with its actual constant and sign. Applying the finite signed axial average kills \((c_e)\), exactly as in the Crown assembly above.

Selecting and localizing the actual geometric current.
The current in this construction is selected from the inherited geometric fields on the same finite-volume history. The selection uses the four-jet bounds and the finite family of quadratic tests, not compactness of all smooth fields. At a fixed depth \(k\), the inherited finite-height construction has error bounds of the form \(2^{- (r + 1)} d_j (\hat{\beta}_k)\) for jet orders \(2 \leqslant j \leqslant 4\). The history used here is the one bound to its own transfers and interfaces by Proposition 13.4. We now include the geometric purchases in that history's finite-stage ledger. Fix a stage profile, kernel choice, and required differentiation order. The local adjacent-field estimates give positive allowable analytic radius \(a_{\ast}\), a carrier constant, and an early derivative constant \(H_{\ast}\). Choose the integer block large enough for the inequalities below, which involve \(H_{\ast}\) only; the late derivative estimate then gives the finite constant \(D_{\ast}\) and radius \(b_{\ast}\). With that block fixed, the coupling increment \(v = \gamma \log L\) is fixed. Increasing the stage entry ensures
\(\displaystyle (\log t)^{- 1} \leqslant \min (b_{\ast}, a_{\ast} / 2), \quad (\log (t + v))^{- 1} \leqslant c_{\ast}, \quad \frac{\log (t + v)}{\log t} \leqslant L, \quad \lceil (\log t)^2 \rceil \geqslant R_{\ast} .\)

Here \(b_{\ast}, c_{\ast} > 0\) are the late derivative and canonical-top radii, and \(R_{\ast}\) is the finite geometric support requirement. The canonical path-field jet bound is also bounded by its logarithmic budget after increasing this same entry. Each inequality follows respectively from inverse-log decay, the log-ratio limit one, cover divergence, or a fixed jet bound divided by a positive log power. The bank inequalities below are paid at that entry as well. The maximum over the finitely many active and next-stage profiles is an admissible common-calendar purchase. Consequently every sufficiently late adjacent transition of this very history has the required geometric derivative, support and bank bounds. No separate history with better estimates is substituted.

Here the inheritance is the geometric pullback through the actual selected sound interface, followed by the fixed main, flux, leaf and trace averages; it is not the coefficient chart's half-accuracy map. Concretely a scalar component of engineering degree \(d\) is pulled back through the selected stationary interpolation and averaged with its specified finite sheaf, then multiplied by \(L^d\). The vector current uses the differentiated interpolation and factor \(L^5\). The stress uses the flux sheaf, the current and error use the main sheaf, and the auxiliary scalar uses the leaf sheaf plus the trace correction of the stress. The twist uses its leaf term and the two antisymmetric trace corrections. These are the exact discrete product-rule corrections: they preserve the trace, symmetry and divergence identities at every step. The interpolation, its derivative and the path entering these formulas are the ones of the actual selected transition, as bound in Proposition 13.4; there is no additional choice of a free interpolating field. Its finite-height estimates are obtained as follows. At the top finite height use the following explicit fields. Let \(F_{ab} (z) = \text{curv} (U (\square_{ab} (z)))\) be the oriented plaquette curvature, with the curvature map and color inner product of the fixed gauge group. Set

\(\displaystyle J^0_{ab} (z) = \frac{\delta_{ab}}{4} \sum_{i, l} \langle F_{il} (z), F_{il} (z) \rangle - \sum_i \langle F_{ai} (z), F_{bi} (z) \rangle .\)

The current \(Y^0_b (z)\) is supported on links starting at \(z\) and has color component \(F_{ba} (z)\) on the link in direction \(a\). Put \(Q^0 = 0\), \(\text{twist}^0 = 0\), and \(E^0_b = \sum_a \nabla_a J^0_{ab} -\mathcal{A} (Y^0_b)\), where \(\mathcal{A}\) is the actual path action's directional variation. Trace zero and symmetry of \(J^0\) follow by summing its displayed formula and symmetry of the inner product; the divergence identity holds by the definition of \(E^0\). Gauge covariance follows from conjugation covariance of plaquette curvature. At the flat field \(F_{ab} = 0\), so the scalar fields have zero constant and first jets. Their four-jet bounds follow by differentiating the finite plaquette products and the fixed smooth curvature map, together with the actual path-action derivative bounds. The quadratic tests are obtained by replacing each plaquette curvature by its first derivative, the discrete curl. On the fixed polynomial test jets this curl agrees with the free curvature as soon as the period contains the test stencil without wrap; products give the stress quadratic form and pairing the current's linear curl with the test direction gives its mixed grade-five form. Thus these tests are eventually exact on the growing-period tail. Their weighted jets through order four have fixed bounds, their quadratic tests converge to the prescribed free forms as the period grows, and their geometric identities hold exactly. At each adjacent step apply the actual inherited derivative estimate, reserving one third of the next jet budget for each summand in the stress, auxiliary and twist formulas. If \(A_{\ast}\) is the fixed total averaging debt and \(H_{\ast}\) the derivative constant, choose the stage block \(L\) so that

\(\displaystyle 3 A_{\ast} 2^{13} H_{\ast}^2 / L < 1 / 2, \qquad 3 L^5 A_{\ast} (2 H_{\ast} / L^2)^s < 1 / 2 \quad (s = 3, 4) .\)

Such an integer block exists because the powers of \(L\) are \(- 1, - 1, - 3\). The second-jet principal contribution is bounded by \(A_{\ast} 2^{13} H_{\ast}^2 (C + (d_2 (t') + C) / L)\); dividing by \(d_2 (t)\), with \(d_s (t) = (\log t)^s\) and \(t' = t + \gamma \log L\), its limit is \(A_{\ast} 2^{13} H_{\ast}^2 / L < 1 / 6\). For orders three and four the full majorant, before division by the coarse budget, is

\(\displaystyle L^5 A_{\ast} \left[ (2 H_{\ast} / L^2)^s d_s (t') + \sum_{\pi \ne \text{atomic}} D_{\ast}^{| \pi |} d_{| \pi |} (t') \right] .\)

The sum is over the finitely many non-atomic ordered derivative partitions, each with \(| \pi | < s\); it is retained in the estimate. The leading quotient tends to \(L^5 A_{\ast} (2 H_{\ast} / L^2)^s < 1 / 6\). Every other chain-rule term has strictly fewer than \(s\) derivative factors, so its quotient contains \((\log t)^{r - s}\) with \(r < s\) and tends to zero. Thus a late stage entry pays all three summands and halves the error budget at each step. The same exact pullback and averaging identities preserve the quadratic tests and the geometric field equations. Induction over the finite number of adjacent steps gives the displayed \(2^{- (r + 1)} d_j\) error bounds. Only after fixing that number do we pass to the large terminal-depth and large-period tail of the same history. These are the stage block and entry purchases already allowed by the common selected-history calendar.

Given a positive error \(\epsilon\), first choose a finite \(r\) making these three bounds less than \(\epsilon\). The fixed-height convergence then gives, on the common \((N, p)\) tail, the inherited fields with jet bound at most \(d_j (\hat{\beta}_k) + 1\) and quadratic-test error at most \(\epsilon\). These statements hold uniformly over sites: otherwise choose a violating site at each bad volume, contradicting the same fixed-height estimate for that sequence of sites. There are only finitely many component and quadratic-test indices, so one tail suffices. Take \(\epsilon = \text{shock} (\hat{\beta}_k)\) at this fixed \(k\), and average over all translations on that same torus. The inherited field equations are linear, the estimates convex, and the quadratic tests translate with their base point, so this average retains all bounds and gives the required exact symmetry of the geometric family. In particular the actual current has the common exponentially weighted four-jet bound \(\text{localizationBound} (t)\) at decay rate \(a = \text{east} (t)\).

Localize this current at radius \(r = \text{rim} (t)\) using the same smooth local extension as the other geometric fields. The localization estimate in the charge construction bounds its lost four-jet bound by

\(\displaystyle C \hspace{0.17em} \mathfrak{M} (P, a / 4) e^{- ar / 4} \text{localizationBound} (t) .\)

The retained current is supported in the box of radius \(4 (r + 2)\). For any fixed derivative order \(s\) its composition derivative bound is at most \(P_s (r) \text{localizationBound} (t)\), with a fixed polynomial \(P_s\). To obtain this last statement, differentiate the local extension: at each derivative order there are finitely many product and chain-rule terms, each involving a bounded derivative of the fixed extension and at most a fixed power of the number of sites in the radius-\(r\) box. The four-jet coefficient bound supplies the coefficients; the extension has only these four polynomial jets before composition. Thus neither this polynomial nor its constants depend on the ambient torus. The clock choices give

\(\displaystyle C\mathfrak{M} (P, a / 4) e^{- ar / 4} \text{localizationBound} (t) \leqslant t^{- 70}, \qquad P_s (r) \text{localizationBound} (t) \leqslant \mathcal{M} (t)^{1 / 200}\)

eventually, for each fixed \(s\). The first is the exponential-tail purchase; the second follows by taking logarithms of the fixed polynomial bound against the monitor. These are the local current and derivative bounds used in the cutoff and finite-difference construction. All fixed orders required at an active stage are paid together before that stage is used. This fixes the current itself before the finite cabinet approximation and before the corrected-primary coefficients are chosen.

The same localization is applied to the scalar geometric stress, its auxiliary scalar field and its first-difference fields. Their local extensions use the same cutoff; the finite algebraic identities between them are preserved by taking the prescribed shifted linear combinations. The product and chain-rule argument just given bounds each fixed-order jet by a polynomial in the localization radius times its inherited bound. Passing to a first difference adds only finitely many translated copies and one fixed enlargement of the support. The geometric current, stress, auxiliary field, twist fields and divergence therefore have common fixed-order analytic bounds at most \(\mathcal{M} (t)^{1 / 50}\) after increasing the stage entry. Their zero and first scalar jets are preserved by the local extension. The healthy quadratic Taylor conversion and the resulting reference-field bound in the charge construction consequently apply to this complete family, including its first differences; they are not inferred from a bound on the current alone.

18.25The local charged-configuration estimate

We isolate the finite-volume algebra that turns a charged configuration into a bounded field and a priced response. This is the local step used before the separation-limit passage. Its quantitative premises are stated as component bounds, so the proof does not hide the estimate inside a single named receipt.

For \(t > e\), put

\(\displaystyle W_a (t) = \exp (a (\log \log t)^2), \qquad c (t) = t^{- 30} .\)

Then \(W_a (t) W_b (t) = W_{a + b} (t)\), \(W_{- 1} (t) \to 0\), and \(c (t) \to 0\) as \(t \to \infty\).

Let \(I\) be a finite set of lattice directions, \(Y\) a finite-dimensional real normed coefficient space, and let \(\ell : Y^I \to \mathbb{R}\) be a continuous linear flux functional. Let \(s : Y^I \to [0, \infty)\) be a size function on coefficient arrays satisfying \(\|A\|_{\infty} \leqslant s (A)\) for every coefficient array \(A\); this domination is the only property of \(s\) used below. Write \(\sigma = \theta^{- 2}\) with \(\theta > 0\) for the local normalization. The nonlinear local coefficient map has already been evaluated in the arrays \(B', K', D' \in Y^I\); these are outputs of that local construction, not images under the core linear projection. Let \(X, W, E, L \in Y^I\). Assume the exact charged-configuration decomposition

\(\displaystyle X = W + B' - K' + D' + E + L, \) (18.228)

and the following local coefficient and flux receipts at scale \(t\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlllll} s (X) & \leqslant \theta^2 W_{1 / 6} (t), & & & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.229)} \\ \ell (\sigma K') & = 0, & \ell (\sigma B') & = h / 12, & |h - m| & \leqslant c (t), \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.230)} \\ s (W) & \leqslant \theta^2 W_{- 1} (t), & s (D') & \leqslant \theta^2 W_{- 1} (t), & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.231)} \\ s (E) & \leqslant \theta^2 W_{- 1} (t), & s (L) & \leqslant \theta^2 W_{- 1} (t) . & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(18.232)} \end{array}\)

Here \(h\) is the finite-volume charge extracted from the distinguished component, and \(m\) is its target bound. These are coefficient-size receipts after the local map has been evaluated. No norm contraction of the nonlinear map is assumed.

Proposition 18.60. (Local field and flux bounds) Under the displayed assumptions,

\(\displaystyle \| \sigma X\|_{\infty} \leqslant W_{1 / 6} (t), \qquad \left| \ell (\sigma X) - \frac{m}{12} \right| \leqslant 4 \| \ell \| W_{- 1} (t) + \frac{c (t)}{12} .\)

Proof. The field bound follows from the size domination and (18.229):

\(\displaystyle \| \sigma X\|_{\infty} \leqslant \sigma s (X) = \theta^{- 2} s (X) \leqslant W_{1 / 6} (t) .\)

For the bound, apply \(\ell\) to the decomposition in (18.228). The \(K'\) term vanishes and the \(B'\) term is \(h / 12\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \ell (\sigma X) - \frac{m}{12} = \ell (\sigma W) + \ell (\sigma D') + \ell (\sigma E) + \ell (\sigma L) + \frac{h - m}{12} .\)

For any array \(A\), continuity gives \(| \ell (\sigma A) | \leqslant \| \ell \| \sigma \|A\|_{\infty} \leqslant \| \ell \| \theta^{- 2} s (A)\). The triangle inequality and (18.230)– (18.232) give the claimed bound. There are four error terms, so the coefficient is exactly four; no direction-count factor is introduced because \(s\) bounds the full array norm.\(\Box\)

In the selected trace response, \(m\) is the finite-volume payment of the configuration under consideration; \(m / 12\) is not exactly the limiting flux. The construction supplies in addition the accuracy bound

\(\displaystyle |m + 12 \kappa | \leqslant t^{- 100},\)

where \(\kappa = s_G > 0\) is the slope constant (12.86). Together with the proposition, this gives

\(\displaystyle | \ell (\sigma X) + \kappa | \leqslant 4 \| \ell \| W_{- 1} (t) + \frac{t^{- 30} + t^{- 100}}{12},\)

and the right side tends to zero as \(t \to \infty\). The proposition and this consequence concern the evaluated array \(\sigma X\) of one finite-volume configuration. The response whose size and flux are used in Subsubsection 18.23.3 is instead the limit, along the separation ultrafilter at a fixed level, of transported finite-volume fields. Passing to it requires two further inputs, which do not follow from the proposition: a transport bound of size \(W_{- 1 / 4} (t)\) for the difference between the transported field and the core projection of \(\sigma X\) (the core projection does not increase the norm and does not change the flux), and the comparison of the arch scale with \(t\). With these, the finite-volume bounds take the form (18.213), continuity of the fixed linear maps transfers them to the ultrafilter limit, and division of the size bound by the arch scale gives a quantity tending to zero by (18.209). That passage is carried out, conditionally on these inputs, in Subsubsection 18.23.3.
